(ix)

INTRODUCTION
I, the Chairman of the Department-related Parliamentary Standing Committee on Health and
Family Welfare, having been authorized by the Committee to present the Report on its behalf,
hereby present this One-Hundred Eleventh Report of the Committee on the Functioning of new
AIIMS (Phase-I) under the Pradhan Mantri Swasthya Suraksha Yojana (PMSSY).
2. The erstwhile Committee had identified the subject “Functioning of AIIMS-like Institutions
Pradhan Mantri Swasthya Suraksha Yojana (PMSSY)” at its meeting held on 
*23rd September, 2015
and @29th September, 2016. The Committee at its meeting held on #25th October, 2017 decided
to constitute a Sub-Committee to examine the said subject. However, it could not conclude its
examination of the subject. The present Committee took up the subject for detailed examination at
its meeting held on ^1st October, 2018.
3. The Committee examined the subject, in its sittings held on 28th August, 16th October and
27th December, 2018.  During the course of the examination, it took oral evidences of representatives
of the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare and Directors of AIIMS, Raipur, AIIMS, Patna,
AIIMS, Jodhpur, AIIMS, Rishikesh, AIIMS, Bhopal, AIIMS Bhubaneshwar, AIIMS, Delhi and Bhopal
Memorial Hospital and Research Centre (BMHRC), Bhopal.  The Committee also heard the views
of the Secretary, Department of Health Research on the subject.
4. The Committee also benefitted from discussions with certain stakeholders and representatives
of the State Governments during its study visit of Sub-Committee I of the Committee to Imphal,
Shillong and Guwahati from 15th to 22nd January, 2018, visit of the Committee to Jodhpur,
Rishikesh and Bhopal from 1st to 8th July, 2018 and to Kolkata and Patna from 26th to 30th
November, 2018.
5. During the finalisation of its Report, the Committee relied upon the following documents/
papers:-
(i) Background Note on the subject received from Department of Health and Family Welfare;
(ii) Oral Evidences tendered by Secretary, Department of Health and Family Welfare;
(iii) Oral Evidence tendered and written submissions of Directors of AIIMS, Patna, Raipur,
Bhubaneswar, Jodhpur, Rishikesh, Bhopal and Delhi;
(v) Replies to the questionnaires received from the Department of Health and Family Welfare/
concerned AIIMS;
(vi) AIIMS Act, 1956 and AIIMS (Amendment) Act, 2012;
(vii) Annual Reports (2016-17) of AIIMS, Patna, Raipur, Bhopal, Bhubaneshwar, Jodhpur and
Rishikesh;
(viii) CAG Report No. 10 of 2018;
(xi)
* Rajya Sabha Parliamentary Bulletin Part II, No.54584, dated 28th September, 2015;
@ Rajya Sabha Parliamentary Bulletin Part II, No.55908, dated 21st October, 2016;
# Rajya Sabha Parliamentary Bulletin Part II, No.57018, dated 13th November, 2017; and
^ Rajya Sabha Parliamentary Bulletin Part II, No.58135, dated  4th October, 2018.

(ix) Articles published in various fora; and
(x) Papers/documents/feedback received during study visits.
6. The Committee while examining the subject came across certain common issues as concern
areas in the functioning of new AIIMS as mentioned below:-
(i) delay in construction work in all the new AIIMS due to non-performance of contracting
agencies and faulty design;
(ii) problem in procurement of medical equipment with respect to installation, delivery,
tendering of equipment, certification issues;
(iii) shortage of faculty as well as non-faculty;
(iv) lack of adequate number of functional OT s, beds/wards, Trauma and Emergency care
facilities, dialysis facilities, etc.
(v) Non-functional Super Speciality/Speciality Departments in all the six AIIMS;
(vi) Lack of co-ordination between agencies and lack of effective monitoring mechanism; and
(vii) Inadequate AYUSH facilities, etc.
7. The Committee considered the Draft Report and adopted the same in its meeting held on 27th
December, 2018.
8. For facility of reference and convenience, the observations and recommendations of the
Committee have been printed in bold letters in the body of the Report and also reproduced at the
end of the Report in ‘Observations/Recommendations-at a Glance’.
PROF. RAM GOPAL YADA V

1
REPORT
CHAPTER - I
INTRODUCTION
1.1 The Pradhan Mantri Swasthya Suraksha Yojana (PMSSY), a Central Sector Scheme, was
announced by the Honorable Prime Minister in the year 2003 with the objectives of correcting
regional imbalances in the availability of affordable/reliable tertiary healthcare services and augment
facilities for quality medical education in the country. The Cabinet Committee on Economic Affairs
(CCEA), in March 2006, approved the scheme of PMSSY proposed by the Ministry of Health and
Family Welfare for, setting up of six AIIMS like (AL) Institutions in the States of Bihar (Patna),
Madhya Pradesh (Bhopal), Odisha (Bhubaneswar), Rajasthan (Jodhpur), Chhattisgarh (Raipur) and
Uttarakhand (Rishikesh) at a cost of ` 332.00 crore per AL institution. Subsequently, due to
substantial changes in the cost and the scope of work, the Ministry in February 2010 submitted
revised cost estimates of ` 820.00 crore per AL institution to CCEA which was approved by the
latter on 19th March 2010. PMSSY has two components, viz (i) Setting up of AIIMS like Institutions
and (ii) Upgradation of Government Medical Colleges/Institutions (GMCs)
1.2 The Scheme is being taken up in phased wise manner. In March, 2006, the Government
approved Phase-I of the PMSSY which comprised of setting up of six AIIMS like institutions (later
re-named as new AIIMS) and up gradation of 13 existing State Government Medical Colleges/
Institutions (GMCs).The implementation of the project for the six AllMS-like institutions covered
three aspects i.e.  a) construction works, b) procurement of equipment and furniture and c)
recruitment. Besides, the  Director  of  each  AIIMS like Institute was  delegated  powers to  incur
expenditure  for  running the Institute.
1.3 The Background Note from the Ministry highlights in detail the status of upgradation of
GMCs as envisaged for improvement in health infrastructure through construction of Super Speciality
Blocks/Trauma Centres and through procurement of medical equipment for selected existing GMCs.
After a review of the coverage area of existing Government institutions, planned institutions and
recommendations of the Working Group for Tertiary Care institutions for 12th Five Year Plan, the
Government decided to increased number of new AIIMS and the GMCs in a phased manner. As
per the plan of action shared with the Committee, twenty new AIIMS are to be set up and 73
GMCs will be upgraded in six-phases as per the details given in table below:-
Phase-wise details of new AIIMS and up gradation of GMCs
   PHASES
PARTICULARS I II III IV V VI TOTAL
AIIMS 6 1 NIL 4 7 2 20
GMCs 13 6 39 13 2 NIL 73
1.4 The scheme envisaged 100 per cent funding by the Centre for setting up of new AIIMS while
funding for upgradation of GMCs has to be on sharing basis between the Central and State
Governments.

2
Legislation
1.5 The six AIIMS like Institutes were formally created by amending the All India Institute of
Medical Sciences Act, 1956 vide Amendment to the Act (No. 37 of 2012). The amended Act has
come into force with effect from 16th July 2012. The amendment has brought new  AIIMS under
the ambit  of AIIMS Act 1956 and also provides for  establishment  of  more  than  one  AIIMS
at  various  locations. Each of the new AIIMS is recognised as an Institution of National Importance
(INI) under the Act.
Organisational Structure in the Ministry and States
1.6 PMSSY division under the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare, Government of India
(Ministry), would be responsible for the overall administration of the scheme including its implementation
and monitoring. At the State level, the scheme is being implemented by the Department of Health
and Family Welfare headed by the Principal Secretary with the Directorate of Medical Education
and Research acting as the nodal office. At level of the institutions, the Dean/Medical Superintendent/
Principal of the concerned GMCI would be responsible for implementation of the scheme.
IMPLEMENTATION FRAMEWORK OF PMSSY
At the Central Government level
1.7 The Committee has been apprised that in March 2007, the Ministry engaged M/s HLL Life
Care Ltd. (M/s HLL) as an in-house consultant for providing technical assistance for the six new
AIIMS being set up during Phase-I. A Design and Detailed Project Report Consultant (DDPRC) for
each new AIIMS was engaged for preparation of master/layout plans, architectural and design
concepts, Detailed Project Reports (DPR) and tender documents. In addition, a Project Consultant
was engaged in each new AIIMS for evaluation of DPR and tender documents, management of bid
process, execution of civil works and quality assurance. A Project cell headed by the Director
concerned of the new AIIMS and consisting of seven other members i.e. Medical Superintendent,
Deputy Director (Administration), Financial Advisor, Superintending Engineer, Executive Engineer
(Civil), Executive Engineer (Electrical) and Administrative Officer was set up for day-to-day supervision
at the project level.
1.8 The Ministry informed the Committee that in case of upgradation of GMCs, M/s Hospital
Services Consultancy Corporation (M/s HSCC), M/s HLL and CPWD were appointed as Project
Consultants/nominated agencies for different GMCs on nomination basis both for civil works and
supply of equipment.
1.9 The Committee has been apprised that for establishing new AIIMS under Phase-II, the Ministry
adopted a different approach and appointed one agency i.e. M/s HSCC in July 2013 for turnkey
execution of the work instead of appointing different agencies and consultants for different services.
So far as procurement of equipment for new AIIMS under Phase-I is concerned, the Ministry
appointed M/s HLL as Procurement Support Agent (PSA) in March 2013. Procurement of equipment
for GMCs was to be made both by the State Government/GMC and the agency appointed by the
Ministry.
Role of the State Governments
1.10 The State Government concerned was responsible for providing land and other infrastructural

3
facilities such as water and power for setting up of new AIIMS. For upgradation of GMCs, the
concerned State Government was required to provide State’s share of funds, land (if required) and
also monitor project implementation. They were also to arrange manpower for the new facilities
and ensure regular maintenance of the upgraded GMCI.
STATUS OF NEW AIIMS
Budgetary Allocation
1.11 The Committee has been informed by the Ministry that the functional six AIIMS are provided
funds through Grants-in-Aid in General, Capital and Salaries category released by the Ministry. As
on now, the total budgetary allocation for all the six AIIMS has been ` 6750.80 crore out of which
` 4987.55 crore has been released.  Percentage of utilization of the funds released has been 73.88
percent. The allocation and expenditure Statement of Grants-in-Aid received by the Ministry through
budgetary allocation and released by the Ministry on basis of demand is given below:-

Status of Construction Work
1.12 The Committee has been given to understand that the six new AIIMS under Phase-I of
PMSSY have been established and are functional at Bhopal, Bhubaneswar, Jodhpur, Patna, Raipur
and Rishikesh with the facility of Super Specialty, Trauma & AYUSH block, Teaching block,
Administration block, Auditorium, and Medical/ Nursing Colleges. Out of total 19 GMCs upgradation
projects sanctioned in Phase I and II of the PMSSY , 15 projects have been completed. Up
gradation of GMCs undertaken in Phase III, Phases-IV and V A are not complete yet as the
construction activities at these Institutes are still in progress.  Details of the progress of construction
work alongwith target dates of the six AIIMS are given below: -

1.14 The Ministry of Health and Family Welfare informed the Committee that the Medical College
and Nursing College at all six AIIMS have started functioning. Regular MBBS batches have started
since 2012 in all six new AIIMS and six batches of MBBS students with 50 students in 2012 and
100 students from 2013 onwards are now receiving education. Further, regular nursing courses
have also started since 2013. Five batches of B.Sc. nursing students (60 Students per year from
2013) are now receiving education. The IPD, OPD and OT Services in all six new AIIMS are
operational.
Status of Faculty/Non-Faculty
1.15 The Committee has been informed that total sanctioned faculty strength and non faculty
strength is 305 and 3776 respectively in each of the six new AIIMS. The Ministry shared some
new initiatives to further strengthen the faculty position by way of bringing people on deputation
from other State Medical Colleges; provision of contractual appointment up to the age of 70 years
and empowering the Empowered Committee at the Institute to take a decision in the matter. The
Ministry informed that within next two years, the faculty strength will reach to 85-90% in all the
six new AIIMS.

1.16 The Committee has been informed that, under Phase-II of PMSSY , AIIMS at Rae Bareli and
up-gradation of 6 GMCs is being taken up. The Central Government has approved up-gradation of
3 GMCs under Phase III. Cabinet has approved establishment of four new AIIMS under Phase IV
at Mangalagiri in Nagpur in Kalyani and Gorakhpur with total financial implication of ` 5960 crore.
Hon’ble Finance Minister, during Budget Speech for the year 2015-16, announced setting up of five
new AIIMS in Assam, Himachal Pradesh, Jammu and Kashmir, Punjab and Tamil Nadu and second
AIIMS like Institute in Bihar. In his Budget Speech of 2017-18, the Finance Minister had announced
setting up of two more AIIMS in Jharkhand and Gujrat.  Recently, two new AIIMS have been
announced for Tamil Nadu and Telangana.
Upgradation Programme Under PMSSY
1.17 Another important component of the PMSSY is the upgradation programme which is carried
out in selected Government Medical Colleges (GMCs) by agencies appointed by the Government
of India under the direct supervision of the Central Government. The upgradation projects involved
augmentation of tertiary healthcare physical infrastructure of the respective GMC by constructing
Super Specialty Blocks (SSBs)/ Trauma Centers along with procurement of required medical equipment.
Post-Graduate seats and additional faculty posts as per norms will be created and filled up in these
GMCs by the respective State/UT Governments. The responsibility of running the facilities created
rests with the State Government. Total 73 GMCs have been approved under different phases of
PMSSY . The upgradation project has been facing various challenges like timely availability of
encumbrance free site in the GMCs, delay in the release of the State share, delays due to time
taken in deciding on the scope of the upgradation project in terms of the physical facilities, number
of beds, list of medical equipment etc. by the GMCs/ State Governments.
Purpose of the Report
1.18 The Committee notes that although six new AIIMS have been made functional, but many
facilities are yet to be made available for the patients. Even though the project was announced long
back in the year 2003, these AIIMS like institutions in phase I are yet to become fully functional
as an Institute of National Importance as AIIMS, Delhi. Till date, the Government has announced
over 20 AIIMS like Institutions in phased manner, but the projects under phase I still have huge
deficiencies and suffer from inadequate facilities. Keeping in view all these inadequacies, the Committee
decided to visit these institutions to have an account of the actual ground realities and obtain first
hand information on the problem areas in implementation of these projects. Prejudice can often be
peddled as analysis but the CAG’s observation created deep concern within the Committee with

7
reference to AIIMS, Patna and hence, the Committee decided to visit AIIMS, Patna specifically in
the second phase to assess the ground reality.  Due to time constraints, the Committee could not
visit AIIMS Raipur and Bhubaneswar, but has tried to assess the situation on the basis of the inputs
provided. The Committee would be visiting Raipur and Bhubaneswar to see the difference between
the projected and the ground reality.  At the cost of sounding repetitive, the Committee decided to
focus on AIIMS-wise examination so that a comparative assessment could be done when the
Committee takes up the subject for examination after the submission of the Action Taken Notes.
This report gives an overview of functioning of these six new AIIMS including status of construction
of projects, management, procurement of medical equipment and devices, status of faculty and non
faculty strength and other staff, patient statistics, medical and nursing colleges, specialty and super-
specialty services and facilities, emergency and trauma care services and others. Upgradation of
Government Medical Colleges is an equally important component of PMSSY , as it involves providing
tertiary healthcare through already established institutions . Therefore, the Committee has dealt with
the status of upgradation of GMCs in a separate chapter.
1.19 The Committee also notes that there have been several discrepancies in the factual information
submitted by the Ministry and the respective AIIMS that has led to errors/distortion in data.  For
instance, the number of surgeries performed in 2018 in AIIMS, Jodhpur, Patna, Rishikesh and
Raipur do not correspond to the data provided by the respective AIIMS. As per the data provided
by the Ministry, the total number of surgeries (major and minor) performed in AIIMS, Patna till
September, 2018 is 4224 whereas the Institute has submitted that it conducted a total of 3174
major and minor surgeries till November, 2018. Similar discrepancies have been observed in many
cases in most of the AIIMS. In case of AIIMS Rishikesh, the Ministry submitted that 5017 major
and 5836 minor surgeries have been conducted till September, 2018 but the data provided by the
Institute states that 3278 major and 4153 minor surgeries have been conducted by the Institute.
The Committee is highly disappointed by the wide variance in the data provided by the Ministry
and the Institute which has made the task of the Committee difficult.

CHAPTER-II
AIIMS, BHOPAL
2.1 AIIMS Bhopal was inaugurated on 26th January, 2013 and is one of the apex healthcare
institutes with a mission to establish a centre of excellence in medical education, training, healthcare
and research imbued with scientific culture, compassion for the sick and commitment to serve the
under-served. The Institute admitted first batch of MBBS in September, 2012, first batch of B.Sc.
(Nursing) in July, 2013and first batch of PG students in January 2017.
2.2 During its visit to AIIMS, Bhopal in July, 2018, the Committee had the opportunity to observe
the infrastructure of AIIMS, Bhopal. The Committee noted that the buildings of hospital, AYUSH,
medical college were completed and functional to an extent. As compared to the year 2017, the
numbers have increased in terms of OPD footfall, major and minor surgeries, IPD admissions,
Laboratory and radiological investigations in 2018. The hospital has 511 bed strength with average
OPD footfall of 1924 patients and 9212 IPD admissions till 30th October 2018. A total of 5819
minor surgeries and 1860 major surgeries were conducted at the institute.

2.4 The Committee observes that the opening balance has always been on the higher side in
AIIMS Bhopal which reflects the Institute’s inability to optimally utilize its funds. The opening
balance was as high as ` 234.49 crore for the year 2017-18. In the year 2016-17, out of the total
allocated funds of ` 331.21 crore, the actual expenditure incurred was only ` 96.71 crore. The
Committee was informed that there was underutilization of funds till 2015-16 due to lags in
construction activity, recruitment and hospital services. The Committee was also given to understand
that since 2017-18, the expenses have been higher than the allocated funds as the pace of activity
in construction, recruitment and hospital services has substantially increased and therefore the fund
utilization is likely to be closer to budget projection.
2.5 The CAG Report highlighted that out of the total allocated funds of `533.10 crore to AIIMS,
Bhopal from the years 2011-17, funds amounting to `275.01 crore have remained unutilized which
is approximately 50% of the total allocated funds. The CAG has reported that the main reasons
for unspent funds were slow progress of construction work and failure on the part of the Institute

10
to factor in available funds while submitting requirement of funds to the Ministry and delays in
filling up of vacancies.
2.6 The Committee notes with concern the observations of CAG that only 50% (approximately)
of the funds allocated for AIIMS, Bhopal had been utilized between 2011 and 2017. The
Committee is of the firm view that the budgetary allocation should have been properly
utilized under a strategic plan of action to prevent underutilization. The Committee observes
the increasing trend of expenditure since 2017-18 when the expansion work got momentum.
The Committee also observes that allocation of funds to the tune of  ````` 99.90 crore in the
financial year 2017-18 was less than the actual expenditure of ````` 237.24 crore in the said
year and the remaining expenditure was met with unspent balances of the prior period.
2.7 The Committee, during its visit to AIIMS, Bhopal observed that construction activities
were still going on. Even the residential accommodation facilities for doctors and students
were yet to be completed by CPWD.  The Committee feels that the slow pace of progress
would have adversely affected the quality of healthcare services and education being imparted
at the Institute. The Committee calls upon the Institute to undertake prudent assessment
of fund requirement and accordingly project its budgetary demand with due justification.
2.8 The Committee is not convinced with the submission of the institute that the
underutilization of funds was due to undue lags in the construction activities, recruitment
process and hospital services. The Committee, therefore, recommends that AIIMS, Bhopal
must formulate a correct estimate for projects, prepare DPR and ensure administrative
approvals in order to complete the remaining projects within set timelines and at the
estimated cost.
Status of Construction Work
2.9 The Committee has been apprised that the construction work of the institute has been divided
into four packages. The details as enumerated below:
(i) Package I includes construction of medical college, nursing college, hostels, service
buildings, auditorium etc.
(ii) Package II consists of construction of Hospital and Heating, Ventilation and Air Conditioning
(HVAC) works for both Packages I & II.
(iii) Package III includes Electrical Services.
(iv) Package IV consists of Estate Services.
2.10 In response to a specific query regarding the present status of construction activities, the
Committee has been informed that under Package I, 98.87% work has been completed and 1.13%
is in progress. 97.8% work has been completed under Package II, while 2.2 % work is in
progress. The project consultant has served a snag list which is being attended by the construction
agency. Under Package III, 97.38 % work has been completed while 2.62 % work is in progress.
Under Package IV , 60% work has been completed and 40 % work is in progress. It was brought
to the notice of the Committee that contract of M/s IVCRL was terminated on 10th August 2016
and remaining work was assigned to CPWD. It was added that the CPWD had completed almost

11
35% of work and the remaining work was likely to be completed by November 2018. As regards
Package IV , none of the works except the solar water heating system adhered to timelines set. The
physical progress of water supply line, sewerage and drainage work and road works has crossed
90% of the completion target. Further, only 50% of the horticulture work has been completed
which was expected to be completed by November, 2018. Only 45% of the other development
works including main gate, connecting bridges, covered walkways, footpath work etc. have been
completed while the set time line for the same was February, 2018. HLL has been deployed as
Project Consultant to monitor the progress of work.
2.11 As regards the hostel and residential accommodation facilities, a total of 1341 rooms have
been constructed in various hostels out of which 154 are double occupancy and remaining are
single occupancy. Further, 185 flats for residential complex have been constructed.
2.12 On a specific query about the undue delay in the construction work resulting in cost escalation
from the original estimates, the Ministry informed that there is delay in construction work especially
with respect to the work under Package-IV and subsequent cost escalation due to cost index but
overall deviation was well within 10 % of overall project cost. It was also submitted that the
project consultant and DDPR did not respond as per contract provisions and left work due to
which progress of different packages got affected. The construction agency of Package-IV (Estate
Services) did not provide civil work which adversely affected the progress of all packages. Non
deployment of adequate manpower and resources by the construction agency, non approval of
drawings, sanction of extra/deviate items by DDPRC and Project Consultant are the main reasons
for delay of construction activities under all the packages. Other reasons for reasons for delays
are as under:
(i) Delayed approval of drawings of UG Service Building, revision of drawing of PG hostel
5th and 6thFloor, auditorium, structural and internal finishing drawing, plumbing drawings
etc. have been attributed as the reasons for delay under package-I.
(ii) Several areas of the hospital building like OT area, Oncology Bunker, Radiology, Kitchen
area were put on hold under Package-II that resulted in delay. Delayed approval of IPD
flooring scheme, Heating, Ventilation, and Air Conditioning (HV AC) scheme and plumbing
drawings were major issues. Further, there was abnormal increase in the number of
toilets in the Hospital area (approx. 250 Nos.) which has resulted in huge deviation in
plumbing items. Quantities of mainly fire fighting system, HV AC system, internal electrical
work and unsatisfactory comments on deviations by DDPRC further resulted into delay.
(iii) Under Package-III, civil work related to installation of DG sets, transformers, panels
were not completed which ultimately contributed in delay of completion of work. The
scheme for DG room CFM both for service Substation and Library Substation were not
finalized. Complete street light scheme and finalization for cable lying also resulted in
delay.
(iv) Under Package-IV , construction agency had not deployed manpower and resources as
per time and maximum levy of compensation imposed as per contract provisions resulted
in delay. Due to delay in Estate work (Package-IV) progress of other packages also got
affected.

12
2.13 The CAG Report highlighted that the State Government allotted 154 acres for the institute out
of which the institute could take possession of only 138.42 acres of land as the remaining area
was under encroachment. Further, under Packages I, II and IV , excess payment of ` 2.08 crore
has been made to the contractor. In the case of contract for Estate Services relating to new
AIIMS-Bhopal, Clause 2 of the agreement provided that a penalty of nine per cent will be levied
if the contractor failed to maintain required progress as per the terms of the contract and there
were delays of work of six months. The contract was awarded to the contractor in February 2012
with due date of completion as 1st February 2013. The contractor did not complete the work
within the stipulated time and was granted Extension of Time (EoT) eight times up to 30 June
2016. As the work still remained incomplete, the institute rescinded the work in August 2016. The
Institute Management informed that EoT case has been prepared by the project consultant and will
be placed before the Empowered Review Committee for a decision on recovery of compensation.
2.14 The Committee observes that the delay in the construction work was mainly due to
non performance by the selected construction agencies along with improper planning and
ineffective monitoring. Though the construction status is near completion in the first three
Packages, under Package IV only 60% of the work is complete. It is high time that the
Institute should strengthen the existing infrastructure and strive to complete the remaining
work within the stipulated time limits as it has been six years since the institute has been
operationalised and the construction work is still incomplete. The Committee is inclined to
observe that the selection of construction agencies was devoid of merit and transparency
resulting in substantial time and cost overrun. The Committee, therefore, recommends that
stringent monitoring mechanism should be put in place to ensure timely completion of work
to avoid time and cost overruns. The Committee recommends that the Ministry should take
necessary steps to take possession of the area which is under encroachment. The Committee
is concerned to note that the project consultant HLL has miserably failed in proper monitoring
of the project and its entire approach reflects unprofessionalism. The Committee, therefore,
recommends suitable remedial action including fixing accountability and appropriate action
keeping in view the observations of the CAG .
Procurement of Medical Equipment
2.15 The Committee has noted that 113 capital equipment and medical devices have been purchased
by AIIMS, Bhopal, out of which, 5 have been procured on proprietary certificate basis. In response
to a query whether AIIMS continue to issue directions/specifications to purchase medical equipment
with USFDA Certification only, it was clarified that the institute has set up two Technical Specialization
Committees (TSCs) as per guidelines issued by the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare ( vide
circular No. G-25012/4/2011-ME-1 dated 2nd June, 2015. While one Committee examines the
procurement of equipment up to estimated cost of ` 30 lakh; the second TSC provides generic and
broad based technical specifications for the equipment to be procured which are not specific to
any particular brand/make to encourage more participation. USFDA Certification is taken into
consideration by the Committee while preparing the technical specifications and other parameters
as fixed by the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare.
2.16 In a specific query regarding number of medical equipment with USFDA certification or other
certification procured at the institute during the last five years, the Ministry informed that out of

13
66 equipment, 50 equipment are with USFDA certification and 16 have certification other than
USFDA. Out of these 66 equipment, five equipment have not been installed yet.
2.17 The Committee understands that USFDA is a regulatory mechanism/certification for
protecting and promoting public health by way of control and supervision of food and drugs
including medical devices with specific reference to the United States but there is hardly any
country in the world besides the United States which makes a provision for allowing USFDA
as a mandatory condition. The Committee fails to comprehend the reasons for procuring 50
equipment with USFDA certification, some of which are easily available with CE/BIS certification.
The Committee, therefore, recommends to the institute to procure equipment with CE/BIS
certification so as to encourage Medical Device Industry in India and participate in the
Government’s “Make in India” initiative.
2.18 On being asked about whether any analysis that has been done for procuring the medical
equipment with respect to the cost of equipment vis-à-vis  its actual utility in catering to the needs
of local population at AIIMS, Bhopal, it was informed that no such analysis was undertaken till
date.
2.19 The Committee fails to understand that no cost-benefit analysis was undertaken by the
institute before procuring medical equipment in AIIMS, Bhopal. The Committee wonders
how AIIMS, Bhopal procured equipment without ascertaining the availability of space, manpower
and infrastructure required for installation etc. The Committee, therefore, recommends to
the Ministry that proper assessment with respect to cost-benefit analysis should be done
before the purchase of any equipment.
2.20 As regards the reasons for procurement of equipment on a single bid basis in AIIMS, Bhopal
during last 3 years, the Committee has been informed that three equipment namely: (i) endobronchial
ultrasound system (Olympus medical system corporation); (ii) Portable RO/WTU and (iii) Hemodialysis
machine have been purchased on a single bid basis so far. On the steps initiated to avoid single
bid purchase, so as to obtain competitive pricing, the Committee has been informed that besides
uploading the NIT on the portal of Central Public Portal, Tender wizard and AIIMS own website,
the advertisement is published in Newspaper for wide publicity.
2.21 The Committee would like to be apprised of the circumstances under which the process
and procedure of single bid system was adhered to in purchasing three equipment viz. (i)
endobronchial ultrasound system (ii) Portable RO/WTU and (iii) Hemodialysis machine.   The
Committee, in this connection, strongly recommends for procurement of equipment on
multiple bidding basis in future so as to have fair competitive pricing and availability of
more options for procurement of best and economical equipment.
2.22 The Committee has been informed that 21 types of equipment costing ` 27.9 crore have been
received by the institute but have not been installed yet and 25 types of equipment costing ` 84.7
crore have not been yet received. CAG Report in 2018 highlighted similar instances wherein nine
equipment costing ` 10.38 crore remained uninstalled for a period ranging from 13 to 40 months.
It, further, highlighted that there were delays ranging from 15 to 26 months in the installation of
4 equipment costing ` 30 lakh.

14
2.23 The Committee believes that the lackadaisical approach of the institute has resulted
in non installation of the equipment which has delayed operationalization of the critical
healthcare facilities, thus, depriving the patients of key diagnostic and in-patient facilities.
The Committee, therefore, recommends to the institute to have a system in place for
ensuring timely procurement as well as installation of the equipment so that the equipment
are properly and optimally utilized for the purpose for which they were procured. The
Committee also recommends for immediate installation of 21 types of equipment costing `````
27.9 crore that have been procured but not installed yet. The Committee further calls upon
the Ministry to look into matter of procurement of 25 equipment costing ````` 84.7 crore which
are yet to be received and installed in AIIMS, Bhopal. In view of the delays in procurement
and installation of equipment in the institute, the Committee  impresses upon the Ministry
to ensure effective monitoring through ABC analysis (Always a better control) by the Technical
Specification Committees at the Ministry and institutional level to ensure  much needed
synchronization and coordination of activities  with regard to procurement and installation
of equipment.
Status of Faculty/Non Faculty Strength
2.24 The Committee has been informed that only 141 faculty posts have been filled up out of total
sanctioned strength of 305 indicating that 164 faculty posts are vacant. As regards non-faculty
posts, out of the total strength of 3772 posts, only 792 posts are filled up. Out of the total filled
up posts of non faculty staff, 44 are filled on regular basis, 324 on contractual basis and 424 have
been outsourced.
2.25 The Committee learnt that though the institute started functioning in the year 2012, the
recruitment rules for the non-faculty posts were formally adopted by the institute Body in its
second meeting on 20.7.2017. Consequently, the recruitment to various non-faculty posts was
started in a phased manner. In the meantime, many non-faculty posts on deputation basis were
filled. During the year 2011, Ministry of Health and Family Welfare took up the first recruitment
process for various faculty posts. The process for recruitment of faculty and non-faculty is still
in progress in a phased manner. The Committee was informed that the recruitment of faculty and
staff is challenging due to delayed adoption of Recruitment Rules in the year 2017.
2.26 The Commit tee notes that there is a huge shortage of faculty at nursing college and other
support staff. In few key Departments like Urology, Surgical Oncology, Psychiatry, Paediatric
Surgery, Nuclear Medicine, Nephrology, Endocrinology and Metabolism, Cardiothoracic Surgery
and Cardiology, there is only one faculty in-position. In Departments like Gastroenterology,
Medical Oncology, Neonatology, Physical Medicine Rehabilitation and Surgical Gastroenterology,
there is no faculty at all at present. In Departments like Nephrology, Cardiology, Cardiothoracic
Surgery, Nuclear Medicine, Urology and Pulmonary Medicine, the posts of Professor, Additional
Professor are lying vacant and these Departments are presently being run only by Assistant
Professors single handedly. Also, in Departments like Surgical Oncology, Psychiatry, Pediatrics
Surgery, Endocrinology and Metabolism, only one Associate Professor is single handedly managing
their Departments. Patients who require emergency care and the facilities for Gastroenterology,
Medical Oncology, Clinical Hematology, Nuclear Medicine and Radiotherapy are referred to other
hospital.

15
2.27 The Committee is dissatisfied with the vacancy positions in different Departments in
AIIMS, Bhopal, where out of 305 sanctioned faculty posts, 164 posts are lying vacant. The
Committee is apprehensive that for an AIIMS like institution, such a gap in workforce
would adversely affect the functional capacity of the institute. The Committee calls upon
the institute to accelerate its administrative procedure for filling up the vacant posts within
a time bound manner. The Ministry also needs to closely monitor the efforts made by the
institute in this regard. The Committee, also, recommends that the vacant posts should be
filled on regular basis within a specified period of time. Measures must be taken for filling
up the vacant posts on permanent basis to ensure continuity and smooth functioning of the
institute.
2.28  The Committee expresses its concern that out of total sanctioned strength of 3776 non
faculty posts in AIIMS, Bhopal, only 792 posts have been filled up, including 424 posts that
have been actually outsourced. In Committee’s opinion non-faculty staff is the backbone of
any medical institution in terms of its successful and smooth functioning. With such a wide
gap in the in-position strength against the sanctioned non faculty posts, it would have been
extremely difficult to manage the day to day functional equilibrium of various Departments
of the institute in an integrated manner. The Committee, therefore, recommends for expediting
the appointment process of non-faculty staff on a regular basis so that all the Departments
have adequate man power to cater to all aspects of patient care. The Committee is of the
considered opinion that a centralized recruitment process for filling up of non-faculty posts
would address the entire non faculty shortage issue and also ensure a transparent recruitment
process. The Committee strongly recommends the Ministry to initiate this process on an
immediate basis.
2.29 The CAG in its Report has drawn attention to Rule 181 (b) of General Financial Rules, which
stipulate that an advertised tender enquiry is required to be issued for award of any work or
service valued at above ` 10 lakh. According to CAG, the AIIMS-Bhopal engaged an agency for
provision of manpower without tendering as stipulated in the GFRs. The Institute had paid ` 5.13
crore up to March 2015 to the agency in addition to service charges @ 1.96 per cent. The
engaging of an agency for providing staff without following the GFR provisions for procurement
of services was irregular.
2.30 The Committee is displeased over the fact that the AIIMS, Bhopal engaged an agency
for provision of manpower without tendering in violation of General Financial Rules. This
lapse deprived the institute from the benefit of the competitive bidding while making
provision for requisite manpower . The Committee, therefore, recommends to the Ministry
to ensure effective monitoring of the whole process of tendering in compliance with GFR
provisions.
Specialty/Super Specialty Departments
2.31 The Committee has been informed that 11 Super specialty services and 16 Specialty Services
are functional at the institute. The following information has been submitted by AIIMS, Bhopal w.r.t.
the number of functional Departments along with the in position/vacancy in various Specialty and
Super Specialty Departments:-

2.32 The Committee reprimands the AIIMS, Bhopal management over the fact that Departments
of Rheumatology, TB and Chest, Gastroenterology, Surgical Gastroenterology, Urology, Medical
Oncology, Clinical Hematology and Neonatology are still not functional at the Institute. The
Committee is of the view that these are key Departments in providing medical aid and in
some cases these Departments can provide life saving assistance to people. The Committee,
therefore, recommends to the Institute to start these Specialty and Super Specialty Department
as early as possible to address sufferings of patient.
2.33 The Committee is deeply anguished to take into account with the present status of the
faculty at the Specialty and Super Departments at the Institute. Only 17 posts have been
filled up against 100 sanctioned posts in the Super Specialist Departments and 83 posts are
lying vacant. Not only that, many Departments such as Cardiology, Cardiothoracic and
Vascular Surgery, Psychiatry, Nephrology, Surgical Oncology, Endocrinology and Metabolic
Diseases, Pediatric Surgery and Nuclear Medicine are being run by a single faculty.  The
Committee is surprised to observe the sorry state of functioning of AIIMS, Bhopal and
concludes that with such huge vacancy, the Institute cannot achieve its objectives of providing
tertiary level health care services to the people. The Committee believes that there is an
urgent need to address the deficiency in the manpower at the Specialist and Super Specialist
level. The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends to the institute to fill up the vacant
posts at the earliest. The Committee also recommends that AIIMS Bhopal should explore
innovative means like special packages/incentives to attract Specialist/Super Specialists from
premier institutes in its different Departments to ensure better functioning of AIIMS Bhopal.
Medical Education
2.34 The Committee has been informed that AIIMS, Bhopal started with an intake of 50 medical
Sl. No. Department Sanctioned Working V acant

18
students in the first batch in the year 2012 and with intake of 100 students every year in the
subsequent years. Over the last 6 years (2012-17), a total of 550 medical students have been
educated and first batch admitted in 2012 have completed their internship in 2018. Further, post
graduation has been initiated in 21 specialties with annual intake that will exceed 100 post-graduates
in the year 2019. In addition, college of nursing offering B.Sc. (Hons.) has also been set up to
supplement the training and service delivery requirements for nursing personnel in the country. It
was started in 2013 with intake of 58 students and 60 students every year in subsequent years.
As regard, the status of PG courses at AIIMS Bhopal and sanctioned vis-à-vis  filled seats of PG
courses Department-wise, the Committee has been informed that there are a total of 33 PG seats
in 17 different Departments at the Institute, out of which 24 seats have been filled as on January,
2018. The Institute will start new courses i.e. DM in Neurology and Pulmonary Medicine, M.Ch.
in Burn and Plastic Surgery and Neurosurgery, Ph.D. program and M.Sc. Nursing from January,
2019.
2.35 The Committee has observed that the admissions in MBBS and B.Sc. Nursing courses
are being done with an intake of 100 and 60 students respectively at AIIMS, Bhopal. The
number of admissions in MD/MS courses in different disciplines has also increased. Out of
33 sanctioned seats for January, 2018 session, 24 admissions have already been done. The
Committee was given to understand by the Ministry that two seats in Departments of
Microbiology and one seat in  Immunohematology and Blood Transfusion were sanctioned
in January, 2018. However, utmost to the surprise of the Committee, none of the seats in
these two Departments have been filled up yet. Further, in Anatomy and Pediatrics Departments,
three seats have been sanctioned for each Department and only one seat in each Department
has been filled up. The Committee, while expressing its serious concern, strongly, recommends
that the Ministry to evaluate the reasons for non-filling up of the seats and undertake
corrective measures to fill up the vacant seats strictly in a time-bound manner so that the
intended benefit to the beneficiaries be made available and the scarce resources, that have
immense opportunity costs, are used in a judicious manner. The Committee, further, recommends
to the Institute to expedite the process of starting new courses within a stipulated time so
that students do not suffer due to non-availability of any medical Department in the Institute.
2.36 The Committee expects AIIMS, Bhopal to deliver quality medical education in all its
Departments so that passing out students serve the society in an excellent manner. For this,
basic infrastructure at the Institute is required apart from improved student faculty ratio
to produce well trained doctors. The Committee also believes that the retention rate of
passing out students in the Institute would determine the standard of quality medical
education being imparted in the Institute.
2.37 In response to a query regarding clinical training of MBBS/Nursing graduates, it was informed
that in the first year, the undergraduate students are imparted training in basic sciences, for which
lecture halls, practical laboratories, and dissection halls were available. In August, 2013, when the
medical students completed their first year of training, outpatient and day-care services had already
initiated and were in full swing in Departments of Medicine, Surgery, Obstetrics and Gynecology,
and Pediatrics where initial training is imparted to medical students. Some disciplines (such as
Obstetrics, and Psychiatry) where deficiency in clinical material was felt, collaborations with existing

19
facilities in nearby Central Government run hospital (BMHRC Bhopal) and State Government run
medical college (GMC Bhopal) were done. It was ensured that students were exposed to all aspects
necessary in their medical training.
2.38  As regards protocol being followed for starting clinical training of B.Sc.(H) nursing students,
the Committee has been informed that the Institute formulates clinical rotation plan, learning objectives
and clinical evaluation plan based on curriculum requirement. An orientation programme to brief
about theory and clinical rearing details is being conducted in the beginning of new session every
year; demonstrations and return demonstrations are conducted in mission foundation lab. The
Committee learnt that there are 4 UG students per senior resident in a ward and number of students
per ward depends upon number of patients in that ward. Students are supervised and guided by
faculty and senior residents and norms of UG and PG teacher as laid down by MCI are followed.
The Institute follows standards of JIPMER, Puducherry and AIIMS Delhi pertaining to matters of
quality of training.
2.39 In a specific query regarding Departments where undergraduates had to go to other hospitals
to complete their clinical training due to non-availability of patients, the Committee was informed
that MBBS students were sent to Government Medical College for attending the medico-legal
autopsies which is a part of their training curriculum in Forensic Medicine and toxicology. However,
since  mortuary services at AIIMS  started 25/9/2017, the students are being imparted the training
in FMT at AIIMS Bhopal. Similarly, the nursing students were sent to BMHRC for practical
exposure in cardiology, nephrology and mental health nursing. But with the recruitment of faculty
in these disciplines at AIIMS Bhopal and initiation of services in these respective disciplines, the
training of nursing is now being done in-house w.e.f. January, 2018.
2.40 The Committee is of the strong view that for a medical or nursing college, it is
imperative to have more exposure of medical cases for students to attain clinical skills in
their respective fields. The Committee is highly apprehensive as to how the Institute will
accomplish the objective to impart training to the PG students, MD/MS, Ph.D. students with
inadequate faculty and limited OPD. The Committee is also skeptical of the clinical training
of the first batch of MBBS and Nursing students who have completed their internship this
year. Since the Institute had huge number of vacancies in various departments, it is obvious
that students would not have been given adequate clinical training as part of their curriculum.
This is not a good indicator for AIIMS, Bhopal for meeting the expectation of high medical
education from an institute of national importance. The Committee has reasons to apprehend
that inadequate training of medical and nursing students will only produce partially skilled
and half trained doctors in the country that would, in fact, become a liability rather than
assets of the country. Therefore, in order to ensure the prospective future of aspiring
doctors and nurses, the Institute must strive towards providing all the requisite resources
for the students to help them to hone their skills adequately during their academic years
so that they acquire desired level of knowledge during their stay in AIIMS.
2.41 The Committee, however, appreciates the efforts made by the Institute in expanding
their medical education services. The Committee feels that paramedical education and training
should also be included in the medical education services at AIIMS, Bhopal as the available
requisite infrastructure can help produce a competent technical workforce in different clinical

20
fields. The Committee, therefore, recommends to the Ministry to contemplate about introducing
the paramedical education and training courses at AIIMS, Bhopal on an urgent basis to
address the problem of shortage of technical manpower in clinical fields and optimize the
utilization of infrastructure of the hospital set up.
Operation Theaters
2.42 The Committee has been informed that the waiting time for surgical procedure varies from
one week to six months due to limited availability of OTs at present. The Ministry has submitted
that total 32 OTs have been sanctioned at AIIMS, Bhopal out of which 24 are modular OTs, 7 for
trauma and emergency unit and one for radiotherapy. It further submitted that currently, 11 OTs
of various surgical disciplines are functional. The Institute is in the process of equipping new OTs
with Medical Gas Pipe Line which is in the final stage of completion. At present, HLL is constructing
OTs at on turnkey basis and the process of procuring OT tables and other equipment is also
underway.
2.43 The Committee is concerned to note that the waiting time in surgical procedures
varies from one week to six months due to limited availability of operation theaters at
present.  The Committee is of the view that AIIMS, Bhopal was established to provide
immediate medical care to the nearby population who rush to AIIMS, Delhi or other adjoining
facilities for specialized care. Therefore, long waiting time at AIIMS, Bhopal for the patients
requiring surgeries would defeat the very purpose of having an AIIMS like Institution in
the State.  It would further increase the out of pocket expenditure of those who cannot
afford high medical expenses. The Committee, therefore, recommends to the Institute to
expedite the construction of OTs and make them fully functional. The Committee would
also like to be apprised of the number of surgeries being conducted (both major and minor)
per day per OT to ensure optimum utilization of the existing functional OTs.
Status of Intensive Care Units and Trauma/Emergency Facilities
2.44 W ith regard to the status of ICUs, the Committee has been informed that 10 bedded ICU
under Department of Anesthesiology, 4 bedded Pediatric ICU and 8 bedded Neurology ICU are
functional at present. The ICUs are well equipped with Critical Care Monitors with one central
work station, fully functional MGPS with (2 Oxygen: 1 vacuum: 1 Medical Air), Ventilators. The
usage statistics of the ventilator service for the year 2015 was 255, 299 in 2016 and 500 in 2017.
A total of 35-55 patients are utilizing ICU facilities on monthly basis. As regards the disaster
preparedness in terms of medical facilities at AIIMS Bhopal, the Institute is gearing up to deal with
casualties in case of any major disaster and is in the process of establishing fully functional 24X7
Trauma and Emergency Services.
2.45 The Committee expresses its deep concern to view that for an institution like AIIMS,
Bhopal the facilities for intensive care units are inadequate. Though the existing units are
functional with optimal equipment, there is scope for expansion in terms of separate ICU
for different Departments like Cardiology, Medicine, etc. The Institute has partially started
24x7 Trauma and Emergency services. However, there are just two faculty for Trauma and
Emergency center which is inadequate to provide dedicated trauma/emergency facilities and
to deal with mass casualties. A lot more needs to be done by AIIMS, Bhopal as a tertiary

21
level medical care hospital to cater to the victims of increasing road accidents and other
trauma cases. The Committee would like to emphasize that the Institute should have a well
developed infrastructure and a better equipped set up with state-of-art medical facilities to
deal with trauma cases. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Institute should
work towards enhancing its spheres/perception of healthcare services to stand out as the
most efficient healthcare provider in Madhya Pradesh.  The Institute needs to increase ICUs
and trauma facilities on a priority basis so that it becomes the best referral centre in the
entire State for Trauma/Emergency Care.
Status of Beds and Wards Strength
2.46 The Committee has been informed that 36 wards are operational in AIIMS Bhopal. 20 beds
have been allocated for Super Specialty and 30 for Specialties. The wards have 4 HDU beds and
rest general beds separately allocated for male and female. The wards have two isolation rooms,
doctor duty room, nursing in-charge room, store room, small teaching room and utility room.
Doctors (faculty, senior residents, junior residents), nursing in-charge, nursing officers, nursing
orderly, sanitary attendant and security personnel are present in each ward. The sanctioned bed
strength of AIIMS Bhopal is 960 beds out of which only 511 beds are functional till date.  More
beds are being added with the handing over of functionally ready hospital areas. Simultaneously,
the procurement of hospital furniture (Beds, Side tables etc.) is also underway. The Department-
wise occupancy of beds is as follows:-

2.47 The Committee is constrained to express its displeasure that functional bed strength
is approximately 50 per cent of the sanctioned bed strength at the Institute.  In Private and
Ophthalmology wards there are 16 beds out of which only 2 are occupied.  It was noted that
there is no separate ward for Cardiology, Nephrology or Oncology at the Institute. Given the
high incidence of number of patients under these categories, the existing facilities are quite
inadequate. Further, the occupancy rate is less than fifty per cent in almost all the wards
except medical, orthopaedics, surgical, and ICUs. The Committee is concerned to note the
abysmally low level of bed occupancy for a tertiary care institute of the level of AIIMS. The
Committee expects AIIMS Bhopal to improve its perception as a healthcare provider so that
bed occupancy/utilization increases substantially. Needless to mention, the Committee is of
the considered opinion that the merger of BMHRC with AIIMS, Bhopal assumes critical
importance to ensure optimum utilization of the tertiary healthcare facilities in AIIMS,
Bhopal.
Dialysis Facilities
2.48 The Committee has been informed that three dialysis machines have been made functional
since May, 2018 and approximately 16 patients (86 procedures) have been catered to till the month
of August.
2.49 The Committee expresses its dissatisfaction that there are only two dialysis machines
at the hospital. The Committee also notes huge shortfall in manpower resources especially
in the Nephrology Department. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Institute
should strive to increase the number of dialysis machines as well as the requisite manpower
to upgrade their Nephrology Department for effective healthcare service delivery.
Blood Bank Facility
2.50 It has been informed by the Institute that blood bank facility will be made available as
equipment for blood components has been ordered and license application is being processed.
2.51 The Committee is disturbed to note the non-availability of blood bank facility at the
Institute even after five years of functional IPD services. The Committee is of the view
that blood bank services are an integral part of any hospital system. The Committee,
therefore, strongly recommends to the Institute to expedite the setting up of fully functional
blood bank facility without further delay.
Issue of Contractual Appointments
2.52 The Committee observes that many employees at AIIMS, Bhopal are working on contractual
basis. From the information given, it was noted that in non-faculty posts, 2 posts in Group A
(faculty Nursery + tutor/Clinical Instructor) have been filled up by contractual appointments. Moreover,
out of 3772 sanctioned posts under non-faculty category, a total 324 posts are filled on contractual
basis. In faculty category, out of 305 sanctioned posts, 12 are on contract basis.
2.53 The Committee has noted that in AYUSH facility at AIIMS, Bhopal, Homoeopathy, Ayurveda
and Yoga services are being offered on a large scale and till September, 2018, 30302, 20363, 20778
patients in each of Homoeopathy, Ayurveda and Yoga respectively have been catered to at AYUSH
Hospital.

23
2.54 The Committee was informed that the doctors of AYUSH Hospital have been working
on contractual basis for the past several years. The Committee during its study visit
found that the OPD footfall in the AYUSH centre was much higher despite limited
infrastructure. The Committee appreciates the work ethics of the three AYUSH doctors in
treating a large number of patients having faith in the Indian system of Medicine. The
Committee feels that given the positive response towards AYUSH services, the Ministry
should contemplate of having a permanent staff instead of engaging contractual workforce.
This would ensure job stability and security to the employees and in turn the organization
would be benefitted from huge experience of these doctors gained during their service at
AYUSH Hospital. The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends the Department to revisit
the rules regarding contractual appointments so as to regularize the existing AYUSH
doctors to permanent appointments in view of their dedicated service and outstanding
performance.
Merger of Bhopal Memorial Hospital and Research Centre (BMHRC) and AIIMS, Bhopal
2.55 The Committee during its visit to AIIMS, Bhopal as well as to BMHRC observed that their
facilities are in close proximity with adequate infrastructure and services to cater to the medical
needs of huge population. The Committee was informed that BMHRC is a 350 bedded hospital
commissioned in year 2000 with 8 mini units (primary healthcare centers). From September, 2015,
administrative control of BMHRC was taken over directly by Department of Health Research,
Ministry of Health and Family Welfare. The hospital is running 18 departments with OPD footfall
for hospital and mini units around 60,000 per month and IPD around 900 per month with an
average OPD of 2000 per day. It caters to nearly 4.5 lakh gas victims and their children for
healthcare.  The Committee observed that despite adequate infrastructure, there are few doctors at
the Institute resulting in under-utilization of the available resources.
2.56 In response to a query regarding the present status of the proposed merger of BMHRC and
AIIMS, Bhopal, it was clarified that the matter is under consideration of the Government. A core
group has been constituted by PMSSY – MoHFW under the chairmanship of Director, AIIMS
Raipur to recommend approach for proposed merger of BMHRC, Bhopal with AIIMS, Bhopal. A
meeting of core group was held on 17.10.17 and they decided to constitute a Sub-Committee to
look at the issue of merger of human resources including both Faculty and Non-Faculty positions
of BMHRC, Bhopal with AIIMS, Bhopal. The mandate of the Sub-Committee was to examine the
cadre-wise sanctioned posts and the pay structure BMHRC staff and prepare a comprehensive
rationalization report taking into account the existing post and pay of AIIMS, Bhopal employees.
2.57 The Secretary, Department of Health Research in his oral deposition before the Committee on
28th August, 2018 accepted that administrative merger can be done subject to the protection of
interest of the staff of BMHRC. He further informed that the Committee of ICMR and NITI Aayog
also recommended that BMHRC should be merged with AIIMS, Bhopal and they are waiting for
the report of the Ministry of Health on the merger of the two institutions,  to be submitted by the
Director AIIMS, Raipur.  Once that report is submitted, the Department can go ahead with the
proposed merger.
2.58 The report of the Core Group of the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare on the proposed
merger was submitted in October, 2018. The Report inter alia  has highlighted the benefits of the

24
proposed merger and has stated that the merger of BMHRC with AIIMS, Bhopal, along with a
comprehensive transformation policy, is imperative and necessary to improve its capabilities.  It will
provide an opportunity to leverage combined assets and capital better and also build a stronger
sustainable healthcare service provider. Specifically, the merger will:-
z Enable optimal utilisation of existing resources through improvement in load factors and
yields on common health related issues. The merger will create a mega Hospital care
Service provider with combined revenue resulting in better services and its utilisation.
z Provide an opportunity to fully leverage strong assets, capabilities and infrastructure and
its optimum utilization.
z Provide an opportunity to leverage skilled and experienced manpower (faculty and non-
faculty) available with both  BMHRC and AIIMS, Bhopal to its finest potential.
z Provide a larger and growth oriented health setup for the needy people and the same
shall be in larger public interest across the State of M.P.
z Provide maximum flexibility to achieve financial and capital restructuring through revaluation
of assets.
z Provide an increased thrust and focus on healthcare R&D activities.
z Provide a platform for revenue synergies which will be driven by integration of the
‘complementary’ setups. Cost and capital productivity synergies will be driven by
opportunities for leveraging economies of scale and opportunities for rationalizing overlapping
facilities and infrastructure.  In addition to these synergies, this amalgamation will also
provide an opportunity to initiate a comprehensive transformation programme to improve
the overall setup of AIIMS, Bhopal.  This, while improving the financial position would
help position and equip the merged entity to better face the current/future challenges
arising out of intense competition.
z The merger will help in providing more dedicated and comprehensive patient care services
to the victims of gas tragedy and their family members.
z Provide every possible avenue, to give patients the high-quality healthcare system they
deserve and will lead to more efficient delivery of higher quality care.
2.59 Hence, the Report of the Core Group concluded by stating the following:-
“Merger of BMHRC Bhopal with AIIMS Bhopal will prove to be the most significant recent
development for central India’ s Healthcare Sector. Managed correctly, the combined entity has
a huge potential as one of the country’ s largest and fastest growing Healthcare service
providers. However, the complexity of overseeing a merger taking place against such a
challenging environment cannot be overstated. The Core Group unanimously recommended
merging BMHRC, Bhopal with AIIMS, Bhopal in the public interest at the sole discretion of
MoHFW. In case, the proposal of merger is accepted by the competent authority, BMHRC will
act as a constituent unit of AIIMS Bhopal headed by Director and CEO, AIIMS Bhopal and
Deputy Director (Administration), Financial Advisor, Superintending Engineer of AIIMS, Bhopal
shall continue to assist him along with other allied officials appointed in concerned areas”.

25
2.60 The Committee welcomes the positive response of the Ministry w.r.t. the merger of
BMHRC with AIIMS Bhopal. The Committee also appreciates the concurrence of Director,
BMHRC and the Department of Health Research to this merger proposed in public interest.
The Committee is of the considered view that at present there is duplication of resources
and services being offered in various medical fields leading to non optimal utilisation of
high cost equipment and medical services. The BMHRC caters to a substantial population
of gas tragedy victims and their dependents, it may continue to do the same subsequent to
the merger. BMHRC is well equipped to train paramedical workforce which can be produced
by the hospital easily, if the requisite faculty is made available. The Committee feels that
merger of both hospitals would help in attracting and retaining medical faculty. The Committee
feels that merger of BMHRC with AIIMS, Bhopal would increase scope of teaching and
training avenues for doctors and students. The Committee, therefore, lends its full support
to the merger of both Institutes for the benefit of the society. The Committee, therefore,
recommends that Department of Health and Family Welfare should work towards chalking
out necessary course of action for bringing BMHRC under its purview by merging it with
AIIMS Bhopal. While doing so, the Department of Health and Family Welfare can look into
utilizing the huge set up of BMHRC, Bhopal for putting in place a dedicated resource center
for both medical faculty and paramedical staff in order to have paramedical/clinical technicians
workforce in various fields.  The synergy arising out of the merger of BMHRC with AIIMS,
Bhopal would result in optimal utilization of the existing infrastructure and resources. The
Committee, therefore, strongly recommends that administrative hurdles towards the merger
of both the institutes should be removed as early as possible.
2.61 The Committee, further, recommends that in order to protect the interest of doctors
of BMHRC, the recruitment rules which are different from AIIMS (which happens to be an
autonomous Institution governed by a separate Act) be retained in the transitory phase till
complete merger is done so that the promotional avenues of the BMHRC doctors are
protected. No fresh recruitment should be done under BMHRC rules and the cadre of
serving doctors be treated as a dying cadre for smooth transition of the merger process. The
Committee recommends for time-bound action in this matter and would like to be apprised
of the progress made within three months of presentation of this Report.

CHAPTER-III
AIIMS, RISHIKESH
3.1 AIIMS, Rishikesh was established with the objective and vision to establish a globally acclaimed
centre of excellence in medical education, research and patient care for rectifying regional imbalances
in quality tertiary level healthcare services. The foundation stone of the Institute was laid on 1st
February, 2004 and the Institute was inaugurated on 20th September, 2012. AIIMS, Rishikesh is
providing quality services to patients who come across from different parts of Uttarakhand and
neighbouring districts of Uttar Pradesh, Himachal Pradesh and Haryana.
3.2 AIIMS, Rishikesh is an 880 bed multi-specialty teaching Institute including 18 Specialty and
17 Super Specialty clinical disciplines. The Institute admitted its first batch of 50 MBBS students
in 2012. Since 2013 onwards 100 MBBS and 60 B.Sc. Nursing students are enrolled annually. The
statistics indicate that average daily OPD footfall was 2054 patients. The Institute also informed
that the Doctor: Patient ratio was 1:4.2 and the Bed: Patient ratio for last one year was 2:1.
3.3 The Ministry informed the Committee that the patient care services in form of OPD started
in AIIMS, Rishikesh on 27th May, 2013 and in-patient services commenced from December, 2013.
The Institute started with 150 bedded IPD and the 880 bedded air-conditioned functional hospital
has 65% average bed occupancy. AIIMS, Rishikesh is providing medical services to patients who
come across from different parts of Uttarakhand and neighbouring districts of Uttar Pradesh,
Himachal Pradesh and Haryana.
Budgetary Allocation

3.5 The Committee observes that the percentage of total utilisation of funds has been very low
till the year 2016-17. It was nil in 2011-12, which increased to 13.5% in 2012-13 and 42.08% in
2013-14. It again declined to 36.08% in 2014-15 and 25.22% in 2015-16. From 2016-17, utilisation
of available funds picked up at a good pace. It was 35.98% in 2016-17, 80.82% in 2017-18 and
93.278% upto 15th October, 2018. The Committee, further, notes that expenditure under heads of
Grant-in-Aid (capital) was very low till 2015-16, but from the year 2016-17 there has been a
drastic increase in expenditure under GiA (capital). It was `101.93 crore in 2016-17, `195.07 in
2017-18 and `110.31 upto 15th October, 2018. During the financial years 2017-18 and 2018-19,
the actual expenditure is more than the funds allocated during the particular years and the excess
expenditure was managed from unspent balances of previous years.
3.6 The Committee was informed that the budgetary allocations made to the Institute were not
sufficient during the years 2017-18 and 2018-19. The projected demand of `1363.46 crore for
2018-19 was placed before 6th Standing Finance Committee, whose meeting was held on 4th May,
2018. However, SFC has drastically curtailed budget demand under GIA (CCA) from `1186.96
crore to ` 250 crores. The aforesaid demand was meant for tertiary care departments and was
based on the approval of Assessment Committee headed by Director AIIMS, Rishikesh. According
to AIIMS, Rishikesh such curtailment in fund allocation may affect establishing of tertiary care
departments such as Gastroenterology, Neurology, Urology, Nephrology etc. and Super Specialty
Departments, where every equipment is costly. Besides, these equipment were also required for
research activities for faculty and academic purposes. The Committee was, further informed that
due to unavailability of funds, as proposed in SFC, no progress could take place to establish
centers like Advanced Ophthalmic Centre, Advance Cardiac Centre, Advance Neurosciences Centre,
Advance Transplant Centre and Advance Oncology Centre so far.
