3.8 The Committee has been informed that the committed liabilities to the tune of `256.45 crore
under GIA (CCA) heads for procurement of equipment for Primary, Secondary and tertiary care
was carried forward the financial year 2017-18 to 2018-19 for want of funds under this sub-head.
3.9 The Committee is anguished to observe there has been underutilisation of funds
till the year 2016-17 in AIIMS, Rishkesh. The Committee notes that out of a total
budget estimate of ````` 426.50 crores during 2018-19, only ````` 165 crores of funds have been
released till 15th October, 2018. The Committee also notes that under GIA (CCA) allocation
has been slashed to Rs 250 crores from the projected demand of Rs 1186.96 crores in
BE 2018-19. The Committee is of the opinion that AIIMS, Rishikesh has set up overambitious
targets for itself and therefore, needs to reconsider these demands. A cost benefit analysis
also needs to be done vis-à-vis  availability of other tertiary care services in the adjoining
area. The Committee recommends that a common criterion along with a rational approach
towards utilization of financial allocation for each AIIMS needs to be done. The Committee
is not convinced with the justification for overall projected demand to the tune of 1363.46
crores which is far above the expenditure incurred in other similar tertiary care center with
much higher OPD/IPD load. Needless to say the Committee is concerned at the audacious
projection which alone is almost equal to the sum total of all the remaining five regional
AIIMS under study. The Committee recommends that suitable benchmark for AIIMS standards

29
be formulated by the Central Institute Body (CIB) so that fiscal discipline and prudence in
the utilization of the resources is done under all circumstances. The Committee recommends
that AIIMS, Rishikesh should review its projections to a more realistic level.
3.10 The Committee observes that in-principle approval of many important centers like
Cardiac Science Center, Neuroscience Center, Advance Eye Center, Lung Center to with
estimated expenditure to the tune of ````` 100 crores each has been given by the Governing
Body but the construction work could not be started due to unavailability of funds. Besides,
the Committee is concerned to note that when there has already been a construction of a
full fledged hospital to seek ````` 97.61 crore for Administrative block, ````` 100 crore for the
academic block, ````` 100 crore for research block and ````` 383 crore for residential accommodation
gives an impression that there is no need for optimum/rational utilization of scarce resources.
The Committee, therefore, recommends to the Ministry to monitor the utilisation of funds
given to AIIMS, Rishikesh with utmost caution and justification. The Committee also
recommends for a comparative cost-benefit analysis of AIIMS, Rishikesh with other regional
AIIMS specifically AIIMS, Bhubaneswar in this regard, as the Committee finds it to be the
better managed in terms of investment outcome scale and rational utilization of limited
resources. The Committee is of the opinion that regional AIIMS should provide tertiary
health care in the neglected region but should not merely duplicate the facilities available
with AIIMS Delhi. The Committee recommends that setting up of advanced centers should
be done only when AIIMS Rishikesh achieves at least 50% of the OPD footfall of AIIMS
Delhi and the existing infrastructure is put to optimum usage. Availability of faculty/Super
Specialists to run these advanced centers should also be kept in consideration. The Committee,
therefore, recommends for consideration of all matters regarding setting up of advanced
centers by the CIB and also to take into account the budgetary implications if these centers
are to be setup replicated in all existing and proposed AIIMS.
Status of Construction Work
3.11 The Committee has been informed that package wise work is in progress whereas package
III got completed on 30th April, 2018. The Ministry, further, submitted that medical college, OPD
Block, oncology bunkers, AYUSH and nursing hostel have been completed. The work at hospital
block, trauma block, nursing college and auditorium is in progress.
3.12 The Committee has been given to understand that delay in construction projects has resulted
in time and cost overruns. The construction of Medical College and Hostel complex under Package-1 was
started on 27th May, 2010 even through the original date of completion was 26th August, 2011.
The construction was terminated due to non-performance of M/s Omaxe Ltd. and balance construction
work was assigned to CPWD. Even after the time overrun of 57 months the physical progress is
only 91 %. The construction for Trauma Centre and Nursing College was started on 17th September,
2017 and the original date for completion was 16th May, 2018. Even after lapse of seven months,
the physical progress has not reached 70%. The new target of completion has been fixed as 31st
December, 2018. The Committee was apprised that due to delay in the construction of hospital
complex, there has been cost overrun of approximately ` 49 crores.
3.13 Under package-II, the construction of hospital complex started on 11th September, 2010 and

30
the original date of completion was 10th September, 2012 but it was actually completed on
30th April, 2018 with a time overrun of 65 months.
3.14 The work for estate services under package IV started on 4th July, 2012 with the original
target date of completion on 3rd July, 2013. There has been time overrun of almost 65 months
and the new target date of completion is 31st December, 2018. Auditorium building will be completed
by February 2019.
3.15 The Committee observes that there has been a delay in the construction of almost all
projects and the target date of completion for most of the projects has been further extended.
The Committee also notes that in most of the cases, delay in the work is due to the non
performance of construction agency. Such instances have led to delay in construction activities
implying poor assessment on part of the Institute in selecting the project development
agency/contractor. The Committee believes that time overruns of 4-5 years cannot be justified
on any grounds and this implies poor planning and lackadaisical approach on the part of
implementing agency. The Committee is of the considered view that project contractor with
a proven track record of successful and timely completion of work should be deployed for
such important projects. The Committee therefore, recommends that Institute must rationally
evaluate the projects and plan and chalk out future course of actions, accordingly, by
prioritizing the projects on the basis of urgency to avoid delay in completion of the projects.
3.16 The Institute informed the Committee that the main reason for the delay in projects at AIIMS,
Rishikesh has been the deficiencies in the design of the project as under:
(i) The important buildings/space such as Academic block, Admin block, Examination hall,
Registration for OPD, Kitchen at third floor which should have been on ground floor
and was not with space, Sports Complex, were not envisaged in the design of AIIMS
Rishikesh
(ii) Faculty rooms are of very small size.
(iii) No space earmarked for Medical and non -medical stores.
(iv) No space for recreational facilities for faculty, officers and students.
(v) DDPR has provisioned basement for HV AC system, which was later shifted to peripheral
area being high water level in region, resulted in to cost escalation and delay in
work.
(vi) No provision was made for Medical Gas Pipeline System (MGPS) building to feed
medical gases to Modular OT.
(vii) No separate tank was designed for Department of Nuclear medicine, which resulted
delay in taking approval from  Atomic Energy Regulatory Board ( AERB).
(viii) While deciding plinth level, no cognizance was taken of nearby barrage, where water
level of Ganga barrage fluctuates 3-4 meters.
(ix) No block for Radiotherapy was designed.
(x) Mortuary has also been shifted to another place due to inappropriate layout designing.

31
(xi) Imbalanced and insufficient drainage system has resulted in to over flow at flooring area
even there is light rainfall.
(xii) No provision for Rain Basera, School etc.
(xiii) Inadequate space to house different labs for 100 students.
(xiv) Nursing college building was not completed and nursing course is being run in a make
shift arrangement in space marked for forensic department.
(xv) Ambiguity in various item of notice inviting tender like vendor name not mentioned in
Auto walk; nomenclature of item of UVGI (ultraviolet Germicidal Irradiation) system not
mentioned; unit of fabric portico wrongly mentioned; no provision of structural steel
below kalzip roofing in Auto walk.
(xvi) No provision of earthing and bio- medical waste in DPR of the Institute.
3.17 The Committee chides the Rishikesh management in the strongest term for not detecting
the designing deficiencies in the detailed project report at the initial stage. The Committee
is distressed to note that the project consultants failed miserably in designing the blueprint
for the Institute and neglected the site specific limitations and concerns related to architectural
and engineering techniques for a hospital set-up. The Committee is shocked to observe that
there is no provision for medical gas pipelines system, sewer lines passing through operation
theaters, basement planning thereby overlooking the water table concerns.  This was a basic
civil engineering concept that was completely overlooked.
3.18 During its visit to AIIMS, Rishikesh, the Committee came across a lot of space
wastage due to structural design faults. The Committee was given to understand that the
Institute took undue time in correcting the structural deficiencies and thus the development
work lagged behind. The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends that the project consultants
must be held accountable for such serious lapse in the designing and construction projects
of the Institute. The Committee is alarmed at almost 150 times deviation in the original
quantities and the actual quantities as compared to the quantities given in the Bill of
Quantities (BoQ) of the Contract as pointed by the CAG in its Report. The Committee is
of the opinion that AIIMS, Rishikesh in itself is a case study of mismanagement, lack of
foresight and wastage of public funds. The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends that
the structural/designing faults in AIIMS Rishikesh be corrected to the extent feasible and
new expansion be done with utmost caution with strict monitoring.
3.19 In response to a query regarding the status of hostel and residential complex at AIIMS,
Rishikesh, the Ministry submitted that area of campus is spread over in 101 acre and no further
space is available for construction of residential quarters and hostel. The requirement of additional
land of 200 acre is long pending and the matter is regularly being put up before State Government
of Uttarakhand, Ministry of Health and Family Welfare and even before the Prime Minister Office
(PMO). During its visit of the Committee to AIIMS, Rishikesh, the Director apprised the Committee
about the announcement of 200 acre land of Indian Drugs and Pharmaceuticals (IDPL) by State
Government to AIIMS, Rishikesh.
3.20 The Committee notes that there is limited land available for expansion as only 101

32
acres is available for construction of hospital, academic and residential blocks. The space
crunch is limiting the expansion of services at the institute. The Committee recommends
for a comprehensive space audit of the Institute by any independent agency so as to ensure
optimum land utilization, within six months of the presentation of this report. The Committee
during the course of its study visit saw for itself wastage of land in ancillary areas like
parking etc. Possibility of vertical expansion of residential/hostel facilities may be explored.
The Committee recommends for an expeditious decision on transfer of 200 acre IDPL land
to address the space crunch for AIIMS, Rishikesh after ensuring optimum/rational utilisation
of the available land.
3.21 The Committee is of the considered view that the hospital staff, the workforce and the
students deserve suitable accommodation facilities in carrying out their respective tasks.
The Committee would like the AIIMS, Rishikesh to take necessary steps in consultation
with construction agency to expedite the on-going construction of hostels without time and
cost overruns.
Procurement of Equipment
3.22 The Committee has been informed that medical devices / equipment are being procured in
AIIMS, Rishikesh through Central Public Procurement Portal since July 2017. A total of 181
tenders have been uploaded at Central Public Procurement Portal so far. Tender process at Central
Public Procurement portal is quite convenient, effective and sustainable, which led to greater
participation in tender process resulting into procurement of quality medical devices and equipment.
3.23 The existing procurement process provides that when a demand for procurement is raised by
Heads of Departments to Faculty In-charge (Equipment Store), who in turn, holds a meeting of
Assessment Committee after approval of Director. Assessment Committee has been setup in compliance
of circular based on the recommendations of Public Account Committee. The Assessment Committee
consists of Director AIIMS Rishikesh, Deputy Director Administration, Financial Advisor, Bio Medical
Engineer and senior faculty members (HODs) of Institute. However, in exigencies, Director (Chairman)
of that Committee issues approvals of procurement for equipment. Specifications of all equipment
which has estimated cost more than `30 lakh is vetted by DGHS representative or External Experts.
Assessment Committee examines all procurement proposals broadly on basis of following parameters:-
(a) Available equipment in Departments and their utilization status through P-II/P-III form.
(b) Medical equipment purchased last year with their installation status.
(c) Whether proposed item is not to be supplied under central procurement process through
HLL  and
(d) Requirement of equipment on considering patient’s need, academic purposes (MBBS,
MD/MS & DM, MCh) and available expertise.
3.24 The Committee has been informed that AIIMS Rishikesh has bought state of the art Da Vinci
Xi Robotic Surgical System with dual console and skill simulator in January 2018. System was
installed on 25 .01.2018. The first robotic surgery was performed on 27-02-2018 and since then,
total of 65 cases have been done successfully by this system. Robotic System is being used by
Department of Urology, General Surgery & Gynecology and in future, more Departments will be

33
using this system. The Ministry has submitted that the cost of surgical consumables per case in
AIIMS Rishikesh is approximately ` 70000 to 100000 High cost of the system (mainly due to
patents of intuitive surgical) should not be a deterrent to have this system. In times to come, the
cost may come down as other companies are also preparing to enter the robotic market of the
world. The increasing cost of robotic surgery is associated with the initial purchase of the robot,
its annual maintenance and cost of disposable equipment. The cost of robotic surgery in urology
can be minimized by addressing issues such as capital costs, maintenance and the learning curve.
Robotic procedures have better short-term outcomes in terms of complications and hospital stay.
3.25 The Ministry has justified the higher price of the equipment “Da Vinci Robotic Arm” as there
are provisions about training of doctors; provisions about the maintenance contract, whether it is
for one year or for five years. The Ministry stated that they have taken it for a comparative lower
cost if extra years of warranty, extra training component and the second console and all these
things are added.
3.26 The Committee, while examining the rationale forwarded by the Institute for the
procurement of da vinci Robotic Arm is of the opinion that conventional laparoscopic surgery
takes much lesser time and does  not involve usage of expensive consumables. The Committee
is of the opinion that these resources could have been better utilized in strengthening other
important areas at this nascent stage. The alibi, given regarding training and extended
warranty as a justification for higher prices, fails to appeal to the Committee, as similar
terms have been given to other corporate hospitals by the Company across the country. The
Committee is not convinced by these woolly arguments to procure prohibitively expensive
equipment for a budding Institution like AIIMS. The Committee notes that it has been over
15 years since the da vinci system (robotic surgery) is being used, but the evidence that it
beats laparoscopic (minimally invasive surgery) is still lacking. The Committee notes that
Robotic surgery is several times more expensive than the traditional laparoscopic surgery
and engages the OT and the surgeon for much longer duration. The Committee strongly
believes that robotic surgery should be used only in centers of excellence like AIIMS Delhi
where the Institute is in a position to evaluate the efficacy, economics, efficiency and utility
of these types of surgical intervention as more research is needed to assess the benefits of
robotic surgery. The Committee is also concerned that the da vinci system still has monopoly
in the market and there is every possibility of the company dictating the price and indulging
in unfair monopolistic practice. The Committee, thus, recommends to the Institute to ensure
transparency besides appropriate cost/benefit analysis in the procurement of equipment to
avoid such prohibitively expensive procurement in future.  The Committee would like to
have a quarterly report on the usage of the DA Vinci Robotic Arm in both Rishikesh and
Jodhpur so as to enable it to arrive at a final conclusion in the matter.
3.27 In a specific query regarding procurement of equipment on single bid basis, the Committee
has been informed that in last three years, 54 types of equipment were purchased on single bid
basis. Only single bids were received for 46 types of equipment and in eight types of equipment
multiple bids were received but only single bidder qualified.
3.28 The Committee is not satisfied by the reply given by the Institute regarding the
procurement of equipment on a single bid/proprietary basis as such practices eliminate

34
competition resulting in  higher procurement costs. The Institute should go for competitive
tendering to defeat monopolistic tendencies. The Committee, further, feels that the notification
of tender should get wider reach so that more and more bids are received.  The committee,
further, desires a thorough investigation into all single bid and propriety purchases. The
Committee may be apprised of the conclusions drawn out of the investigation.
3.29 In a specific query regarding the procurement of medical equipment with USFDA certification
and those with any other certification, the Committee has been informed that 108 types of equipment
have been procured with USFDA certification.
3.30 The Committee finds the reply submitted by the Institute as evasive, as the reasons
for the procurement of equipment with USFDA certification is not submitted. The Committee
finds that even some of the basic equipment have been purchased with USFDA certification.
The Committee feels that price of equipment with USFDA certification is quite high which
increases the procurement cost. The Committee feels that the Institute should insist on
purchasing equipment with Generic CE/BIS standards so as to promote the “Make in India”
campaign. The Committee, thus, recommends to the Institute to go for equipment with CE/
BIS standards in future and recommends to the Ministry to issue strict guidelines for the
procurement of equipment with these standards.
3.31 Attention of the Committee has also been drawn to the observation made by the CAG in its
10th report in 2018 according to which installation of 76 equipment costing `28.41 crore has been
delayed for a period ranging from 3 to 29 months.
3.32 The Committee was given to understand that the Institute procured equipment without
ascertaining the availability of space, manpower and infrastructure required for installation.
Due to non installation of medical equipment, patients were deprived of the benefits. Further,
operationalization of key diagnostic and in-patient facilities would also have been adversely
affected. The Committee also feels that delay in the installation of equipment may also
result in lapse of warranty/guarantee of the equipment procured. The Committee recommends
to the Institute to procure equipment only when there is adequate infrastructure and manpower
required for the functioning of the equipment, so that there is no scope for idling or non
installation of procured equipment.
Status of Faculty/Non Faculty Strength
3.33 It has been given to understand that only 229 faculty posts have been filled up against the
sanctioned strength of 305 which means 25% faculty posts are still vacant as on October, 2018.
Further, with regard to non-faculty posts, out of the total strength of 3776 posts, only 2639 posts
are filled up and thus around 30% of  posts are lying vacant. Further, 23 posts for Professor, 16
posts for Additional Professor and 37 posts for Associate Professor are lying vacant at the Institute,
out of the total sanctioned strength of 54, 45 and 81 respectively. So far as non faculty posts are
concerned it was informed that, 365 Group A posts, 614 Group B posts and 158 Group C posts
have not been filled up yet.
3.34 The Committee observes that faculty position in AIIMS, Rishikesh has improved,
however the Committee strongly feels that the present strength of faculty is insufficient for
a premier tertiary care institute. This has adversely impacted the quality of medical services

35
and standards of education being imparted by the Institute. The Committee, thus, recommends
to the Institute to expedite the process of recruitment and take immediate steps to avoid
unnecessary administrative delays in faculty recruitment.
3.35 At present 30 Specialty and Super Specialty Departments are functional at the Institute. The
Departments of Endocrinology& Metabolism, Rheumatology, Surgical Gastroenterology and Nephrology
are not functional. Moreover there are limited DM/MCH posts and inadequate super specialists to
provide tertiary health care to patients.
3.36 The following new facilities are being proposed at the Institute:-
(i) Ophthalmic centre
(ii) Cardiac centre
(iii) Neurosciences center
(iv) Transplant center
(v) Oncology center
3.37 The Committee underlines the need to augment the Specialty and Super Specialty
workforce at the Institute. The Committee observes that in 16 Departments the posts for
Professor are lying vacant. Further, four Departments i.e. Physical Medicine & Rehabilitation,
Transfusion Medicine, Gastroenterology and Biochemistry are being handled by a single
faculty. The Committee would like the Institute to seriously introspect the issues of faculty
and staff shortage and take necessary actions on a priority basis. The Committee feels that
having an experienced workforce is absolutely necessary not only for providing best medical
services to the patients but also for enhancing the practical expertise of the resident doctors.
The Committee is of the considered view that adequate incentives should be offered at
Super Specialty level to attract faculty at senior level from premier institutes like AIIMS,
Delhi/JIPMER/PGI etc. for strengthening the healthcare services in the Institute.
3.38 The Committee also notes that while on the one hand the Institute has announced its
plans for expansion, it has not been able to recruit even the sanctioned strength. The
Committee strongly recommends that additional posts beyond the initially projected posts
should be contemplated only when the existing sanctioned faculty is recruited to the extent
of almost 100% and there is optimum utilization of the existing infrastructure Any addition
in faculty/Non faculty posts should be done only after ensuring that there is substantial
enhancement in patient load.
3.39 The Committee during its study visit to AIIMS, Rishikesh observes that the Institute
has created a serious regional imbalance in the State of Uttarakhand by taking faculty from
adjoining medical colleges, creating a deficit/vacuum in that area. The idea behind setting
up of AIIMS in these areas was to create additional capacity and bring the AIIMS, Delhi
like facilities and services at the tertiary level health care centers in these areas. However,
it is distressing to note that the Institute has created a serious deficit of medical professional/
faculty in the entire State of Uttarakhand. The Committee strongly recommends that recruitment
from adjoining medical colleges in the same state should be done only after obtaining NOC

36
from their parent Department. The Committee also recommends that efforts should be
made to recruit faculty from AIIMS, Delhi so as to create AIIMS like culture in the
regional AIIMS. The Committee is distressed to note that there are hardly any Head of
Departments/Professors from premier Institutes like AIIMS Delhi/JIPMER/PGI etc. in the
Institute. Almost 25% of the faculty has been taken with teaching experience in private
Institutions and not from any premier Institute. The Committee would also like to have
detailed information with respect to applicants with adequate teaching experience from large
tertiary premier Government Institutes/hospitals who have applied for faculty posts till date
in AIIMS Rishikesh and the reasons for their being not found suitable.
3.40 The Committee also recommends that faculty from AIIMS, Delhi or equivalent Institute
be encouraged to impart teaching at AIIMS, Rishikesh as visiting/ guest faculty on a
regular basis.
3.41 The Committee takes serious note of the discrepancies in the information submitted
by AIIMS, Rishikesh with reference to its queries. As an illustration, the sanctioned faculty
strength was quoted as 321 in the Power Point Presentation during the study tour of
Committee but later on, it was reduced to 305 in the information submitted by the Institute.
While the sanctioned strength for Group A non-faculty posts was stated as 782 posts as on
5th November, 2018 but later it was reproduced as 924 posts on 6th December, 2018. The
Committee advises the Institute to exercise due caution and diligence in submitting information
to the Parliamentary Standing Committee in future.
Medical Education
3.42 AIIMS, Rishikesh offers undergraduate programmes like MBBS and B.Sc. Nursing with 50
and 60 students in each program respectively. The first batch of MBBS got medical education and
training and passed out in December 2017 and most of them are pursuing post-graduation from
various institutes of India. Similarly, first batch of nursing students passed out and some of them
are pursuing post graduation and few of them have joined in AIIMS Rishikesh.
3.43 At present AIIMS Rishikesh is running various courses i.e. MD (Doctor of Medicine); MS
(Master of Surgery); M.Sc. (Nursing/ Anatomy/Physiology/Bio-Chemistry/Pharmacology/Reproductive
and Clinical embryology). The sanctioned seats of Junior Residents are 301 and filled up seats in
PG Courses are 211 across 23 departments. Moreover, 13 candidates of DM/MCH are working
across 15 specialties. The sanctioned UG seats in medical education are 100 per year and the total
seats in nursing education is proposed to be increased from present 60 per to 100 seats.
3.44 A comparative status of the courses started/approved for the year 2016-17 and 2017-18 is
as under:-

3.45 During the Committee’s visit to AIIMS, Rishikesh, the management of the Institute raised
concerns over the limited posts of DM/MCh (in faculty and academic residents) and inadequate
super specialty services at their Institute which strains the medical facilities of a super specialty
hospital. The Institute has requested to increase in the DM/MCh posts at par with other clinical
departments.
3.46 The Committee observes that there are new courses being offered at the Institute viz.
Ph.D., M.Sc. Nursing, DM/MCh and there has been a increase in the number of seats in
existing courses as also the number of courses being offered at the Institute.  The Committee
recommends that enhancement of the PG seats at the Institute should be done only after
ensuring that AIIMS, Rishikesh meets at least the minimum benchmarks as per MCI
norms applicable to other medical college throughout the country. The Committee does not
accept the argument of the Institute that it is governed by the AIIMS Act and hence the
minimum bench mark as per MCI norms are not applicable to it. This argument puts a
question mark over the quality of learning and clinical experience given to graduates passing
out of regional AIIMS. The Committee is of considered opinion that graduates and post
graduate passing out of AIIMS, Rishikesh should be equal to other medical graduates
passing out of premier Institutes of the country. The Committee is of the considered
opinion that autonomy as per AIIMS Act was given only with respect to AIIMS, Delhi which
has always exceeded the MCI benchmarks in every single aspect. However, the Committee
is extremely concerned that the reference to the AIIMS Act and consequent autonomy
available to regional AIIMS by virtue of this Act is being audaciously misused to dilute the
medical education standards in a very blatant manner with the justification that the Institute
meets US/UK standards. The Committee, therefore, recommends to the Ministry to ensure
that autonomy must not be used to dilute educational standards under any circumstances.
The Committee is distressed at the medical education standards of the new AIIMS and is
of the opinion that vacant PG seats in these Institutes is a reflection of lower standards of
medical education.
Intensive Care Units (ICUs)
3.47 On the status of intensive care units at AIIMS, Rishikesh, 7 department wise ICU facilities
at AIIMS, Rishikesh which are as follows:-

3.48 The Committee has been informed that designated Modular Operation Theaters and ICU as
per DDPR are under construction at present and NICU is well equipped with ventilators, monitors,
warmers as well as other essential equipment.
3.49 The Committee observes that there are only 47 beds, with 18 ventilators and 31
monitors in ICUs in seven Departments, thereby indicating that the number of monitors
and ventilators are not commensurate with the number of beds in ICUs if there is 100%
occupancy at any given point in time. Such inadequate critical care facilities at the Institute
that is a tertiary level care institute indicates a dismal state of affairs with respect to
intensive care facilities for critical patients. The Committee apprehends that the institute
is not equipped, adequately, to deal with complicated cases that require hospitalization and
critical care. The Committee therefore, is of the considered view that more intensive care
units must be set up in various departments to have distinct and specialised medical care
facilities.  The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends that the institute should expand
the scope of critical care services to best possible extent with adequate infrastructure and
equipment. It is also a matter of grave concern to the Committee that the institute has
started a fellowship programme in ICU/Critical care without a properly functional ICU. The
Committee is of the considered view that fellowship programmes are run in Centers of
Excellence once they are well established so that the standards of these programmes are not
lowered.
Operation Theatres (OTS) and Trauma and Emergency Centre
3.50 As regard the operation theaters (OTs) at AIIMS, Rishikesh, the Committee has been informed
that 24 modular OTs have been sanctioned at the Institute. Since IPD services are functional at
AIIMS, Rishikesh from 30th December, 2013, major OT Complex as envisaged in the DDPR was
not functional, hence minor OTs were operationalized for treatment of patients. All of these are
rooms in IPD / OPD which are being utilized as minor OTs. Though 20 OTs are well equipped,
supply of medical gas pipeline installation work is in progress. The Committee was further informed
that with addition of new OTs, the total OTs for the institute would be 40.

39
3.51 The institute further informed that corrective steps have been taken with regard to modular
OTs i.e. their location was rearranged as sewerage lines were passing through OTs; provision of
medical gas pipeline system was made; provision for retention of effluent of Nuclear Medicine
department and construction of septic tank was done. Regular follow-ups are being taken from the
construction agencies concerned and consultants to expedite finalization of OTs.
3.52 The Committee takes cognizance of the defective design project of the modular OTs
that had many flaws although the same have been re-designed but not without time and
cost overruns. The Committee wants to highlight the shortsightedness of the institute in
approving the design and floor plan of AIIMS, Rishikesh and therefore, desires that the
responsibility must be fixed for defective design of modular OTs in the hospital set up. The
Committee also notes the steps being taken by the institute in accelerating the construction
of OTs. The Committee in this regard recommends that the institute should ensure timely
completion of the construction of OT complex, recruitment of specialists and the procurement
of equipment, simultaneously, so as to have a ready to use functional OT units within the
stipulated timeframe. The Committee strongly recommends for a cohesive, well planned
time bound action plan to rectify the designing faults in AIIMS Rishikesh.
3.53 The Committee has not been able to assess the number of surgical procedures carried
out in AIIMS Rishikesh due to variation in the data furnished by the institute and the
Ministry. The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends that precise data with respect to
major and minor surgeries done (patient wise and Department-wise) till date since inception
of the institute may be submitted to the Committee, within three months of presentation
of this Report.
3.54 The Committee was given to note that only 50 bedded trauma and emergency center has been
approved as per DPR agreement. A provision of expansion to 100 beds has been made. At present
39 bed of trauma center is being run in OPD block as building of trauma center is under
construction by CPWD.
3.55 The institute has informed that due to limited number of OTs, the waiting time for the
surgical procedures is high as indicated below:-

3.56 The Committee is dismayed at the sorry state of affairs with regard to patients getting
surgical procedures done after long waiting time. The Committee observes that at present
only 13 operation theaters are functional at AIIMS, Rishikesh due to which there is a long
waiting period for the surgical procedures to be done at the institute. The Committee
observes that in Urology, Neurosurgery, and Burns and Plastic Departments, there is two
months waiting time and in general surgery, there is 45 days waiting time for the procedures
to be done besides six months for ENT. The Committee feels that such instances of long
waiting time due to non-availability of operation theaters defies the very purpose of setting
up of AIIMS like institution as patients have to face hardships. The Committee finds the
reasons for long waiting time at AIIMS, Rishikesh are inadequate infrastructure and constraints
even after six years of its operationalization. The Committee recommends for optimum
utilization of the existing OT infrastructure. The Committee also recommends for a comparative
analysis with AIIMS Delhi regarding the number of major/minor surgeries per OT per day.
Despite these lacunae the institute is aiming for setting up of advanced centres at exorbitant
costs without getting the basics right. The Committee recommends that optimum functional
status of the originally conceived infrastructure be achieved and resources be utilized for
achieving this objective instead of just focusing on rapid expansion.
3.57 The Committee notes that AIIMS, Rishikesh is not prepared to manage  huge number
of casualties in case of any disaster due to non-establishment of trauma center. The Committee,
therefore, recommends that AIIMS, Rishikesh management must expedite the construction
work for establishment of Trauma center for treatment of casualties in case of disaster, as
Uttarakhand is prone to disasters.

CHAPTER-IV
AIIMS, JODHPUR
4.1 AIIMS Jodhpur is one of the six new AIIMS established by the Ministry of Health and Family
Welfare, with the aim of correcting regional imbalances in quality tertiary level health care in the
country and attaining self sufficiency in graduate and post graduate medical education. The foundation
stone of AIIMS Jodhpur was laid on 31.01.2004, but was made operational with its first academic
session on 17th September, 2012; AIIMS Jodhpur was second after AIIMS, New Delhi to start
functioning. OPD services were started on 27th July 2013 while IPD services commenced from
February, 2014.
4.2 The Ministry apprised the Committee that till 30th October 2018 average, daily OPD footfall
was 2461 patients, total IPD admissions was 29037 and bed strength was 684. At present, the
Patient Doctor Ratio is 3.8:1 and patient to bed ratio is 3.6:1.

4.4 The Committee is constrained to express its concern that total utilisation budgetary
allocation in respect of AIIMS, Jodhpur picked up very slowly. The Committee wonders that
the percentage utilization was nil in the year 2011-12, 39.48% in 2012-13, 47.64% in 2013-
14, 61.56% in 2014-15, 32.53% in 2015-16, 50.63% in 2016-17, 74.16% in 2017-18 and
70.09% in 2018-19 (up to 15.10.2018). The Committee berates that Jodhpur management
failed to monitor over the implementing agencies during the course of execution of construction
projects resulting into under-utilisation of funds in the initial years for Grants-in-Aids
(capital). The Committee laments that the trend of expenditure witnessed an increase only
after the year 2016-17 that speaks of limited absorption capacity of implementation agencies.
4.5 The Committee was given to understand by the Ministry/institute that the funds received
were sufficient to meet the construction activities and on-going development projects at the
institute. The Committee believes that the problematic aspects relate to timely execution of
the projects. The Committee, therefore, arrives at the conclusion that had the implementing

43
agencies executed all segments of the projects in time, with required absorption capacity,
there would have been optimum utilization of allocated funds and lesser delays. The Committee
is of the considered view that had the institute and the Ministry undertaken proper planning
and periodic monitoring of the project work there would have been optimal and judicious
utilization of resources. The Committee, therefore, recommends to the Ministry to institute
an enquiry to assess the actual reasons for under-utilization of allocated funds for execution
of construction projects and accordingly apprise the Committee of the action taken against
the implementing agencies as per terms and conditions of the contract.
4.6 The Committee has been informed that funds were earmarked with respect to GIA Capital for
payment of certain medical equipment and payment to HLL. However, the same could not be made
since the inspection and installation of the equipment was in progress. Most of the payments have
been released in the month of April and May, 2018. Also, non implementation of the Seventh pay
commission recommendations resulted in underutilization of GIA salary.
4.7 The Committee takes note of that various efforts made by the institute for effective utilisation
of funds which included (i) seeking funds only as required by way of preparing realistic budget
estimates, (ii) placing the funds in flexi premium current account instead of normal current account,
(iii) expediting the procurement process of devices and consumables, (iv) ensuring expenditure
within the budget allocated and (v) exercising economy in day to day expenditures.
4.8 The Committee is not convinced with the reasons given for underutilization of funds
as these routine activities are often faced in the functioning of any institution. The delay
in the completion of projects have an adverse impact on the health care services to be
provided by the institute.  The Committee expects the Department to uphold the principles
of fiscal discipline and sound financial management by judiciously utilizing the remaining
funds. Needless to say, that only proper planning, co-ordination with implementing agencies
and suitable monitoring mechanism could have ensured optimum utilisation of funds.
4.9 In response to a query regarding instances of diversion of funds, the Committee has been
informed that Grants in Aid (GIA) received under the revenue head expenses was utilized for the
creation of capital assets for ` 2.14 crore. During the financial year 2014-15, excess expenditure
of `1.52 crore was incurred on payment of salary of outsourcing staff over and above GIA
available in General Head.
4.10 The Committee opines that diversion of funds is a result of lack of effective financial
control and monitoring of actual utilisation. Such diversion amounts to misappropriation of
allocated fund for the intended purpose. The Committee, thus, recommends framing of
proper guidelines to control such diversion. The Committee, in this connection, strongly
recommends for strengthening of financial management, effective monitoring mechanism
and adoption of proper accounting procedure for an effective internal financial control.

have been completed. The work of package II of Hospital complex buildings is still in progress
and likely to be completed by 31.12.2018. In addition to above four packages, work of residential

4.12 In response to a specific query regarding major reasons for delay of construction activities
under package II, the Committee was informed that delay in release of GFC drawings from DDPR
resulted in delay of construction activities. The work was on hold for want of equipment for
MGPS, Modular OT, CSSD, Kitchen etc. and due to change in layout of D&T OT block from 2nd
to 6th floor from the year 2013 onwards. The reasons for changes in layout of D&T OT block
were given as follows:
(i) Dedicated AHUs for individual OT were not provided in the original drawing and same
was added as per latest requirements.
(ii) Space for UPS for Modular OTs was not provided in the original drawings.
(iii) Connectivity between ICU and OT was not provided in the original drawings.
(iv) Many modifications were required by Modular OT vendor for installing the modular OT
as per BOQ issued.

45
4.13 The Committee expresses its anguish to observe faulty drawings in the construction
project and subsequent shifting of OT from 2nd floor to 6th floor in AIIMS, Jodhpur as the
same constituted the reasons for delay in the completion of construction projects. The
Committee wonders that the faulty design in the original drawing was not taken seriously
at the initial stage. The Committee, therefore, recommends that accountability for such
faulty design should be fixed and a detailed report on the reasons for the delay in the
operationalisation of the OTs be submitted to the Committee for its consideration.  The
Committee also recommends that the remaining work of package II be completed within
the specified deadline. The Ministry in tandem with institute management must oversee and
ensure timely execution of all projects.
4.14 The Ministry informed the Committee that there has been no cost escalation from the original
estimate due to delay in completion till date. Out of four packages, final bills of three packages
have been received without any cost escalation despite delay. The Committee has been informed
that under package I, increase in cost was not due to delay in completion of project but because
of variations/mismatch between Bill of Quantity (BOQ) and GFC drawings. Escalation clause would
not have operated as per agreement as work was completed under original agreement. Under
package II, likely completion cost of ` 235 crore is inclusive of price escalation as per agreement
as work was completed under original agreement.
4.15 The Committee would like to draw the attention of the institute to the recent observation
by the CAG in context of AIIMS Jodhpur, where it has mentioned about price escalation in
violation of contract, repeated grant of time extension, release of payment despite poor
workmanship, excess release of mobilization advance and avoidable/extra contractual payments.
The Committee is also of the view that replies of the Ministry are not at all acceptable and
the difference between the original and the final cost cannot be considered beyond the
purview of cost escalation. From the information provided by the Ministry, it is evident that
there has been cost overrun of ````` 9.00 crore under package I and ````` 18.00 crore under
package II. Even if the cost of construction of these projects did not increase during the
staggered period of six years, it implies that cost evaluation/calculation was inflated to such
a great extent in the initial stage itself. The Committee wonders as to how the original cost-
estimation of the projects was done. The Committee, therefore, seeks reasons/explanation
over the cost-estimation of the projects in all the four packages.
4.16 The Committee observes that the construction under all the packages have been completed
except package II. The construction of package II was started on 16th September, 2010 and the
original date of completion was 15th September, 2012. The Committee points out inordinate delay
in completion of the project even after the time over run of more than six years and still the,
physical progress is 96.42 per cent by December, 2018.
4.17 The Committee, further, observes that under package III and package IV , there has
been time overrun of approximately three years. The Committee feels that the delayed
execution of the projects in turn, has severely hampered the healthcare services to the
patients during this period.  Such delays have impaired the capacity of the institute to
provide the envisaged levels of treatment, health care and medical education. The Committee
desires the Ministry to assess the genuine reasons for delay in the execution of the projects

46
under construction and apprise the Committee in this regard. The Ministry  must conduct
detailed site investigation, hold regular meetings and formulate effective monitoring mechanism
to ensure that the project is managed efficiently after meticulous scheduling of work.
4.18 On the accommodation facilities at AIIMS, Jodhpur, the institute has informed that inadequacy
of housing facilities for faculty, residents, students and staff was a challenge which would now
be taken care of by the additional 67 acres of land allotted to the Institute for the construction
projects. The construction projects approved by Governing Body includes 100 type V quarters, 250
type III quarters, girls’ hostel for 350 students, boys’ hostel for 300 students, and administrative
block. The projects approved by Standing Finance Committee includes 132 Type V quarters, 528
Type III quarters, academic block, student activity centre, faculty/ officers’ recreation centre and
shopping complex. In addition to that, Advanced Centers for Transplant, Oncology, Cardiac Sciences,
Neuro Sciences and Ophthalmology, Department of Biostatics ,Department of Bio-technology ,
expansion of School of Public Health and a Dharmshala for the relatives of the patients admitted
at AIIMS, Jodhpur have also been planned.
4.19 The Committee calls upon the Ministry to expedite the process of granting necessary
approvals for the construction of proposed accommodation facilities. The Ministry may approve
the Advanced Centers for Transplant, Oncology, Cardiac Sciences, Neuro Sciences and
Ophthalmology, Department of Biostatics, Department of Bio-technology only after optimum
utilization of existing infrastructure and ensuring availability of adequate human resources.
The Committee is of the view that the establishment of advance centers should only be
considered when the institute achieves at least 50% of the OPD footfall of AIIMS Delhi
subject to approval by CIB. However, the Committee would like to emphasize to undertake
expansion of School of Public Health and a Dharmshala for the relatives of the patients
admitted at AIIMS, Jodhpur. The Committee also recommends the Ministry to ensure completion
and optimum utilization of the originally envisaged projects in a time bound manner before
approving any new project.
Procurement of Medical Equipment
4.20 The Committee was given to understand that AIIMS, Jodhpur has implemented Central Public
Procurement Portal of Government of India for procurement of all medical devices and equipment
since November, 2016. The success rate of implementation of this portal is 100% and the institute
is benefitted by implementation of CPP portal which undoubtedly encourages competition and
ensures transparency at a large scale, in the procurement procedure.
4.21 On a specific query as to whether any analysis was done for procuring the medical equipment
w.r.t the cost of equipment vis-à-vis  its actual utility in catering to the needs of local population
at AIIMS, Jodhpur, the Ministry informed the Committee that after receiving demand from respective
Department to procure equipment, the demands are assessed by the Assessment Committee in view
of patient needs and load, augmentation of services and available expertise. The Assessment Committee
consists of faculty of respective Departments, other senior faculty and members from administration
and finance. However, no separate analysis was done for procuring medical equipment w.r.t the
cost of equipment vis-à-vis  its actual utility in catering to the health needs of local population.
4.22 The Committee appreciates the role played by the Assessment Committee in procurement

47
of medical equipment. However, the Committee is constrained to note that no separate
analysis has been done for the procurement of equipment, thus indicating that the equipment
have been purchased without analyzing the cost of the equipment vis-à-vis  its actual utility.
The Committee is of the view that procurement of equipment without proper cost-benefit
analysis may result in under utilization/non-utilization/ duplication of equipment and procurement
of equipment at higher prices. The Committee, therefore, recommends to the institute/
Ministry that proper cost-benefit analysis must be done by the Assessment Committee
before the procurement of the equipment so that the utility of the equipment is explicit
commensurating to its cost leading of effective delivery to health services.  This analysis
should form the part of the procurement process. The Committee also reiterates its observation
made in context of AIIMS, Rishikesh (para 3.26) for the procurement of da vinci  robotic
arm. The Committee also recommends that the Assessment Committee must go into the
details of reasons for proprietary/ single bid purchases and submit a status Report to the
Committee along with a quarterly update on the usage of da-vinci  robotic arm.
4.23 On being asked about the delay in installation of equipment, the Committee has been informed
that installation of the following two instruments/equipment procured through HLL is pending/ as
per the reason given below:-
Sl. No. Item NOA No. No. of pending Supplier Remarks

4.24 The Committee is anguished to observe the delay in installation of equipment on the
account of procrastination in the construction of new OTs. This is a peculiar situation when
equipment has been purchased without ascertaining a place for its installation.  The Committee
believes that delay in installation of equipment may lead to expiry of guarantee/warranty
of the equipment, service period. The Committee, thus, recommends that equipment should
be procured only when space for their installation is ready so as to avoid undue delay in
the installation and allay the fear of expiry of guarantee/warrantee of the equipment. Not
just this, equipment should not be allowed to remain idle/unutilised. The Committee, further,
recommends that the Ministry should expedite the process of construction of new OTs
immediately so that the installation of procured equipment is ensured at the earliest.
4.25 Regarding procurement of equipment on single bid basis, the Committee has been informed
that there are few equipment purchased by AIIMS, Jodhpur during the last three years on single
bid basis after complying General Financial Rules for purchase. In most of these cases, tender had
been floated three times to ensure sufficient competition. There are 8 types of equipment for which
single technical bid were received even after 3 extensions of time. Besides there are 24 types of

48
equipment where more than one bid is received but technically single bid was approved while other
bids were disqualified on the recommendation of the Technical Committee. The reason given was
that the bidder could not comply with the eligibility criteria as laid down in the tender document.
4.26 The Committee expresses its concern that 32 types of equipment were procured on a
single bid basis at AIIMS Jodhpur. Such situation leads to minimizing the options for
procurement of equipment for the Institute. In case of single bid, the cost of equipment is
likely to be higher than multiple bids. The Committee is of the view that multiple bids is
the only way for procurement of economical and best equipment for the Institute. The
Committee cautions that the Institute has not followed the General Financial Rules for the
procurement of equipment. As per General Financial Rules, procurement from a single
source may be resorted to only when there is a single manufacturer, in case of emergency
and for standardization of machinery or spare parts to be compatible to the existing sets
of equipment.  In the event where more than one bid is received, it means that there is
more than one supplier of the required equipment. The Committee, thus, recommends to
the Ministry to comply with the GFRs issued by the Government of India and go for
procurement of equipment on multiple bid bases so as to achieve competitive advantage.
The Committee recommends for a probe and analysis for all cases of single bid/proprietary
certificate procurements and a status report be submitted to the Committee within three
months of the presentation of the Report.
4.27 On the issue of medical equipment with USFDA certification and those with any other
certification which have been procured at the Institute, the Committee has been informed that in
last three years, most of the equipment purchased at AIIMS, Jodhpur are USFDA/European CE/
CE/ISO certified. Certified equipment have been procured with the recommendation of concerned
Department/Technical Committee. The broad reasons for such procurement are as below:-
(i) Some of the best quality high end equipment are available only with USFDA certification
and if equipment with such certification are purchased, the quality of patient care will
be compromised immensely.
(ii) Where any other standard other than USFDA are not available.
(iii) High quality equipment are also required in view of the fact that AIIMS Jodhpur being
an Institute of National Importance and a tertiary care Hospital.
4.28 The Committee has been informed that there are 40 types of equipment that have procured
with USFDA certification in last two years. Further, 184 equipment have been procured with other
certification at the Institute during the last two years.  It was ensured that quality is not compromised
while purchasing equipment bearing other certifications.
4.29 The Committee understands that AIIMS, Jodhpur is meant for the needy and poor
patients requiring tertiary healthcare facilities where quality of equipment can be life-saving
for the patients. The Committee, however, observes that since basic equipment like OT
Light System, Laparoscopic Set, Suction Machine, Multipara Monitor and many others are
easily available with European and also with Indian manufacturers. The procurement of
these medical equipment with USFDA certification should have been resorted to only in the
rarest of circumstances, after ensuring, in writing, that no alternative with CE/BIS certification

49
is available. The Committee desires to be apprised of the reasons for procurement of the
above-mentioned devices with USFDA certification when equivalent quality and lower price
CE/BIS certified equipment were available.
4.30 The Committee is of the considered view that procurement of equipment with BIS
certification will encourage companies to manufacture their products in India and also
increase their investment. This will also contribute to “Make in India”, programme of the
Government of India to promote domestic manufacturers. The Committee, therefore, recommends
to the Ministry to issue strict directions to all AIIMS like institutions to procure equipment
with wider certification standards so as to promote competitive pricing and also to encourage
“Make in India” scheme.

4.32 The Committee observes that only 145 faculty posts have been filled up out of total sanctioned
strength of 305 and 160 faculty posts are still vacant as on 18th October, 2018. Further, with
regard to non-faculty posts, out of the total strength of 3776 posts, only 1717 posts are filled up
and 2059 are vacant. Of these total  non-faculty staff, 975  have been filled on regular basis, 31
are on deputation basis, 73 on contractual basis and 638 have been out outsourced.
4.33 As regards the vacancy of non-faculty staff, the Committee has been informed that there are
646 promotional posts including (129 Group A, 477 Group B, and 39 Group C). These can only
be filled up through promotion or deputation. Recruitment Rule for many posts is under amendment.
Many a time exams were conducted to fill up the posts, however, suitable candidates were not
found.
4.34 The Committee has further been informed that it was difficult to recruit faculty because of
non-availability of candidates as applications were not received in certain specialties inspite of
repeated advertisements. Therefore, faculty that can match the requisite qualification is not available.
4.35 The Committee is constrained to observe that out of 305 sanctioned faculty posts, 160
posts are lying vacant. Again, 2059 posts are vacant in non-faculty staff that amounts to
approximately 55 per cent of the total sanctioned non-faculty posts. The Committee finds
the vacancy level at Professor/Additional professor level quite alarming. The Committee is
of the view that deficient number of teaching faculty at the Institute adds woes to basic
health infrastructure at the Institute besides diluting the standard of medical education.
Such a huge number of vacancies of the faculty/staff would definitely have an adverse
impact on the functioning of the Institute. Vacancy of one category of health personnel is
restricted not only to a particular Department but also affects the overall functioning of a
healthcare Institute. The Committee, thus, recommends to the Ministry to make all out
efforts to expedite the process of filling up of the posts in a time-bound manner in order
to impart the medical education at the desired level and to ensure that tertiary level healthcare
services are provided to the patients. The Committee also recommends to the Ministry to
offer better pay structure and incentives to attract Specialist/Super Specialist doctors from
premier Institutes like AIIMS/JIPMER/PGI, etc. to join the Institute. The Committee is
constrained to observe almost negligible presence of faculty from these similar premier
Government Institutes and would also like to be apprised of the number of applicants who
had applied from these Medical Institutes but were not found suitable, along with the
reasons for rejection.
Super Specialty Services
4.36 The Committee has been apprised that Department of Biotechnology and Department of

51
Biostatistics are in the process of being set up. Further, Sub-specialties of Dentistry including
Orthodontics, Oral Surgery, Periodontology, Conservative Dentistry and Endodontics and Prosthodontics
are also being proposed. AIIMS Jodhpur is also in the process of establishing Advanced Cardiac
Centre, Neurosciences Centre, Centre for Oncology, Advanced Eye centre, Research block, Organ
Transplant Service Centre. The Institute is planning to start Organ Transplant services and had
already applied to get license for transplant services.
4.37 It was learnt that AIIMS, Jodhpur does not have faculty in the Departments of Medical
Gastroenterology, Medical Oncology and Nuclear Medicine due to which the patients are being
referred to other hospitals as and when required.
4.38 The Committee is displeased to note that three Departments i.e. Medical Gastroenterology,
Medical Oncology and Nuclear Medicine are not functional at AIIMS, Jodhpur. These
Departments are important branches of medicine and are indispensable part of any Institute
providing healthcare services to the people. The Committee would like the Institute to
make these Departments functional without much procrastination.
4.39 The Committee desires the Ministry to submit a status note on the cost-benefit analysis
for establishment of Advanced Cardiac Center, Neuroscience Centre, Advanced Eye Centre,
Centre for Oncology, Reserved block and Organ Transplant Service Centre. The Committee,
in this connection, recommends that Advanced Centers be established only after AIIMS,
Jodhpur achieves the desired level of faculty and manpower needed to run these Super-
Specialties Departments and attains the daily OPD of at least 50% of AIIMS, Delhi. The
Committee also recommends that all the decisions regarding setting up of advance centers
should be referred to the CIB.
4.40 AIIMS, Jodhpur is presently providing services in following Specialties and Super Specialties
with the following status of faculty posts as submitted by the Institute:-
