6.9 The Committee notes that the work under Package-I was started on 30th May, 2010 and
expected to be completed within 15 Months i.e. on 29th August, 2011.  Even after the time overrun
of 86 months, the physical progress is 99.85% and the new target date of completion is 31st
January, 2019. The unreasonable delay resulted in the cost overrun of ` 13 crore. The work under
Package-II started on 9th November, 2010 and original date of completion was 9th October, 2012.
There has, however, been the time overrun of 74 months in the completion of Package-II resulted
in cost overrun of ` 40 crore. The work under Package-III commenced on 19th August, 2011 and
original target date of completion was 18th December, 2012.   Till now only 96% of work has
been completed even after a time overrun of 73 months resulting into cost overrun of ` 3 crore.
The new target date of completion is 31st December, 2018. The work under Package-IV began
on 2nd February, 2012 and was expected to be completed by 2nd January, 2013. Even after a time
overrun of over 70 months, only 87% physical progress has been achieved under Package-IV . The
new target date of completion is 31st January, 2019.
6.10 It has been informed to the Committee t hat main reasons for the delays were abandonment of
work by previous project consultant i.e. M/s Mukesh and Associates and non-responsive nature of
earlier DDPR consultant i.e. M/s Hospitech Management Consultants Pvt. Ltd. Final finishing of

85
various areas got delayed due to on-going works which started at later dates and lack of smooth
coordination among different agencies.
6.11 The Ministry has shared the following strategies for completion of project within stipulated
time frame:-
(i) Better coordination among different agencies has been ensured by conducting regular
meetings and the work is expected to be completed by January, 2019.
(ii) ERC (Empowered Review Committee) Meetings, chaired by Director AIIMS, Raipur are
held at regular intervals. 32 ERC Meetings have been held so far.
(iii) Regular review meetings are held by MoHFW, New Delhi to monitor the progress of
the work and to provide necessary guidance for urgent issues.
(iv) Project Cell Meetings/Review Meetings are held at regular intervals.
(v) Inspection rounds are held at regular intervals
(vi) Pending cases/designs and drawings, which were pending due to non-responsive nature
of old DDPR Consultant i.e. M/s Hospitech were assigned to Institutes like VNIT,
Nagpur, NIT Raipur etc. to get their expertise in the matter to expedite the progress of
the work.
(vii) Posting of full time SE, AIIMS, Raipur is required on deputation basis at Project Cell,
AIIMS, Raipur.
(viii) A ppointment of In-House Consultant (Civil and Electrical) for AIIMS, Raipur from M/s
HSCC (India) Ltd.
(ix) Nomination of Experts (Civil and Electrical) from NIT, Raipur and CPWD to be done
as per requirement for the ERC meeting. The same is already being done.
6.12 The Committee is anguished at the slow pace of the construction activities at the
AIIMS, Raipur as none of the four Packages have been completed even after delays ranging
from 70 to 90 months. Surprisingly, the main reasons for delay under all the Packages were
abandonment of work by the construction agencies and lack of co-ordination among various
agencies. The delay in the completion of the construction has resulted in cost escalation of
````` 58.18 crore which is expected to increase further if the remaining work is not completed
within the revised date of completion. The Committee is of the view that though various
reasons have been identified by the Institute, corrective measures  should have been taken
much earlier in order to rectify the mistakes to avoid time and cost overruns. Such inordinate
delays have resulted in indefinite postponement of delivery of health care services to the
people. The Committee, therefore, advocates strict monitoring by the Ministry so that the
project work is completed within the revised deadlines without any further delay. The
Committee recommends for submission of a detailed enquiry report along with action taken
against the errant project consultants and contractors.
6.13 The Committee notes that major delay have taken place on the account of abandonment
of work by the implementing agencies. The engagement of HSCC/CPWD also cannot be

86
construed as a remedy because these agencies have also not been able to perform within
the quality, cost and time parameters. The Committee, therefore, recommends engagement
of only those construction agencies with a proven track along with a reputation of delivering
work in time and within the sanctioned cost.
6.14 W ith regard to hostel and residential complex, the Committee has been informed that AIIMS,
Raipur has separate hostels for boys and girls as well as for residents with all basic amenities and
canteen facility. Currently, no problem has been reported regarding accommodation for teaching as
well as non-teaching staff. The Institute has already undertaken the work of construction of phase-II
w.r.t. expansion of residential complex at Kabir Nagar, Raipur. M/s HSCC was nominated as DPR
and Project Management Consultant for this work. The DPR amounting `98.70 crore was approved
in second SFC meeting held on 16th March, 2015 and the agreement for DPR Consultant was
entered with M/s HSCC in August, 2015. The proposed architectural plans were submitted to Nagar
Palik Nigam, Raipur in January, 2016 for approval. Tender documents as per the approved plans
i.e. Bill of Quantities (BOQ) and technical specification of works were submitted in November,
2017 and detailed drawings in May, 2018.
6.15 In August, 2018, however, it was felt by AIIMS, Raipur that the requirement of residential
accommodation needed to be relooked and it was decided that additional boys and girls hostels
were required rather than Type-V residences.  Hence, the concept plans were again revised accordingly
and were approved in October, 2018. The revised drawings, revised estimate and tender documents
were under preparation. These shall be ready by end of November, 2018 and put up for tendering
subsequently. The estimated time required for tendering, award and execution of work is 22
months.
6.16 The Committee observes that the AIIMS, Raipur has failed in evaluating the requirement
for the residential accommodation while planning the Phase-II expansion program. The
planning process for expansion started in March, 2015 and continued till May, 2018 with the
submission of detailed drawings of the project. By then, it was realized that there is a need
to revive the entire plan. The Committee is appalled at the laid back approach of the
Institute in execution of the project. The changes in the design at such a later stage would
have a negative impact on the expansion program, in terms of additional resources, cost and
extended project duration. It seems to the Committee that obviously the Institute had not
conducted any proper study before planning the expansion of phase-II residential complex.
The preparation of revised drawing, revised detailed estimates and tender documents would
require additional time resulting in cost and time overruns. The Committee, therefore,
underlines the need for proper planning and evaluation by engaging reputed agencies before
undertaking any construction projects.
Status of Faculty and Non Faculty Strength
6.17 It has been informed to the Committee that only 125 faculty posts have been filled up out
of total sanctioned strength of 305 and 180 faculty posts are still lying vacant. As per data
submitted by the Ministry, out of the total 3776 sanctioned non-faculty strength, only 1453 posts
are filled up whereas 2323 are vacant.
6.18 The Committee learnt that only 11 Professors are presently working out of 54 sanctioned

87
posts and 43 posts are lying vacant. The Institute has been able to fill up 33 posts of Additional
Professors out of 45 sanctioned posts, 53 posts of Associate Professors against 81 sanctioned
posts and 51 posts of Assistant Professor out of 125 sanctioned posts.
6.19 The Committee has been reiterating that shortage of qualified medical professional is
one of the key challenges that new AIIMS are presently facing. The Committee notes that
huge number of posts in both faculty and non faculty posts are lying vacant at AIIMS, Raipur.
Out of total sanctioned posts of 4089, 2636 posts are lying vacant that constitutes more than
64 per cent of the total sanctioned posts. The Committee, further, observes that majority of
the vacancies are at the Professor/Additional Professor level which indicates that the Institute
is suffering from dearth of experienced personnel. The Committee believes that health care
is one of the most labour-intensive sectors where human resources are crucial. Merely developing
infrastructure and increasing capital expenditure would not help, instead it is adequate manpower
at the health care Institutes that ensures efficient and prompt delivery of health care services
to the patients.  The Committee, therefore, would like the Institute to make concerted efforts
and accord equal priority to fill up the vacant posts.
6.20 The Institute needs to engage itself with continuous assessment of the gap in demand
and supply of human resources and take suitable steps/measures to fill up the gap. The
Committee is of the view that career aspiration, socio-economic status, organizational policies,
work environment, growth opportunities, infrastructural facilities are the main factors that
influence the decision of any health personnel to join any health care Institute. The Committee,
therefore, recommends to the Institute to focus on strengthening these crucial parameters
to attract adequate number of health care personnel.
6.21 The Committee is perturbed to observe that majority of the non faculty posts are
engaged either on contract or have been outsourced. The Committee believes that such
adhoc arrangements for deployment of medical professionals on contractual basis results in
disturbing issues of inconsistency in carrying on daily task that ultimately affects the
overall functioning of the Institute. It also affects the motivational level of the staff due
to prevailing uncertainties about job security and stability and thereby, lack of sense of
belongingness towards the organization.  Hence, the Committee calls upon the Institute to
strive for employing staff on permanent basis for ensuring continued and better delivery of
services.

6.23 The Committee finds that the status of vacancies in 18 Specialty and 12 Super Specialty
Departments is equally appalling. Under Specialty Departments,   out of a total of 146
sanctioned posts, 68 posts are lying vacant. While in Super-Specialty Departments, out of
81 sanctioned posts, 60 posts are vacant. The Committee expresses its concern that 16
Departments are suffering with vacancies of more than 50% of sanctioned posts. The
Departments i.e. Radiotherapy, Physical Medicine and Rehabilitation, Physical Medicine and
Rehabilitation, CTVS, Endocrinology and Metabolic Diseases and Neonatology are being handled
by a single faculty. Such huge vacancy will hamper the specialized services provided by the
Institute.  The main purpose of having Super Specialty services at the medical Institute is
to save the patients of the State from the compulsion of going out for treatment to other
metro cities but that very purpose is defeated due to the shortage of faculty.  The Committee,
therefore, strongly recommends to the Institute to fill up the vacant posts at the earliest
so as to provide best medical services to the patients who are visiting hospital with the hope
best of tertiary level healthcare services at lower cost. The Committee desires the Ministry
to take necessary action to get the vacant posts filled up without delay.
6.24 The Committee is distressed to observe that crucial Super Specialty Departments like
Surgical Gastroenterology, Nephrology, Medical Oncology/Hematology, Surgical Oncology and
Gastroenterology are not functional at AIIMS, Raipur. The Committee believes that there are
many diseases which require specialty diagnosis and treatment necessitating the availability
of Specialty and Super Specialty Departments. The Committee, therefore, recommends to the
Institute to make these Departments functional without any delay so that tertiary healthcare
services is made available for the patients from Chhattisgarh and neighbouring States.
Procurement of Equipment
6.25 The Committee has been informed that AIIMS, Raipur has implemented Central Public Procurement
Portal since 08-03-2017 and 100% tender cases are being uploaded in this portal. The use of
Central Public Procurement Portal not only provides wide publicity, but also helps in ensuring
competition and better rates. The Institute is also using GeM (Government e-market place) for
procurement of various items/equipment for its use.
6.26 The CAG in its 10th report has pointed out delay of 3 to 23 months in supply of 58 items
of equipment including ICU ventilator, CT 128 slice, orthopaedic operation theatre and MRI machine
valuing ` 44.46 crore in AIIMS Raipur.  42 equipment costing ` 33.80 crore were installed with
delay ranging from 4 to 41 months.  Further, 76 equipment costing ` 3.85 crore procured during
1      2 3 4 5 6

90
July, 2013 to December, 2016  were either not functional or remained unutilized/underutilised. The
reasons for equipment being non-functional were poor after sales service, equipment being defective
at the time of installation, breakdowns and absence of required manpower for operating the equipment.
6.27 The Committee deprecates the Institute for poor management of crucial equipment
worth crores. Procurement process involves commissioning, installation, training, warranty,
after sales services like post-warranty maintenance and assured availability of spares.  The
Committee is of the view that delay in the installation of procured equipment may jeopardize
after sales services for warranty period may lapse as per the contract. The Committee would
like the Institute to install the equipment as soon as these equipment are delivered. Further,
the equipment must be purchased only when the suitable infrastructure and manpower for
their installation and operation is available.
6.28 The Committee feels that delay in the delivery or installation of the equipment at the
Institute is certainly not a healthy trend. It deprives patients of diagnostic/therapeutic
benefits through the usage of such equipment. The delay in the supply of the equipment
is the fault on the part of the PSA/supplier/manufacturer and the Institute must take
suitable action against the errant supplier. The Committee is of the view that procurement
of equipment is a key area and therefore the Institute must resolve these issues by making
the PSA accountable for any delay and lapse.
6.29 The Committee is of the further view that quality of medical equipment needs to be
looked into because defective equipment result in frequent break-down of healthcare services.
Given the importance of medical equipment in diagnosis as well as treatment and the high
price involved in purchase of several hi-tech equipment, it is imperative that the equipment
are put to their best use for optimum utilization of resources. Non-installation of equipment
is indicative of lackadaisical approach of the Institute in letting them remain idle which is
mere wastage of resources at the cost of taxpayers’ money. The Committee, accordingly,
recommends the Institute to take strict action against the manufacturers for delayed delivery
or delivery of defective equipment. The Committee, further, recommends that Institute
should either exchange the defective equipment with new equipment or scrap the contract
with the manufacturers by seeking adequate legal redressal. The Committee recommends
for an action taken status report on the specific observations of the CAG in context of
procurement of medical equipment and recommends the Ministry to take strict action against
the errant officials.
6.30 In a specific query regarding the total number of medical equipment and procured with USFDA
certification and those with any other certification that have been procured at the Institute, the
Committee has been informed that in last two years, 159 types of medical equipment have been
procured with USFDA certification and 52 types of equipment have been procured with other certification.
6.31 The Committee observes that majority of 159 types of medical equipment during the
last two years have been purchased with USFDA certification. The Committee is concerned
at the substantial number of procurement done in AIIMS, Raipur with USFDA certification.
The Committee apprehends that some of them may be available with European/Indian
manufacturers. The Committee, in this connection, reiterates its recommendation made on

91
other AIIMS that the Institute must give preference to general CE/BIS certification to
increase competitive bidding. The Committee feels that where the equipment with BIS
certification is available, the Indian manufacturers should be given priority over the USFDA
or other certifications. The Institute should make efforts to contribute to the “Make in
India”, scheme of Government of India to give boost to Indian manufacturers. Further,
USFDA certification involves higher cost of equipment which would unfortunately be passed
on to the patients seeking treatment and care and thereby increasing their out of pocket
expenditure. The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends that USFDA certification may
be resorted to only in exceptional circumstances where other manufacturers do not match
the high parameters or urgency of equipment with higher standard is called for. The Committee
desires that the Institute should submit a status report on all medical devices/equipment
procured with USFDA certification/single bid/proprietary certification alongwith reasons for
doing so, within three months of presentation of this report to the Parliament.
6.32 The Committee hopes that AIIMS Raipur is adhering to procedure of multiple tendering
in the purchase of medical equipment and avoiding single bid purchase to ensure transparency
and public trust in procurement system of medical equipment.
Medical Education
6.33 AIIMS, Raipur started its academic journey in the September, 2012 with the first batch of
50 students joining the MBBS programme. From the year 2013, each batch has 100 students joining
the MBBS course. The College of Nursing started functioning in 2013 with intake of 60 students
each year. The Institute has started Post Graduate from July, 2013 in 22 Departments. A Post
graduate course have also commenced in the College of Nursing from July, 2018.
6.34 The Committee was given to understand that out of the 30 total PG seats, only 22
seats were filled in July, 2018. The seats in six Departments i.e. Anatomy, Biochemistry,
Forensic Medicine and Toxicology, Microbiology, Pharmacology and Physiology remained vacant.
The Committee expects the Institute to assess the reasons for the vacant seats and take
remedial action so that all seats get filled up from the next session onwards.
6.35 The Ministry has informed the Committee that clinical training is imparted MBBS students
from third semester onwards upto the ninth semester and also during one year compulsory
rotatory internship programme. For nursing students, the clinical training is imparted from first
year onwards upto the fourth year. For postgraduate students of clinical Departments, the entire
three years period is devoted to clinical training while for students of non-clinical and para-clinical
Departments, relevant clinical training is imparted as per the approved curriculum. Undergraduate
students have been imparted hands-on clinical training by faculty members and senior residents.
Adequate numbers of faculty members are available in the Institute for providing clinical training
to undergraduate students. The ratio of number of undergraduate students per Senior Resident in
a ward is approximately 20:1.
6.36 The Committee feels that clinical training is a vital part in a medical education for
becoming a successful doctor. The Committee is, however, apprehensive about the quality of
the clinical training imparted at the Institute to the undergraduate and postgraduate students
due to shortage of faculty at the Institute as many key Departments are being handled by

92
a single faculty. The Committee wonders how an Institute is accomplishing the objective of
imparting clinical training to the medical students with limited number of faculty. The
Committee also notes that seats under many PG courses are lying vacant which raises a
question mark about the quality of clinical training facilities available at the Institute.
Poor clinical training hampers the professional pursuit of doctors which amounts to risking
the lives of the patients. The Committee, therefore, recommends to the Institute to ensure
adequate faculty at the Institute for imparting quality medical education and clinical training
to the students aspiring to be well-trained doctors.
Status of Operation Theatre (OTs) and Intensive Care Unit (ICUs)
6.37 The Committee has been informed that 28 Modular Operation Theaters (MOTs) have been
sanctioned for the Institute. Presently, 8 MOTs are functioning with state-of-the-art equipment and
fully functional medical gas pipeline system. Commissioning of remaining 20 MOTs is under
progress. Remaining MOTs will be completed by December, 2018.
6.38 The Committee is of the view it is unfortunate that even after a lapse of considerable
period of time, only 8 operation theatres have been made functional. AIIMS, Raipur was
established with the aim of providing economical and reliable healthcare services to the
people visiting the hospital. Thus, the limited availability of OTs will definitely have an
adverse effect on the services provided by the Institute. The Committee hopes that the
Institute will expedite the process of construction of the remaining OTs to ensure that all
OTs are made functional without procrastination.
6.39 The Committee has not been able to assess the number of surgical procedures carried
out in AIIMS Raipur due to the variation in the data furnished by the Institute and the
Ministry. The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends that precise data with respect to
major and minor surgeries done (patient-wise and Department-wise) till date since the
inception of the Institute, may be submitted to the Committee within three months of
presentation of this Report.
6.40 On  a query related to  status of  ICUs at AIIMS, Raipur, the Committee was  informed that
there are four ICUs namely surgical ICU with 7 beds, Neonatal Intensive Care Unit (NICU) with
6 beds, Paediatric Intensive Care Unit (PICU) with 8 Beds and High Dependency Unit (HDU) with
5 beds. All the beds in these ICUs are equipped with ventilators, monitors, syringe pumps and
defibrillators. Out of 100 sanctioned beds, only 26 are functional. A total of 35 ventilators are
available in the hospital. The ICU facility in different clinical Departments requires high-end critical
care by fully trained personnel.
6.41 The Committee notes with concern that only 26 beds out of total sanctioned strength
of 100 beds are functional at the Institute. ICUs are used to provide specialized rehabilitative
care to patients as they start to recover from their critical illness or injury. The Committee
believes that bed scarcity in ICU may result in refusal of admission to initial patients,
thereby, depriving them the benefit of ICU services. The Committee observes that there are
as many as 35 ventilators for 26 ICU beds, indicating that 9 ventilators are lying unused
in the ICUs. The Committee feels that the value of the equipment starts depreciating
immediately after they are procured and non-functioning of the equipment results in loss

93
of their value. The Committee, therefore, recommends to the Institute to increase the
number of beds so that critically ill patients are not refused the service of ICUs due to
scarcity of beds and focus on optimum utilization of resources available.
Dialysis Facilities
6.42 The Committee has been informed that there is no faculty member in the Nephrology Department.
Earlier one faculty member had joined but resigned after six months. The dialysis facility will start
only after faculty member in Nephrology Department are appointed. The Institute is actively pursuing
faculty selections in various Super-Specialty Departments including Nephrology.
6.43 The Committee believes that Nephrology Department constitutes an important part of
any health institution in providing Super Specialty health care services to the patients.
Absence of the faculty would mean that patients requiring dialysis or treatment related to
renal diseases would be turned away due to non-functioning of the Department. The Committee,
therefore, recommends to the Institute to recruit the faculty as early as possible so that
dialysis services become functional at the Institute without any delay.
Ayush
6.44 The Committee was informed that AIIMS, Raipur started the AYUSH services in the year
2014. Till 2015, Homoeopathy and Ayurveda services were available at the Institute. In the year
2017-18, the Institute treated 4078 OPD patients, it is however, not providing IPD services in
AYUSH.
6.45 The sanctioned strength for Senior Medical Officer AYUSH and Yoga Instructor is one each
in the Institute and sanctioned strength for Medical Officer AYUSH is 5. At present one Senior
Medical Officer in the field of Ayurveda is working in AIIMS Raipur for the last four years and
serving patients in Ayurveda OPD. One Medical Officer (Homeopathy) was also working from
28.08.2014 to 28.12.2015. The doctor to patient ratio is 1:30 (approximately) for OPD.
6.46 The Committee observes that out of 5 AYUSH services, only Ayurveda services are
functional at the Institute at the present. The Committee underlines the need for starting
other branches of AYUSH system at the Institute so that the patients avail the facilities and
services of an alternate system of medicine. Patients would be benefitted immensely as
AYUSH medicines are not only cost effective but also widely used as a standalone option or
as adjunct with bio medicine in the diseases entailing long duration of treatment. The
Committee feels that the relevance of AYUSH has become more prominent due to this
paradigm shift in the health sector. The Committee, therefore, recommends to the Institute
to start other Ayush services as well as IPD services at the Institute without any further
delay.
6.47 The Committee notes that out of 7 sanctioned posts, only one post has been filled up
yet. One Senior Medical Officer (Ayurveda) is working on contractual basis since 1st September
2014. The Committee believes that the Institute needs to strengthen its in AYUSH wing and
provide cost effective and equitable AYUSH health care services throughout the country by
improving access to the services as envisaged under National Ayush Mission. To ensure the
optimal development and propagation of AYUSH systems of health care, there is the need

94
to have adequate faculty in the AYUSH Department of the Institute. The Committee, thus,
recommends to the Institute to fill up the vacant posts in the AYUSH Department on
regular basis without further delay.
6.48 The Committee finds AIIMS, Raipur to be lacking behind on almost all parameters.
However, the Committee would be taking AIIMS, Raipur for detailed examination and will
conduct a study visit which could not be done this time due to time constraints.

CHAPTER-VII
AIIMS, BHUBNESHWAR
7.1 All India Institute of Medical Sciences, Bhubaneswar is one of the apex healthcare Institutes
established under the Pradhan Mantri Swasthya Suraksha Yojna (PMSSY) with a mission to establish
a centre of excellence in medical education, training, health care and research imbued with scientific
culture, compassion for the sick and commitment to serve the underserved. AIIMS, Bhubaneswar
was established on 16th July, 2012.
7.2 The Ministry apprised the Committee that average OPD footfall was 2949 and IPD footfall
till 30th September 2018 was 14165 and the functional bed strength was 716 in 2018. Out of 53
sanctioned wards, 32 wards are functional at the Institute. As per information submitted by AIIMS,
Bhubaneswar, it has conducted 4873 major surgeries and 3348 minor surgeries till September,
2018. Out of 305 sanctioned faculty post and 3776 sanctioned non faculty posts, 184 faculty posts
and 836 non faculty posts have been filled up.
Budgetary Allocation
7.3 In response to the Committee’s query about the total allocation given to AIIMS, Bhubaneswar
till date, the Ministry has submitted the following Information:

7.4 The Committee notes that the percentage utilisation of funds was low till the year 2016-17.
In the year 2011-12, the percentage utilisation of funds was nil which increased to 20 percent in
the year 2012-13, 55 percent in the year 2013-14, but declined to 43 percent in the year 2014-15.
Thereafter, there has been steady increase in the percentage utilization of available funds. It has
been 45 percent in the year 2015-16, 55 percent in 2016-17, 75 percent in 2017-18 and 73.6
percent in the year 2018-19.
7.5 The Committee, further, notes that the utilisation of funds under the head GiA (CCA) was
only 16.7 percent in 2014-15, 42 percent in 2015-16, 58 percent in 2016-17, 63 percent in 2017-
18 and 57 percent in 2018-19. The Committee notes that the Grant in Aids remained unaudited
for the year 2017-18. In the years 2016-17 and 2017-18, the expenditure was more than the
funds allocated during the previous years which was managed from unspent balances of prev ious
years.
7.6 The Committee has been informed that there was no dearth of funds under the head GiA-

97
Creation of Capital Assets from which cost of construction activities and development projects
are met. However, the release of funds was insufficient under the head GiA-General in the year
2017-18.
7.7 The Institute has submitted following reasons with regard to under utilisation of funds:-
(i) In the year 2015-16 as against budget allocation of ` 98 crore under GiA-CCA, the fund
released was ` 121 crore.
(ii) In the year 2016-17, as far as GiA-Salaries is concerned, it was expected that posts
relating to F aculty and Non-Faculty category shall be filled up to a sizeable extent
which could not take place due to non-joining/belated joining of employees. As regards
expenditure under C.C.A, construction work at hospital was slowed down due to
technical prob lem.
(iii) In the year 2017-18, the expenditure under Salaries was less due to partial payment
(80%) of the arrear salary of faculty members owing to non-finalization of the anomalies
in their salary structure as per the 7th CPC recommendations (CCS RP Rules2016).
(iv) The financial power of Director which is presently ` 5 crore was earlier ` 2 crore.
only, which resulted in slowing down the expenditure.
7.8 The Committee expresses concern over the constant underutilisation of funds in AIIMS,
Bhubaneswar from the year 2011 to 2017.  There has been abysmal increase in the percentage
utilisation of funds from 2016-17 onwards, however, it remained below 75 percent of the
total available funds. The Committee feels that improper planning and lack of monitoring
mechanism in the initial years led to under-utilization of Grants in Aid under the head
CCA that adversely affected the construction and other developmental activities at the
Institute and resulted in cost overruns. This is indicative of financial mismanagement
within the Institute. The underutilization of available funds will only result in inordinate
delay in realization of both the short as well as long-term objectives of the Institute. The
Committee, therefore, is of the view that the funds allocated to the Institute needs to be
reviewed from time to time. An effective strategy ensuring fiscal discipline by continuous
and efficacious monitoring would help in strengthening the financial management in the
Institute.
7.9 The Committee finds that funds allocated in the year 2017-18 remained unaudited. The
Committee underlines the need of an audit of the allocated funds on a regular basis.
Needless to say, audit ensures financial reliability and compliance with laws and statutory
regulations.
Status of Construction Work

AIIMS, Bhubaneswar. The last to be completed was the hospital complex building which was to
be completed in 31.10.2018. The brief status of construction work at AIIMS, Bhubaneswar as on
15th October, 2018 is given below:-

7.11 The management of the Institute has conceded that the main reasons for the delay in construction
activities were preparation of erroneous bills, delays in issue of drawings by design consultants,
slow progress of works by contractor and Project Consultant and Design and Detail Project Report
(DDPR).
7.12 The Committee takes serious note of the delay in most of the cases due to non-
performance of construction agency. The work of Package-I that includes construction of
medical complex building commenced on 27th May, 2010 and the original date of completion
was 26th August, 2011. The work was actually completed on 31st August, 2017 with time
overrun of 72 months and cost overrun of ````` 8.56 crore. Package-II that includes construction
of Hospital Complex buildings was started on 16th September, 2010 and original date of
completion was 15th September, 2012. The work under this package has seen time overrun
of 73 months and the cost of the project has increased to ````` 297.07 crore from ````` 261.65 crore
as estimated originally. Similarly, the work under Package-III that includes electrical
services got started on 16th September, 2010 and original target date of completion was
11th January, 2013 but the work was completed on 31st July, 2015 with a time overrun
of 30 months and cost overrun of ````` 1.51 crore. The work under Package-IV had started on
4th July, 2012 with scheduled date of completion by 3rd July, 2013 but the work was actually
completed on 28th February, 2018 with a time overrun of 55 months. The total cost escalation
under the four Packages is approximately ````` 35.36 crore.
7.13 The Committee takes cognizance of the observation of CAG Report that delay in
execution of construction projects that is reproduced below:-
“The main reasons for the delays were preparation of erroneous bills of quantities, delays in
issue of drawings by design consultants, delays in  providing work sites, delays in clearance
of deviations, extra items and substituted items, cash flow problems of agencies due to
withheld payments due to delays in granting clearances, slow progress of work by contractors,
vacancies in engineering positions in the Project Cell of the new AIIMS and abandonment
of works by Project Consultants and delay in making alternate arrangements. These reasons
were indicative of deficient project and contract management, administrative laxity and weak
monitoring. It was incumbent upon the departments to take effective steps where necessary to
mitigate the delays, remove bottlenecks and expedite the progress.”
7.14 The Committee is of the view that underutilisation of the funds during years 2011-2017
is the main reason behind the delay in completion of the project and cost overruns. The
Committee expresses its concern over delay and cost overruns in different projects of
various packages. Time and cost over runs are undesirable in projects under construction.
The delays in construction project have delayed delivery of health services. The Committee
observes that delay in completion of the project was on account of poor planning, unrealistic
scheduling of the dates, lack of communication between the contracting agency and the
authorities concerned at the Institute and poor monitoring.
7.15 The delay in the completion of the project has also been attributed to services by
former Project Consultant. The Committee believes that the track record of delivery of
projects of the contractor as well as Project Consultant must be taken into account before
awarding such important project work to them. The Committee, therefore, recommends the

100
Ministry to examine the reasons for delay in the construction projects and fix responsibility
and accountability for delayed work in all four packages. The Committee further recommends
to the Ministry/Institute to engage other professional agencies or other reputed construction
majors for upcoming new AIIMS to ensure cost effective and timely execution of projects
especially in projects where implementing agencies have failed to ensure timely completion.
7.16 W ith regard to residential complex, the Committee has been informed that the residential
project as per the contract has been completed by M/s HSCC (I) Ltd. but it is not as per staff
strength. The work for residential complex was started on 18th September, 2008 and original date
of completion was 3rd March, 2016 but the work actually got completed on 26th September, 2017.
The Committee has been informed that the Institute is having sufficient number of hostel accommodation
for the present strength of students. The demand for residential accommodation from the faculty
and non-faculty is minimal as private accommodation is available in the adjoining area. However,
action is being taken to construct new accommodation for senior residents, nursing and para-medical
staff in the second phase. The Committee has been informed that there is more requirement of the
physical layout/infrastructure of the Institute to accommodate all Departments/teaching faculties.
7.17 The Committee is happy to note that management of AIIMS Bhubaneswar has explored
the idea of private residential accommodation in the adjoining areas. The Committee understands
that new AIIMS, still facing infrastructure constraints can easily avoid undue stress through
arrangement of private accommodation in the nearby areas and the limited resource can
better be utilized on creation of much needed physical infrastructure. The Committee
acknowledges AIIMS Bhubaneswar for its sound judgment and right emphasis on the more
important requirements and demands of the Institute. The Committee also appreciates the
Institute for completing all the construction projects under the four Packages. The Committee
recommends the Institute to expedite and monitor the other construction activities and
work on creating the required infrastructure for accommodating all its Departments.
7.18 The Committee takes note of the expansion programmes of the Institute as envisaged in the
forthcoming years:-
(i) Construction of New Administrative Building (G+8).
(ii) Construction of Examination Hall (G+2).
(iii) Setting up a high-end simulation laboratory and computer lab for teaching.
(iv) Setting up a Burns Unit.
(v) Setting up a Trauma Centre.
(vi) Setting up Centres of Excellence in various super-specialty Departments.
(vii) Development of Training Complex at Tangi for rural training of doctors/students.
7.19 The Committee is given to understand that the present infrastructure of the Institute
is not sufficient to accommodate all Departments within the Institute. The Committee,
however, emphasizes the necessity of having a Burns Unit and Trauma Centre in the Institute
on a priority basis. The Committee, therefore, recommends to the Institute to take necessary
steps to expedite the process of formulating necessary plans, prepare DPR and seek administrative

101
approvals for projects to be undertaken under the expansion programme to accommodate
the construction of Burns Unit, Trauma Centre, Rural Training Complex at Tangi, Centre
of excellence in various super-specialty Department, high-end simulation laboratory and
computer lab, examination hall and new Administrative building within set timelines and at
the estimated cost. The Committee would specifically like to point out that setting up of
centers of excellence in various Super Specialty should only be done when there is ample
availability of faculty/super-specialists and keeping in mind the budgetary implications. However,
the final decision regarding setting up of advanced centres of super-specialty should be
taken by the CIB.
Procurement of Medical Equipment
7.20 AIIMS, Bhubaneswar has shared the following status of procurement of equipment in AIIMS,
Bhubaneswar:-
(a) All the equipment worth above `2.50 lakh are being procured by AIIMS, Bhubaneswar
through e-tender/tender. The number of bidders in e-tender/tender are very few and in
technical evaluation of the bids, sometimes all the bidders get disqualified due to not
fulfillment of technical requirements. Retendering process with revised specification is
quite time consuming as well.
(b) Sometimes, after due technical evaluation, it is found that only one bidder is qualifying
in the e-tender/tender. Taking into urgency of patient care and certificate from the
indenter, the Institute has to finalize some cases in single bid after following the relevant
provision of GFR.
(c) The e-tender/tender terms and conditions are made as per the instructions of the
MoHFW which provides that there should be 5 years warranty and 5 years CMC after
completion of warranty period. The same perhaps is seen as a bottleneck to get
responses from more bidders and it also makes the price quoted by bidder at higher
side.
(d) The Institute presently finalizes the technical specification of equipment costing more
than `30 lakh with the involvement of the DGHS nominee as per the extant instructions
of the Ministry. This process takes a lot of time due to non-availability of DGHS
nominee in time for finalizing the technical specification. As a result, the procurement
process gets delayed. In this regard, it is suggested that broad based technical specifications
for equipment may be finalized either by technical expert for all the AIIMS regarding
procurement of high end/sophisticated equipment for all AIIMSs or technical specification
of the AIIMS, New Delhi for similar equipment may be followed.
(e) Sometimes it is felt that after finalisation of the tendering process, some of the bidders
are back-tracking to execute the order due to high fluctuation in value of foreign
currency.
7.21 The Committee takes cognizance of the problems highlighted by the Institute in the
procurement of the equipment. The Committee observes that sometimes even after multiple
floating of tender, only single bid is received and there are few cases when multiple bids

102
are received but only single bid gets qualified on technical grounds. The Committee believes
that there is a need to advertise the tender to be floated on a large scale so that more and
more manufacturers submit their bids leading to competitive pricing and availability of
many options for procurement of equipment. The Committee, thus, recommends to the
Institute to advertise their tenders in the national dailies and also integrate into the system
of the Central Public Procurement Portal for wider dissemination of information regarding
the floating of tender so as to make it accessible to large number of manufacturers. The
Committee, further, recommends to the Institute to list out the technical requirements
more clearly by making them generic so that there are fewer chances of bids getting
disqualified on technical grounds. The tendency to deliberately design specifications to ensure
that procurement is done on proprietary/single bid basis should be avoided under all
circumstances.  The Committee would like to highlight that the problem areas as identified
by AIIMS, Bhubaneswar are welcome since it would help the procedural hurdles and help
in overall streamlining of the whole process of procurement of medical equipment. The idea
of finalizing uniform technical specification for requirement especially high end equipment
is certainly a step in the right direction.  The Committee strongly supports and recommends
for appropriate time bound action on the suggestions of AIIMS, Bhubaneswar for improving
the procurement process.
7.22 For procurement of medical devices and equipment AIIMS, Bhubaneswar, has been using
Tender wizard of M/S-ITI Ltd. for e-tendering similar to Central Public Procurement Portal. The
overall policy and procedure for procurement of medical equipment of AIIMS, Bhubaneswar is as
per the GFR-2017 and instructions of the MoHFW. The Institute has also engaged M/S HITES
(a PSU of MoHFW) as Procurement Service Agent for procurement of high value medical equipment
as done by the Ministry for Central procurement.
7.23 In a specific query regarding procurement of equipment on single bid basis, the Committee
has been informed that during last two years, there were 14 types of equipment for which only
single bid were received and for six types of equipment multiple bids were received but only the
single bidder qualified.
7.24 The Committee observes that AIIMS, Bhubaneswar has been using Tender Wizard for
e-tendering for procurement of medical equipment. The Committee, therefore, desires that
the Institute should implement/use the Central Public Procurement Portal of Government
of India along with Tender Wizard for procurement of medical devices and equipment. The
Committee believes that both the portals may help in facilitating all the Central Government
Organisations to publish their tender enquiries, corrigendum and award of contract details.
These portals bestow a single point access to the information on procurements made across
various Central Government Organizations. This enables the manufacturers of the medical
devices and equipment to have access to the information regarding the floating of the
tender by the Government. The Committee is of the view that the main idea of using both
the portals is to disseminate information about the tenders on a wide platform.
7.25 In reply to a specific query regarding procurement of medical equipment with USFDA certification,
the Committee has been informed that there are 143 types of equipment that have been procured
with CE/USFDA certification in last two years. Further, 130 types of equipment have been procured

103
with ISO/BIS certification at the institute during the same period. The USFDA certification products
are being procured as per the requirement set by the doctors to fulfill patient safety measures,
user-friendly and non-availability of Indian manufacturers.
7.26 The Committee appreciates that AIIMS Bhubaneswar has procured 130 types of equipment
with ISO/BIS certification and encourages AIIMS Bhubaneshwar to carry out all the necessary
procurement from non USFDA/Indian manufactur ers. The Committee, however, recommends
AIIMS Bhubaneswar to avoid inappropriate procurement of medical devices/equipment with
USFDA certification for basic equipment such as Bipolar Forceps, Microscope etc. The Committee
believes that standard of quality, price and other basic requirements should be reviewed
closely along with the endorsing the Make in India campaign.
7.27 The Committee has been informed that the following equipment were not installed due to lack
of manpower:-

7.28 The Committee is dismayed to note that three equipment under the Cardiothoracic and
Vascular Surgeons (CTVS) Department remained uninstalled for a period ranging from 12
to 24 months due to non-availability of the personnel under the Department. The Committee
takes serious note of such delay in installation of equipment which may have resulted in
lapse of warranty/guarantee of the equipment. The Committee, therefore, chides the Institute
for delayed installation of the equipment. The Committee, in this regard, recommends that
in future, equipment should be procured only when the required faculty and infrastructure
for the functioning of the equipment is available.
Status of Faculty and Non-Faculty Strength
7.29 The Committee has been informed that the total sanctioned strength of teaching faculty in
Medical College is 305 and against which only 184 faculties are on the roll while 121 posts are
lying vacant. Out of 3776 non faculty posts, only 836 posts are filled up whereas 2940 posts are
still lying vacant.  Out of the total filled up posts of non faculty staff, 638 are filled on regular
basis and 198 on contractual basis. Similarly, there are 45 sanctioned posts of faculty for Nursing
College out of which 38 posts are filled up presently.
7.30 On the issue of vacant posts, the Ministry has clarified that all vacancies in faculty were
advertised twice, the selection process have been completed but candidates were not found.  No
eligible candidates have applied for some posts. Many reserved posts in super- specialties remained
vacant due to non availability of suitable candidates and lack of response for senior positions such
as Professor and Additional Professor.

104
7.31 As for non-faculty posts, the Recruitment Rules were approved in the year 2016. The process
for engaging a third party recruitment agency to make the selection process more transparent and
objective took time.
7.32 The following steps have been taken by the Institute to expedite the filling up of vacant posts:-
(i) A third party recruitment agency had been engaged to conduct on-line computer based
written test for Group-B and Group-C posts.
(ii) V acant Group-A posts will be advertised very soon. Similarly the vacancy in Group-C
will be advertised in phase-wise as per the requirement.
(iii) Advertisements for selection of Senior Residents under Central Residency Scheme are
issued twice in a year and walk-in-interviews are also conducted to meet urgent requirements
of Senior Residents.
7.33 The Committee is perturbed to note that approximately 40 per cent of faculty posts and
78 per cent non-faculty posts are lying vacant at the Institute. The Committee notes that out
of 54 sanctioned posts for Professor, only 17 posts have been filled up and under 24 Departments,
posts for Professor are lying vacant. Further, 13 Departments are being run either by an
Associate or Assistant Professor. The Committee believes that shortage of health personnel
to this extent is a critical stressor for hospitals. The Committee underlines the need for
requisite number of health personnel in place for smooth functioning of the Institute.
7.34 The Committee is not satisfied with the reasons for large number of vacancies at the
Institute. The Committee believes that special attractive package and incentives should be
offered by the Institute to attract the required talent at the super specialty level. The
vacancies should be advertised in national dailies so that it has wider reach and larger
number of candidates should apply for the posts. Moreover, manpower planning needs to be
reoriented to determine the requirement of adequate numbers of medical specialists to meet
the future demand of medical personnel for all healthcare. The Committee desires the
Institute to expedite the process of recruitment commencing from re-advertising the vacant
posts for selection of faculty and non-faculty staff without further procrastination. The
Committee also recommends for a common all India examination for all the non-faculty
posts through a third party recruitment agency for all AIIMS which will make the selection
process more transparent and objective.

7.36 The Committee is dismayed to note that most of the Specialty and Super-Specialty
Departments are functional despite inadequate number of faculty in place.  Barring Dentistry,
all other Departments are plagued with shortage of faculty. The status of faculty in Departments
of Radio-Diagnosis, Nephrology, Urology, Surgical oncology, Clinical hematology, Burns and
Plastic surgery, Nuclear medicine is very pathetic. The Departments of Endocrinology and
Metabolic diseases and Nephrology are run by one faculty single handedly. The Committee
believes that quality treatment and adequate care would only be possible with adequate
faculty. The Committee, therefore, recommends the Institute to employ adequate number
of faculty in each Department.
7.37 The Committee observes that Surgical Gastroenterology Department is not functional
at the Institute. Undoubtedly, this amounts to utmost neglect of the patients suffering from
diseases related to the human gastrointestinal tract. The Committee, therefore, calls upon
the Institute to start the Surgical Gastroenterology Department at the Institute with adequate
manpower and infrastructure without any delay.
Medical Education
7.38 AIIMS, Bhubaneswar started its academic journey in August, 2012 with the first batch of 50
students joining the MBBS programme. From the year 2013 onwards, one batch of 100 students
has joined the MBBS course every year. The College of Nursing started functioning in September,
2013 and admits 60 students each year. The first clinical posting of B.Sc. Nursing students started
in AIIMS, Bhubaneswar from 3rd February, 2014 and students were posted in various wards based
on their clinical requirements. The Post Graduate (M.Sc. Nursing) has started in 2018 with strength
of 20 per batch. The internship programme of the first MBBS Batch, 2012 was completed on 31st
December, 2017 and the B.Sc. (H) Paramedical course started from July, 2016. The students who
1       2    3 4 5

107
got admission in the year 2012-13 got required clinical exposure during the course duration as the
hospital became operational in the last quarter of 2013.
7.39 As regards the status of PG courses at AIIMS, Bhubaneswar, the Ministry has submitted that
there are 86 sanctioned PG seats in 23 different Departments and 45 seats have been filled up in
the year 2018.
7.40 The Committee observes that while there has been 100% admission in MBBS and nursing
course, only 45 seats have been filled up out of the 86 sanctioned PG seats. It is indeed a
saddening affair to observe the vacant PG seats at an Institute of national importance as it
indicates sheer wastage of resources. The Committee, however, recommends to the Institute to
ascertain the detailed reasons for not filling up of the PG seats at the Institute and take
immediate steps to remove obstacles if any, in filling up of the PG seats with immediate effect.
7.41 It was informed to the Committee that MBBS students are posted in various clinical Departments
from 3rd semester onwards in small batches for Basic Life Support (BLS) training. Undergraduate
students get clinical training under the supervision of residents and faculty members.  PG students
are given a PG orientation programme on communication skills like breaking bad news, written and
oral communication skills like discharge summary, filling up MLC form, death certificate etc. They
are also sensitized in ethics and professionalism. Individual Departments train students on protocols
being followed in OT, ICU etc. Interns are having an Interns Orientation Programme to introduce
them to protocols such as blood transfusions, OT surgical check-list, Advanced Trauma Life
Support (ATLS), infection control, medication safety etc.
7.42 The Committee observes that the present strength of faculty at the Institute is not
sufficient to offer clinical training to the students. The Committee feels that deficient
clinical training may be one of the reasons for not filling up the PG seats at the Institute.
The Committee is of the view that clinical training is crucial for emphasizing the overall
development of the medical insight of the students. A medical student will become more
efficient and productive if he/she is trained well. The Committee, thus, recommends to the
Institute to make the clinical training programme more effective with sufficient faculty and
adequate infrastructure for training to the students.
Status of Operation Theatres and Wards/Beds
7.43 A total of 25 Modular OTs (MOTs) have been sanctioned at AIIMS Bhubaneswar out of
which 7 OTs are functional at the Institute. 14 MOTs are expected to be functional by December,
2018 and remaining MOTs may be functional by January/February, 2019. The new OTs are well
equipped, and the work of medical gas pipe line is also under progress.
7.44 The waiting time for surgical procedures ranges from one week to 3-4 months. The situation
is likely to improve with the commissioning of 25 OTs, 112 ICU beds, increasing the bed strength
to 978 and introducing screening OPD.
7.45 The Committee takes note that out of 25 sanctioned OTs, only 7 are functional at
present resulting into waiting time of one week to four months for surgical procedures
under various Departments. The Committee is of the view that the Institute was established
with the aim of providing affordable and reliable tertiary healthcare services to the people
and preventing them from shuttling to other metro cities in search of tertiary level health care

108
services. Further, the Committee finds the non-availability of gas pipe line in OTs unacceptable.
The Committee recommends to the Institute to make the remaining 18 sanctioned OTs
functional without any delay. The Institute should also ensure that OTs are equipped with
latest equipment including Medical Gas Pipeline System (MGPS).
7.46 The Committee has been informed that out of 53 sanctioned wards, 32 wards are functional
at the Institute with bed strength of 716, though the sanctioned bed strength at the institute is 978.
At present, the Trauma and Emergency services, OTs/ICUs are working with limited available
resources. The Institute has arrangements for 50 disaster beds (foldable) to meet the emergency
situation which have been used several times. With the improvement of infrastructure AIIMS,
Bhubaneswar will be well equipped to deal with the large number of casualties.
7.47 The Committee is not satisfied with the functional status of the Institute as only 32
wards with 716 beds are functional at the Institute. The Institute is also not properly
equipped to cater to the need for a quick and responsive care in medical emergency. Good
trauma care means getting the patient to the right place at the right time for appropriate
care. The Committee, thus, recommends to the Institute to step up the strength of wards
and beds as per the sanctioned strength. It also needs to expedite the process of construction
of remaining OTs equipped with latest equipment, specialized resources, and a specially
trained trauma and emergency team for quality and timely care. It is more so required for
treatment of the patients suffering from life threatening illnesses and traumatic injuries.
The Committee, therefore, expects that concerted efforts will be made in this regard and
the matter should be taken up on a priority basis.
7.48 Attention of the Committee has been drawn to the high cost per bed in AIIMS Bhubaneswar.
The Committee notes that in AIIMS Raipur the current per bed construction cost is ` 1.14 crore
which is inclusive of the construction, equipment and furniture cost for 960 beds.
7.49 The Committee is surprised to note the wide difference in the cost per bed in these
two Institutes that have the same functional capacity and the same number of beds. The
Committee takes a serious note of this high cost incurred by AIIMS, Bhubaneswar especially
when the other AIIMS like Institutions, providing the same tertiary healthcare facilities,
have submitted relatively less cost. The Committee, therefore, recommends the Institute to
limit its redundant expenses. The Committee demands that the Institute should submit a
status note on how the cost per bed was calculated and the reasons behind this high cost
so that cost per bed may be calculated for other AIIMS also on the same parameters.
Dialysis Facilities
7.50 The Committee also notes that in AIIMS, Bhubaneswar, Department of Nephrology is non-
functional and the process of setting up a dialysis is still under process.
7.51 The Committee recommends that AIIMS, Bhubaneswar should expedite the setting up
of dialysis unit especially in the wake of rising demand for dialysis services.
AYUSH Facilities
7.52 As regards the status of AYUSH facility at AIIMS, Bhubaneswar, the Committee has been
informed that AIIMS, Bhubaneswar started the AYUSH services in February, 2015. Ayurveda and

109
Yoga OPD services are functioning in AIIMS, Bhubaneswar. Since the start of AYUSH services,
the Institute has seen 34750 OPD patients in AYUSH. Further, the Institute is not providing IPD
services in AYUSH.
7.53 Sanctioned strength for AYUSH Department is 6 posts against which only one medical officer
is working that too on contractual basis. The selection process of 5 permanent AYUSH doctors is
undergoing. In addition, there is a project of CCRYN (Central Council for Research in Yoga &
Naturopathy) in which there is support for patient care services in terms of manpower, equipment
etc. All Naturopathy treatments are being given to patients.
7.54 The Committee observes that out of five services under AYUSH, only two services i.e.
Y oga and Ayurveda OPD services are functional at the AIIMS, Bhubaneswar. The Committee
is of the considered view that the AYUSH systems offer an alternative system of medicine
to the patients and is generally cheaper and affordable with lesser side effects.  Gaps in
India’s healthcare sector are huge with a large proportion of the population virtually being
denied access to healthcare facilities. To address these gaps, there is a need to support
AYUSH systems of medicine and education, particularly in vulnerable and far flung areas.
The Committee desires the Institute to make earnest efforts to start other AYUSH services
as well as IPD services at the Institute. The Committee recommends that apprehensions
about the efficacy and safety of AYUSH systems need to be allayed through undertaking
suitable desired level of scientific research and development of good AYUSH practices.
Further Standard Treatment protocols need to be developed also to build public trust in
these systems.
7.55 The Committee is distressed to note that out of six sanctioned posts at the Ayush
Department, only one post has been filled up so far and that too on contractual basis. The
Committee believes that without adequate manpower, the Institute will not be able to
achieve the objective of strengthening AYUSH services. The Committee, therefore, recommends
to the Institute to fill up the vacant posts on regular basis without further delay.

CHAPTER-VIII
UPGRADATION OF GOVERNMENT MEDICAL COLLEGES/
INSTITUTIONS UNDER PMSSY
8.1 Upgradation of several existing Government Medical Colleges/Institutions in different States of
the country is one of the two components under the PMSSY . The programme for upgradation of
Government Medical Colleges/Institutions (GMCIs) broadly includes the following (i) construction
of Super Specialty Blocks/Trauma Centers, (ii) procurement of medical equipment; (iii) generation
of employment opportunities; and (iv) enhancement of number of seats of Post Graduate and
Graduate courses in GMCs under PMSSY .
8.2 Post-Graduate seats and additional faculty posts as per norms will be created and filled up
in these GMCs by the respective State /UT Governments. The responsibility of running the new
facilities rests with the State Government which has to make all provisions, including complete
Human Resource (Specialist/ Super-specialists and paramedics etc). The Government decided to
increase the number the GMCs to be upgraded in subsequent phases of the scheme. After a review
of the coverage area of existing Government institutions and planned institutions and on the
recommendations of the Working Group for Tertiary Care Institutions for 12th Five Year Plan.
8.3 As per the information submitted by the Ministry, as of October 2018, 73 GMCs has been
approved for upgradation under the six-phases of PMSSY . A Total of 19 GMCs upgradation
projects were sanctioned in Phase I and II of the PMSSY , out of which 15 projects have been
completed. Upgradation of GMCs undertaken in Phase III and Phases-IV & V A are yet not complete
and construction activities at these Institutes are in progress.
8.4 The Ministry further informed the Committee that for the Phase-III and later phases upgradation
projects, Ministry in consultation with the State Governments has also focused on augmentation of
the PG seats in the process of creation of the Super-specialty infrastructure. Under the gap analysis
process, agreement of the State Government has also been taken for creation of the PG seats
which would become feasible with the operationalization of the new facilities.
Budgetary Allocation and Funding Pattern
8.5 The Ministry apprised the Committee about the details of funds released by MoHFW for up-
gradation of medical colleges under PMSSY , the year wise list of funds released for the upgradation
project has been given by the Ministry as enumerated in the following table:
(` in Crore)

8.6 The funding pattern for GMCs Up-gradation projects, was to be on sharing basis between the
Central and State Government. The funding Pattern for GMCs upgradation projects is as below:
(` In Crore)
GMC/Institute                               Approved outlay ( ` Crore)
