8.7 Though the scheme envisaged funding for upgradation of GMCs on sharing basis between the
Central and State Governments, a few States have given/committed to give higher share to meet
the additional cost of the project over and above Rs 150 or Rs 200 Crore. In such cases, the share
of Government of India however has not changed. A total amount of ` 5239.71 crore has been
released by the Ministry till date for the upgradation project of GMCs under PMSSY .
8.8 The Committee has been informed that in case of GMCs upgradation works under Phase III
of PMSSY , the State share is pending in about 50% cases (Annexure-C) . PMSSY division had
taken up this matter at senior level with the State Governments in meetings and through Letters
to State Governments. The state officials have assured early release of the State share. The delay
in the release of the State share has basically been due to internal approval processes in the States.
8.9 The CAG Report (2018) on PMSSY has highlighted that Clause 10 of the MoU States that
the GMCs/State Government shall not divert the grants for any other purpose and unutilized grants
shall be refunded to the Ministry. It was observed that Four GMCs namely BJMC-Ahmedabad,
BMCRI-Bangalore, NIMS-Hyderabad and RIMS-Ranchi diverted funds amounting to ` 26.71 crore
for other purposes. For example, funds for procurement were diverted to meet cost escalation of
civil works and installation of gas manifold system, comprehensive maintenance contract and
purchase of consumables items. It was also observed that funds for computerization were diverted
for centralized air conditioning and minor civil works. The cases relate to GMCs which were
within the jurisdiction of the Institutes and State Governments. The CAG report further highlights
that in five GMCIs, funds amounting to ` 63.85 crore, received for civil works and procurement
of equipment, remained idle for periods ranging from two to five years.
8.10 The Committee takes into account the budgetary allocation and funding pattern  of
select 73 GMCs under five phases in PMSSY for which the Ministry of Health and Family
Welfare has released a total amount of `````5239.71 crore.  However, the State share is pending
in about 50% case of GMCs for upgradation works under Phase III. The Committee observes
that delay in release of State share has impacted the upgradation projects adversely. The
Committee desires the Ministry to urgently take the matter with State Governments concerned
to release their share at the earliest so that the work of GMCs does not suffer. The
Committee, in this regard, would like to emphasize that the Ministry/State Governments
concerned must comply with the fund sharing commitment to maintain the sanctity of

112
project. The Ministry needs to put in place a mechanism for release of funds within a
stipulated time period from both Centre and State Governments. The Committee, therefore,
recommends concerted action on part of both the Centre and State Governments to make
the upgradation project successful. The Committee, therefore, recommends that all out
efforts need to be taken by the Ministry to get all the procedural formalities/ necessary
approvals done well in time and the State Governments concerned need to be persuaded and
advised regularly in this regard. A quarterly status report on the pending position with
respect to the State share may also be provided to enable the Committee to take up the
matter with the concerned State Government as most of the delays are due to internal
processes of the States.
8.11 The Committee is of the view that the funds allocated to the project should be effectively
and efficiently utilized and not remain idle or underutilised as that would otherwise adversely
impact the smooth execution of the project. Moreover, such instances reflect faulty planning
and improper administration with regard to utilization of the allocated funds. The Committee
views this as a matter of concern. The Committee observes that upgradation of GMCs is
under the direct supervision of the Central Government, therefore, under utilization of the
allocated funds should not become a routine affair as the same would lead to further delays
in completion of projects causing time and cost overruns. The Committee, therefore, strongly
recommends that the Ministry must act promptly and monitor the effective and timely
utilization of funds.
8.12 The Committee views diversion of funds as criminal misappropriation of allocated fund
and violation of fiscal discipline. Such trend not only results in the shortfall of the funds
for completion of construction activities but is against the provisions of the MoU signed
between the Ministry and the States. Effective monitoring and accountability need to be
ensured so that funds released are utilised for the intended purpose. The Committee expects
the Ministry to issue strict advisories against the diversion of funds and fixing of responsibility
on GMCs/State Governments for the same.
Key Concern Areas
8.13 In reply to a specific query about the areas of concern/challenges in upgradation of GMCs,
the Ministry has submitted the following:-
(i) Timely availability of encumbrance free site in the GMCs has been one of the key
challenges faced in several projects under Phase III.  In many projects, the State
Governments have taken long time in making available encumbrance free sites.  There
have been delays in removal of pre-existing physical structures, services and, in some
cases, trees standing on the site by the respective State Governments.
(ii) Delays in deciding on the scope of the upgradation project in terms of the physical
facilities, number of beds, list of medical equipment etc.  by the GMCs/State  Governments
has been another concern.  Most of the States have found it difficult to firm up the
scope under the gap analysis, as, generally as per the perception of the States, the
requirements are much more than what the limited budget can accommodate.
(iii) There have also been instances of delay in the release of the State share. However, all

113
the States have given commitment to provide the State share. MoU with the State
Governments also have specific provision regarding the commitment of the State
Governments for the State share.  The delay in the release of the State share has
basically been due to the internal approval processes in the States. The issue has been
taken up at senior level in the State Governments through letters and over phone and
the State s have also been taking action for early release of the share.
8.14 The Ministry has taken up the various issues, with the State Governments and has flagged
in the Ministerial level interactions with the States at the level of Health and Family Welfare Minister
and MoS.  However, the progress of the projects has not been allowed to be suffered due to this
delay as the Ministry has ensured timely release of the central share to the Executive agencies
(EAs) and to the Procurement Support Agent (PSA).
CAG’s Observation
8.15 CAG Report highlighted many areas of concern in upgradation of GMCs which are as
follows:-
(i) Delay in commencement of projects in all three phases.
(ii) Inordinate delays in completion of construction works of GMCs after around of work
in the first three phases of PMSSY .
(iii) Non-compliance with codal and contract provisions as evident in Bangalore Medical
College and Research Institute (BMCRI), Bangalore, institute of medical science (IMS),
Varanasi, B.J. Medical College (BJMC), Ahmedabad, Gajra Raja Medical College (GRMC),
Gwalior.
(iv) Award of works at higher cost.
(v) Non-availability of equipment due to placement of orders, change in model of the
equipment and lack of follow up by the Institute.
(vi) Non-installation/delay in installment of equipment and idle/non-functional equipment.
(vii) Shortage of human resources in upgraded GMCs.
(viii) Non-functioning of super specialty facilities due to shortage of staff and due to lack of
equipment
8.16 The Committee takes note of the key concern areas of GMCs upgradation. The Committee
observes that the Ministry has put the entire onus on the State Governments for successful
implementation of the scheme. The Committee is of the view that the Central Government
has a key role in planning, guiding and monitoring the scheme. The Ministry needs to
consider State Governments as its partner in resolving the constraints being encountered in
administration of projects and sort out practical difficulties faced in implementation thereof.
There is a need for an efficacious monitoring mechanism to avoid inordinate delays in
completion of upgradation projects. The Committee, thus, recommends to the Ministry to
make a realistic assessment of the challenges faced and formulate suitable plan of action
in active coordination with the State Governments to overcome the same. All out efforts

114
must be made by both the Central Government and the State Governments to strive towards
completion of upgradation of GMCs within specified the timeline.
8.17 The attention of the Committee has been drawn to the CAG findings wherein serious
areas of concern have been highlighted especially w.r.t non-compliance of codal and contract
provision and awards of work at higher cost. The Committee is of the firm view that strict
compliance with codal and contract provision need to be adhered in all circumstances and
award of work should be assigned on merit and proven track record.
Selection of the GMCs for upgradation
8.18 In a specific query regarding the selection of GMCs for upgradation, the Committee has been
informed that  the Ministry, while finalizing the upgradation projects has given higher priority to the
States generally considered “backward” with respect to the broad socio-economic indicators. However,
the State has to also bear the State share and also operate and maintain the facilities created;
therefore, the willingness of the State to contribute its share and participate in the scheme is also
important apart from backwardness. As per the Expenditure Finance Committee (EFC) notes available
with the Ministry, all projects have been selected on the basis of demands raised by States and
therefore the States were fully involved in the selection process.
8.19 The Committee takes note that the recent CAG Report No. 10 of 2018 shows a different
picture altogether that the Ministry had not formulated any criteria or procedure for selection of
GMCs for upgradation as brought out below:–
I. In Bihar, two GMCs were selected for upgradation under PMSSY Phase-III by the
Ministry without consulting the Government of Bihar;
II. Three GMCs in Madhya Pradesh were approved for upgradation in Phase-III without
obtaining any preliminary project report from the State Government;
III. In Maharashtra, selection of six GMCs was done based on proposals submitted by their
respective Deans and not on the basis of any State level study;
IV . In Rajasthan, no record was available with the State Authorities of any request/proposal
for upgradation of GMCs under PMSSY; and
V . Ministry selected B.J. Medical College, Ahmedabad and Pandit Deendayal Upadhyay
Government Medical College, Rajkot for upgradation under the scheme in first and third
phase respectively without any proposal from the Government of Gujarat.
8.20 Attention of the Committee is drawn to the CAG report which further highlighted that there
is arbitrary selection and distribution of work amongst consultancy agencies. The Project Monitoring
Committee (PMC) in May, 2006 decided that for upgradation of GMCs, there was no need to
appoint separate Project Consultant for each Institute for upgradation of GMCs. It was instead
decided to engage CPWD, M/s HSCC or any other PSU already engaged in the Health sector as
consultant to assist the Ministry in effective implementation of the upgradation projects. Accordingly,
upgradation projects were allotted to the CPWD, M/s HSCC and M/s HLL/(M/s HITES) Ltd.
8.21 The CAG report further mentions that the distribution of GMCs among the three identified
agencies was done on nomination basis without any identifiable criteria for allocation of work.

115
Phase-I projects were allotted to CPWD and all GMCs in the Southern region were allotted to
M/s HLL without any detailed assessment of their capacities for undertaking works assigned to
them. In Phase-II, four out of six GMCs were allotted to M/s HLL only on the basis of direction
of the Minister in Charge and remaining two GMCs were allotted to M/s HSCC where no reasons
were available on record for allotting the work of Dr. Rajendra Prasad Government Medical College
(RPGMC), Tanda to the company. In the case of Phase-III projects, allocations were made on the
recommendations of a Technical Committee which met and made recommendations for allocation
on the basis of past performance on the same day that it was formed i.e. on 14 February, 2014.
However, the assessment of the past performance of the three agencies was not on record. The
works entrusted to these agencies in Phase-I and II suffered delays ranging from three months to
seven years, none of the works of Phase-III had been completed. The Ministry, in February, 2018
stated that there were no delays in works executed through M/s HSCC and M/s HLL. It added that
there was no need to evaluate performances for each phase as the PSUs were always under the
scrutiny in the Ministry. The reply of the Ministry is not acceptable as there is no record of any
assessment of the capabilities of the agencies before allocation of the work. Further, the fact that
all the GMCs entrusted to these agencies had faced significant delays in implementation undermines
Ministry’s claim of proper assessment of the capabilities of the agencies while allocating the work.
8.22 The CAG report highlighted that the Ministry had given an undertaking to the Planning
Commission at the scheme appraisal stage that it would sign a MoU with State Governments for
the running of the upgraded GMCIs and for ensuring that funds were being utilized. A commitment
was to be obtained from the State Governments for providing staff as per Medical Council of India
requirements and for taking up regular maintenance of the upgraded facilities. However, the Ministry
did not enter into any MoU with the State Governments concerned in respect of the GMCIs
upgraded during Phase-I and Phase-II. In the absence of such a MoU, in several cases, State
Governments did not provide required manpower for operation and maintenance of the upgraded
facilities.
8.23 The Committee feels that the Ministry has not devised scientific criteria/parameters
for the selection of GMCs under PMSSY. The Committee feels that the criteria of socio-
economic indicators, mainly the presence of already existing tertiary healthcare facilities,
should also be taken into account. The Committee also take into cognizance that  many
GMCs have been selected without consulting the respective States and even without considering
their financial condition and even without entering into any MoU with the State Governments.
Further, the selection of the consultancy agencies was purely arbitrary and no past record
of the agencies has been taken into consideration.  To consider HLL/HSCC as a panacea for
all execution is a area of serious concern as these agencies are being given preferential
treatment just because they are PSUs of the Ministry not because of their proven track
record. The Committee, thus, recommends to the Ministry to formulate set parameters for
selection of GMCs to ensure that the GMCs which actually require upgradation are selected
in consultation with State Governments. The Committee, further, recommends that the
Ministry, while appointing consultancy agencies, should also take into consideration past
reputation and good track record of the agencies in order to avoid time and cost overruns.
Accordingly, the Committee recommends for involvement of better professional agencies or
other reputed construction majors to ensure cost effective and timely execution especially

116
in projects where HSCC has failed to ensure timely completion of the construction projects.
The Committee also takes a serious note on part of the PSA in undertaking a proper gap
analysis in GMCs where requisite medical equipment were not provided on a timely basis.
Site Selection and Status of Construction Activities in GMCs
8.24 The ap proval for Phases-I, II and III of work relating to upgradation of GMCs was given
in June 2006, February 2009 and November 2013 respectively with stipulated period of completion
being three years from the date of approval for Phases-I and II and 43 months for Phase-III. Work
for Phase-I sites selected could, however, be awarded only during the period from January 2007
to April 2011. Under Phase-II and Phase-III the works were awarded during the period from
January 2011 to June 2016 and May 2016 to December 2016 respectively. As such, there were
considerable delays in planning and commencement of works in all three phases.
8.25 W ith respect to the status of the construction work, the Committee has been informed that
the upgradation has been completed in 12 out of 13 GMCs approved for upgradation in the Phases-I
and three out of six GMCs under Phase-II of PMSSY . Only one college out of 39 colleges
approved under Phase-III is completed yet and construction activities at other Institutes are still in
progress. Further, construction has been started in some GMCs under Phase-IV and V and tender
has been floated in case of other GMCs. The upgradation of 15 GMCs under Phase-IV and V A has
been approved in August, 2016 and scheduled to be completed in March, 2020.
8.26 It has also been submitted that for the Phase-III and later phases of upgradation projects,
Ministry in consultation with the State Governments has focused on augmentation of the post
graduation (PG) seats in the process of creation of the super-specialty infrastructure. Under the
gap analysis process, agreement of the State Government has also been taken for creation of the
PG seats as would become feasible with the operationalization of the new facilities. Upgradation of
GMCs undertaken in Phase-III and Phases-IV and V A are yet not complete. Status of upgradation
of GMCs under all the phases of PMSSY has been given in Annexure-D .
8.27 The CAG in its report highlighted that  as per MoU between State Governments and the
Ministry for upgradation of GMCs during Phase-III of the scheme, the State Governments had to
ensure availability of encumbrance free land for construction of the Super Specialty Blocks (SSB)
of the GMC within 30 days of approval of the DPR. However, in five instances, the State
Governments concerned failed to provide a clear site in time leading to delay in the upgradation
of these GMCs. In some GMCs, work was awarded at higher cost. In Nizam’s Institute of Medical
Sciences (NIMS), Hyderabad, tender for construction of Super specialty Block and the Accident
Trauma Hospital were awarded at a cost of ` 125.91 crore which was 22.37 per cent above the
approved cost and 12.9 per cent above the justified market rate.
8.28 The Committee during the course of its study visit observes that construction activities
at various Institutes are delayed. The reasons for delays are manifold including (i) non-
availability of encumbrance free land (ii) delays in getting clearances for excavation
(iii) tree cutting and other site related conditions (iv) changes made in the scope and
quantum of work (v) post contract changes in drawings and quantities (vi) delays in providing
drawings (vii) delay in release of mobilization advance and payments to contractor (viii)
delay in provision of services (ix) delay in work due to shortage of labour (x) delay in

117
finalisation/tendering of medical equipment (xi) changes in site after award of work delay
in shifting of existing electrical lines (xii) internal disturbances in some States etc. The
Committee urges the Ministry to flag these issues with the State Government concerned to
undertake appropriate steps to remove the bottlenecks causing delay in the completion of
construction activities at various GMCs.
8.29 The Committee, further, observes that the work of 12 GMCs out of 13 approved under
Phase-I, scheduled to be completed in March, 2013, have been completed with delays ranging
from eight months to seven years. In other six GMCs approved under Phase-II, the upgradation
of only three GMCs has been completed though with the delay of several years. In the
remaining three colleges, the work has not been completed even after many years. The
revised date of completion has been extended to 31st December, 2018 for GMCs which
initially that were scheduled to be completed in July, 2011.
8.30 The Committee takes serious note of issue of awarding of tender at the cost higher
than the approved cost indicating financial irregularities. The Committee believes that the
whole process should be objective and on merit rather than on any other consideration so
that the agency with the most economical and best offer of services is awarded the tender
for completion of the projects.
8.31 The Committee takes into account that only one GMC out of 39 GMCs of Phase-III i.e.
GMC, Kota has been completed so far. 18 GMCs that were scheduled to be completed by the year
2017 have not been completed yet and the revised date of completion has been set. Further,
13 GMCs in phase-III that were scheduled to be completed till 2018, have not been completed yet.
Out of these 13 GMCs, work of upgradation has not crossed even 70 per cent in 8 GMCs. The
work for these GMCs had commenced only during the period from May, 2016 to December, 2016.
8.32 The Committee believes that the Ministry cannot in any way justify the inordinate
delays in the completion of the projects. Procedural and preparatory exercises need to be
done beforehand including availability of encumbrance free site by the respective State
Government. The Committee also recommends to the Ministry to frame operational guidelines
that would guide and regulate implementation of the scheme across the States and take
steps to expedite the completion of remaining work by better monitoring of the projects.
The Committee apprehends that such delays will increase the work load at the fag end of
completion schedule. The Committee, therefore, is of the considered view that priority
should be given to such projects for which there is imminent need to expedite the process
of granting necessary approvals only to avoid time and cost overruns are avoided.  The
Committee also strongly recommends that no new project be taken before completion of
these 73 projects.
8.33 The Committee understands that there may be certain practical difficulties that are
encountered on work sites during the course of upgradation project. However the, considerable
delays in planning and commencement of works in all phases of upgradation of GMCs under
PMSSY and the slow progress, both at the stage of planning and contracting indicates that
both planning of works and contract management were inadequate and improper, thus causing
adverse impact in the delivery of services. The Committee is of the firm view that the

118
Ministry/Central Government, instead of taking the responsibility, has shifted the blame on
the State Governments for the delay in the commencement of upgradation of GMCs under
PMSSY. The Committee feels that instead of blame game, the Ministry should work in
tandem with the State Governments and help in ironing out problem areas so that the
objectives of the scheme is not defeated by delays and faulty planning. The Committee,
therefore, recommends to the Ministry to review the status of construction timelines and
set realistically achievable targets in consultation with the State Governments concerned.
Procurement of Equipment
8.34 The Committee has been given to understand that as per the procurement procedure policy
for medical equipment under Phase I, II and III of PMSSY , procurement of medical equipment
would be handled by designated Procurement Support Agency (PSA) i.e., HITES which is a 100%
subsidiary of HLL Life care Ltd. for Phase I, II and III of PMSSY . It was the role and responsibility
of Procurement Support Agent to:–
(i) ensure procurement of state of art, genuine, reliable medical equipment with emphasis
on ensuring protection of public interest through savings quality etc.
(ii) strict compliance of GFR/CVC guidelines.
(iii) ensure multi-vendor participation and procurement of Made in India medical equipment
wherever applicable.
(iv) provide all possible support to Ministry as and when required.
(v) link up process of procurement with construction sites.
8.35 The Committee was further informed that, although HLL has been appointed as PSA for
procurement of medical equipment for Phase-I and II, yet there have been considerable delays in
procurement mainly due to several approvals needed from the Ministry. That necessitated revision
of existing procedure and policy to simplify the process and accordingly the policy and procedure
for procurement of medical equipment was revised as enshrined below-
(i) PSA is entrusted with responsibility to prepare preliminary List of Requirement (LoR)
and draft technical specification by conducting survey and gap viability analysis and
preparing LoR for each Institution. This is done by the PSA through a Technical
Specification Committee (TSC), members of which are to drawn from AIIMS and other
Institutes of National Importance (INI). The Ministry will, then obtain the list of the
specialty-wise panel of experts from AIIMS, INIs and will make this list available to the
PSA. PSA may choose required number of experts, minimum two, from the panel given
by the Ministry for each specialty and must ensure that multi vendor and genuine
manufacturer participation is achieved in a transparent manner. Approval of specification
should be made within 45 days from the date of receipt of approved LoR and technical
Specifications from all medical colleges.
(ii) PSA is, then, responsible for getting approval of pre-bid amendments in technical specification
from  TSC  within  15 days from  the  date  of  pre-bid meeting and also for adherence
to all extant Government of India guidelines, rules instructions etc. PSA should then

119
examine and suitably dispose the representations/complaints received with this regard
within 7 days from the date of receipt of representation/complaint. After approval of
pre-bid amendments, the Technical, Commercial and Financial bid evaluation will be
done by PSA in accordance with the provisions of GFR, DFPR¸CVC and any other
financial/commercial notifications. The evaluation report will be submitted to the Ministry
for concurrence of IFD before submission to IPC. PSA should then make placement in
IPC in consultation with the Ministry within two weeks from the date of receipt of IFD
concurrence from Ministry.
(iii) Based on the decision taken in by the IPC, the Ministry shall communicate approval of
the competent authority to the PSA with concurrence of IFD for issue of Notification
of Award (NOA) and will release the necessary funds to PSA with concurrence of IFD.
PSA then, within 7 days from the date of receipt of approval, should place orders in
terms of the approval communicated. At the end, PSA will procure Consignee Receipt
Certificate (CRC) and Final Acceptance Certificate and submit to the Ministry. Time
taken from techno-commercial evaluation to placement of order should be completed
within two months.
8.36 The status of procurement of medical equipment under the various up-gradation projects is
given below:–

8.37 On being asked about the policy and procedure for procurement of medical equipment, the
Committee was informed that Procurement Support Agent (PSA) ensures that the tender enquiry
document issued is in with accordance standard conditions viz. industry friendly/generic specifications
meeting user requirement, wide publicity, pre bid meeting, sufficient time for formulation of tenders
etc. are fulfilled. Mostly in case of first time tendering, single bid situation (where there are large
number of manufacturer available) the tender is re-issued/re-floated after incorporating necessary
modifications so to encourage more participation. However, if after scrutiny, it is found that all
such aspects were fully taken care of and in spite of that if they end up with single responsive

120
bidder only, the item is processed further, subject to the fact that the price quoted by the single
responsive bidder was found to be reasonable after establishing price reasonability with respect to
reference orders, estimated price and prevailing market rates.
8.38 The CAG report (2018) highlighted many discrepancies in the procurement of equipment. As
per the procurement guidelines and provisions of the MoU signed between GMCs and the Ministry,
GMCs had to ensure that no alterations and additions were made to the list of equipment and
specifications firmed up by the medical experts. In RIMS-Ranchi, JNMC-Aligarh, BMCRI-Bangalore,
IMS-Varanasi, GMC-Mumbai and GMC-Nagpur, 293 medical equipment costing ` 19.86 crore were
procured for installation in the upgraded facilities and these equipment did not fall in the approved
list of medical equipment of the Ministry. Further, as per the instructions, the equipment cost was
to include the cost of warranty and the Institutions were to enter into Comprehensive Maintenance
Contracts (CMC) at the cost of the State Government before expiry of warranty period. It was,
however, found that that the warranty period of 147 medical equipment costing ` 27.90 crore in
GMKMC-Salem, IMS-Varanasi and GMC-Amritsar had expired three months to three years ago.
However, the concerned GMCs had not entered into Comprehensive Maintenance Contract CMCs
for the equipment.
8.39 The CAG report (2018) further highlighted that due to improper gap analysis for equipment
costing ` 12.86 crore were procured/being procured from State  funds had also been included in
the list of equipment for procurement under PMSSY . Thus, there was duplication in the plan for
procurement of equipment. At the same time, requirement for various equipment of five departments
of GMC-Kota were not included in the gap analysis report. At BJMC-Ahmedabad, the Ministry
approved (February, 2009) procurement of 294 equipment at an estimated cost of ` 37.69 crore
for BJMC-Ahmedabad. Subsequently, in August, 2013, BJMC-Ahmedabad was asked to review its
equipment list as it contained items not directly connected with tertiary healthcare viz. CCTV, lift,
ramps, laundry machine, etc. The Institute submitted a revised list of equipment to the Ministry
(October, 2013) including three new equipment costing ` 9.58 crore. The three new equipment
were not accepted by the Ministry (January, 2014) on the ground that those items had not been
included in the gap analysis. Thus, the Institute could not obtain funds for equipment required by
it due to improper gap analysis.
8.40 In a specific query about instances where in the process of tendering, multiple bids were
received but due to disqualification of other bidders, tenders were left with a single eligible bid,
the Ministry submitted details of GMCs with single responsive bid. The Ministry submitted that in
several cases even through multiple bids were received for procurement of equipment, only single
bid was found techno commercially responsive and the tenders were processed as per Ministry’s
IFD guidelines.
8.41 The Committee notes with concern the observations of CAG regarding improper gap
analysis, lacunae in CMC provisions and procurement of equipment which did not fall in the
approved list of medical equipment of the Ministry. The CAG Report further highlights that
against the procurement orders for 818 equipment costing ````` 482.21 crore for 11 GMCs, 191
equipment costing ````` 51.72 crore were yet to be made available. The Committee is concerned
to note that there are several tenders which are pending since very long and are being
retendered again and again. The Committee notes that the basic purpose of having a PSA

121
stands defeated if such delays are going to occur. The Committee, in this connection,
strongly recommends for a properly monitored mechanism, so as to ensure that time and
cost overruns in the procurement process are avoided. The Committee is surprised to note
that a total of 141 tenders for the procurement of equipment are pending under Phase-III
of the upgradation project under PMSSY. The Committee observes that these 141 tenders
have been retendered by the Ministry in the year 2018. Out of these, 4 tenders for surgical
loupes, extra corporeal membrane oxygenator, head saver and surgical xenon head light and
light sources have again been floated. Also, 24 types of equipment under Phase-I and II and
7 types of equipment under Phase-III have been procured on a single bid basis. The Committee
desires the PSA to submit a status note in all the procurement carried out on single bid
basis in Phase-I and Phase-II including reasons for the same, within three months of
presentation of this report.
8.42 The Committee observes that there is an acute shortage of equipment at the various
GMCs approved for upgradation under PMSSY. The primary reasons being delays in placement
of orders, change in the model of the equipment and lack of follow up by Institutes with
the nominated PSA after placement of orders. The Committee apprehends that in the
absence of the equipment, the scheme gets affected and patients are deprived of upgraded
services. The CAG has further highlighted a grave concern i.e. 75 equipment have been
supplied by HLL at a cost of ````` 3.26 crore but are not anywhere in record either with HLL
or the concerned GMCs.  The Committee takes a serious note of this lapse and recommends
for suitable remedial action to address this criminal wastage of public fund. The Committee
would also like to be apprised of the action taken by the Ministry for fixing accountability
for this lapse.
8.43 The Committee is, therefore, of the considered view that procurement of equipment on
single bid basis reduces the competition in the market and minimizes the opportunity of
competitive bidding. The Committee would like the Ministry to adhere to the procedure of
the multiple bids in order to achieve competitive pricing.
8.44 The Committee observes that the revised policy and procedure formulated for procurement
of medical equipment has not been followed in letter and spirit. The Committee strongly
recommends that there should be minimum pendency and in the procurement process and
all pending procurement tenders be closed without any further delay. The Ministry should
also formulate effective monitoring mechanism for timely receipt, installation and proper
functioning of equipment at GMCs so that the equipment are utilized properly for the
purpose for which they were procured. A quarterly update on gap analysis, delivery of
equipment, delays if any, with respect to all GMCs should be submitted to the Committee
by the PSA.
Enhancement of PG Seats
8.45 Pertaining to the status of Post Graduate Seats and Graduate courses in GMC’s under
PMSSY , the Committee has been informed that since the GMC upgradation projects essentially
involve augmentation of the tertiary healthcare infrastructure in already existing GMCs, enhancement
in UG seats has not been envisaged. In the case of the upgradation projects under Phase-I and II,
there had been no specific agreement with the State Governments regarding augmenting of the PG

122
seats. However, in the case of Phase-III and later phase upgradation projects, the attempt has been
made to also seek agreement of the State Governments in the matter of augmentation of the PG
seats in addition to adding of super-specialty disciplines. The States would need to take up with
the authorities concerned for creation of the additional PG seats. As per the jointly agreed gap
analysis and DPRs, number of PG seats in 39 GMCs under Phase-III projects would be 666 PG
seats and in 14 GMCs under Phase-IV and V projects 355 PG seats would be envisaged, has to
be created.
8.46 The Committee notes that as per the joint gap analysis and DPR, the number of PG
seats would be 666 in 39 GMCs under Phase-III Projects and 355 PG seats in 14 GMCs
under Phase-IV and V Projects. The Committee, however, is of the view that the enhancement
of PG seats without having adequate faculty/staff would not be result-yielding. The Committee,
therefore, recommends to the Ministry to focus on the employment of the faculty along
with enhancement of the PG seats to the desired level of faculty-student ratio. Hence, the
Ministry/State Governments must strive to fill up the vacant seats within a stipulated time
period and basic other infrastructure for teaching must be in place for facilitating the
imparting of medical education to extended number of students.
8.47 The Committee, further, recommends to the Ministry that the PG seats in GMCs
should be increased in view of the existing infrastructure and adequate faculty. When
compared to new AIIMS like Institutions, GMCs are better equipped to provide medical
education and clinical exposure to the PG students. GMCs are in a better position to address
the regional imbalances in States and enhancing their PG seats could increase the outreach
of tertiary health care in the remote areas of the country. The Committee strongly recommends
for a substantial enhancement in the PG seats in all the GMCs so as to address this critical
gap in medical education. The Committee recommends that the Ministry should prepare a
comprehensive action plan for augmenting PG seats in GMCs which are being upgraded
under PMSSY.
Super Specialty Blocks
8.48 The upgradation projects have involved augmentation of tertiary healthcare physical infrastructure
of the respective GMC by constructing Super Specialty Blocks (SSBs)/Trauma Centers along with
required Medical equipment.
8.49 The CAG report (2018) highlighted that according to guidelines for gap analysis issued by the
Ministry in September, 2014 in respect of Phase-III, gaps in medical departments were to be
analyzed by the respective State Governments and a gap analysis report was to be submitted to
the Ministry. In CAG report, the shortcomings in the gap analysis of GMC, Kota were reported.
Medical Council of India (MCI) had suggested that super speciality services may be set up in a
manner that the maximum number of super speciality services is provided in a particular State. A
Department of Neurology already existed in the Maharav Bheem Singh Hospital attached to GMC-
Kota which fulfilled the norms of MCI for education in the super speciality course in neurology.
However, a Department of Neurology was included in the newly created Super Speciality Block
(SSB). Hence, the creation of a facility that was already available in the newly proposed SSB
lacked justification and the resources could have been deployed for setting up some other
Depart ment.

123
8.50 The attention of the Committee was also drawn on the status of the CATH lab in the
upgrading GMCs. The Committee observes that 17 GMCs out of 39 approved under Phase-III do
not have a CA TH lab facility.
8.51 The Committee is distressed to observe that out of the GMCs (58) approved under first
three phases of the upgradation project, nine GMCs do not have Super Specialty Services.
These are Institute of Medical Sciences, BHU, Varanasi (Ph.I), Jawaharlal Nehru Medical
College, AMU, Aligarh (Ph.II), Srinagar Medical College (Ph.I), B.J. Medical College, Ahmadabad
(Ph.I), Grant Medical College, Mumbai (Ph.I), GMC, Nagpur (Ph.II), Govt. Medical College,
Malda (PH III), Vijayanagar Institute of Medical Sciences, Bellary (PH III), Pt. B.D. Sharma
PGIMS, Rohtak (Ph.II). The Committee, further, notes that none of the GMCs approved
under Phase-I, II and III have all the super specialty facilities as originally envisaged under
the upgradation project. The Committee apprehends that unless the upgradation projects
under the Phase-III is not completed, the Ministry would not be in a position to focus on
GMCs in the later phases. The Committee is of the considered view that the scheme would
be successful only when Super Specialty Blocks (SSB) envisaged under the scheme are
functional in all the GMCs approved for upgradation. The Committee, therefore, recommends
the Ministry to focus on the non-functional SSBs of GMCs for capacity building to provide
super-specialty services to suffering patients. The Committee also strongly recommends for
a gap analysis in the availability of super-speciality services in GMCs in locations where
there are functional regional AIIMS or new ones are proposed.
8.52 The Committee is, therefore, of the firm view that in the modern age where heart
disease have became so common, super specialty services without a CATH lab would be
short of special facility for delivery of adequate service to the suffering heart patient.  The
Committee, therefore, recommends that all the SSB envisaged should include functional
CATH lab under the project should be made functional without much procrastination.
Generation of Employment
8.53 As regards the generation of employment under the upgradation project, it is informed that
the responsibility of running of the physical facilities created under the GMC upgradation projects
rests with the State Government which has to make all provisions, including complete HR (Specialist/
Super-specialists and paramedics etc) A total of 19 GMC upgradation projects were sanctioned in
Phase-I and II of the PMSSY . Out of these, 15 projects have been completed. The Phase-III and
later phase projects are in progress. The HR in these Institutes would be deployed by the States
once the facilities are ready. However, commitment of the States in the matter has been elicited
from the State Governments through MoUs in which there is clear stipulation that the State
Government would ensure provisioning of required manpower for running of the facilities.
8.54 The CAG report (2018) highlighted shortages in manpower deployed in the GMCs which
adversely impacted service delivery resulting in non-achievement of objectives of the scheme. At
BJMC-Ahmedabad State Government had sanctioned 62 medical and 329 Para-medical staff in
April, 2007 but the institution had failed to fill up 60 per cent staff 43 medical and 193 Para-
medical staff. Further 22 medical officers, out of 25 sanctioned posts of medical officers were
vacant in Trauma and Emergency Medicine Departments.

124
8.55 The Committee observes with concern that no action has been taken by the Ministry
and the State Governments concerned to generate employment at the GMCs approved for
upgradation under PMSSY. The Committee is of the considered view that health care sector
has tremendous potential for employment generation. The Committee, therefore, strongly
recommends that the Central and State Government must to chalk out suitable strategy to
explore various avenues for employment generation in different segments of healthcare
sector. The Committee, in this regard, believes that higher investment in the health sector
would yield greater opportunities for employment generation. The Committee is, however,
perturbed to observe that many Departments at the various GMCs are not functional due
to the shortage of the manpower. The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends to the
Ministry to analyze the manpower requirement of each GMC and advise the State Governments
to create and fill up adequate number of posts, accordingly. The Committee also recommends
that vacant posts must be filled up as early as possible so as to pave the way for smooth
delivery of health services to the people.
Monitoring Mechanism
8.56 As regards the monitoring mechanism for the implementation of up-gradation scheme under
PMSSY , the Committee was informed that the Ministry has developed an online dashboard to
facilitate a closer and more effective monitoring. The Executive Agencies update the dashboard on
monthly basis. The executing agencies have also been mandated to deploy and use Project Management
Software to bring about improved project management at their end. Fortnightly progress review
meetings with the Executing Agencies are being held at the level of Joint Secretary. The issues and
hindrances at the field requiring remedial actions on the part of the Government Medical College/
State Government are taken up with the concerned GMCs/State Government at senior levels through
letters and meetings. Video conference/ PMC meeting with the State Government are also held to
highlight and resolve issues/hindrances. Provision for setting up of a State level Steering Committee
under the Chairmanship of Chief Secretary has also been made in the MoU signed with the State
Governments in regard to the Phase-III and IV works. The State level steering Committees have
been set up/ being set up in most of the States. Project Monitoring Group (PMG) meeting under
the chairmanship of Chief Secretary of respective States are being held to review progress and
resolve local issues. The Ministry ensures its representation in the PMG meetings and any issues/
hindrances with the State are flagged for discussion and dissolutions.
8.57 The CAG report highlighted that the State Level PMC under the chairmanship of Principal
Secretary (Health/Medical Education) was required to meet on regular basis viz. at least once in
a month, to review the progress of upgradation of GMCs and share its views with the Ministry.
It was, however, noted that State PMCs were not constituted in eight GMCs. Further, as per the
MoU signed between the Ministry and the State  Governments for upgradation of GMCs during
Phase-III, a State level Steering Committee under the chairmanship of Chief Secretary was to be
constituted for expediting project completion. A representative of PMSSY Division of the Ministry
is a member of the Committee that is required to meet each quarter. The Ministry, however, had
no record on the status of constitution of these Committees by the State Governments. Audit noted
that these Committees were either not constituted or if constituted, were not active. The Ministry
had requested the State Governments (November 2007) to put in place a system of Third Party

125
Quality Assurance (TPQA) but that was not done. Audit noted that TPQA was not undertaken in
15 GMCs.
8.58 The Committee is dissatisfied with the response of the Ministry regarding the existing
institutional arrangement for monitoring the implementation of upgradation Scheme of
Government Medical Colleges under PMSSY. The existing monitoring mechanism to review
the progress through an online dashboard and Project Management Software by the executive
agencies is not effective enough to ensure timely execution of upgradation projects.  In this
regard, the Committee also notes with concern the observation of CAG made in its report
that in eight GMCs mandatory State PMCs under the Chairmanship of Principal Secretary
(Health/Medical Education), to review the progress of upgradation scheme have not been
constituted.  Similarly, in many States, a State level Steering Committee, under the Chairmanship
of Chief Secretary, required to meet each quarter to review progress and resolve local
issues were  either not constituted or if constituted were not active. Utmost to the surprise
of the Committee, the Ministry has no record about the working of these Committees.
Moreover, the Ministry’s direction to have a system of Third Party Quality Assurance
(TPQA) have also not been complied with in 15 GMCs.  The Committee during its visit to
West Bengal and Bihar found the absence of these Committees in various GMCs, therefore,
the Committee concludes that ‘all is not well’ with the monitoring system over the execution
of programmes for upgradation schemes of GMCs under the PMSSY.  The Committee,
therefore, strongly recommends that mandatory provision of constitution of PMCs, State
Level Steering Committees must be complied with and the system of Third Party Quality
Assurance (TPQA) be placed in order to make resilient monitoring mechanism for efficient
and effective execution of upgradation projects in all GMCs.
Study Visit of the Committee
8.59 A Sub-Committee of the Committee on Health and Family Welfare visited Guwahati Medical
College on 21st January, 2018 to review its progress made and the status of ongoing projects for
establishment of Super Specialty Hospital. The Committee found that free medicines were not
available at the emergency and the trauma care center of the institute, the AC in the Emergency
Wing was not functional, the CR Machine in the OT of the Emergency was malfunctioning. The
Committee, further, noticed that the Guwahati Medical College was in pathetic condition which
reflected poor health scenario and health facilities of Assam. Despite so many years of construction
of building, the same was not complete, implying that nothing had been accomplished except total
loss to the Government exchequer. The Committee also found building flaws especially, in designing
of the plinth area of the super specialty new building. Further, Assam has sound medical education
facilities still doctors there do not serve in the State  and prefer to serve elsewhere, particularly
indicating shortage in Specialty Department.
8.60 The Committee, therefore, is of the view that actual condition of the upgradation
project in GMCs is totally different from what is reflected on paper. The Committee feels
that even after the utilization of the allocated funds there is no perceptible change at the
ground level which clearly means the Government funds have not been properly utilised
under the project. The Committee, therefore, recommends to the Ministry to ensure adherence
to contract provisions in execution of work and provision of services. The Committee feels

126
that accountability needs to be fixed for expenditure without adequate justification or where
Government funds have not been utilized properly or when there is misappropriation of
funds. Moreover, the Committee would like to reiterate that professional agencies with a
proven track record must be involved in the construction process so as to avoid building
flaws leading to wasteful expenditure.
8.61 The Committee visited Patna from 28th-30th November, 2018 for examining the upgradation
of Government Medical Colleges (GMCs) under PMSSY . In this connection, the Committee visited
Patna Medical College Hospital (PMCH) and also held discussion with the Chief Secretary of the
State and the Principals of Government Medical Colleges, Gaya, Darbhanga, Muzaffarpur and
Bhagalpur.  The main objective of the Committee’s study visit was to assess the ground realities
and key concern areas in the upgradation of GMCs under PMSSY scheme including reasons for
delay in execution of projects and the areas where assistance from the Central Ministry of Health
and Family Welfare was required.
8.62 The Committee noted that more than two years have passed since the inclusion of
PMCH in the upgradation program under Phase IV of PMSSY but the foundation stone of
the Super Speciality Department is yet to see the light of day. PMCH submitted that the
delay in transfer of land measuring merely 500 meter has stalled the construction of the
Super Speciality Block that cast aspersion on the administrative will and skill of PMCH
management. The Committee, however, welcomes the Institute’s proposal to increase the
number of seats in MBBS and also advocated the increase in the number of seats in PG
course. The Committee also endorsed PMCH’s suggestion for inclusion of important Departments
such as Cardiology, Onco-Haemetology in the Super Speciality Block.
8.63 In the oral deposition before the Committee, the representatives from the Bihar State Government
assured the Committee that the State will transfer its share of funds and fortnightly monitor the
upgradation program. The land will be transferred to PMCH for establishing the Super Speciality
Department. The State Government also submitted that a system for quality assurance for the
GMCs has not been developed in the State and the State has not evaluated the performance of
GMCS under the upgradation program. The Committee was also apprised that the State Level
Steering Committee under the chairmanship of Chief Secretary has been constituted for monitoring
the upgradation of GMCs during Phase III but a formal meeting is yet to be held. Attention of the
Committee was also drawn to the shortage of super specialty doctors in the GMCs. Non availability
of suitable faculty has been a major bottleneck in the delivery of healthcare services.
8.64 The Committee is appalled at the lax attitude of the State in monitoring the progress
made under PMSSY and recommends that the State should conduct regular review meetings
with the GMCs to address the various hurdles in its smooth functioning. The Committee
strongly recommends that the State should transfer its share of funds well in time for the
upgradation of all the Government Medical Colleges. The Committee also strongly recommends
the State to develop a Third Party Quality Assurance Audit for all the GMCs.
8.65 The Committee also notes that wide regional imbalance exists in the delivery of Health
Care Services in the State. The remote regions of the State are still out of the reach of
tertiary health care services as most of such facilities are established in and around Patna.
There is a severe lack of adequate medical care and facilities in Government Sector in

127
North Bihar, especially in the districts of Saharsha, Madhepura, Purnea, Katihar, Kishanganj
and Farbishganj, i.e., Koshi region.  The Committee desires that the Ministry may explore
the possibility for setting up of AIIMS like institutions in this extremely poor and backward
region to cater to the health facilities and services to the poor at the economical/lower cost.
The Committee, therefore, strongly urges upon the Central and the State Governments to
take into account the need and requirement of health care services in these economically
deprived regions and work towards decentralization of the health care facilities.
8.66 The Committee notes that the shortage of specialists is a major problem in all the
AIIMS like Institutions and Government Medical Colleges. The Committee again emphasizes
the need to develop special packages and incentives for attracting the specialists to serve
in this part of the region.
Conclusion
8.67 The Committee observes that upgradation project of GMCs under PMSSY has failed to
provide the desired results which were expected from the project. The Committee opines
that there is a big gap between planning and the execution of the project due to lack of
synchronization and coordination of activities and serious gaps between planning and
implementation of the scheme. The Committee feels that the inadequate project management
and related delays impacted the delivery of envisaged benefits in terms of improvement in
health infrastructure. The reasons for delay in upgradation project are mainly due to non-
availability of encumbrance free land, delays in getting clearances for excavation and tree
cutting and other site related conditions, changes made in the scope and quantum of work,
delay in finalisation/tendering of medical equipment. Apart from all these, poor planning
and coordination contributed to delays in upgradation of GMCs . The Committee appreciates
the potential in the scheme to transform the Government Medical Colleges in providing
quality health services to the people. The Committee is therefore of the firm view that
projects require meticulous planning and consistent efforts for timely execution/completion.
The Committee believes that preparatory activities should be completed beforehand which
should include realistic assessment of requirement of equipment, availability of site, analysis
of manpower and utilization of funds for the purpose it is earmarked. The Committee
recommends to the Ministry to work in tandem with the State Governments to work in a
coordinated manner to accomplish the objective and mission of upgradation of GMCs as
envisaged in PMSSY.

CHAPTER-IX
OBSERV ATIONS/RECOMMENDATIONS ON COMMON ISSUES
9.1 The Committee visited AIIMS Jodhpur, Rishikesh, Bhopal and Patna as part of its study to
assess for itself the progress of these important institutions which are going to be a milestone in
India’s journey towards providing tertiary health care. The idea behind the Committee’s visit to
these institutes was to get an actual and fair assessment of the ground reality which often becomes
opaque in the powerpoint based approach of the Ministry/Institute. The Committee also made an
assessment of the progress made in the upgradation programme of Government Medical Colleges
in Rajasthan, Madhya Pradesh, Bihar and West Bengal.  On the basis of these study visits and the
interaction with various stakeholders, certain common issues were shortlisted by the Committee for
specific reference and action thereon. These issues have been listed as Observations/Recommendations
on common issues. The Committee desires that adequate focus of the Ministry be drawn on these
Observations/Recommendations so that timely corrective action is taken and the same mistakes are
not repeated.
Financial Assessment
9.2 Attention of the Committee has been drawn to the fact that the capital cost of each new
AIIMS under Phase-I of PMSSY was estimated to be ` 284.50 crore ( ` 1,707 crore for all 6
AIIMS) by the Ministry in 2004. In 2006, a revised capital cost of ` 332 crore was approved for
each new AIIMS. However in March 2010, the Ministry obtained a revised approval of ` 820 crore
for each of the new AIIMS.
9.3 The Committee is taken aback at this skewed financial assessment of the Government’s
flagship health care service programme. This fluctuating assessment speaks volume of the
Ministry’s lack in planning and failure to formulate any operational guidelines for AIIMS
like Institutions. The Committee also notes that the approval of the revised cost was
obtained in March, 2010 i.e. after four years of the initial approval in 2006.  This represents
a 188% and 145% increase in capital costs since 2004 and 2006 respectively. The Committee,
therefore, recommends that the Ministry must undertake proper and practical financial
assessment with respect to the capital cost of new AIIMS like institutions in the subsequent
phases.
Site Selection of AIIMS like Institutions
9.4 The Committee is of the view that site selection for any AIIMS like Institution requires
a detailed assessment of the geographical location and the demographic distribution. Easy
accessibility and transportation availability is one major concern. The Committee is also
concerned at the duplication and concentration of tertiary care services at a particular place.
The Committee notes that at several places like Guwahati, Patna and now in Madurai
(where a new AIIMS has just been announced) the Government had already selected GMCs
for upgradation programme under PMSSY. In the places that have been selected for setting
up of AIIMS like Institutions, the critical gap in the tertiary health care is already being
filled by private sector.  The Committee strongly believes that AIIMS should be located in
an area with low Human Development Index that lags behind in tertiary health care.
128

129
9.5 Attention of the Committee has also been drawn to a strange criterion for selection
of site for AIIMS like Institute. Air connectivity to the place has been one of the major
basis for site selection for AIIMS. It has been reasoned out that airport services will
facilitate employment of better trained human resources as most doctors are reluctant to
serve in remote areas.
9.6 The Committee observes that all the six AIIMS under Phase I are well connected and
are major cities in their respective States but still these new AIIMS are plagued by acute
shortage of manpower. There is no faculty from AIIMS Delhi or other premier Institutes
working full time as Head of Department or as Professors in the new AIIMS. Therefore,
the Committee is of the opinion that the perception that availability of an airport facility
results in faculty retention is based on a false premise. The Ministry and the Institute could
have addressed this issue with better manpower planning rather than focussing on feasibility
of airport.
9.7 The Committee strongly recommends that the Ministry while deciding the location of
the new AIIMS must take note of the already existing healthcare infrastructure in the
vicinity of the earmarked area so that duplication of healthcare facilities are avoided. The
healthcare system should be such that delivery of medical facility and care is regionally
distributed and even the remotest region is well within its outreach so as to address regional
imbalances in provision of health care facilities.
Selection of Project Consultants/Contractors
9.8 The Committee has been informed that the Project Consultants appointed by the Ministry for
the new AIIMS abandoned the work site and failed to complete the project which has led to undue
delay in the construction activities at each of the new AIIMS. CAG Report 10 2018 has also
highlighted the same issue and noted that no action was taken against the defaulting project
consultants for abandoning the contracts or for not adhering to its terms. Moreover, in 2016, the
Ministry appointed M/s HLL and M/s HSCC as project consultants for the remaining work on
nomination basis at a consultancy fee of five percent of the value of balance work, instead of
selecting agencies with better credentials in the outcome/quality parameters.
9.9 The Committee is of the view that the careless selection of the Project consultants did
more harm than good and caused unwarranted delays in the construction activity. The
Ministry failed to make a comprehensive assessment of selection of project-consultants/
contractors before the execution of the scheme. The CAG has also raised concerns regarding
the award of consultancy work to PSUs on nomination basis in its Report. The Committee
also notes that M/s HSCC and M/s HLL were appointed as consultants on nomination basis
under the garb of Rule 176 of General Financial Rules (GFR) 2005. This rule is always read
with the section 1.5.3 that states the selection by direct negotiations/ nomination is permissible
in terms of Rule 176 of General Financial Rules, 2005 only under exceptional circumstances
and such selection may normally be restricted to a financial ceiling of Rs ten lakhs. The
Committee notes that the cases did not fall under any of the special circumstances stipulated
in the section 1.5.3.
9.10 The Committee during the examination of the Demands for Grants has repeatedly

130
seen unprofessionalism and delay in almost all the projects being executed by HSCC.  During
the course of its study visit to Guwahati the Committee had a firsthand view of the manner
in which improper planning by HSCC had resulted in major design flaws.  The Committee,
therefore, strongly feels that the Ministry should follow a fair and transparent method for
the selection of the project consultants and ensure that the agencies involved are technically
competent to complete such big infrastructure projects, given the mixed track record of
HLL/HSCC in other projects.
9.11 The Committee observes that in all the new AIIMS like institutions, undue delay in construction
activities resulted in cost and time overrun. It is surprising to note that the timeline for completion
of the construction project was not met in any of the new AIIMS and was further extended by
5 to 7 years.  CAG in its Report No 10 had also observed that the scheduled dates of completion
of six new AIIMS of Phase-I were between August 2011 and July 2013. Construction works were
not completed by scheduled dates in any of the new AIIMS and there were delays of about four
to five years.
9.12 The Committee also observes that there were major architectural design faults in the buildings
which in turn led to more delays and escalated the cost. A lot of time had to be invested in
correcting the structural flaws caused due to the inconsiderate design planning. Moreover, various
arbitration cases led to stalling of the project as the companies waited for dispute resolutions and
settlements. The Committee also notes that till date, only AIIMS Bhubhaneshwar has completed the
construction activities under all the four packages.
9.13 The Committee is aggrieved at the failure of the Ministry to monitor the timely
completion of the project, mainly the construction activities in all the new AIIMS like
institutions. The Ministry should also ensure proper planning and timely execution for
completion of project. The Institute should always conduct detailed site investigations with
careful monitoring, collective working and effective coordination among all the stakeholders.
The poor performance of the project consultants and the contractors has been cited as the
most common reason behind the inordinate delay. The Committee is of the view that the
Ministry could have explored the idea of appointing a single agency for architectural design/
project consultancy and construction. The Committee also understands that employing a
single agency may raise concern about the flaws in any phase of the project being ignored.
Therefore, the Committee recommends that the Ministry should make a detailed assessment
of the consultants/contractors and employ a trusted company with a proven track record of
executing such infrastructural projects. The Committee also believes that designing and
planning is fundamental for the optimum utilisation of the space available. The Ministry
should learn from the architectural /design failures in AIIMS Rishikesh and ensure that
such mistakes are avoided in the construction of other AIIMS.
9.14 The Committee notes that there have been several cases of arbitration under various construction
projects in PMSSY . The Ministry has justified these cases to be routine matter and procedural
issues as the total arbitral amount awarded was low compared to the total project cost.
9.15 The Committee is concerned with the manner in which arbitration cases have been
dealt resulting in grant of compensations to contractors. The Committee recommends for an

131
arbitration mechanism/cell to be institutionalized in the Ministry that would deal with all
the arbitration cases and ensure speedy resolution. The Committee is concerned with arbitration
issues arising in case of reputed companies like L&T and would like to be apprised of all
such cases.  The Committee recommends that a report on the status of all arbitration cases
with respect to projects under PMSSY be provided to it so that a fair assessment of the
impact of arbitration cases can be done.
Expansion Programme of AIIMS
9.16 The Committee understands that land availability and acquisition is a major concern for the
expansion programme of AIIMS like institutions. Attention of the Committee has been drawn to the
National Building Codes (NBC) guidelines that lays restriction in the height of buildings and does
not allow vertical expansion of hospitals.
9.17 During the Committee’s visit to Patna, AIIMS, the Health Secretary urged upon the Committee
to recommend to the central Government to bring further necessary amendment in NBC guidelines
allowing for vertical expansion of hospital building in view of problem due to shortage of land or
in acquisition of land at the site.
9.18 The Committee is of the view that AIIMS like Institutes and hospitals should be
excluded from the NBC guidelines or some amendments in the guidelines can be made so
that AIIMS like Institutions are allowed to carry out vertical expansion. This will not only
ensure the optimum utilisation of land but also resolve the delays caused due to unavailability
of land. Vertical expansion will also lead to an organised set up. The OPDs can be operated
from the top floors and the other critical centres such as trauma and emergency, ICUs,
HDUs, OTs,  etc can be functional from the lower floors of the building. The Ministry of
Health and Family Welfare should take up this proposal with the Ministry of Housing and
Urban Affairs and study the feasibility and implications of the recommendation so as to
facilitate vertical expansion in all AIIMS.
Procurement of Medical Devices and Equipment
9.19 The Committee has been given to understand that HLL has been appointed as a Procurement
Support Agency under PMSSY . The Committee notes with concern that the role of PSA was to
ensure timely procurement along with adherence to quality and price benchmarks but it has failed
to do so.
9.20 CAG in its Report has also observed that in six new AIIMS (Bhopal, Bhubaneswar, Jodhpur,
Patna, Raipur and Rishikesh), 195 equipment costing ` 72.04 crore, though received, were not
installed due to reasons like pending civil work, non-availability of site, non-availability of skilled
manpower etc. These equipment were lying uninstalled in the hospitals for the period ranging
between three months and four years as of March 2017. In four new AIIMS (Bhopal, Bhubaneswar,
Patna and Rishikesh), there was delay ranging from 3 months to over three years in installation
of 850 equipment costing to ` 76.40 crore.
9.21 The Committee notes that the Procurement Support Agency (PSA) failed miserably in
its task to ensure timely delivery and installation of the medical equipment. Reasons cited
for non-installation of the medical equipment clearly highlight the fact that the PSA failed

132
to coordinate with the Institute that led to unnecessary delays. The Committee is of the
opinion that the PSA in collaboration with the Institute should have resolved the user
requirement, site details and other specific requirements with respect to the Medical Equipment
well in advance. The Committee is highly disappointed by the performance of PSA and
recommends for a strong monitoring of the function of PSA along with quarterly submission
of a status Report on the procurement of medical devices/equipment to the Committee
under the PMSSY scheme.
9.22 The Committee notes that several medical devices and other equipment have been procured
on single bid basis in all the AIIMS. In cases where multiple bids were allowed, many companies
were rejected on technical grounds. The Institutes have insisted on US FDA certification even for
basic equipment for which BIS/CE certified equipment of equivalent quality were available in the
market. Superfluous expenditure was done to purchase beds, cushions, LED TV etc with US FDA
certification.
9.23 The Committee strongly underlines the need to follow a competitive tendering mechanism
for procurement of any equipment rather than a single bid tender procurement.  Downright
rejection on technical grounds should be avoided and any such rejection, if made, should
always be substantiated by a detailed evaluation.  The requirement of US FDA certification
for even some of the basic equipment is not only a burden on the exchequer but is also
an outright contempt of the Make in India campaign. The Committee, therefore, strongly
recommends that procurement of equipment/medical devices by the Institutes and the
procurement agencies should be determined by quality, price and fair trade practice.
9.24 The Committee again emphasizes that non-transparent methods in tendering and award
of contracts raises high chances of corruption, irregularities, non-value addition and encourage
restrictive trade practices. Fair and equal opportunity to all the tenders promotes healthy
competition and also offers the best price to the procurement agency.
9.25 Attention of the Committee has also been drawn to the multiple rejections of bids on
technical grounds and unnecessary delays caused during finalising the technical specification
of the equipment by individual AIIMS. In this regard, the Committee appreciates the suggestion
of AIIMS Bhubaneswar and strongly recommends finalising uniform technical specifications
for equipment especially high end/sophisticated equipment for all the AIIMS. The technical
specifications of all the equipment should be finalized by a team of technical experts only
after a detailed and scientific assessment. The Committee strongly recommends that the
Ministry/Institute should give serious consideration to the suggestions of AIIMS Bhubaneswar
for streamlining the procurement process.
9.26 The Committee further recommends that the other AIIMS like Institutions should
emulate AIIMS Bhubaneswar that has procured 130 medical equipment with ISO/BIS certification.
This would not only minimize the cost but also promote the domestic manufacturers thus
boosting Government’s flagship Make in India campaign.
9.27 The Committee also appreciates the guidelines regarding requirement/non-requirement
of US FDA /CE certification etc. in the procurement of medical devices. The Para 2(i) of the
guideline clearly states that wherever the Indian Standards are available the Hospitals/

133
Health Institutes/Organisations shall not insist on USFDA or CE certification. The Committee
is concerned regarding the misuse of Para 2(ii) of these guidelines that gives an opportunity
to the indenting organization to purchase medical equipment with USFDA certification in the
name of quality.
9.28 The Committee notes that even simple devices are being procured with USFDA mandatory
certification in the backdrop of the discretionary element in the guidelines. The Committee
terms such provision as just another attempt to camouflage the USFDA requirement in the
name of quality which is a setback to competitive pricing. The Committee, therefore, strongly
recommends that the Ministry should fix certification requirement as USFDA or CE or
Bureau of Indian Standards (BIS) or any other internationally accepted certification in the
interim till BIS standards are established universally so as to leave no option for discretion
and its misuse. BIS through its Medical Devices Management System Certification Scheme
IS/ISO 13485 gives certification to the medical equipment which are Made in India . The
Committee is of the view that procurement of indigenously produced medical equipment will
promote and encourage companies to manufacture their products under the  “Make in
India” scheme creating thereby additional employment opportunities and empowering secondary
and tertiary sector of the country.  The Committee, therefore, recommends that spirit of
the guideline under reference must be executed on the ground level to encourage flagship
“Make in India” programme. The matter may also be taken up on priority basis, with the
BIS so that every single equipment procured in India has a BIS standard which would be
equivalent to international quality certification.
9.29 The Committee has also come across several discrepancies in the procurement exercise.
Despite similar CMC/training/warranty conditions, a substantial price differential has been
observed in the procurement of the same medical equipment by the private corporate
hospitals and the new AIIMS. The Committee is concerned to note that AIIMS like Institutions
have purchased the same medical equipment at a higher cost than their counterparts in the
health industry. The Committee recommends that the Ministry should carry out a detailed
assessment in all such cases where price differential is more than 10% in the procurement
of equipment by private/corporate hospitals and AIIMS like Institutions.
9.30 The Committee would also like to flag the observations of CAG in its Report No. 10
of 2018 which inter alia  highlights the following:
(i) In six new AIIMS (Bhopal, Bhubaneswar, Jodhpur, Patna, Raipur and Rishikesh) 1,318
equipment valued at ` 454 crore remained undelivered as on 31 March 2017 for period
over two years from the due date of delivery . (Para 4.5.2)
(ii) Discrepancies in records relating to receipt and supply of medical equipment
costing ````` 3.26 crore.   Audit noted that 75 equipment stated to have been supplied and
installed by M/s HLL at a cost of ` 3.26 crore in three GMCIs viz. BMCRI-Bangalore,
RIMS-Ranchi and JMC-Jammu were either not received by the Institutes or were not
traceable in the records /premises of the institutes. (Case Study 5.1)
(iii) Procurement of equipment for GMCIs was to be made by the agencies appointed by
the Ministry or the State Government/GMCI Against the procurement orders for 818

134
equipment costing ` 482.21 crore for eleven GMCIs, 151 equipment costing ` 51.72
crore were yet to become available. (Para 5.5)
(iv) In ten GMCIs8, 408 equipment costing ` 71.25 crore were either not installed or
installed with delay ranging from three months to over seven years as on 31 March,
2017. The reasons for non-installation or delayed installation were again improper procurement
planning, pending civil and electrical works, non-availability of skilled manpower, etc.
 (Para 5.6)
(v) In nine GMCIs 9, 977 equipment costing ` 34.99 crore were idle/non-functional as on
31 March, 2017 due to lack of manpower, software problems lack of supporting equipment/
infrastructure, defect. (Para 5.7)
(vi) As per the procurement guidelines and provisions of the MoU signed between GMCIs
and the Ministry, GMCIs had to ensure that no alterations and additions were made to
the list of equipment and specifications firmed up by the medical experts. In RIMS-
Ranchi, JNMC-Aligarh, BMCRI-Bangalore, IMS-Varanasi, GMC-Mumbai and GMC-Nagpur
293 medical equipment costing ` 19.86 crore were procured for installation in the
upgraded facilities. Audit noted that these equipment did not fall in the approved list of
medical equipment of the Ministry. (Para 5.8)
9.31 The Committee takes a serious view of the observations of CAG with respect to the
performance of the PSA and would like to be apprised of the time-bound corrective actions
taken on the CAG observations so that the procurement process can be streamlined.
Shortage of Faculty and Non-Faculty Staff
9.32 The Committee is deeply aggrieved at the shortage of faculty and non-faculty staff in all the
AIIMS like Institutions. There is a dire need to address this acute shortage as it has a direct impact
on the quality of health service and medical education in the Institutes. CAG Report has also
highlighted that in six new AIIMS (Bhopal, Bhubaneswar, Jodhpur, Patna, Raipur and Rishikesh)
shortage of faculty posts ranged from 55 per cent to 83 per cent.  Similarly, the shortage of non-
faculty posts ranged from 77 per cent to 97 per cent.
9.33 The Ministry in its submission stated that with a view to streamline the recruitment process
and attain speedier and better outcomes, the Ministry is considering common recruitment process
for all the AIIMS together by clubbing together vacancies of all the AIIMS. It is envisaged that
one AIIMS will hold recruitment of particular categories for all other AIIMS as well. In this way,
the various non-faculty categories are proposed to be assigned amongst the different AIIMS,
distributing the workload for taking up recruitment exercise. Recently, AIIMS, Delhi carried out the
recruitment for nursing cadre in which vacancies of AIIMS, Jodhpur were also clubbed. This
approach as a policy matter will be taken up in the next Central Institute Body (CIB).
9.34 The Committee appreciates that the Ministry is also considering common recruitment
process for the non-faculty of all AIIMS by clubbing together all the vacancies. The Committee
concurs with the view of the Ministry that one AIIMS can hold recruitment of a particular
