category for all the other AIIMS like Institutions. The Committee is of the view that the
Ministry should take this proposal with the Central Institute Body (CIB) and take necessary

135
steps to execute the same on ground level. The Committee expects that CIB would play a
pro-active role in this matter.
9.35 The Committee strongly recommends for a centralised recruitment process for all non-
faculty staff preferably monitored by AIIMS, Delhi or through a centralised reputed agency,
so that the irregularities in the recruitment process are minimised and the issue of staff
shortage is addressed through a nationwide selection process. The issues related to shortage
of faculty staff have been dealt in respective AIIMS Chapter.
9.36 The Committee would also like to be apprised of the number of faculty that have been
employed from other premier Medical Institutes  like AIIMS, JIPMER, PGI, etc. and are
working full time in the new AIIMS like Institutions. The Institutes should also submit a
status note on the number of applications received from such candidates and the number
of candidates employed and rejected/not found suitable. The Committee also desires the
Institute to submit reasons for the rejection of such candidates.
Quality of Medical Education
9.37 The Committee observes that providing quality education and producing better trained doctors
is a major challenge for the new AIIMS like Institutions. The Committee also notes that some of
the new AIIMS have started Post Graduate courses in different disciplines. Attention of the Committee
has been drawn to the fact that the Post Graduates from these Institutes lack the required clinical
exposure and practical training which should have been integral for all Graduates/Post Graduates
in the AIIMS.
9.38 The Committee is of the considered view that starting PG courses is irrelevant until
and unless the Institute is able to provide quality training under the supervision of full time
expert faculties. The Committee strongly emphasizes the importance of proper clinical training
of the post-graduate students. The Committee further recommends that the AIIMS like
Institutions should emphasize on the applied aspects of various disciplines through practical
training. AIIMS like Institutions should be the front-runner in filling the critical gaps in
the health sector by producing well trained specialists in different disciplines.
9.39 The Committee is also concerned to note that most of the AIIMS do not have any
faculty at the HoD/Professor level from AIIMS Delhi or PGI or JIPMER or any premier
National Institute. The experience and expertise of faculty/HoD has an impact on the
quality of Medical Graduates. In most of the AIIMS, the faculty lacks any teaching
experience from any of the premier Institutes. This raises serious concern about the
dilution of the AIIMS Delhi benchmark. The Committee would also like to be apprised of
the number of clinical cases, discipline-wise that have been dealt by the PG students from
various Departments.
Low Bed Occupancy Rate
9.40 Attention of the Committee has been drawn to the low bed occupancy in the AIIMS like
Institutions. In case of AIIMS Bhopal, the bed occupancy rate is just 10% in the Ophthalmology
Department against 16 beds. The Committee is surprised to note that even in an important Department
like Obstetrics and Gynaecology, the bed occupancy is just 37% against 40 beds.

136
9.41 The Committee is of the opinion that the AIIMS like Institutions that were anticipated
to serve as an excellent centre of medical care have failed miserably to accomplish the
desired objective. The low bed occupancy rate speaks volumes about the prevalent distrust
in the healthcare facilities of the new AIIMS. The Committee is astonished to note that
even in Obstetrics and Gynaecology Department, only 37% bed occupancy exist.
9.42 The Committee is coerced to draw comparison with a hitherto neglected institute i.e.
Regional Institute of Medical Sciences (RIMS) Imphal where the Committee witnessed for
itself during the course of its study visit that the bed occupancy rate is 100% in RIMS
Imphal. The Committee also noted that there were 210 beds in Department of Obstetrics
and Gynaecology with 100% occupancy throughout the year and RIMS carried out  11,000
deliveries per year. The Committee would appreciate if the new AIIMS like Institutions
match at least 25% of the performance delivery of RIMS Imphal which the Committee
witnessed for itself during the study visit of the Committee.
9.43 The Committee recommends for quarterly assessment of the bed occupancy rate in all
AIIMS. Further, all the AIIMS should submit a status note citing reasons for its failure
towards optimum utilisation of the tertiary care facilities and Low Bed Occupancy Rate.
9.44 The Committee is of the opinion that the AIIMS brand in itself should reflect a premium
medical institute. Therefore, it is more imperative for the new AIIMS like institutions to take all
possible measures to ensure that the high standard of medical education and care is maintained.
Governing Body (GB)
9.45 The Committee is aware that the regular meetings of the Governing Body/Institute Body/
Standing Finance Committee are not conducted in the new AIIMS which is important for strengthening
the governance structure of all AIIMS like Institutions.
9.46 The Committee strongly believes regular review and progress meetings instils a sense
of accountability in the Institute and this system of check and balance is very important
for smooth running of the Institute. The Committee also recommends that all major decisions
by the Director and the President of the Regional AIIMS, should be approved by the IB/
GB and any new project should be taken up after due approval of the IB/GB.  The Committee
also recommends that no ex-post-facto  approval should be given under any circumstances.
The Ministry should explore the possibility to assign overall super visionary role to CIB,
while keeping the functional autonomy of each AIIMS intact. The Committee also recommends
that the vacancy in respect of Members of Parliament in the Governing Body of all new
AIIMS must be filled up without further delay. This will not only ensure representation of
the people in the Governing body but will also result in its better functioning in a democratic
manner.
9.47 The Committee also welcomes the recent process of decentralization in all the six
AIIMS with the appointment of their respective Presidents which would entail greater
accountability and better monitoring. The constitution of a Central Institute Body (CIB) is
also a step in the right direction, as this would facilitate the evolution of common standards
across all AIIMS on all important benchmarks like human resources, procurement, expansion
and project execution. The Committee would also like to appreciate the increasing receptivity

137
and meticulous approach of the Ministry in taking corrective measures in rectifying the
flaws in the execution of PMSSY scheme and hopes that objective of the PMSSY scheme i.e.
to provide tertiary healthcare facilities in the remotest regions of the country is realized.
Monitoring Mechanism
9.48 The Committee strongly feels that a proper monitoring mechanism could have easily addressed
the problems of construction delays, under-utilization of budget etc. that are plaguing the new
AIIMS like Institutions. The lack of such surveillance mechanism led to  unnecessary delays and
cost escalation. The Committee is aware that the State Project monitoring Committee was required
to meet at least once in a quarter to monitor the progress of the AIIMS in its State but such
Committees were either not formed or never held any meetings.
9.49 The Committee strongly recommends that the State Project Monitoring Committee
(PMC) should meet regularly and monitor the progress of the work being carried out in
each AIIMS and GMCs. Each Institute should submit a quarterly report to the Ministry
updating it about the status of infrastructure, faculty and construction activities.
9.50 The Committee also strongly recommends that AIIMS like Institutions should be included
in the Mera Aspataal/My hospital initiative of the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare
that captures patient feedback for the services received at the hospital through user-friendly
multiple channels such as Short Message Service (SMS), Outbound Dialling (OBD) mobile
application and web portal. The Committee is of the opinion that patients feedback is
critical for ensuring accountability of the healthcare system in the country. Therefore, the
Committee recommends for inclusion of all AIIMS like Institutions in the Mera Aspataal
initiative in order to enhance its operational proficiency.
9.51 The Committee also recommends that the Secretary/Divisional Head Level Officers
from the PMSSY should make frequent field visits to all the AIIMS like Institutions to
assess the ground realities of the Institutes. The Committee strongly urges the officers
from the PMSSY division to carry out regular inspections of the sites rather than just
relying on the progress report submitted by the respective Institutes. Ministry should also
ensure that the work on paper corresponds to the actual performance of the Institute.
9.52 The Committee notes that there are several resource constraints in all the States in
the Health Sector. The Committee is of the view that despite the existing constraints, all
AIIMS like Institutions have got preferential treatment from the Ministry and the State but
these Institutes have failed to use this preferential treatment to their advantage. The
Committee is disappointed to note that all the AIIMS like Institutions struggle in optimally
utilizing the available resources. The Committee strongly recommends the Institutes to
take suo moto  cognizance of the delay in construction activities and play a more pro-active
role in establishing themselves as centre of apex medical care and facilities.
Dialysis Facilities
9.53 The Committee notes that in AIIMS Bhopal, only three out of sanctioned eight dialysis
machines are functional; in AIIMS Bhubaneswar, setting up of a dialysis unit with 14 dialysis
machines is still under process; in AIIMS Jodhpur, 11 dialysis machines are functional; in AIIMS

138
Rishikesh 3 dialysis machines are functional; in AIIMS Patna and AIIMS Raipur, the Department
of Nephrology is not yet functional.
9.54 The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends that all new AIIMS should establish
a fully equipped Nephrology Department immediately. In view of the rising demand of
dialysis services, a fully functional Nephrology Department is critical in providing tertiary
healthcare facilities. The Committee also recommends that all AIIMS like Institutions should
ensure availability of adequate number of functional dialysis machines and skilled manpower
in the Institute.
9.55 Attention of the Committee has also been drawn to the procurement of Dialysis machine
at a much higher price in the new AIIMS. The Committee observes that same dialysis
machines with the same specifications have been procured by State Government at a much
lower price. Moreover, the same dialysis machines have been procured under the MPLAD
scheme at one third of the price paid by new AIIMS. The Committee takes this as a serious
irregularity in the procurement policy and process. The Committee, therefore, strongly
recommends for instituting an enquiry into the matter and accordingly apprise the Committee
about the reasons and compelling circumstances under which the dialysis machines were
purchased at such higher prices in the new AIIMS.
Higher Education Financing Agency (HEFA)
9.56 The Committee has been given to understand that the lack of budgetary support for PMSSY
with specific reference to AIIMS would be compensated through loans taken from HEFA.  It may
be mentioned that:
(i) Higher Education Financing Agency (HEFA) is a joint venture company of Canara Bank
and Ministry of Human Resource Development, GoI.  HEFA provides financial assistance
for creation of education infrastructure and R and D in India’s premier education Institutions.
(ii) HEFA will mobilize resources from the Market through equity and by issue of bonds.
9.57 The Committee is aware that raising funds from the market through HEFA is a
herculean task and there would be a requirement of pledging of Government resources as
collateral to HEFA to obtain loans. It has also been further mentioned that these loans
would be serviced through internal accruals. The Committee is concerned to note that non-
judicious usage of loans from HEFA by the regional AIIMS will create a vicious cycle
wherein these AIIMS would be subject to a debt trap and would again seek budgetary
support to service the loans obtained from HEFA. The Committee is of the considered
opinion that any loan proposal sent to HEFA should be duly vetted by the CIB after obtaining
a comparative assessment from all the six AIIMS regarding the sourcing of funds and the
ability of AIIMS to service the funds.  The Committee foresees a situation where reckless
expenditure from the loans obtained from HEFA may result in a situation where the incumbent
Directors of the regional AIIMS may retire but the new incumbent would be faced with a
fresh challenge of servicing the loans obtained from HEFA.
9.58 The Committee would like to be updated on a quarterly basis regarding all loan/
advances obtained from HEFA alongwith the measures/proposals under consideration towards

139
timely repayment of these loans. The Committee also recommends that accountability of
these loans be clearly fixed so that there are no legal issues in this regard in future.   The
Committee also recommends tapping donations and CSR funds as has been done by AIIMS
Bhubaneswar and AIIMS Patna for projects like Dharmshala, etc. by the regional AIIMS.
Speciality/Super Speciality Departments and Advance Centres
9.59 The Committee understands that Speciality/Super Speciality Departments are important for
providing specialized quality health care to the patients. Therefore, it is imperative for the Institutes
of National importance like AIIMS to establish Speciality and Super Speciality Departments in the
Institutes.
9.60 The Committee appreciates the good intention behind construction of such Departments
but after a broad study of all the AIIMS like Institutes, the Committee realizes that there
is an urgent need to scrutinize the proper functioning of such Departments. In many
AIIMS, the required infrastructure is present but due to acute shortage of the medical
faculty/specialist, the super-speciality departments are non-functional. This is a typical example
of constructing buildings without foundation. With no staff, the infrastructure and the
resources are lying untapped and unutilized.
9.61 Attention of the Committee has also been drawn to the different proposals of AIIMS like
Institutions for establishing Advance Ophthalmic Centre, Advance Cardiac Centre, Advance Neurosciences
Centre, Advance Transplant Centre and Advance Oncology Centre.
9.62 The Committee is especially worried about the viability of such Advance Centres in
these six AIIMS like Institutions that have a very low average daily OPD footfall, with the
highest being 2949 at AIIMS Bhubaneswar  and the lowest being 1300 at AIIMS Raipur. The
Committee cannot comprehend the need for establishing advance centre for Institutes with
such low OPD footfall, especially when these Institutes have failed to make optimum utilization
of the available resource and infrastructure. The Committee also emphasizes that many
faculty posts are still lying vacant in various Departments and even Speciality and Super
Speciality Departments are non-functional. In such scenario, these Institutes vying for
Advance Centres only reflect a poor understanding of the existing grim reality.
9.63 The Committee strongly recommends that the Ministry/Institute should carry out a
cost benefit analysis before any proposal for building an advance centre in the new AIIMS
is passed. The Committee would like the Ministry/Institute to furnish a status note and a
cost benefit analysis on the proposed and sanctioned advance centers in all the AIIMS like
Institutions. The Committee is of the view that the decisions regarding establishment of
Advance Centers in all the new AIIMS should be taken by Central Institute Body.  The
Committee also strongly recommends that in the new AIIMS, an advance centre should only
be established if the Institutions are able to achieve an OPD level which is 50% of the
average daily OPD footfall of AIIMS Delhi. The utility of such advance centres has to be
closely scrutinized so that the available resources are optimally put to use.
9.64 The Committee also flags the fellowship programmes in different Departments that are
being carried out by these Institutes. The Committee strongly recommends that such fellowship

140
programmes should be carried out only when the Institutes are capable of providing the
required infrastructure and quality training and are operating at its full capacity.
OPD Footfall
9.65 The Committee also hopes that the possibility of duplicating OPD footfall data should
be avoided. The Committee strongly recommends that all the patients should be given a
unique ID that should form the basis of measuring patient load in an Institute. The possibility
to exaggerate OPD footfall/number of procedures (major and minor surgeries) when the
same patient visits multiple Departments in a day should be avoided. The Committee strongly
recommends all the Institutes to measure accurate OPD footfall/procedures and avert from
extrapolating the OPD/surgery numbers. The Committee strongly advocates strengthening
the system of online registration for all the AIIMS like Institutions and work towards
effective implementation of Hospital Management Information System.
9.66 The Committee would also like to be apprised whether the establishment of AIIMS in
the six States has had any impact on the patient load in AIIMS Delhi from these States.
The idea behind regional AIIMS was to provide tertiary health care facilities in all the
States so that the patients from these States do not have to rush to AIIMS Delhi for even
basic medical care. Therefore the Committee desires AIIMS Delhi to submit a report on the
impact these new AIIMS like Institutions had on patient load. AIIMS Delhi should also
submit a report on the number of Patients that were transferred from the regional AIIMS
to AIIMS Delhi.
Operation Theatres and Surgery Issues
9.67 The Committee notes that in all the AIIMS like Institutions the number of functional Operation
Theatres are far less than the sanctioned strength. The Institutes have cited this low number as
the reason behind the long waiting period for surgeries. The Committee also notes that there is no
uniform classification of surgeries into major and minor category which has led to varied unreliable
data from each Institute.
9.68 The Committee strongly recommends all the AIIMS like Institute to expedite the
construction of the Operation Theatres and provide the required infrastructure. The Committee
also recommends all the AIIMS Like Institutes to carry out a detailed analysis of the
operation theatre utilisation at their respective Institutes and submit the same to the
Committee. The Committee also believes that there is an urgent need to follow strict
Operation Theatre guidelines so that OTs start on time and the time between two surgeries
in a single day is optimally utilised and not wasted. Better planning of operation schedule
and timings can definitely increase the efficiency of OTs. The Committee also takes a
strong note of the existing large discrepancies in the data provided by each Institute. The
Committee strongly recommends following a uniform criteria for classification of surgeries
as minor and major so that accurate data related to the number of surgeries conducted can
be obtained.
9.69 CAG in its Report No 10 of 2018 has highlighted the findings of previous audit in the
following paragraph:-

141
‘The Audit Report of the CAG (Report No.19 of 2013 - Union Government-Civil) contained
observations relating to irregularities in selection of project consultants, irregular expenditure
on escalation charges amounting to ` 1.56 crore, incorrect release of mobilization advance
amounting to ` 8.32 crore to contractors, excess payment of ` 25.20 lakh to in-house consultant
and release of payment to PSU consultants based on estimated fund requirements instead of
on actual progress of work. In reply to the audit comments, the Ministry had stated that the
audit findings were noted for avoidance of such occurrence in future. However, irregularities
of similar nature have been noticed during the current audit as mentioned in Paras 2.5, 3.11,
4.4.2 (b) and 4.4.3 (iii) of this Report.’
9.70 The CAG Report on PMSSY has highlighted various inadequacies and problem areas in
setting up of new AIIMS and upgradation programme of Government Medical Colleges. The
CAG Report No. 10 contains observations relating to cost and time overrun, excess payment
of mobilisation advance, delay in delivery and installation of medical equipment, shortage
of faculty and non-faculty posts, non-functional speciality and super speciality Departments
etc. The Committee would like to be apprised of the measures that have been taken by the
Ministry and the Institutes to overcome all the inadequacies and address the CAG observations
in its audit Report.
9.71 The Committee beside recommending for decentralization of healthcare facilities in the
most remote areas of the country, is inclined to emphasize that central Government and
State Government must make policy and chalk out strategy and formulate/appropriate course
of action for delivering Health Care Services at the Doorstep with the purpose of making
Ayushman Bharat a successful scheme.

142
RECOMMENDATIONS/OBSERV ATIONS  —  AT A GLANCE
The Committee notes with concern the observations of CAG that only 50% (approximately)
of the funds allocated for AIIMS, Bhopal had been utilised between 2011 and 2017. The
Committee is of the firm view that the budgetary allocation should have been properly
utilised under a strategic plan of action to prevent underutilisation. The Committee observes
the increasing trend of expenditure since 2017-18 when the expansion work got momentum.
The Committee also observes that allocation of funds to the tune of  ````` 99.90 crore in
the financial year 2017-18 was less than the actual expenditure of ````` 237.24 crore in the
said year and the remaining expenditure was met with unspent balances of the prior
period. (Para 2.6)
The Committee, during its visit to AIIMS, Bhopal observed that construction activities
were still going on. Even the residential accommodation facilities for doctors and students
were yet to be completed by CPWD.  The Committee feels that the slow pace of progress
would have adversely affected the quality of healthcare services and education being
imparted at the Institute. The Committee calls upon the Institute to undertake prudent
assessment of fund requirement and accordingly project its budgetary demand with due
justification. (Para 2.7)
The Committee is not convinced with the submission of the Institute that the
underutilisation of funds was due to undue lags in the construction activities, recruitment
process and hospital services. The Committee, therefore, recommends that AIIMS, Bhopal
must formulate a correct estimate for projects, prepare DPR and ensure administrative
approvals in order to complete the remaining projects within set timelines and at the
estimated cost. (Para 2.8)
STATUS OF CONSTRUCTION WORK
The Committee observes that the delay in the construction work was mainly due to
non performance by the selected construction agencies along with improper planning and
ineffective monitoring. Though the construction status is near completion in the first three
Packages, under Package IV only 60% of the work is complete. It is high time that the
Institute should strengthen the existing infrastructure and strive to complete the remaining
work within the stipulated time limits as it has been six years since the Institute has been
operationalised and the construction work is still incomplete. The Committee is inclined to
observe that the selection of construction agencies was devoid of merit and transparency
resulting in substantial time and cost overrun. The Committee, therefore, recommends that
stringent monitoring mechanism should be put in place to ensure timely completion of work
to avoid time and cost overruns. The Committee recommends that the Ministry should take
necessary steps to take possession of the area which is under encroachment. The Committee
is concerned to note that the project consultant HLL has miserably failed in proper monitoring
of the project and its entire approach reflects unprofessionalism. The Committee, therefore,
recommends suitable remedial action including fixing accountability and appropriate action
keeping in view the observations of the CAG . (Para 2.14)

143
PROCUREMENT OF MEDICAL EQUIPMENT
The Committee understands that USFDA is a regulatory mechanism/certification for
protecting and promoting public health by way of control and supervision of food and drugs
including medical devices with specific reference to the United States but there is hardly any
country in the world besides the United States which makes a provision for allowing USFDA
as a mandatory condition. The Committee fails to comprehend the reasons for procuring 50
equipment with USFDA certification, some of which are easily available with CE/BIS certification.
The Committee, therefore, recommends to the Institute to procure equipment with CE/BIS
certification so as to encourage Medical Device Industry in India and participate in the
Government’s “Make in India” initiative. (Para 2.17)
The Committee fails to understand that no cost-benefit analysis was undertaken by the
Institute before procuring medical equipment in AIIMS, Bhopal. The Committee wonders
how AIIMS, Bhopal procured equipment without ascertaining the availability of space, manpower
and infrastructure required for installation etc. The Committee, therefore, recommends to
the Ministry that proper assessment with respect to cost-benefit analysis should be done
before the purchase of any equipment. (Para 2.19)
The Committee would like to be apprised of the circumstances under which the process
and procedure of single bid system was adhered to in purchasing three equipment viz.
(i) endobronchial ultrasound system (ii) Portable RO/WTU and (iii) Hemodialysis machine.
The Committee, in this connection, strongly recommends for procurement of equipment on
multiple bidding basis in future so as to have fair competitive pricing and availability of
more options for procurement of best and economical equipment. (Para 2.21)
The Committee believes that the lackadaisical approach of the Institute has resulted
in non installation of the equipment which has delayed operationalization of the critical
healthcare facilities, thus, depriving the patients of key diagnostic and in-patient facilities.
The Committee, therefore, recommends to the Institute to have a system in place for
ensuring timely procurement as well as installation of the equipment so that the equipment
are properly and optimally utilized for the purpose for which they were procured. The
Committee also recommends for immediate installation of 21 types of equipment costing
````` 27.9 crore that have been procured but not installed yet. The Committee further calls
upon the Ministry to look into matter of procurement of 25 equipment costing ````` 84.7 crore
which are yet to be received and installed in AIIMS, Bhopal. In view of the delays in
procurement and installation of equipment in the Institute, the Committee  impresses upon
the Ministry to ensure effective monitoring through ABC analysis (Always a better control)
by the Technical Specification Committees at the Ministry and institutional level   to ensure
much needed  synchronization and coordination of activities  with regard to procurement
and installation of equipment. (Para 2.23)
STATUS OF FACULTY/NON FACULTY STRENGTH
The Committee is dissatisfied with the vacancy positions in different Departments in
AIIMS, Bhopal, where out of 305 sanctioned faculty posts, 164 posts are lying vacant. The
Committee is apprehensive that for an AIIMS like Institution, such a gap in workforce

144
would adversely affect the functional capacity of the Institute. The Committee calls upon
the Institute to accelerate its administrative procedure for filling up the vacant posts within
a time bound manner. The Ministry also needs to closely monitor the efforts made by the
Institute in this regard. The Committee also recommends that the vacant posts should be
filled on regular basis within a specified period of time. Measures must be taken for filling
up the vacant posts on permanent basis to ensure continuity and smooth functioning of the
Institute. (Para 2.27)
The Committee expresses its concern that out of total sanctioned strength of 3776 non
faculty posts in AIIMS, Bhopal, only 792 posts have been filled up, including 424 posts that
have been actually outsourced. In Committee’s opinion non-faculty staff is the backbone of
any medical institution in terms of its successful and smooth functioning. With such a wide
gap in the in-position strength against the sanctioned non-faculty posts, it would have been
extremely difficult to manage the day to day functional equilibrium of various Departments
of the Institute in an integrated manner. The Committee, therefore, recommends for expediting
the appointment process of non-faculty staff on a regular basis so that all the Departments
have adequate man power to cater to all aspects of patient care. The Committee is of the
considered opinion that a centralized recruitment process for filling up of non-faculty posts
would address the entire non faculty shortage issue and also ensure a transparent recruitment
process. The Committee strongly recommends the Ministry to initiate this process on an
immediate basis. (Para 2.28)
The Committee is displeased over the fact that the AIIMS, Bhopal engaged an agency
for provision of manpower without tendering in violation of General Financial Rules. This
lapse deprived the Institute from the benefit of the competitive bidding while making
provision for requisite manpower . The Committee, therefore, recommends to the Ministry
to ensure effective monitoring of the whole process of tendering in compliance with GFR
provisions. (Para 2.30)
SPECIALTY/SUPER SPECIALTY DEPARTMENTS
The Committee reprimands the AIIMS, Bhopal management over the fact that Departments
of Rheumatology, TB & Chest, Gastroenterology, Surgical Gastroenterology, Urology, Medical
Oncology, Clinical Hematology and Neonatology are still not functional at the Institute. The
Committee is of the view that these are key Departments in providing medical aid and in
some cases these Departments can provide life saving assistance to people. The Committee,
therefore, recommends to the Institute to start these Specialty and Super Specialty Department
as early as possible to address sufferings of patient. (Para 2.32)
The Committee is deeply anguished to take into account with the present status of the
faculty at the Specialty and Super Departments at the Institute. Only 17 posts have been
filled up against 100 sanctioned posts in the Super Specialist Departments and 83 posts are
lying vacant. Not only that, many Departments such as Cardiology, Cardiothoracic and
Vascular Surgery, Psychiatry, Nephrology, Surgical Oncology, Endocrinology & Metabolic
Diseases, Pediatric Surgery and Nuclear Medicine are being run by a single faculty.  The
Committee is surprised to observe the sorry state of functioning of AIIMS, Bhopal and

145
concludes that with such huge vacancy, the Institute cannot achieve its objectives of providing
tertiary level health care services to the people. The Committee believes that there is an
urgent need to address the deficiency in the manpower at the Specialist and Super Specialist
level. The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends to the Institute to fill up the vacant
posts at the earliest. The Committee also recommends that AIIMS Bhopal should explore
innovati ve means like special packages/incentives to attract Specialist/Super Specialists
from premier institutes in its different Departments to ensure better function ing of AIIMS
Bhopal. (Para 2.33)
MEDICAL EDUCATION
The Committee has observed that the admissions in MBBS and B.Sc Nursing Courses
are being done with an intake of 100 and 60 students respectively at AIIMS, Bhopal. The
number of admissions in MD/MS courses in different disciplines has also increased. Out of
33 sanctioned seats for January, 2018 session, 24 admissions have already been done. The
Committee was given to understand by the Ministry that two seats in Departments of
Microbiology and one seat in  Immunohematology and Blood Transfusion were sanctioned
in January, 2018. However, utmost to the surprise of the Committee, none of the seats in
these two Departments have been filled up yet. Further, in Anatomy and Pediatrics Departments,
three seats have been sanctioned for each Department and only one seat in each Department
has been filled up. The Committee, while expressing its serious concern, strongly recommends
that the Ministry to evaluate the reasons for non filling up of the seats and undertake
corrective measures to fill up the vacant seats strictly in a time bound manner so that the
intended benefit to the beneficiaries be made available and the scarce resources, that have
immense opportunity costs, are used in a judicious manner. The Committee, further, recommends
to the Institute to expedite the process of starting new courses within a stipulated time
so that students do not suffer due to non-availability of any medical Department in the
Institute. (Para 2.35)
The Committee expects AIIMS, Bhopal to deliver quality medical education in all its
Departments so that passing out students serve the society in an excellent manner. For this,
basic infrastructure at the Institute is required apart from improved student faculty ratio
to produce well trained doctors. The Committee also believes that the retention rate of
passing out students in the Institute would determine the standard of quality medical
education being imparted in the Institute. (Para 2.36)
The Committee is of the strong view that for a medical or nursing college, it is
imperative to have more exposure of medical cases for students to attain clinical skills in
their respective fields. The Committee is highly apprehensive as to how the Institute will
accomplish the objective to impart training to the PG students, MD/MS, PhD students with
inadequate faculty and limited OPD. The Committee is also skeptical of the clinical training
of the first batch of MBBS and Nursing students who have completed their internship this
year. Since the Institute had huge number of vacancies in various departments, it is obvious
that students would not have been given adequate clinical training as part of their curriculum.
This is not a good indicator for AIIMS, Bhopal for meeting the expectation of high medical
education from an institute of national importance. The Committee has reasons to apprehend

146
that inadequate training of medical and nursing students will only produce partially skilled
and half trained doctors in the country that would, in fact, become a liability rather than
assets of the country. Therefore, in order to ensure the prospective future of aspiring
doctors and nurses, the Institute must strive towards providing all the requisite resources
for the students to help them to hone their skills adequately during their academic years
so that they acquire desired level of knowledge during their stay in AIIMS. (Para 2.40)
The Committee, however, appreciates the efforts made by the Institute in expanding
their medical education services. The Committee feels that paramedical education and training
should also be included in the medical education services at AIIMS, Bhopal as the available
requisite infrastructure can help produce a competent technical workforce in different clinical
fields. The Committee, therefore, recommends to the Ministry to contemplate about introducing
the paramedical education and training courses at AIIMS, Bhopal on an urgent basis to
address the problem of shortage of technical manpower in clinical fields and optimize the
utilization of infrastructure of the hospital set up. (Para 2.41)
OPERATION THEATERS
The Committee is concerned to note that the waiting time in surgical procedures
varies from one week to six months due to limited availability of operation theaters at
present.  The Committee is of the view that AIIMS, Bhopal was established to provide
immediate medical care to the nearby population who rush to AIIMS, Delhi or other adjoining
facilities for specialized care. Therefore, long waiting time at AIIMS, Bhopal for the patients
requiring surgeries would defeat the very purpose of having an AIIMS like Institution in
the State. It would further increase the out of pocket expenditure of those who cannot
afford high medical expenses. The Committee, therefore, recommends to the Institute to
expedite the construction of OTs and make them fully functional. The Committee would
also like to be apprised of the number of surgeries being conducted (both major and minor)
per day per OT to ensure optimum utilization of the existing functional OTs. (Para 2.43)
STATUS OF INTENSIVE CARE UNITS AND TRAUMA/EMERGENCY FACILITIES
The Committee expresses its deep concern to view that for an institution like AIIMS,
Bhopal the facilities for intensive care units are inadequate. Though the existing units are
functional with optimal equipment, there is scope for expansion in terms of separate ICU
for different Departments like Cardiology, Medicine, etc. The Institute has partially started
24x7 Trauma and Emergency services. However, there are just two faculty for Trauma and
Emergency center which is inadequate to provide dedicated trauma/emergency facilities and
to deal with mass casualties. A lot more needs to be done by AIIMS, Bhopal as a tertiary
level medical care hospital to cater to the victims of increasing road accidents and other
trauma cases. The Committee would like to emphasize that the Institute should have a well
developed infrastructure and a better equipped set up with state of art medical facilities to
deal with trauma cases. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Institute should
work towards enhancing its spheres/perception of healthcare services to stand out as the
most efficient healthcare provider in Madhya Pradesh.  The Institute needs to increase ICUs
and trauma facilities on a priority basis so that it becomes the best referral centre in the
entire State for Trauma/Emergency Care. (Para 2.45)

147
STATUS OF BEDS AND WARDS STRENGTH
The Committee is constrained to express its displeasure that functional bed strength
is approximately 50 per cent of the sanctioned bed strength at the Institute.  In Private and
Ophthalmology wards there are 16 beds out of which only 2 are occupied.  It was noted that
there is no separate ward for Cardiology, Nephrology or Oncology at the Institute. Given the
high incidence of number of patients under these categories, the existing facilities are quite
inadequate. Further, the occupancy rate is less than fifty per cent in almost all the wards
except medical, orthopedics, surgical, and ICUs. The Committee is concerned to note the
abysmally low level of bed occupancy for a tertiary care institute of the level of AIIMS. The
Committee expects AIIMS Bhopal to improve its perception as a health care provider so
that bed occupancy/utilization increases substantially.  Needless to mention, the Committee
is of the considered opinion that the merger of BMHRC with AIIMS, Bhopal assumes
critical importance to ensure optimum utilization of the tertiary healthcare facilities in
AIIMS, Bhopal. (Para 2.47)
DIALYSIS FACILITIES
The Committee expresses its dissatisfaction that there are only two dialysis machines
at the hospital. The Committee also notes huge shortfall in manpower resources especially
in the Nephrology Department. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Institute
should strive to increase the number of dialysis machines as well as the requisite
manpower to upgrade their Nephrology Department for effective health care service
delivery. (Para 2.49)
BLOOD BANK FACILITY
The Committee is disturbed to note the non-availability of blood bank facility at the
Institute even after five years of functional IPD services. The Committee is of the view
that blood bank services are an integral part of any hospital system. The Committee,
therefore, strongly recommends to the Institute to expedite the setting up of fully functional
blood bank facility without further delay. (Para 2.51)
ISSUE OF CONTRACTUAL APPOINTMENTS
The Committee was informed that the doctors of AYUSH Hospital have been working
on contractual basis for the past several years. The Committee during its study visit found
that the OPD footfall in the AYUSH centre was much higher despite limited infrastructure.
The Committee appreciates the work ethics of the three AYUSH doctors in treating a large
number of patients having faith in the Indian system of Medicine. The Committee feels that
given the positive response towards AYUSH services, the Ministry should contemplate of
having a permanent staff instead of engaging contractual workforce. This would ensure job
stability and security to the employees and in turn the organization would be benefitted
from huge experience of these doctors gained during their service at AYUSH Hospital. The
Committee, therefore, strongly recommends the Department to revisit the rules regarding
contractual appointments so as to regularize the existing AYUSH doctors to permanent
appointments in view of their dedicated service and outstanding performance. (Para 2.54)

148
MERGER OF BHOPAL MEMORIAL HOSPITAL & RESEARCH CENTRE (BMHRC) AND AIIMS,
BHOPAL
The Committee welcomes the positive response of the Ministry w.r.t. the merger of
BMHRC with AIIMS Bhopal. The Committee also appreciates the concurrence of Director,
BMHRC and the Department of Health Research to this merger proposed in public interest.
The Committee is of the considered view that at present there is duplication of resources
and services being offered in various medical fields leading to non optimal utilization of
high cost equipment and medical services. The BMHRC caters to a substantial population
of gas tragedy victims and their dependents, it may continue to do the same subsequent to
the merger. BMHRC is well equipped to train paramedical workforce which can be produced
by the hospital easily if the requisite faculty is made available. The Committee feels that
merger of both hospitals would help in attracting and retaining medical faculty. The Committee
feels that merger of BMHRC with AIIMS, Bhopal would increase scope of teaching and
training avenues for doctors and students. The Committee, therefore, lends its full support
to the merger of both Institutes for the benefit of the society. The Committee, therefore,
recommends that Department of Health & Family Welfare should work towards chalking
out necessary course of action for bringing BMHRC under its purview by merging it with
AIIMS, Bhopal. While doing so, the Department of Health & Family Welfare can look into
utilizing the huge set up of BMHRC, Bhopal for putting in place a dedicated resource center
for both medical faculty and paramedical staff in order to have paramedical/clinical technicians
workforce in various fields.  The synergy arising out of the merger of BMHRC with AIIMS,
Bhopal would result in optimal utilization of the existing infrastructure and resources. The
Committee, therefore, strongly recommends that administrative hurdles towards the merger
of both the institutes should be removed as early as possible. (Para 2.60)
The Committee, further, recommends that in order to protect the interest of doctors
of BMHRC, the recruitment rules which are different from AIIMS (which happens to be an
autonomous Institution governed by a separate Act) be retained in the transitory phase till
complete merger is done so that the promotional avenues of the BMHRC doctors are
protected. No fresh recruitment should be done under BMHRC rules and the cadre of
serving doctors be treated as a dying cadre for smooth transition of the merger process. The
Committee recommends for time bound action in this matter and would like to be apprised
of the progress made within three months of presentation of this Report. (Para 2.61)
BUDGETARY ALLOCATION
The Committee is anguished to observe there has been underutilisation of funds till
the year 2016-17 in AIIMS, Rishkesh. The Committee notes that out of a total budget
estimate of ````` 426.50 crores during 2018-19, only ````` 165 crores of funds have been released
till 15th October, 2018. The Committee also notes that under GIA (CCA) allocation has
been slashed to ````` 250 crores from the projected demand of ````` 1186.96 crores in BE 2018-19.
The Committee is of the opinion that AIIMS, Rishikesh has set up overambitious targets
for itself and therefore, needs to reconsider these demands. A cost benefit analysis also
needs to be done vis-à-vis  availability of other tertiary care services in the adjoining area.
The Committee recommends that a common criterion along with a rational approach towards

149
utilisation of financial allocation for each AIIMS needs to be done. The Committee is not
convinced with the justification for overall projected demand to the tune of ````` 1363.46 crores
which is far above the expenditure incurred in other similar tertiary care center with much
higher OPD/IPD load. Needless to say the Committee is concerned at the audacious projection
which alone is almost equal to the sum total of all the remaining five regional AIIMS under
study. The Committee recommends that suitable benchmark for AIIMS standards be formulated
by the Central Institute Body (CIB) so that fiscal discipline and prudence in the utilization
of the resources is done under all circumstances. The Committee recommends that AIIMS,
Rishikesh should review its projections to a more realistic level. (Para 3.9)
The Committee observes that in-principle approval of many important centers like
Cardiac Science Center, Neuroscience Center, Advance Eye Center, Lung Center to with
estimated expenditure to the tune of ````` 100 crores each has been given by the Governing
Body but the construction work could not be started due to unavailability of funds. Besides,
the Committee is concerned to note that when there has already been a construction of a
full fledged hospital to seek ````` 97.61 crore for Administrative block, ````` 100 crore for the
academic block, ````` 100 crore for research block and ````` 383 crore for residential accommodation
gives an impression that there is no need for optimum/rational utilization of scarce resources.
The Committee, therefore, recommends to the Ministry to monitor the utilization of funds
given to AIIMS, Rishikesh with utmost caution and justification. The Committee also
recommends for a comparative cost-benefit analysis of AIIMS, Rishikesh with other regional
AIIMS specifically AIIMS, Bhubaneswar in this regard, as the Committee finds it to be the
better managed in terms of investment outcome scale and rational utilization of limited
resources. The Committee is of the opinion that regional AIIMS should provide tertiary
health care in the neglected region but should not merely duplicate the facilities available
with AIIMS Delhi. The Committee recommends that setting up of advanced centers should
be done only when AIIMS Rishikesh achieves at least 50% of the OPD footfall of AIIMS
Delhi and the existing infrastructure is put to optimum usage. Availability of faculty/Super
Specialists to run these advanced centers should also be kept in consideration. The Committee,
therefore, recommends for consideration of all matters regarding setting up of advanced
centers by the CIB and also to take into account the budgetary implications if these centers
are to be setup replicated in all existing and proposed AIIMS. (Para 3.10)
STATUS OF CONSTRUCTION WORK
The Committee observes that there has been a delay in the construction of almost all
projects and the target date of completion for most of the projects has been further extended.
The Committee also notes that in most of the cases, delay in the work is due to the non
performance of construction agency. Such instances have led to delay in construction activities
implying poor assessment on part of the Institute in selecting the project development
agency/contractor. The Committee believes that time overruns of 4-5 years cannot be justified
on any grounds and this implies poor planning and lackadaisical approach on the part of
implementing agency. The Committee is of the considered view that project contractor with
a proven track record of successful and timely completion of work should be deployed for
such important projects. The Committee therefore, recommends that Institute must rationally

150
evaluate the projects and plan and chalk out future course of actions, accordingly, by
prioritizing the projects on the basis of urgency to avoid delay in completion of the
projects. (Para 3.15)
The Committee chides the Rishikesh management in the strongest term for not detecting
the designing deficiencies in the detailed project report at the initial stage. The Committee
is distressed to note that the project consultants failed miserably in designing the blueprint
for the Institute and neglected the site specific limitations and concerns related to architectural
and engineering techniques for a hospital set-up. The Committee is shocked to observe that
there is no provision for medical gas pipelines system, sewer lines passing through operation
theaters, basement planning thereby overlooking the water table concerns.  This was a basic
civil engineering concept that was completely overlooked. (Para 3.17)
During its visit to AIIMS, Rishikesh, the Committee came across a lot of space
wastage due to structural design faults. The Committee was given to understand that the
Institute took undue time in correcting the structural deficiencies and thus the development
work lagged behind. The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends that the project consultants
must be held accountable for such serious lapse in the designing and construction projects
of the Institute. The Committee is alarmed at almost 150 times deviation in the original
quantities and the actual quantities as compared to the quantities given in the Bill of
Quantities (BoQ) of the Contract as pointed by the CAG in its Report. The Committee is
of the opinion that AIIMS, Rishikesh in itself is a case study of mismanagement, lack of
foresight and wastage of public funds. The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends that
the structural/designing faults in AIIMS Rishikesh be corrected to the extent feasible and
new expansion be done with utmost caution with strict monitoring. (Para 3.18)
The Committee notes that there is limited land available for expansion as only 101
acres is available for construction of hospital, academic and residential blocks. The space
crunch is limiting the expansion of services at the institute. The Committee recommends
for a comprehensive space audit of the Institute by any independent agency so as to ensure
optimum land utilization, within six months of the presentation of this report. The Committee
during the course of its study visit saw for itself wastage of land in ancillary areas like
parking etc. Possibility of vertical expansion of residential/hostel facilities may be explored.
The Committee recommends for an expeditious decision on transfer of 200 acre IDPL land
to address the space crunch for AIIMS, Rishikesh after ensuring optimum/rational utilization
of the available land. (Para 3.20)
The Committee is of the considered view that the hospital staff, the workforce and the
students deserve suitable accommodation facilities in carrying out their respective tasks.
The Committee would like the AIIMS, Rishikesh to take necessary steps in consultation
with construction agency to expedite the on-going construction of hostels without time and
cost overruns. (Para 3.21)
PROCUREMENT OF EQUIPMENT
The Committee, while examining the rationale forwarded by the Institute for the
procurement of da vinci  Robotic Arm is of the opinion that conventional laparoscopic surgery

151
takes much lesser time and does  not involve usage of expensive consumables. The Committee
is of the opinion that these resources could have been better utilized in strengthening other
important areas at this nascent stage. The alibi, given regarding training and extended
warranty as a justification for higher prices, fails to appeal to the Committee, as similar
terms have been given to other corporate hospitals by the Company across the country. The
Committee is not convinced by these woolly arguments to procure prohibitively expensive
equipment for a budding Institution like AIIMS. The Committee notes that it has been over
15 years since the da vinci  system (robotic surgery) is being used, but the evidence that it
beats laparoscopic (minimally invasive surgery) is still lacking. The Committee notes that
Robotic surgery is several times more expensive than the traditional laparoscopic surgery
and engages the OT and the surgeon for much longer duration. The Committee strongly
believes that robotic surgery should be used only in centers of excellence like AIIMS Delhi
where the Institute is in a position to evaluate the efficacy, economics, efficiency and utility
of these types of surgical intervention as more research is needed to assess the benefits of
robotic surgery. The Committee is also concerned that the da vinci  system still has monopoly
in the market and there is every possibility of the company dictating the price and indulging
in unfair monopolistic practice. The Committee, thus, recommends to the Institute to ensure
transparency besides appropriate cost/benefit analysis in the procurement of equipment to
avoid such prohibitively expensive procurement in future. The Committee would like to
have a quarterly report on the usage of the DA Vinci Robotic Arm in both Rishikesh and
Jodhpur so as to enable it to arrive at a final conclusion in the matter. (Para 3.26)
The Committee is not satisfied by the reply given by the Institute regarding the
procurement of equipment on a single bid/proprietary basis as such practices eliminate
competition resulting in higher procurement costs. The Institute should go for competitive
tendering to defeat monopolistic tendencies. The Committee, further, feels that the notification
of tender should get wider reach so that more and more bids are received.  The Committee,
further, desires a thorough investigation into all single bid and propriety purchases. The
Committee may be apprised of the conclusions drawn out of the investigation. (Para 3.28)
The Committee finds the reply submitted by the Institute as evasive, as the reasons
for the procurement of equipment with USFDA certification is not submitted. The Committee
finds that even some of the basic equipment have been purchased with USFDA certification.
The Committee feels that price of equipment with USFDA certification is quite high which
increases the procurement cost. The Committee feels that the Institute should insist on
purchasing equipment with Generic CE/BIS standards so as to promote the “Make in India”
campaign. The Committee, thus, recommends to the Institute to go for equipment with CE/
BIS standards in future and recommends to the Ministry to issue strict guidelines for the
procurement of equipment with these standards. (Para 3.30)
The Committee was given to understand that the Institute procured equipment without
ascertaining the availability of space, manpower and infrastructure required for installation.
Due to non installation of medical equipment, patients were deprived of the benefits. Further,
operationalization of key diagnostic and in-patient facilities would also have been adversely
affected. The Committee also feels that delay in the installation of equipment may also

152
result in lapse of warranty/guarantee of the equipment procured. The Committee recommends
to the Institute to procure equipment only when there is adequate infrastructure and manpower
required for the functioning of the equipment, so that there is no scope for idling or non
installation of procured equipment. (Para 3.32)
STATUS OF FACULTY/NON FACULTY STRENGTH
The Committee observes that faculty position in AIIMS, Rishikesh has improved,
however the Committee strongly feels that the present strength of faculty is insufficient for
a premier tertiary care institute. This has adversely impacted the quality of medical services
and standards of education being imparted by the Institute. The Committee, thus, recommends
to the Institute to expedite the process of recruitment and take immediate steps to avoid
unnecessary administrative delays in faculty recruitment. (Para 3.34)
The Committee underlines the need to augment the Specialty and Super Specialty
workforce at the Institute. The Committee observes that in 16 Departments the posts for
Professor are lying vacant. Further, four Departments i.e. Physical Medicine and Rehabilitation,
Transfusion Medicine, Gastroenterology and Biochemistry are being handled by a single
faculty. The Committee would like the Institute to seriously introspect the issues of faculty
and staff shortage and take necessary actions on a priority basis. The Committee feels that
having an experienced workforce is absolutely necessary not only for providing best medical
services to the patients but also for enhancing the practical expertise of the resident
doctors. The Committee is of the considered view that adequate incentives should be
offered at Super Specialty level to attract faculty at senior level from premier institutes
like AIIMS, Delhi/JIPMER/PGI etc. for strengthening the healthcare services in the
Institute. (Para 3.37)
The Committee also notes that while on the one hand the Institute has announced its
plans for expansion, it has not been able to recruit even the sanctioned strength. The
Committee strongly recommends that additional posts beyond the initially projected posts
should be contemplated only when the existing sanctioned faculty is recruited to the extent
of almost 100% and there is optimum utilization of the existing infrastructure Any addition
in faculty/Non faculty posts should be done only after ensuring that there is substantial
enhancement in patient load. (Para 3.38)
The Committee during its study visit to AIIMS, Rishikesh observes that the Institute
has created a serious regional imbalance in the State of Uttarakhand by taking faculty from
adjoining medical colleges, creating a deficit/vacuum in that area. The idea behind setting
up of AIIMS in these areas was to create additional capacity and bring the AIIMS, Delhi
like facilities and services at the tertiary level health care centers in these areas. However,
it is distressing to note that the Institute has created a serious deficit of medical professional/
faculty in the entire State of Uttarakhand. The Committee strongly recommends that recruitment
from adjoining medical colleges in the same State should be done only after obtaining NOC
from their parent Department. The Committee also recommends that efforts should be
made to recruit faculty from AIIMS, Delhi so as to create AIIMS like culture in the
regional AIIMS. The Committee is distressed to note that there are hardly any Head of
Departments/Professors from premier Institutes like AIIMS Delhi/JIPMER/PGI etc. in the

153
Institute. Almost 25% of the faculty has been taken with teaching experience in private
Institutions and not from any premier Institute. The Committee would also like to have
detailed information with respect to applicants with adequate teaching experience from large
tertiary premier Government Institutes/hospitals who have applied for faculty posts till date
in AIIMS Rishikesh and the reasons for their being not found suitable. (Para 3.39)
The Committee also recommends that faculty from AIIMS, Delhi or equivalent Institute
be encouraged to impart teaching at AIIMS, Rishikesh as visiting/ guest faculty on a
regular basis. (Para 3.40)
The Committee takes serious note of the discrepancies in the information submitted
by AIIMS, Rishikesh with reference to its queries. As an illustration, the sanctioned faculty
strength was quoted as 321 in the Power Point Presentation during the study tour of
Committee but later on, it was reduced to 305 in the information submitted by the Institute.
While the sanctioned strength for Group A non-faculty posts was stated as 782 posts as on
5th November, 2018 but later it was reproduced as 924 posts on 6th December, 2018. The
Committee advises the Institute to exercise due caution and diligence in submitting information
to the Parliamentary Standing Committee in future. (Para 3.41)
MEDICAL EDUCATION
The Committee observes that there are new courses being offered at the Institute viz.
PhD, MSc Nursing, DM/MCh and there has been a increase in the number of seats in
existing courses as also the number of courses being offered at the Institute.  The Committee
recommends that enhancement of the PG seats at the Institute should be done only after
ensuring that AIIMS, Rishikesh meets at least the minimum benchmarks as per MCI
norms applicable to other medical college throughout the country. The Committee does not
accept the argument of the Institute that it is governed by the AIIMS Act and hence the
minimum bench mark as per MCI norms are not applicable to it. This argument puts a
question mark over the quality of learning and clinical experience given to graduates passing
out of regional AIIMS. The Committee is of considered opinion that graduates and post
graduate passing out of AIIMS, Rishikesh should be equal to other medical graduates
passing out of premier Institutes of the country. The Committee is of the considered
opinion that autonomy as per AIIMS Act was given only with respect to AIIMS, Delhi which
has always exceeded the MCI benchmarks in every single aspect. However, the Committee
is extremely concerned that the reference to the AIIMS Act and consequent autonomy
available to regional AIIMS by virtue of this Act is being audaciously misused to dilute the
medical education standards in a very blatant manner with the justification that the Institute
meets US/UK standards. The Committee, therefore, recommends to the Ministry to ensure
that autonomy must not be used to dilute educational standards under any circumstances.
The Committee is distressed at the medical education standards of the new AIIMS and is
of the opinion that vacant PG seats in these Institutes is a reflection of lower standards of
medical education. (Para 3.46)
INTENSIVE CARE UNITS (ICUs)
The Committee observes that there are only 47 beds, with 18 ventilators and 31

154
monitors in ICUs in seven Departments, thereby indicating that the number of monitors
and ventilators are not commensurate with the number of beds in ICUs if there is 100%
occupancy at any given point in time. Such inadequate critical care facilities at the Institute
that is a tertiary level care institute indicates a dismal state of affairs with respect to
intensive care facilities for critical patients. The Committee apprehends that the Institute
is not equipped, adequately, to deal with complicated cases that require hospitalization and
critical care. The Committee therefore, is of the considered view that more intensive care
units must be set up in various departments to have distinct and specialized medical care
facilities.  The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends that the Institute should expand
the scope of critical care services to best possible extent with adequate infrastructure and
equipment. It is also a matter of grave concern to the Committee that the Institute has
started a fellowship programme in ICU/Critical care without a properly functional ICU. The
Committee is of the considered view that fellowship programmes are run in Centers of
Excellence once they are well established so that the standards of these programmes are not
lowered. (Para 3.49)
OPERATION THEATERS (OTS) AND TRAUMA AND EMERGENCY CENTER
The Committee takes cognizance of the defective design project of the modular OTs
that had many flaws although the same have been re-designed but not without time and
cost overruns. The Committee wants to highlight the shortsightedness of the Institute in
approving the design and floor plan of AIIMS, Rishikesh and therefore, desires that the
responsibility must be fixed for defective design of modular OTs in the hospital set up. The
Committee also notes the steps being taken by the Institute in accelerating the construction
of OTs. The Committee in this regard recommends that the Institute should ensure timely
completion of the construction of OT complex, recruitment of specialists and the procurement
of equipment, simultaneously, so as to have a ready to use functional OT units within the
stipulated timeframe. The Committee strongly recommends for a cohesive, well planned
time bound action plan to rectify the designing faults in AIIMS Rishikesh. (Para 3.52)
The Committee has not been able to assess the number of surgical procedures carried
out in AIIMS Rishikesh due to variation in the data furnished by the Institute and the
Ministry. The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends that precise data with respect to
major and minor surgeries done (patient wise and Department wise) till date since inception
of the Institute may be submitted to the Committee, within three months of presentation
of this Report. (Para 3.53)
The Committee is dismayed at the sorry state of affairs with regard to patients getting
surgical procedures done after long waiting time. The Committee observes that at present
only 13 operation theaters are functional at AIIMS, Rishikesh due to which there is a long
waiting period for the surgical procedures to be done at the Institute. The Committee
observes that in Urology, Neurosurgery, and Burns and Plastic Departments, there is two
months waiting time and in general surgery, there is 45 days waiting time for the procedures
to be done besides six months for ENT. The Committee feels that such instances of long
waiting time due to non-availability of operation theaters defies the very purpose of setting
up of AIIMS like institution as patients have to face hardships. The Committee finds the

155
reasons for long waiting time at AIIMS, Rishikesh are inadequate infrastructure and constraints
even after six years of its operationalization. The Committee recommends for optimum
utilization of the existing OT infrastructure. The Committee also recommends for a comparative
analysis with AIIMS Delhi regarding the number of major/minor surgeries per OT per day.
Despite these lacunae the Institute is aiming for setting up of advanced centres at exorbitant
costs without getting the basics right. The Committee recommends that optimum functional
status of the originally conceived infrastructure be achieved and resources be utilized for
achieving this objective instead of just focusing on rapid expansion. (Para 3.56)
The Committee notes that AIIMS, Rishikesh is not prepared to manage  huge number
of casualties in case of any disaster due to non-establishment of trauma center. The Committee,
therefore, recommends that AIIMS, Rishikesh management must expedite the construction
work for establishment of Trauma center for treatment of casualties in case of disaster, as
Uttarakhand is prone to disasters. (Para 3.57)
BUDGETARY ALLOCATION
The Committee is constrained to express its concern that total utilization budgetary
allocation in respect of AIIMS, Jodhpur picked up very slowly. The Committee wonders
that the percentage utilization was nil in the year 2011-12, 39.48% in 2012-13, 47.64% in
2013-14, 61.56% in 2014-15, 32.53% in 2015-16, 50.63% in 2016-17, 74.16% in 2017-18 and
70.09% in 2018-19 (up to 15.10.2018). The Committee berates that Jodhpur management
failed to monitor over the implementing agencies during the course of execution of construction
projects resulting into under-utilization of funds in the initial years for Grants-in-Aids
(capital). The Committee laments that the trend of expenditure witnessed an increase only
after the year 2016-17 that speaks of limited absorption capacity of implementation agencies.
(Para 4.4)
The Committee was given to understand by the Ministry/Institute that the funds received
were sufficient to meet the construction activities and on-going development projects at the
Institute. The Committee believes that the problematic aspects relate to timely execution
of the projects. The Committee, therefore, arrives at the conclusion that had the implementing
agencies executed all segments of the projects in time, with required absorption capacity,
there would have been optimum utilization of allocated funds and lesser delays. The Committee
is of the considered view that had the Institute and the Ministry undertaken proper planning
and periodic monitoring of the project work there would have been optimal and judicious
utilization of resources. The Committee, therefore, recommends to the Ministry to institute
an enquiry to assess the actual reasons for under-utilization of allocated funds for execution
of construction projects and accordingly apprise the Committee of the action taken against
the implementing agencies as per terms and conditions of the contract. (Para 4.5)
The Committee is not convinced with the reasons given for underutilization of funds
as these routine activities are often faced in the functioning of any institution. The delay
in the completion of projects have an adverse impact on the health care services to be
provided by the Institute.  The Committee expects the Department to uphold the principles
of fiscal discipline and sound financial management by judiciously utilizing the remaining

156
funds. Needless to say, that only proper planning, co-ordination with implementing agencies
and suitable monitoring mechanism could have ensured optimum utilization of funds.
(Para 4.8)
The Committee opines that diversion of funds is a result of lack of effective financial
control and monitoring of actual utilization. Such diversion amounts to misappropriation of
allocated fund for the intended purpose. The Committee, thus, recommends framing of
proper guidelines to control such diversion. The Committee, in this connection, strongly
recommends for strengthening of financial management, effective monitoring mechanism
and adoption of proper accounting procedure for an effective internal financial control.
(Para 4.10)
STATUS OF CONSTRUCTION WORK
The Committee expresses its anguish to observe faulty drawings in the construction
project and subsequent shifting of OT from 2nd floor to 6th floor in AIIMS, Jodhpur as the
same constituted the reasons for delay in the completion of construction projects. The
Committee wonders that the faulty design in the original drawing was not taken seriously
at the initial stage. The Committee, therefore, recommends that accountability for such
faulty design should be fixed and a detailed report on the reasons for the delay in the
operationalization of the OTs be submitted to the Committee for its consideration.  The
Committee also recommends that the remaining work of package II be completed within
the specified deadline. The Ministry in tandem with institute management must oversee and
ensure timely execution of all projects. (Para 4.13)
The Committee would like to draw the attention of the Institute to the recent observation
by the CAG in context of AIIMS Jodhpur, where it has mentioned about price escalation in
violation of contract, repeated grant of time extension, release of payment despite poor
workmanship, excess release of mobilization advance and avoidable/extra contractual payments.
The Committee is also of the view that replies of the Ministry are not at all acceptable and
the difference between the original and the final cost cannot be considered beyond the
purview of cost escalation. From the information provided by the Ministry, it is evident that
there has been cost overrun of ````` 9.00 crore under package I and ````` 18.00 crore under
package II. Even if the cost of construction of these projects did not increase during the
staggered period of six years, it implies that cost evaluation/calculation was inflated to such
a great extent in the initial stage itself. The Committee wonders as to how the original cost-
estimation of the projects was done. The Committee, therefore, seeks reasons/explanation
over the cost-estimation of the projects in all the four packages. (Para 4.15)
The Committee, further, observes that under package III and package IV , there has
been time overrun of approximately three years. The Committee feels that the delayed
execution of the projects in turn, has severely hampered the healthcare services to the
patients during this period.  Such delays have impaired the capacity of the Institute to
provide the envisaged levels of treatment, health care and medical education. The Committee
desires the Ministry to assess the genuine reasons for delay in the execution of the
projects under construction and apprise the Committee in this regard. The Ministry  must

157
conduct detailed site investigation, hold regular meetings and formulate effective monitoring
mechanism to ensure that the project is managed efficiently after meticulous scheduling
of work. (Para 4.17)
The Committee calls upon the Ministry to expedite the process of granting necessary
approvals for the construction of proposed accommodation facilities. The Ministry may approve
the Advanced Centers for Transplant, Oncology, Cardiac Sciences, Neuro Sciences and
Ophthalmology, Department of Biostatics, Department of Bio-technology only after optimum
utilization of existing infrastructure and ensuring availability of adequate human resources.
The Committee is of the view that the establishment of advance centers should only be
considered when the Institute achieves at least 50% of the OPD footfall of AIIMS Delhi
subject to approval by CIB. However, the Committee would like to emphasize to undertake
expansion of School of Public Health and a Dharmshala for the relatives of the patients
admitted at AIIMS, Jodhpur. The Committee also recommends the Ministry to ensure completion
and optimum utilization of the originally envisaged projects in a time bound manner before
approving any new project. (Para 4.19)
PROCUREMENT OF MEDICAL EQUIPMENT
The Committee appreciates the role played by the Assessment Committee in procurement
of medical equipment. However, the Committee is constrained to note that no separate
analysis has been done for the procurement of equipment, thus indicating that the equipment
have been purchased without analyzing the cost of the equipment vis-à-vis  its actual utility.
The Committee is of the view that procurement of equipment without proper cost-benefit
analysis may result in under utilization/non-utilization/ duplication of equipment and procurement
of equipment at higher prices. The Committee, therefore, recommends to the Institute/
Ministry that proper cost-benefit analysis must be done by the Assessment Committee
before the procurement of the equipment so that the utility of the equipment is explicit
commensurating to its cost leading of effective delivery to health services.  This analysis
should form the part of the procurement process. The Committee also reiterates its observation
made in context of AIIMS, Rishikesh (para 3.26) for the procurement of da vinci robotic
arm. The Committee also recommends that the Assessment Committee must go into the
details of reasons for proprietary/ single bid purchases and submit a status Report to the
Committee along with a quarterly update on the usage of da-vinci robotic arm.
(Para 4.22)
The Committee is anguished to observe the delay in installation of equipment on the
account of procrastination in the construction of new OTs. This is a peculiar situation when
equipment has been purchased without ascertaining a place for its installation. The Committee
believes that delay in installation of equipment may lead to expiry of guarantee/warranty of
the equipment, service period. The Committee, thus, recommends that equipment should be
procured only when space for their installation is ready so as to avoid undue delay in the
installation and allay the fear of expiry of guarantee/warrantee of the equipment. Not just
this, equipment should not be allowed to remain idle/unutilized. The Committee, further,
recommends that the Ministry should expedite the process of construction of new OTs immediately
so that the installation of procured equipment is ensured at the earliest. (Para 4.24)

158
The Committee expresses its concern that 32 types of equipment were procured on a
single bid basis at AIIMS Jodhpur. Such situation leads to minimizing the options for
procurement of equipment for the Institute. In case of single bid, the cost of equipment is
likely to be higher than multiple bids. The Committee is of the view that multiple bids is
the only way for procurement of economical and best equipment for the Institute. The
Committee cautions that the Institute has not followed the General Financial rules for the
procurement of equipment. As per General Financial Rules, procurement from a single
source may be resorted to only when there is a single manufacturer, in case of emergency
and for standardization of machinery or spare parts to be compatible to the existing sets
of equipment.  In the event where more than one bid is received, it means that there is
more than one supplier of the required equipment. The Committee, thus, recommends to
the Ministry to comply with the GFRs issued by the Government of India and go for
procurement of equipment on multiple bid bases so as to achieve competitive advantage.
The Committee recommends for a probe and analysis for all cases of single bid/ proprietary
certificate procurements and a status report be submitted to the Committee within three
months of the presentation of the Report. (Para 4.26)
The Committee understands that AIIMS, Jodhpur is meant for the needy and poor
patients requiring tertiary health care facilities where quality of equipment can be lifesaving
for the patients. The Committee, however, observes that since basic equipment like OT
Light System, Laparoscopic Set, Suction Machine, Multipara Monitor and many others are
easily available with European and also with Indian manufacturers. The procurement of
these medical equipment with USFDA certification should have been resorted to only in the
rarest of circumstances, after ensuring, in writing, that no alternative with CE/BIS certification
is available. The Committee desires to be apprised of the reasons for procurement of the
above mentioned devices with USFDA certification when equivalent quality and lower price
CE/BIS certified equipment were available. (Para 4.29)
The Committee is of the considered view that procurement of equipment with BIS
