certification will encourage companies to manufacture their products in India and also
increase their investment. This will also contribute to “Make in India”, programme of the
Government of India to promote domestic manufacturers. The Committee, therefore, recommends
to the Ministry to issue strict directions to all AIIMS like institutions to procure equipment
with wider certification standards so as to promote competitive pricing and also to encourage
“Make in India” scheme. (Para 4.30)
STATUS OF FACULTY AND NON-FACULTY STRENGTH
The Committee is constrained to observe that out of 305 sanctioned faculty posts, 160
posts are lying vacant. Again, 2059 posts are vacant in non faculty staff that amounts to
approximately 55 percent of the total sanctioned non faculty posts. The Committee finds the
vacancy level at Professor/Additional professor level quite alarming. The Committee is of
the view that deficient number of teaching faculty at the Institute adds woes to basic health
infrastructure at the Institute besides diluting the standard of medical education. Such a
huge number of vacancies of the faculty/staff would definitely have an adverse impact on
the functioning of the Institute. Vacancy of one category of health personnel is restricted

159
not only to a particular Department but also affects the overall functioning of a healthcare
Institute. The Committee, thus, recommends to the Ministry to make all out efforts to
expedite the process of filling up of the posts in a time bound manner in order to impart
the medical education at the desired level and to ensure that tertiary level health care
services are provided to the patients. The Committee also recommends to the Ministry to
offer better pay structure and incentives to attract Specialist/Super Specialist doctors from
premier Institutes like AIIMS/JIPMER/PGI, etc.  to join the Institute. The Committee is
constrained to observe almost negligible presence of faculty from these similar premier
Government Institutes and would also like to be apprised of the number of applicants who
had applied from these Medical Institutes but were not found suitable, along with the
reasons for rejection. (Para 4.35)
SUPER SPECIALTY SERVICES
The Committee is displeased to note that three Departments i.e. Medical
Gastroenterology, Medical Oncology and Nuclear Medicine are not functional at AIIMS,
Jodhpur. These Departments are important branches of medicine and are indispensable
part of any Institute providing health care services to the people. The Committee would like
the Institute to make these Departments functional without much procrastination.
(Para 4.38)
The Committee desires the Ministry to submit a status note on the cost-benefit analysis
for establishment of Advanced Cardiac Center, Neuroscience Centre, Advanced Eye Centre,
Centre for Oncology, Reserved block and Organ Transplant Service Centre. The Committee,
in this connection, recommends that Advanced Centres be established only after AIIMS,
Jodhpur achieves the desired level of faculty and manpower needed to run these Super-
Specialties Departments and attains the daily OPD of at least 50% of AIIMS, Delhi. The
Committee also recommends that all the decisions regarding setting up of advance centers
should be referred to the CIB. (Para 4.39)
The Committee observes that out of 285 sanctioned posts in 37 functional Super
Specialties Departments at the AIIMS Jodhpur, only 142 Seats have been filled up which
constitutes only 50 per cent of the sanctioned seats. The Committee is appalled to note that
the Departments of Cardiology, Cardiothoracic Surgery, Endocrinology and Metabolism, General
Medicine, Nephrology, Neurology, Surgical Oncology and Radio Therapy are functioning with
single person manning these Departments.  Further, in the Neonatology Department, there
are only two faculty and one of them is working on contractual basis. These Departments
cater to cases of heart, kidney, and neurological disease. The Committee is of the view that
Super Specialty Departments offer specialized diagnosis and treatment protocol dealing with
a particular disease as it is not possible for a general physician to deal with patients
suffering from all kinds of diseases. The gap in the actual position and the sanctioned
strength of Super Specialists will adversely affect the working efficiency of the Institute as
patients would be denied the services of the institute due to lack of Specialty/Super Specialty
Departments. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Institute must fill up the
vacant faculty posts at the Professor/HOD level so as to provide best services to the patients
visiting the Institute. (Para 4.41)

160
MEDICAL EDUCATION
The Committee notes that in AIIMS, Jodhpur there has been 100% admissions in
MBBS and nursing courses. Meanwhile, the number of seats in PG course has increased
from 56 to 114 in the year 2018.  However, out of 114 seats, only 84 seats have been filled
up. The Ministry has not furnished reasons to the Committee as to why the remaining PG
course seats could not be filled up. The Committee would like the Ministry to furnish a
note apprising reasons for not filling up of PG course seats along with steps taken to
address faculty shortage and other benchmarks required for a Medical college of AIIMS
standard. (Para 4.44)
The Committee apprehends that inadequate faculty as well as limited clinical training
could be one of the reasons for PG seats not being filled up in the year 2018. The Committee
believes that exposure of students to proper clinical training would help enriching their
skills and experience in medical education. The Committee, therefore, expects the Institute
to improve the level of its faculty as well as clinical standards and ensure that from next
session, henceforth, that all the PG seats are filled up. The Committee also recommends to
the Institute to start the newly approved PG courses after assessing availability of adequate
faculty and requisite patient load.  The Committee has been apprised of a abysmally low
level of clinical exposure to PG students in AIIMS, Jodhpur specifically MD (Anesthesiology).
The Committee recommends that a quarterly status note be submitted to the Committee on
the clinical exposure of PG Students (discipline-wise) so as to ensure that educational standards
are not diluted. (Para 4.46)
The Committee opines that an effective and relevant training module expands the
capacity building of the medical and nursing students and it therefore, plays an important
role in their career development. Clinical trainings help the healthcare professionals to
better understand the subject and prepare them for the challenges they will face in their
career. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Institute must start clinical training
programmes for the students and ensure that there is adequate faculty at the Institute to
support these programmes. (Para 4.47)
STATUS OF OPERATION THEATRES
The Committee is dismayed to note that 3 major and 2 minor OTs in Trauma OT
complex do not have medical gas pipe line at present and the supply of gas is ensured by
using Cylinders. The Committee is of the view that MGPS is a life-supporting element
crucial to life of a patient. The Committee, therefore, recommends to the Ministry to
ensure that all the OTs are equipped with MGPS and make them fully equipped with the
latest equipment without any delay especially in trauma complex so as to cater to the
critical patients. (Para 4.49)
The Committee is surprised at the long waiting period for basic diagnostic tests and
demands the institute to submit the waiting period for all the diagnostic tests in AIIMS
Jodhpur specifically MRI. The Committee is saddened to note that despite limited functional
OPD to AIIMS standards there is a waiting time for surgeries and even ENT procedure. If
AIIMS, Jodhpur has to enhance its perception, there has to be minimal waiting time for
surgeries and other procedures. (Para 4.51)

161
The Committee during the course of its study visit to Jodhpur found that not a single
modular OT was functioning. The 9 major OTs and 3 Minor OTs were make shift OTs in
the OPD blocks and not were in consonance with the original drawings submitted by the
DDPRC which had been duly approved. These OT designs were arbitrarily changed on the
pretext that there were designing faults. The Committee has been further apprised that
there was no provision for AHU in the original drawing. The Committee is constrained to
observe that the matter was not taken up with the DDPRC and nor was it penalized or its
contract terminated. The Committee would like to be apprised of the action taken by the
Ministry against the DDPRC and also whether the Ministry had provided for the provision
of Modular OTs in the initial design itself. The Committee is of the opinion that this
negligence has led to severe delays with great financial implications in the form of arbitration
claims submitted by the contractor L&T in this case besides creating structural stability
concerns. The Committee strongly recommends for a thorough probe in the entire matter
including fixing responsibility. The Committee would also like to be apprised whether this
has resulted in any cost/time overruns besides creating grounds for arbitration and submit
the same to the Committee within three months of submission of this report.
(Para 4.52)
The Committee would also like to be apprised of the reasons for time overrun of the
project from the originally planned two years to almost eight years and whether this has
lead to the reported `````150 crore arbitration claims by L&T. The Committee desires that a
report on the arbitration status along with the position taken by the contractor and the
Ministry may also be provided so that the matter can be expeditiously resolved without any
loss to the exchequer. (Para 4.53)
The Committee also recommends AIIMS Jodhpur to expedite the construction of OTs
in a time bound manner and reduce the waiting time period for patients awaiting surgical
procedures thereby reducing their distress and out of pocket expenditure.  The Committee
would also like the Institute to submit a quarterly report on the number of procedures done
per OT per day in order to gauge the optimum utilization of the functional OTs.
(Para 4.54)
The Committee has not been able to assess the number of surgical procedures carried
out in AIIMS Jodhpur due to the variation in the data furnished by the Institute and the
Ministry. The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends that precise data with respect to
major and minor surgeries done (patient wise and Department wise) till date since inception
of the Institute may be submitted to the Committee, within three months of presentation
of this Report. (Para 4.55)
STATUS OF INTENSIVE CARE UNITS (ICUS), TRAUMA AND EMERGENCY SERVICES AND
WARDS
The Committee observes that availability of 17 ventilators against 19 beds would not
cater in case of 100 percent occupancy at any given time. Even 100 percent bed occupancy
with mere 19 beds in the ICUs in normal circumstances would not cater to emergency
situations because all units would be fully occupied. The Committee recommends that the
number of bed in ICU be increased to accommodate all critically ill patients visiting the

162
hospital. The Committee also notes that emergency services have not yet started on 24x7x365
days basis and only referred cases are being taken inspite of the fact that the trauma
building was completed in 2014-15. The Committee however appreciates the integrated approach
of the Institute to have a synergy with the Indian army so as to cater to emergency
situations and recommends the institute to start 24 X 7 X 365 days trauma/emergency care.
(Para 4.59)
The Committee finds the figure of 684 functional beds, 70 per cent of the total
sanctioned bed strength (960) in 20 different wards functional at the Institute. The lower
number of functional beds and even further lower level of bed occupancy reflects poor
perception of healthcare services being provided by the Institute. The Committee is concerned
that existing beds are insufficient to cater to the growing health needs of the people. Such
inadequacies have forced people across socio-economic strata to unaffordable private healthcare
facilities. The Institute needs to urgently increase the bed occupancy by improving its
perception to address the health services for the people of the region. (Para 4.61)
BURNS AND PLASTIC SURGERY DEPARTMENT
The Committee is of the view that given the high incidences of burn injuries in the
State of Rajasthan and adjoining areas, a separate Burn Unit at the Institute is the need
of the hour. This will ensure that timely and adequate treatment to burn patients in terms
of reducing their mortality, complications and rehabilitation. The Committee, thus, strongly
recommends that the Institute must start a separate Burns Unit in IPD with sufficient
manpower and necessary equipment to improve the care and facilities for such patients at
the earliest. The Committee, in this connection, recommends that ambulance services equipped
with advanced features for transportation of patient from accident site to the burns unit of
the Institute should be made available. (Para 4.63)
AYUSH FACILITIES
The Committee observes that only Ayurveda and Homoeopathic OPD services are functional
at the AIIMS, Jodhpur.  The Committee considers that there is a need to integrate the
Ayurveda, Y oga, Naturopathy, Unani, Siddha, and Homeopathy (AYUSH) system of indigenous
medicine to meet the shortage of health care professionals and strengthen the delivery
system of the health care service through an alternate system of Indian medicine. The
Committee, therefore, recommends to the Ministry to start other AYUSH services at the
Institute. The Committee also recommends to the Ministry to ensure the accessibility and
availability and choice of AYUSH services to all patients depending in these specific needs.
The Committee recommends to the institute to chalk out strategy for strengthening AYUSH
services and to support this system with adequate infrastructure and manpower in a time
bound manner. (Para 4.66)
The Committee is constrained to observe that out of seven sanctioned posts for AYUSH
faculty, only two faculties are presently working at AIIMS, Jodhpur and that too on contract
basis. The Committee observes that posts of Senior Medical Officer AYUSH and Y oga instructor
are lying vacant. The Committee, thus, recommends the Ministry to fill up the posts for
AYUSH doctors on regular  basis at the earliest. (Para 4.67)

163
GOVERNING BODY
The Committee during its deliberations and visit to AIIMS, Jodhpur found that the
meetings of the Governing Body at the Institute were not being held on a regular basis.
The Members of the Governing Body are not consulted while taking decisions during the
meeting and all the decisions are taken by few incumbents. The Committee is of the firm
view that the meetings of the Governing Body at the Institute should be conducted on time
and participation of all members of the body should be ensured while taking important
decisions.  All AIIMS like institutions should strive to conduct regular meetings and participation
of all members be encouraged while taking important decisions of the Institute. The Committee,
accordingly, recommends that AIIMS, Jodhpur must give due importance to all the Members
of the Governing Body while taking any important decision to make it democratically
decentralized.  All out efforts must be made for strengthening of Governing Body and other
Committees for better functioning of AIIMS, Jodhpur. (Para 4.68)
The Committee is of the considered opinion that autonomy and accountability need to
be balanced. The Committee welcomes the appointment of the Presidents of the Institute
and the constitution of the CIB at the Central level. This will ensure a sense of responsibility
in the decision making process. (Para 4.69)
BUDGETARY ALLOCATION
The Committee expresses its deep concern that the percentage of underutilisation
with respect to total Grants-in-aid of AIIMS, Patna has been 64.72% in 2012-13, 37.98%
in 2013-14, 47.15% in 2014-15, 32.33% in 2015-16, 42.86% in 2016-17 and 62.61% in 2017-18.
The closing balance was as high as ````` 195.95 crore for the year 2015-16 and 208.70 crore for
the year 2016-17 that express low absorption capacity of implementing agencies. The Committee
also observes that in the year 2014-15, the percentage of utilization under GiA (capital) was
18.73% which further decreased to 12.93% in 2015-16. In 2016-17, the opening balance
under capital grants in aids was 140.73 crore implying that out of the total 161.63 crore
allotted under GiA (Capital) only ````` 20.90 crore were spent in capital formation in 2015-16.
All these date speak volume of poor financial planning estimation and control execution of
construction scheme. (Para 5.4)
The Committee laments the fact that AIIMS Patna that is envisaged as an apex
institute of medical learning and affordable tertiary health care has failed miserably in
optimum utilization of its budget. The budgetary expenditure pattern of AIIMS Patna exposes
the lackadaisical attitude of the Institute and brings to the foreground the Institute’s
incompetency to use the financial resources to its fullest potential. This gross underutilisation
of resources is a grave matter of concern especially when AIIMS Patna lags behind on many
fronts and struggles to provide quality healthcare to the citizens. (Para 5.5)
The Committee taking cognizance of CAG’s view on unutilized funds by AIIMS, Patna
holds the Ministry equally responsible for the utter idling of funds and increase in capital
costs. The Committee, in this connection, recommends that the Ministry should immediately
develop a mechanism for monitoring the budget estimates and ensure utilization of funds
by AIIMS Patna and other new AIIMS so that obstacles and hindrances, if any, are identified

164
at an early stage and the cost and time overrun are avoided, while implementing the project
under PMSSY. (Para 5.7)
STATUS OF CONSTRUCTION WORK
The Committee deprecates the slow pace of construction activities at AIIMS Patna. As
per the information given by AIIMS Patna, the construction of hospital complex under
Package II was originally deemed to be completed by 10th September, 2012 but even after
6 years, the project is incomplete. Under package III and IV , the work of electrical services
and Estate services were to be completed by December, 2012 and February, 2013 respectively
but it is yet to be completed.  The Committee wills to attribute the delay in execution of
construction projects to mismanagement on the part of AIIMS, Patna. (Para 5.9)
It is a proven fact that delay in any construction activity leads to unnecessary additional
costs and time extensions. Inflation, abrupt ending of contracts, arbitration cases, disputes
etc. further increases the cost of any delayed project. Systemic analysis at the inception of
any project and regular monitoring can easily control all these factors and minimize the
chances of delay. Sadly, the Ministry and the Institute remained ignorant of both time and
cost deadlines for the completion of the project as envisaged for AIIMS Patna.
(Para 5.10)
The Committee observes with anguish that AIIMS Patna has failed to realize the
implications of this sluggish rate of construction activities and is of the view that the
reasons cited by AIIMS Patna for the construction delays are inexcusable and could have
been easily avoided had the Institute been alert and committed for the Project. Moreover,
the argument of AIIMS Patna that professional expertise in constructing hospital building
is missing in the country is not tenable and bereft of fact and logic. (Para 5.12)
The Committee is dismayed to note that no project management tool/strategy has been
employed for construction of an AIIMS like institution that is intended to be the beacon of
light for the tertiary healthcare sector in the State. A PERT or Program Evaluation Review
Technique Chart is one such project management tool that helps in better analysis of the
resources and the timeline of any project.  In the absence of any evaluation technique, the
Committee is really skeptical about the completion of the construction work under the
remaining packages. The Committee recommends that a special project monitoring system
for AIIMS Patna must be devised to track the progress of construction activities undertaken
by the Institute so as to ensure better project management. The Committee strongly recommends
that the work under Package II, III and IV must be completed on time with no further
delays. (Para 5.13)
The Committee is at pains to take into account the improper estimation of quantities
of items pointed out by CAG in its report. Surprisingly, the deviation is upto 173% in case
of AIIMS Patna which clearly indicates that the requirement of different items had been under-
assessed and unrealistic assessment of the site conditions were made. The green building norms
and the Energy Conservation Building Code (ECBC) should have been implemented before the
beginning of the construction activities. The Committee is highly distressed at the inadequate
technical estimation made before the construction of AIIMS Patna. (Para 5.16)

165
The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry should make a detailed
technical and realistic assessment before setting up of other new AIIMS under PMSSY by
carrying out proper field and site surveys and the errors while constructing AIIMS Patna
should not be repeated. (Para 5.17)
EXPANSION PROGRAMME OF AIIMS PATNA
The Committee while scrutinizing the expenditure pattern of funds in AIIMS Patna
finds that between 2013 and 2018, the Institute had unspent balance under Grants-in-Aid
Capital Head. The Committee understands that construction activities require substantial
amount of funds and approval in advance, but at the same time the Committee cannot
ignore the poor track record of the Institute wherein inordinate delays have caused huge
time and cost overrun under different packages of construction activities.  The Committee
is of the firm view that the demand for funds should be realistic and should also commensurate
with the trend of utilization. The Committee, therefore, recommends that the Ministry of
Health and Family Welfare must evaluate the demand of AIIMS Patna on realistic grounds
and forward the same to the Ministry of Finance for consideration. (Para 5.20)
The Committee understands that land is critical for the expansion programme of the
institute. Therefore, the Committee urges upon the Ministry to take up the matter with the
State Government to provide the required amount of land to the Institute. The Committee
while underlining the need for allocation of required funds to AIIMS Patna for infrastructure
construction also advises the Ministry to hold regular review meetings to monitor the new
construction projects so that the mistakes committed in the first phase of construction
activities are not repeated at all costs. The Ministry should also carry out regular inspections
and seek monthly report from the Institute on the status and progress of the projects.
(Para 5.21)
PROCUREMENT OF MEDICAL EQUIPMENT
The Committee is dismayed to note that 16 items of equipment (8.33%) costing ````` 56
crore remain undelivered while 34 items of equipment (19.54%) costing ````` 34 crore have
been delivered but not installed. Consignee Receipt Certificate (CRC) has not been issued
for 20 items of equipment (11.49%) costing ````` 3 crore. The Committee is shocked over the
state of affairs in this institute of National importance where such grave underutilization
of resources has become a common phenomenon. The Committee cannot fathom that how
can the Institute turn a blind eye to the number of medical equipment that are lying
uninstalled in the hospitals for such long periods. (Para 5.27)
The Committee is not at all convinced by the reasons given by AIIMS Patna for the
delayed supplies and non-installation of the medical equipment. Delays due to the non-
availability of space or complete power etc. could have been easily addressed by the Institute.
The Committee believes that the institute had plenty of time at its disposal to obtain site
approvals and the site should have been prepared well in advance. This undue delay in
installation also highlights the failure of the Procurement Support Agency in ensuring
timely supply and installation of the medical equipment in the Institute. The Committee is

166
also highly concerned regarding the role of the Procurement Support Agency that should
have analyzed the area and power requirement for the medical equipment and communicated
the same to the Institute. The Committee holds the Institute and the Procurement Support
Agency responsible for the delay in operationalization of these equipment and also warns
that this lax attitude of the institute has proved to be the biggest roadblock in its path to
establish itself as a centre of excellence in medical education. (Para 5.28)
The Committee, therefore strongly recommends that the institute should be proactive
in taking suo moto  cognizance of the delay in delivery of the equipment and ensure that
medical equipment are delivered on time. The Institute should also make sure that unnecessary
delay in installation of equipment does not take place due to shortage of skilled manpower
or lack in infrastructure. The Committee also recommends that all the medical equipment
should be accepted only after a technical inspection by a technical Inspector and should
receive routine maintenance and calibration. The Institute should also have a contingency
plan in case of break-down of equipment so that the diagnostic services are not adversely
effected. (Para 5.29)
The Committee fails to understand that why even simple items such as museum jar
and LED TV were procured through single bid. The Committee notes that multiple bids were
received in these two cases but were rejected on the basis of technical specifications. The
Committee strongly feels that technical requirements should not be misused as a means to
limit the number of bids or minimize competition as is evident by the data submitted by
AIIMS Patna. The Committee recommends that the institute should clearly specify the
minimum technical and financial requirements ensuring an equal and fair chance to all the
bidders. (Para 5.33)
The Committee also observes that the price at which the equipment were procured
may not have been the best price as the Institute failed to hold a competitive procurement
process. The Committee also desires to be apprised of the number of bidders, the reasons
for the rejection of their proposal and the compliance proposal of the winning bidder in each
case for procurement of museum Jar, LED TV, Co2 Laser, Carl Zesis OPMI Vario and
Computerised Virtual Dissection Table. The Committee desires AIIMS Patna to submit a
written technical evaluation summary for each case of proprietary/single bid procurement
till date to it within three months from the presentation of this Report. The Committee also
recommends that the Director of the Institute should be made accountable for all cases of
single bid/proprietary procurement wherever irregularities are reported/observed in future.
(Para 5.34)
AIIMS Patna also submitted that US FDA and CE certification was put in the specification
due to unavailability of a similar Indian Model.  The Committee is shocked by the false
claims of AIIMS Patna that Indian model for basic instruments such as hand drill, mouth
gags, rib, hooks, chisels etc. are not available in the market. The Committee is of the
opinion that items such as Navigation system, Surgical Helmet and Accessories, Surgical
consumable items and other instruments are easily available with the Indian manufacturers
but AIIMS Patna, by insisting on US FDA and CE certification even for such basic instruments,
has clearly shown contempt for the Indian manufacturing sector. (Para 5.35)

167
On one side where the Government is taking measures to reduce the country’s dependency
on imported medical devices, it is unfortunate to find an institute of national importance
openly following a mandatory US FDA certification policy for purchasing medical equipment.
The Committee is distressed to note that medical devices like defibrillator, ultrasound
machine, neonatal ventilator, surgical instruments etc. are being purchased with US FDA
certification even though there are many other manufacturers with CE certification. The
Committee strongly recommends to AIIMS Patna to follow a buy “Make in India” policy and
insist on Bureau of Indian Standard (BIS) certification. (Para 5.36)
The Committee is shocked at the audacity of the Institute by its claims to have
purchased beds with cushions, equipment for PMR Department, two battery operated hand
drill accessories etc. with US FDA certification. As per the information submitted by AIIMS
Patna, it procured 250 fowler beds with cushion at a price of ````` 1.1 crore with an extra
Comprehensive Maintenance Contract (CMC) price of ````` 24 lakhs, two battery operated hand
drill accessories at ````` 46 lakhs with CMC price of ````` 7.9 lakhs, equipment for PMR Department
at ````` 14 lakhs with CMC price of ````` 4.6 lakh. This superfluous spending by AIIMS Patna
raises serious concern over the lack of monitoring of the procurement Policy. The Committee
recommends for an enquiry into the procurement of fowler beds with cushions as these are
easily available with Indian manufacturers at a much lower price paid by AIIMS, Patna. The
Committee senses large irregularities in the procurement process of the Institute and seeks
a detailed probe of all the equipment that have been procured in the last two years. The
Committee desires AIIMS Patna to submit a detailed report on the same, within three
months of the presentation of this report to the Parliament.
(Para 5.37)
STATUS OF FACULTY/NON-FACULTY STRENGTH
The Committee notes that 122 posts have been filled up against the sanctioned strength
of 305 for Professors/Additional Professor/Associate Professor/Assistant Professor whereas
183 Posts are lying vacant in different Departments of AIIMS Patna. None of the posts have
been filled up in Trauma and Emergency, Nuclear Medicine, Nephrology, Hospital Administration
and Endocrinology and Metabolism Departments. The Committee observes with concern that
important Departments such as Cardiology, Medical Oncology and Haematology, Neonatology,
Neurology, Radiotherapy, Surgical Oncology, Urology are functioning with only one doctor in
AIIMS Patna. The Committee also notes with serious concern that AIIMS Patna has the
lowest faculty strength among all the new AIIMS established under Phase 1 of PMSSY. This
casts aspersion on the functioning of AIIMS, Patna with regard to Human Resource Management
as well. (Para 5.39)
The Committee is disturbed by the acute shortage of Professors/Additional Professor/
Associate Professor/Assistant Professor in AIIMS Patna and other AIIMS like Institutes.
The Committee during its study visit to AIIMS Patna also discussed the shortage of facility
with the Director and representatives of the Ministry. The Committee recommends that
AIIMS Patna should make immediate efforts to implement all the initiatives for recruitment
of faculty. The Committee notes that provision has been made to engage faculty from other

168
institutes and Government medical colleges on deputation but the Committee would also
like to emphasize that engaging faculty from the GMCs should not result in vacancies in
the nearby GMCs. (Para 5.41)
The Committee understands that special packages/incentives or higher salary may not
always be a driving factor to serve in this part of the country, therefore, the Institute has
to explore alternative means such as paid research collaboration, high end technology,
better working hours, security,  enough leisure time etc.  to attract qualified doctors to the
Institute. (Para 5.42)
Non-Faculty
The Committee understands that the shortage of doctors and specialist is a macro level
problem considering the poor doctor: patient ratio in the country but the Committee fails
to understand the reasons for huge number of vacancy in the non-faculty positions at
AIIMS Patna. The Committee strongly recommends that the Institute should expedite the
recruitment process for the non-faculty posts. (Para 5.44)
The Committee also feels that AIIMS Patna should not ignore the importance of
nurses in the healthcare system and recommends expediting the recruitment of 500 staff
nurses for which AIIMS Patna has already issued advertisement. The institute should also
speed up its efforts to start different medical and para-medical courses to fill the existing
gaps in their medical services. (Para 5.45)
The Committee feels that this huge shortage against faculty and non-faculty posts in
AIIMS Patna has an adverse effect on the functioning of many important Departments in
the Institute impacting the overall quality of medical service provided to the patients.
Therefore, the Committee recommends a common centralised recruitment policy for filling
up the vacancies in all the AIIMS. This will not only lower the cost of recruitment process
but also offer a uniform and fair selection criteria for all the AIIMS. (Para 5.46)
SPECIALITY AND SUPER SPECIALITY DEPARTMENTS
The Committee believes that Speciality and Super Speciality Departments are fundamental
for an Institute like AIIMS Patna that is envisaged as a flag bearer in providing tertiary
healthcare and medical education. The Committee however is anguished to note that important
Departments like Endocrinology, Nephrology, Urology, and Nuclear Medicine are not functional
till date in the Institute. Considering the fact that around 2.2 lakh new patients suffering
from End Stage Renal Disease (ESRD) are reported every year which results in an additional
demand of 3.4 crore dialysis every year, the status of crucial Department like Nephrology
being non-functional in AIIMS Patna is a matter of grave concern.
(Para 5.50)
The Institute needs to be considerate towards the expenses borne by poor patients
when they are referred to other hospitals because of non-availability of the medical facility
in AIIMS Patna. The Committee, thus, recommends to the Institute to start the Specialty
and Super Speciality Departments as early as possible in the best interest of the people of
the State. (Para 5.51)

169
The Committee recognises the fact that there is a dire need to address the shortage
of specialist and start the recruitment process for each Department. AIIMS Patna’s contention
that the decision for offering incentives to Specialists/Super Specialist will be taken by the
Ministry amounts to shirking off its responsibilities and therefore, the Committee strongly
recommends that the proposal of offering special incentives and packages to Specialist and
Super Specialists in AIIMS Patna be submitted to the Ministry for consideration without
much delay. (Para 5.52)
MEDICAL EDUCATION
The Committee is unhappy to note the slow progress in starting Post Graduate courses
in different Departments of the Institute. The Institute should take cognizance of the acute
shortage of specialists in the country in general and in Bihar in particular and therefore
need to focus on producing better qualified specialists. The Committee, at the same time,
is aggrieved at the insufficient number of faculty staff in AIIMS Patna and worries that in
the absence of the required number of Professors/Additional Professor/Associate Professor/
Assistant Professor in the institute, the quality of medical education may suffer. Therefore,
the Committee recommends that AIIMS Patna, before starting any Post graduate course in
the Institute, should ensure that the necessary infrastructure and human resource is available
with the Institute. (Para 5.55)
The Committee reprimands that AIIMS Patna does not have a human resource development
cell for undertaking regular induction and in service training programmes for capacity
building of the staff. The Committee believes that training of personnel is critical for
development of high standards of medical education in AIIMS Patna. The Committee, therefore,
strongly recommends creation of a Human Resource Development Cell in AIIMS Patna that
would conduct regular training for the doctors, nurses and the staff. The Institute must also
conduct special training for ensuring palliative care to the patients. (Para 5.56)
The Committee is sceptical about the standard of clinical training provided as the
Institute has shortage of faculty in almost every Department. The Committee is anguished
to find that there are insufficient number of Operation Theatres and laboratories as the
construction work is yet to be completed. The Committee strongly emphasizes that the
assessment of the budding doctors should be based on real clinical cases rather than on
rote learning. Ideally, each hospital should have senior consultants to advise the resident
doctors on treatment or supervise the key procedure and surgeries but the shortage of
trained specialists in AIIMS Patna may produce graduates and post graduates with no
practical medical skills. Therefore, the Committee lays more impetus on imparting practical,
technical and skill based knowledge to the doctors. The Committee is of the view that
AIIMS Patna should, therefore, focus on producing better trained doctors who later become
competent enough to work in a clinical setting and possess sharp clinical and medical
acumen. (Para 5.58)
OPERATION THEATRES
From the data submitted by AIIMS Patna, the Committee observes with deep concern
that AIIMS, Patna has conducted on an average 10 surgeries per day in its 13 Operation

170
Theatres. Moreover, only 506 emergency surgeries have been conducted. The Committee
finds that these 13 OTs remain highly underutilized although AIIMS Patna cited limited
availability of OTs as the reason behind the waiting time in surgical procedures. The Committee
also recommends to AIIMS, Patna for planning higher number of surgeries to be carried out
in a day so that one OT can be used for more than one surgery in a day, thus, resulting
in optimum utilisation of the Operation Theatre. (Para 5.60)
As per the information submitted by the Ministry, AIIMS Patna has conducted 4224
major and minor surgeries till September, 2018, the Committee is displeased with such huge
variation in the data and would like AIIMS Patna to submit precise data with respect to
surgical procedures (patient-wise and Department-wise) till date, since inception of the Institute,
within three months of presentation of this Report. (Para 5.61)
TRAUMA AND EMERGENCY SERVICES
The Committee takes it to exception that all the faculty positions in the Trauma and
Emergency Care Centre are vacant. Considering this fact, AIIMS Patna’s claim that it has
a functional trauma/emergency centre is absolutely farce. The Committee reprimands the
Institute for making such false claims and misleading the Committee. It is unfortunate that
despite the required infrastructure for Trauma and Emergency centre, it is not fully functional
due to the shortage of skilled manpower. The Committee strongly recommends that recruitment
to various posts is carried out and Trauma and Emergency Centre is made functional in the
real sense of the term with adequate manpower so that critical patients are given urgent
and prompt treatment and care. The Committee takes serious note of the manner in which
AIIMS Patna has tried to evade Parliamentary scrutiny. (Para 5.63)
A V AILABILITY OF BEDS AT AIIMS PATNA
The Committee observes with deep concern that the numbers of beds in some important
Departments are very low. The low bed strength in each Department also reflects the
Institute’s lack of preparedness in case of an unforeseen disaster/natural calamity. Even the
distribution of beds for various Departments appears faulty. The Committee is surprised to
observe that Urology Department has 5 beds but the Department is not yet functional in
the Institute. This again highlights the Institute’s failure to make optimum utilization of
the available resources. The Committee also notes that the number of patients in Ayush
Department is comparatively low when compared to other Departments. The Committee
therefore is of the opinion that the beds under Ayush Department can be used for other
Departments on a need basis. The Committee strongly recommends that AIIMS Patna
should carry out a detailed assessment of the patient load in different Departments and
accordingly carry out the distribution of beds in each Department. This will certainly help
in better management of the wards and facilitate better bed occupancy in each Department.
The Committee recommends that the construction activities should be expedited so that the
sanctioned bed strength of 960 is achieved in the Institute at the earliest. (Para 5.65)
OTHER INFRASTRUCTURE DEVELOPMENT
The Committee is surprised to note that minor issues like non-installation of centralized
AC and procurement of equipment has stalled the progress of mortuary service in AIIMS

171
Patna which is critical for preservation and forensic/pathological investigation of the dead
body. AIIMS Patna, therefore, must accelerate the remaining work in the Mortuary building
and immediately place the proposal for inspection of the building. All equipment must be
procured and installed in time so that Mortuary Service is provided in time. AIIMS Patna
should also ensure that the timeline for completion of Burns and Plastic surgery Department
is met and unwarranted delays do not hamper the functioning of the Department.
(Para 5.67)
MEDICAL IMAGING MODALITIES
The Committee is shocked at the long waiting period for Ultrasound, CT scan, and
MRI OPD Cases. The Committee during its study visit to Patna also came across patients
with waiting period as long as one year. The medical imaging facilities are fundamental for
the correct diagnosis of any disease but such long waiting period render the whole medical
care process futile and life threatening. The Committee is not at all convinced by the plea
of AIIMS Patna’s management that shortage of MRI machines led to such long waiting
period for MRI scans. The Committee also notes that AIIMS Patna on an average conducted
only 5 MRI per day, 2 Mammography and 18 CT scan which is very low when compared to
the available infrastructure and manpower. The Committee believes that even with one MRI
machine at its disposal, the Institute could have easily carried out more than twenty scans
in one day as is being done in other AIIMS and private hospitals. The grim data furnished
by AIIMS Patna clearly reflects gross underutilization of the available resources and sheds
light on the failure of the institute to work in its full capacity. The Committee during its
earlier study visit observed that RIMS Imphal conducted 30 MRI scans per day on a very
old machine and also carried out over 50 CT scans. The Committee desires a quarterly
update from AIIMS Patna on the per day usage of MRI machine along with the daily
pending cases.  The Committee strongly recommends that the procurement of second MRI
machine should only be done when the present MRI machine is optimally utilized.
(Para 5.70)
AYUSH
The Committee believes that in a country like India with acute shortage of medical
professionals, AYUSH through its traditional methods of treatment can go a long way in
providing basic healthcare facilities to the citizens. National AYUSH Mission was launched
in 2014 with the same vision, to provide cost effective and equitable AYUSH healthcare
throughout the country by improving access to AYUSH services. AIIMS Patna being the
foremost institute of medical care should understand the importance of medical pluralism
and make every effort to strengthen the AYUSH system. The Committee, therefore, strongly
recommends that other branches of AYUSH should be operationalized at AIIMS Patna without
delay and recruitment to the sanctioned posts must be carried out immediately.
(Para 5.72)
COMMUNITY OUTREACH SERVICES
The Committee appreciates AIIMS Patna in its initiative of providing medical consultation
through telecommunications and holding of medical camps in remote areas which has benefited

172
patients from far flung areas of the State. The Committee feels that AIIMS Patna has set
an example for other AIIMS like Institutions for their initiative with respect to community
outreach programmes like telemedicine and accordingly recommends that other new AIIMS
to start similar programmes and mobile applications for their respective regions.
(Para 5.74)
GOVERNING BODY
The Committee strongly recommends that the meetings of the Governing body, Academic
Committee and Standing Finance Committee should be held frequently to discharge its
entrusted responsibilities. The Committee also notes that the course of action decided for
some agenda in the first meeting of the Governing Body had not been implemented even
after one year and was again taken up in the second meeting of the Governing Body. The
Committee, therefore, feels that the Ministry must devise a follow up plan for the agenda
discussed in such meetings. The Ministry must ensure that the decisions of the Governing
body/Academic Committee/Standing Finance Committees are speedily implemented at the
ground level and undue delays are avoided. The Committee also urges the State Government
of Bihar to play a more active role in making AIIMS Patna a Centre of Excellence in
medical learning and healthcare treatment. (Para 5.76)
MONITORING MECHANISM
The Committee is of the considered view that the success of any project lies in
effective implementation and evaluation. State Project Monitoring Committee (State PMC)
were entrusted with the responsibility to monitor the progress of new AIIMS in the State.
This Committee was required to meet at least once in a quarter to review the progress of
AIIMS. In the State of Bihar no such State project Monitoring Committee (State PMC) has
been constituted and in the absence of the required mechanism, no meetings could be
conducted. The Committee feels that the various issues plaguing AIIMS Patna could have
been easily solved had the concept of State PMC was implemented on ground.
(Para 5.77)
AIIMS Patna, an Institute of National Importance today trails behind in many areas
that are fundamental for providing quality healthcare. The Committee expects AIIMS Patna
to make extra efforts to build itself into a world class institute as envisioned while establishing
the institute. (Para 5.78)
BUDGETARY ALLOCATION
The Committee observes with concern that the objectives of the PMSSY scheme are
not being realized in AIIMS, Raipur because of massive underutilization of funds in the
initial years. The utilization of the fund has constantly remained below 50 per cent in all
the years except for the year 2017-18 where it was 56.19 per cent. The Committee, further,
observes despite the fact that there was no dearth of funds in the initial years of construction
activities because of lack of absorption capacity of implementing agencies, technical problems
and non-availability of project consultants, the construction work got delayed. The released
funds under the head GIA (CCA) for two years i.e. 2015-16 and 2016-17 were more than
the funds sanctioned to the Institute. The main problem does not seem to be inadequate

173
budget outlay but poor utilization of funds by the Institute. The Committee observes that
the main reason for unspent funds was the slow progress of construction work. It is a
matter of great concern and, therefore, requires introspection to assess the reasons for
inability of the Institute in judicious utilization of funds. (Para 6.6)
The Committee believes that the under-utilization of plan outlays by the Institute can
be attributed to the institutional and procedural bottlenecks in the process of implementation
of planned activities. The Committee also take into cognizance the observation of the CAG
Audit Report (2018) that pointed out the Ministry had not formulated any operational
guidelines for PMSSY, thereby, lacking in overall framework for scheme implementation.
Approval and adoption of ad-hoc approach in term of project planning and fund management,
delay in selection of consultant led to delay in establishment of the AIIMS. The institute
did not obtain clear title over the land provided by the State Government for construction
of residential complex and some Departments and centre of Institutes, that left the institute
open to further land disputes and encroachment. Needless to say, deficiencies in the planning
process and poor financial management in the Institute caused all these sorry state of
affairs. The Committee is of the view that effective financial management along with prudent
budgetary projection would have enabled optimum utilization of funds. The Committee,
therefore, recommends the Institute to strengthen its financial management for optimum
utilization of financial resources at the Institute. The Committee, further, recommends to
the Institute to have effective monitoring and supervision over the implementing agencies
so as to avoid underutilization and idling of funds. (Para 6.7)
STATUS OF CONSTRUCTION
The Committee is anguished at the slow pace of the construction activities at the
AIIMS, Raipur as none of the four Packages have been completed even after delays ranging
from 70 to 90 months. Surprisingly, the main reasons for delay under all the Packages were
abandonment of work by the construction agencies and lack of co-ordination among various
agencies. The delay in the completion of the construction has resulted in cost escalation of
````` 58.18 crore which is expected to increase further if the remaining work is not completed
within the revised date of completion. The Committee is of the view that though various
reasons have been identified by the Institute, corrective measures  should have been taken
much earlier in order to rectify the mistakes to avoid time and cost overruns. Such inordinate
delays have resulted in indefinite postponement of delivery of healthcare services to the
people. The Committee, therefore, advocates strict monitoring by the Ministry so that the
project work is completed within the revised deadlines without any further delay. The
Committee recommends for submission of a detailed enquiry report along with action taken
against the errant project consultants and contractors. (Para 6.12)
The Committee notes that major delay have taken place on the account of abandonment
of work by the implementing agencies. The engagement of HSCC/CPWD also cannot be
construed as a remedy because these agencies have also not been able to perform within
the quality, cost and time parameters. The Committee, therefore, recommends engagement
of only those construction agencies with a proven track along with a reputation of delivering
work in time and within the sanctioned cost. (Para 6.13)

174
The Committee observes that the AIIMS, Raipur has failed in evaluating the requirement
for the residential accommodation while planning the Phase II expansion program. The
planning process for expansion started in March, 2015 and continued till May, 2018 with the
submission of detailed drawings of the project. By then, it was realized that there is a need
to revive the entire plan. The Committee is appalled at the laid back approach of the
Institute in execution of the project. The changes in the design at such a later stage would
have a negative impact on the expansion program, in terms of additional resources, cost and
extended project duration. It seems to the Committee that obviously the Institute had not
conducted any proper study before planning the expansion of phase II residential complex.
The preparation of revised drawing, revised detailed estimates and tender documents would
require additional time resulting in cost and time overruns. The Committee, therefore,
underlines the need for proper planning and evaluation by engaging reputed agencies before
undertaking any construction projects. (Para 6.16)
STATUS OF FACULTY AND NON FACULTY STRENGTH
The Committee has been reiterating that shortage of qualified medical professional is
one of the key challenges that new AIIMS are presently facing. The Committee notes that
huge number of posts in both faculty and non faculty posts are lying vacant at AIIMS,
Raipur. Out of total sanctioned posts of 4089, 2636 posts are lying vacant that constitutes
more than 64 per cent of the total sanctioned posts. The Committee, further, observes that
majority of the vacancies are at the Professor/Additional Professor level which indicates that
the Institute is suffering from dearth of experienced personnel. The Committee believes
that health care is one of the most labour-intensive sectors where human resources are
crucial. Merely developing infrastructure and increasing capital expenditure would not
help, instead it is adequate manpower at the health care Institutes that ensures efficient
and prompt delivery of health care services to the patients.  The Committee, therefore,
would like the Institute to make concerted efforts and accord equal priority to fill up the
vacant posts. (Para 6.19)
The Institute needs to engage itself with continuous assessment of the gap in demand
and supply of human resources and take suitable steps/measures to fill up the gap. The
Committee is of the view that career aspiration, socio-economic status, organizational policies,
work environment, growth opportunities, infrastructural facilities are the main factors that
influence the decision of any health personnel to join any health care Institute. The Committee,
therefore, recommends to the Institute to focus on strengthening these crucial parameters
to attract adequate number of health care personnel. (Para 6.20)
The Committee is perturbed to observe that majority of the non faculty posts are
engaged either on contract or have been outsourced. The Committee believes that such
adhoc arrangements for deployment of medical professionals on contractual basis results in
disturbing issues of inconsistency in carrying on daily task that ultimately affects the
overall functioning of the Institute. It also affects the motivational level of the staff due
to prevailing uncertainties about job security and stability and thereby, lack of sense of
belongingness towards the organization.  Hence, the Committee calls upon the Institute to
strive for employing staff on permanent basis for ensuring continued and better delivery of
services. (Para 6.21)

175
SPECIALTY AND SUPER SPECIALTY SERVICES
The Committee finds that the status of vacancies in 18 Specialty and 12 Super Specialty
Departments is equally appalling. Under Specialty Departments,   out of a total of 146
sanctioned posts, 68 posts are lying vacant. While in Super-Specialty Departments, out of
81 sanctioned posts, 60 posts are vacant. The Committee expresses its concern that 16
Departments are suffering with vacancies of more than 50% of sanctioned posts. The
Departments i.e. Radiotherapy, Physical Medicine and Rehabilitation, Physical Medicine and
Rehabilitation, CTVS, Endocrinology and Metabolic Diseases and Neonatology are being handled
by a single faculty. Such huge vacancy will hamper the specialized services provided by the
Institute.  The main purpose of having Super Specialty services at the medical Institute is
to save the patients of the State from the compulsion of going out for treatment to other
metro cities but that very purpose is defeated due to the shortage of faculty.  The Committee,
therefore, strongly recommends to the Institute to fill up the vacant posts at the earliest
so as to provide best medical services to the patients who are visiting hospital with the
hope best of tertiary level health care services at lower cost. The Committee desires the
Ministry to take necessary action to get the vacant posts filled up without delay.
(Para 6.23)
The Committee is distressed to observe that crucial Super Specialty Departments like
Surgical Gastroenterology, Nephrology, Medical Oncology/Hematology, Surgical Oncology and
Gastroenterology are not functional at AIIMS, Raipur. The Committee believes that there
are many diseases which require specialty diagnosis and treatment necessitating the availability
of Specialty and Super Specialty Departments. The Committee, therefore, recommends to
the Institute to make these Departments functional without any delay so that tertiary
healthcare services is made available for the patients from Chhattisgarh and neighboring
States. (Para 6.24)
PROCUREMENT OF EQUIPMENT
The Committee deprecates the Institute for poor management of crucial equipment
worth crores. Procurement process involves commissioning, installation, training, warranty,
after sales services like post-warranty maintenance and assured availability of spares.  The
Committee is of the view that delay in the installation of procured equipment may jeopardize
after sales services for warranty period may lapse as per the contract. The Committee would
like the Institute to install the equipment as soon as these equipment are delivered. Further,
the equipment must be purchased only when the suitable infrastructure and manpower for
their installation and operation is available. (Para 6.27)
The Committee feels that delay in the delivery or installation of the equipment at the
Institute is certainly not a healthy trend. It deprives patients of diagnostic/therapeutic
benefits through the usage of such equipment. The delay in the supply of the equipment
is the fault on the part of the PSA/supplier/manufacturer and the Institute must take
suitable action against the errant supplier. The Committee is of the view that procurement
of equipment is a key area and therefore the Institute must resolve these issues by making
the PSA accountable for any delay and lapse. (Para 6.28)

176
The Committee is of the further view that quality of medical equipment needs to be
looked into because defective equipment result in frequent break down of health care
services.  Given the importance of medical equipment in diagnosis as well as treatment and
the high price involved in purchase of several hi-tech equipment, it is imperative that the
equipment are put to their best use for optimum utilization of resources. Non-installation
of equipment is indicative of lackadaisical approach of the Institute in letting them remain
idle which is mere wastage of resources at the cost of taxpayers’ money. The Committee,
accordingly, recommends the Institute to take strict action against the manufacturers for
delayed delivery or delivery of defective equipment. The Committee, further, recommends
that Institute should either exchange the defective equipment with new equipment or scrap
the contract with the manufacturers by seeking adequate legal redressal. The Committee
recommends for an action taken status report on the specific observations of the CAG in
context of procurement of medical equipment and recommends the Ministry to take strict
action against the errant officials. (Para 6.29)
The Committee observes that majority of 159 types of medical equipment during the
last two years have been purchased with USFDA certification. The Committee is concerned
at the substantial number of procurement done in AIIMS, Raipur with USFDA certification.
The Committee apprehends that some of them may be available with European/Indian
manufactures. The Committee, in this connection, reiterates its recommendation made on
other AIIMS that the Institute must give preference to general CE/BIS certification to
increase competitive bidding. The Committee feels that where the equipment with BIS
certification is available, the Indian manufacturers should be given priority over the USFDA
or other certifications. The Institute should make efforts to contribute to the “Make in
India”, scheme of Government of India to give boost to Indian manufacturers. Further,
USFDA certification involves higher cost of equipment which would unfortunately be passed
on to the patients seeking treatment and care and thereby increasing their out of pocket
expenditure. The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends that USFDA certification may
be resorted to only in exceptional circumstances where other manufacturers do not match
the high parameters or urgency of equipment with higher standard is called for. The Committee
desires that the Institute should submit a status report on all medical devices/equipment
procured with USFDA certification /single bid/proprietary certification alongwith reasons for
doing so, within three months of presentation of this report to the Parliament.
(Para 6.31)
The Committee hopes that AIIMS Raipur is adhering to procedure of multiple tendering
in the purchase of medical equipment and avoiding single bid purchase to ensure transparency
and public trust in procurement system of medical equipment. (Para 6.32)
MEDICAL EDUCATION
The Committee was given to understand that out of the 30 total PG seats, only 22 seats
were filled in July, 2018. The seats in six Departments i.e. Anatomy, Biochemistry, Forensic
Medicine and Toxicology, Microbiology, Pharmacology and Physiology remained vacant. The
Committee expects the Institute to assess the reasons for the vacant seats and take remedial
action so that all seats get filled up from the next session onwards. (Para 6.34)

177
The Committee feels that clinical training is a vital part in a medical education for
becoming a successful doctor. The Committee is, however, apprehensive about the quality of
the clinical training imparted at the Institute to the undergraduate and postgraduate students
due to shortage of faculty at the Institute as many key Departments are being handled by
a single faculty. The Committee wonders how an Institute is accomplishing the objective of
imparting clinical training to the medical students with limited number of faculty. The
Committee also notes that seats under many PG courses are lying vacant which raises a
question mark about the quality of clinical training facilities available at the Institute. Poor
clinical training hampers the professional pursuit of doctors which amounts to risking the
lives of the patients. The Committee, therefore, recommends to the Institute to ensure
adequate faculty at the Institute for imparting quality medical education and clinical training
to the students aspiring to be well-trained doctors. (Para 6.36)
STATUS OF OPERATION THEATRE (OTs) AND INTENSIVE CARE UNIT (ICUs)
The Committee is of the view it is unfortunate   that even after a lapse of considerable
period of time, only 8 operation theatres have been made functional. AIIMS, Raipur was
established with the aim of providing economical and reliable health care services to the
people visiting the hospital. Thus, the limited availability of OTs will definitely have an
adverse effect on the services provided by the Institute. The Committee hopes that the
Institute will expedite the process of construction of the remaining OTs to ensure that all
OTs are made functional without procrastination. (Para 6.38)
The Committee has not been able to assess the number of surgical procedures carried
out in AIIMS Raipur due to the variation in the data furnished by the Institute and the
Ministry. The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends that precise data with respect to
major and minor surgeries done (patient-wise and Department-wise) till date since the
inception of the Institute, may be submitted to the Committee within three months of
presentation of this Report. (Para 6.39)
The Committee notes with concern that only 26 beds out of total sanctioned strength
of 100 beds are functional at the Institute. ICUs are used to provide specialized rehabilitative
care to patients as they start to recover from their critical illness or injury. The Committee
believes that bed scarcity in ICU may result in refusal of admission to initial patients,
thereby, depriving them the benefit of ICU services. The Committee observes that there are
as many as 35 ventilators for 26 ICU beds, indicating that 9 ventilators are lying unused
in the ICUs. The Committee feels that the value of the equipment starts depreciating
immediately after they are procured and non functioning of the equipment results in loss
of their value. The Committee, therefore, recommends to the Institute to increase the
number of beds so that critically ill patients are not refused the service of ICUs due to
scarcity of beds and focus on optimum utilization of resources available. (Para 6.41)
DIALYSIS FACILITIES
The Committee believes that Nephrology Department constitutes an important part of
any health institution in providing Super Specialty health care services to the patients.
Absence of the faculty would mean that patients requiring dialysis or treatment related to

178
renal diseases would be turned away due to non-functioning of the Department. The Committee,
therefore, recommends to the Institute to recruit the faculty as early as possible so that
dialysis services become functional at the Institute without any delay. (Para 6.43)
AYUSH
The Committee observes that out of 5 AYUSH services, only Ayurveda services are
functional at the Institute at the present. The Committee underlines the need for starting
other branches of AYUSH system at the Institute so that the patients avail the facilities and
services of an alternate system of medicine. Patients would be benefitted immensely as
AYUSH medicines are not only cost effective but also widely used as a standalone option or
as adjunct with bio medicine in the diseases entailing long duration of treatment. The
Committee feels that the relevance of AYUSH has become more prominent due to this
paradigm shift in the health sector. The Committee, therefore, recommends to the Institute
to start other Ayush services as well as IPD services at the Institute without any further
delay. (Para 6.46)
The Committee notes that out of 7 sanctioned posts, only one post has been filled up
yet. One Senior Medical Officer (Ayurveda) is working on contractual basis since 1st September
2014. The Committee believes that the Institute needs to strengthen its in AYUSH wing and
provide cost effective and equitable AYUSH health care services throughout the country by
improving access to the services as envisaged under National Ayush Mission. To ensure the
optimal development and propagation of AYUSH systems of health care, there is the need
to have adequate faculty in the AYUSH Department of the Institute. The Committee, thus,
recommends to the Institute to fill up the vacant posts in the AYUSH Department on
regular basis without further delay. (Para 6.47)
The Committee finds AIIMS, Raipur to be lacking behind on almost all parameters.
However, the Committee would be taking AIIMS, Raipur for detailed examination and will
conduct a study visit which could not be done this time due to time constraints.
(Para 6.48)
BUDGETARY ALLOCATION
The Committee expresses concern over the constant underutilization of funds in AIIMS,
Bhubaneswar from the year 2011 to 2017.  There has been abysmal increase in the percentage
utilization of funds from 2016-17 onwards, however, it remained below 75 per cent of the
total available funds. The Committee feels that improper planning and lack of monitoring
mechanism in the initial years led to under-utilization of Grants in Aid under the head
CCA that adversely affected the construction and other developmental activities at the
Institute and resulted in cost overruns. This is indicative of financial mismanagement
within the Institute. The underutilization of available funds will only result in inordinate
delay in realization of both the short as well as long-term objectives of the Institute. The
Committee, therefore, is of the view that the funds allocated to the Institute needs to be
reviewed from time to time. An effective strategy ensuring fiscal discipline by continuous
and efficacious monitoring would help in strengthening the financial management in the
Institute. (Para 7.8)

179
The Committee finds that funds allocated in the year 2017-18 remained unaudited. The
Committee underlines the need of an audit of the allocated funds on a regular basis.
Needless to say, audit ensures financial reliability and compliance with laws and statutory
regulations. (Para 7.9)
STATUS OF CONSTRUCTION WORK
The Committee takes serious note of the delay in most of the cases due to non-
performance of construction agency. The work of Package I that includes construction of
medical complex building commenced on 27th May, 2010 and the original date of completion
was 26th August, 2011. The work was actually completed on 31st August, 2017 with time
overrun of 72 months and cost overrun of ````` 8.56 crore. Package II that includes construction
of Hospital Complex buildings was started on 16th September, 2010 and original date of
completion was 15th September, 2012. The work under this package has seen time overrun
of 73 months and the cost of the project has increased to ````` 297.07 crore from ````` 261.65 crore
as estimated originally. Similarly, the work under Package III that includes electrical services
got started on 16th September, 2010 and original target date of completion was 11th January,
2013 but the work was completed on 31st July, 2015 with a time overrun of 30 months and
cost overrun of ````` 1.51 crore. The work under Package IV had started on 4th July, 2012 with
scheduled date of completion by 3rd July, 2013 but the work was actually completed on 28th
February, 2018 with a time overrun of 55 months. The total cost escalation under the four
Packages is approximately ````` 35.36 crore. (Para 7.12)
The Committee takes cognizance of the observation of CAG Report that delay in
execution of construction projects that is reproduced below:-
“The main reasons for the delays were preparation of erroneous Bills of Quantities, delays
in issue of drawings by design consultants, delays in  providing work sites, delays in clearance
of deviations, extra items and substituted items, cash flow problems of agencies due to
withheld payments due to delays in granting clearances, slow progress of work by contractors,
vacancies in engineering positions in the Project Cell of the new AIIMS and abandonment
of works by Project Consultants and delay in making alternate arrangements. These reasons
were indicative of deficient project and contract management, administrative laxity and weak
monitoring. It was incumbent upon the departments to take effective steps where necessary
to mitigate the delays, remove bottlenecks and expedite the progress.” (Para 7.13)
The Committee is of the view that underutilization of the funds during years 2011-2017
is the main reason behind the delay in completion of the project and cost overruns. The
Committee expresses its concern over delay and cost overruns in different projects of
various packages. Time and cost over runs are undesirable in projects under construction.
The delays in construction project have delayed delivery of health services. The Committee
observes that delay in completion of the project was on account of poor planning, unrealistic
scheduling of the dates, lack of communication between the contracting agency and the
authorities concerned at the Institute and poor monitoring. (Para 7.14)
The delay in the completion of the project has also been attributed to services by
former Project Consultant. The Committee believes that the track record of delivery of

180
projects of the contractor as well as Project Consultant must be taken into account before
awarding such important project work to them. The Committee, therefore, recommends the
Ministry to examine the reasons for delay in the construction projects and fix responsibility
and accountability for delayed work in all four packages. The Committee further recommends
to the Ministry/Institute to engage other professional agencies or other reputed construction
majors for upcoming new AIIMS to ensure cost effective and timely execution of projects
especially in projects where implementing agencies have failed to ensure timely completion.
(Para 7.15)
The Committee is happy to note that management of AIIMS Bhubaneswar has explored
the idea of private residential accommodation in the adjoining areas. The Committee understands
that new AIIMS, still facing infrastructure constraints can easily avoid undue stress through
arrangement of private accommodation in the nearby areas and the limited resource can
better be utilized on creation of much needed physical infrastructure. The Committee
acknowledges AIIMS Bhubaneswar for its sound judgment and right emphasis on the more
important requirements and demands of the Institute. The Committee also appreciates the
Institute for completing all the construction projects under the four Packages. The Committee
recommends the Institute to expedite and monitor the other construction activities and
work on creating the required infrastructure for accommodating all its Departments.
(Para 7.17)
The Committee is given to understand that the present infrastructure of the Institute
is not sufficient to accommodate all Departments within the Institute. The Committee,
however, emphasizes the necessity of having a Burns Unit and Trauma Center in the Institute
on a priority basis. The Committee, therefore, recommends to the Institute to take necessary
steps to expedite the process of formulating necessary plans, prepare DPR and seek administrative
approvals for projects to be undertaken under the expansion programme to accommodate
the construction of Burns Unit, Trauma Centre, Rural Training Complex at Tangi, Centre
of excellence in various super-specialty Department, high-end simulation laboratory and
computer lab, examination hall and new Administrative building within set timelines and at
the estimated cost. The Committee would specifically like to point out that setting up of
centers of excellence in various Super Specialty should only be done when there is ample
availability of faculty/super-specialists and keeping in mind the budgetary implications. However,
the final decision regarding setting up of advanced centers of super specialty should be
taken by the CIB. (Para 7.19)
PROCUREMENT OF MEDICAL EQUIPMENT
The Committee takes cognizance of the problems highlighted by the Institute in the
procurement of the equipment. The Committee observes that sometimes even after multiple
floating of tender, only single bid is received and there are few cases when multiple bids
are received but only single bid gets qualified on technical grounds. The Committee believes
that there is a need to advertise the tender to be floated on a large scale so that more and
more manufacturers submit their bids leading to competitive pricing and availability of
many options for procurement of equipment. The Committee, thus, recommends to the
Institute to advertise their tenders in the national dailies and also integrate into the system

181
of the Central Public Procurement Portal for wider dissemination of information regarding
the floating of tender so as to make it accessible to large number of manufacturers. The
Committee, further, recommends to the Institute to list out the technical requirements
more clearly by making them generic so that there are fewer chances of bids getting
disqualified on technical grounds. The tendency to deliberately design specifications to ensure
that procurement is done on proprietary / single bid basis should be avoided under all
circumstances.  The Committee would like to highlight that the problem areas as identified
by AIIMS, Bhubaneswar are welcome since it would help the procedural hurdles and help
in overall streamlining of the whole process of procurement of medical equipment. The idea
of finalizing uniform technical specification for requirement especially high end equipment
is certainly a step in the right direction.  The Committee strongly supports and recommends
for appropriate time bound action on the suggestions of AIIMS, Bhubaneswar for improving
the procurement process. (Para 7.21)
The Committee observes that AIIMS, Bhubaneswar has been using Tender Wizard for
e-tendering for procurement of medical equipment. The Committee, therefore, desires that
the Institute should implement/use the Central Public Procurement Portal of Government
of India along with Tender Wizard for procurement of medical devices and equipment. The
Committee believes that both the portals may help in facilitating all the Central Government
Organizations to publish their tender enquiries, corrigendum and award of contract details.
These portals bestow a single point access to the information on procurements made across
various Central Government organizations. This enables the manufacturers of the medical
devices and equipment to have access to the information regarding the floating of the
tender by the Government. The Committee is of the view that the main idea of using both
the portals is to disseminate information about the tenders on a wide platform.
(Para 7.24)
The Committee appreciates that AIIMS Bhubaneswar has procured 130 types of equipment
with ISO/BIS certification and encourages AIIMS Bhubaneshwar to carry out all the necessary
procurement from non USFDA/Indian manufacturers. The Committee, however, recommends
AIIMS Bhubaneswar to avoid inappropriate procurement of medical devices/equipment
with USFDA certification for basic equipment such as Bipolar Forceps, Microscope etc. The
Committee believes that standard of quality, price and other basic requirements should be
reviewed closely along with the endorsing the Make in India campaign.
(Para 7.26)
The Committee is dismayed to note that three equipment under the Cardiothoracic
and Vascular Surgeons ( CTVS ) Department remained uninstalled for a period ranging
from 12 to 24 months due to non-availability of the personnel under the Department. The
Committee takes serious note of such delay in installation of equipment which may have
resulted in lapse of warranty/guarantee of the equipment. The Committee, therefore,
chides the Institute for delayed installation of the equipment. The Committee, in this
regard, recommends that in future, equipment should be procured only when the required
faculty and infrastructure for the functioning of the equipment is available.
(Para 7.28)

182
STATUS OF FACULTY AND NON-FACULTY STRENGTH
The Committee is perturbed to note that approximately 40 percent of faculty posts and
78 percent non faculty posts are lying vacant at the Institute. The Committee notes that out
of 54 sanctioned posts for Professor, only 17 posts have been filled up and under 24 Departments,
posts for Professor are lying vacant. Further, 13 Departments are being run either by an
Associate or Assistant Professor. The Committee believes that shortage of health personnel
to this extent is a critical stressor for hospitals. The Committee underlines the need for
requisite number of health personnel in place for smooth functioning of the Institute.
(Para 7.33)
The Committee is not satisfied with the reasons for large number of vacancies at the
Institute. The Committee believes that special attractive package and incentives should be
offered by the Institute to attract the required talent at the super specialty level. The
vacancies should be advertised in national dailies so that it has wider reach and larger
number of candidates should apply for the posts. Moreover, manpower planning needs to be
