reoriented to determine the requirement of adequate numbers of medical specialists to meet
the future demand of medical personnel for all healthcare. The Committee desires the
Institute to expedite the process of recruitment commencing from re-advertising the vacant
posts for selection of faculty and non-faculty staff without further procrastination. The
Committee also recommends for a common all India examination for all the non faculty
posts through a third party recruitment agency for all AIIMS which will make the selection
process more transparent and objective. (Para 7.34)
SPECIALITY/SUPER SPECIALITY DEPARTMENTS
The Committee is dismayed to note that most of the Specialty and Super-specialty
Departments are functional despite inadequate number of faculty in place.  Barring Dentistry,
all other Departments are plagued with shortage of faculty. The status of faculty in Departments
of Radio-Diagnosis, Nephrology, Urology, Surgical oncology, Clinical hematology, Burns and
Plastic surgery, Nuclear medicine is very pathetic. The Departments of Endocrinology and
Metabolic diseases and Nephrology are run by one faculty single handedly. The Committee
believes that quality treatment and adequate care would only be possible with adequate
faculty. The Committee, therefore, recommends the Institute to employ adequate number
of faculty in each Department. (Para 7.36)
The Committee observes that Surgical Gastroenterology Department is not functional
at the Institute. Undoubtedly, this amounts to utmost neglect of the patients suffering from
diseases related to the human gastrointestinal tract. The Committee, therefore, calls upon
the Institute to start the Surgical Gastroenterology Department at the Institute with adequate
manpower and infrastructure without any delay. (Para 7.37)
MEDICAL EDUCATION
The Committee observes that while there has been 100% admission in MBBS and
nursing course, only 45 seats have been filled up out of the 86 sanctioned PG seats. It is
indeed a saddening affair to observe the vacant PG seats at an Institute of national importance
as it indicates sheer wastage of resources. The Committee, however, recommends to the

183
Institute to ascertain the detailed reasons for not filling up of the PG seats at the Institute
and take immediate steps to remove obstacles if any, in filling up of the PG seats with
immediate effect. (Para 7.40)
The Committee observes that the present strength of faculty at the Institute is not
sufficient to offer clinical training to the students. The Committee feels that deficient
clinical training may be one of the reasons for not filling up the PG seats at the Institute.
The Committee is of the view that clinical training is crucial for emphasizing the overall
development of the medical insight of the students. A medical student will become more
efficient and productive if he/she is trained well. The Committee, thus, recommends to the
Institute to make the clinical training programme more effective with sufficient faculty and
adequate infrastructure for training to the students. (Para 7.42)
STATUS OF OPERATION THEATRES AND W ARDS/BEDS
The Committee takes note that out of 25 sanctioned OTs, only 7 are functional at
present resulting into waiting time of one week to four months for surgical procedures
under various Departments. The Committee is of the view that the Institute was established
with the aim of providing affordable and reliable tertiary healthcare services to the people
and preventing them from shuttling to other metro cities in search of tertiary level health
care services. Further, the Committee finds the non-availability of gas pipe line in OTs
unacceptable. The Committee recommends to the Institute to make the remaining 18 sanctioned
OTs functional without any delay. The Institute should also ensure that OTs are equipped
with latest equipment including Medical Gas Pipeline System (MGPS). (Para 7.45)
The Committee is not satisfied with the functional status of the Institute as only
32 wards with 716 beds are functional at the Institute. The Institute is also not properly
equipped to cater to the need for a quick and responsive care in medical emergency.
Good trauma care means getting the patient to the right place at the right time for
appropriate care. The Committee, thus, recommends to the Institute to step up the
strength of wards and beds as per the sanctioned strength. It also needs to expedite the
process of construction of remaining OTs equipped with latest equipment, specialized
resources, and a specially trained trauma and emergency team for quality and timely
care. It is more so required for treatment of the patients suffering from life threatening
illnesses and traumatic injuries. The Committee, therefore, expects that concerted efforts
will be made in this regard and the matter should be taken up on a priority basis.
(Para 7.47)
The Committee is surprised to note the wide difference in the cost per bed in these
two Institutes that have the same functional capacity and the same number of beds. The
Committee takes a serious note of this high cost incurred by AIIMS, Bhubaneswar especially
when the other AIIMS like Institutions, providing the same tertiary health care facilities,
have submitted relatively less cost. The Committee, therefore, recommends the Institute to
limit its redundant expenses. The Committee demands that the Institute should submit a
status note on how the cost per bed was calculated and the reasons behind this high cost
so that cost per bed may be calculated for other AIIMS also on the same parameters.
(Para 7.49)

184
DIALYSIS FACILITIES
The Committee recommends that AIIMS, Bhubaneswar should expedite the setting up
of dialysis unit especially in the wake of rising demand for dialysis services.
(Para 7.51)
AYUSH FACILITIES
The Committee observes that out of five services under AYUSH, only two services i.e.
Y oga and Ayurveda OPD services are functional at the AIIMS, Bhubaneswar. The Committee
is of the considered view that the AYUSH systems offer an alternative system of medicine
to the patients and is generally cheaper and affordable with lesser side effects.  Gaps in
India’s healthcare sector are huge with a large proportion of the population virtually being
denied access to healthcare facilities. To address these gaps, there is a need to support
AYUSH systems of medicine and education, particularly in vulnerable and far flung areas.
The Committee desires the Institute to make earnest efforts to start other AYUSH services
as well as IPD services at the Institute. The Committee recommends that apprehensions
about the efficacy and safety of AYUSH systems need to be allayed through undertaking
suitable desired level of scientific research and development of good AYUSH practices.
Further Standard Treatment protocols need to be developed also to build public trust in
these systems. (Para 7.54)
The Committee is distressed to note that out of six sanctioned posts at the AYUSH
Department, only one post has been filled up so far and that too on contractual basis. The
Committee believes that without adequate manpower, the Institute will not be able to
achieve the objective of strengthening AYUSH services. The Committee, therefore, recommends
to the Institute to fill up the vacant posts on regular basis without further delay.
(Para 7.55)
BUDGETARY ALLOCATION AND FUNDING PATTERN
The Committee takes into account the budgetary allocation and funding pattern  of
select 73 GMCs under five phases in PMSSY for which the Ministry of Health and Family
Welfare has released a total amount of `````5239.71 crore.  However, the State share is pending
in about 50% case of GMCs for upgradation works under Phase-III. The Committee observes
that delay in release of State share has impacted the upgradation projects adversely. The
Committee desires the Ministry to urgently take the matter with State Governments concerned
to release their share at the earliest so that the work of GMCs does not suffer. The
Committee, in this regard, would like to emphasize that the Ministry/State Governments
concerned must comply with the fund sharing commitment to maintain the sanctity of
project. The Ministry needs to put in place a mechanism for release of funds within a
stipulated time period from both Centre and State Governments. The Committee, therefore,
recommends concerted action on part of both the Centre and State Governments to make
the upgradation project successful. The Committee, therefore, recommends that all out
efforts need to be taken by the Ministry to get all the procedural formalities/necessary
approvals done well in time and the State Governments concerned need to be persuaded and
advised regularly in this regard. A quarterly status report on the pending position with

185
respect to the State share may also be provided to enable the Committee to take up the
matter with the concerned State Government as most of the delays are due to internal
processes of the States. (Para 8.10)
The Committee is of the view that the funds allocated to the project should be effectively
and efficiently utilized and not remain idle or underutilized as that would otherwise adversely
impact the smooth execution of the project. Moreover, such instances reflect faulty planning
and improper administration with regard to utilization of the allocated funds. The Committee
views this as a matter of concern. The Committee observes that upgradation of GMCs is
under the direct supervision of the Central Government, therefore, underutilization of the
allocated funds should not become a routine affair as the same would lead to further delays
in completion of projects causing time and cost overruns. The Committee, therefore, strongly
recommends that the Ministry must act promptly and monitor the effective and timely
utilization of funds. (Para 8.11)
The Committee views diversion of funds as criminal misappropriation of allocated fund
and violation of fiscal discipline. Such trend not only results in the shortfall of the funds
for completion of construction activities but is against the provisions of the MoU signed
between the Ministry and the States. Effective monitoring and accountability need to be
ensured so that funds released are utilized for the intended purpose. The Committee expects
the Ministry to issue strict advisories against the diversion of funds and fixing of responsibility
on GMCs/State Governments for the same. (Para 8.12)
KEY CONCERN AREAS
The Committee takes note of the key concern areas of GMCs upgradation. The Committee
observes that the Ministry has put the entire onus on the State Governments for successful
implementation of the scheme. The Committee is of the view that the Central Government
has a key role in planning, guiding and monitoring the scheme. The Ministry needs to
consider State Governments as its partner in resolving the constraints being encountered in
administration of projects and sort out practical difficulties faced in implementation thereof.
There is a need for an efficacious monitoring mechanism to avoid inordinate delays in
completion of upgradation projects. The Committee, thus, recommends to the Ministry to
make a realistic assessment of the challenges faced and formulate suitable plan of action
in active coordination with the State Governments to overcome the same. All out efforts
must be made by both the Central Government and the State Governments to strive towards
completion of upgradation of GMCs within specified the timeline. (Para 8.16)
The attention of the Committee has been drawn to the CAG findings wherein serious
areas of concern have been highlighted especially w.r.t non compliance of codal and contract
provision and awards of work at higher cost. The Committee is of the firm view that strict
compliance with codal and contract provision need to be adhered in all circumstances and
award of work should be assigned on merit and proven track record. (Para 8.17)
SELECTION OF THE GMCS FOR UPGRADATION
The Committee feels that the Ministry has not devised scientific criteria/parameters
for the selection of GMCs under PMSSY. The Committee feels that the criteria of socio-

186
economic indicators, mainly the presence of already existing tertiary healthcare facilities,
should also be taken into account. The Committee also take into cognizance that  many
GMCs have been selected without consulting the respective States and even without considering
their financial condition and even without entering into any MoU with the State Governments.
Further, the selection of the consultancy agencies was purely arbitrary and no past record
of the agencies has been taken into consideration.  To consider HLL/HSCC as a panacea for
all execution is a area of serious concern as these agencies are being given preferential
treatment just because they are PSUs of the Ministry not because of their proven track
record. The Committee, thus, recommends to the Ministry to formulate set parameters for
selection of GMCs to ensure that the GMCs which actually require upgradation are selected
in consultation with State Governments. The Committee, further, recommends that the
Ministry, while appointing consultancy agencies, should also take into consideration past
reputation and good track record of the agencies in order to avoid time and cost overruns.
Accordingly, the Committee recommends for involvement of better professional agencies or
other reputed construction majors to ensure cost effective and timely execution especially
in projects where HSCC has failed to ensure timely completion of the construction projects.
The Committee also takes a serious note on part of the PSA in undertaking a proper gap
analysis in GMCs where requisite medical equipment were not provided on a timely basis.
(Para 8.23)
SITE SELECTION AND STATUS OF CONSTRUCTION ACTIVITIES IN GMCS
The Committee during the course of its study visit observes that construction activities
at various Institutes are delayed. The reasons for delays are manifold including (i) non-
availability of encumbrance free land (ii) delays in getting clearances for excavation
(iii) tree cutting and other site related conditions (iv) changes made in the scope and
quantum of work (v) post contract changes in drawings and quantities (vi) delays in providing
drawings (vii) delay in release of mobilization advance and payments to contractor (viii)
delay in provision of services (ix) delay in work due to shortage of labour (x) delay in
finalization/tendering of medical equipment (xi) changes in site after award of work delay
in shifting of existing electrical lines (xii) internal disturbances in some States etc. The
Committee urges the Ministry to flag these issues with the State Government concerned to
undertake appropriate steps to remove the bottlenecks causing delay in the completion of
construction activities at various GMCs. (Para 8.28)
The Committee, further, observes that the work of 12 GMCs out of 13 approved under
Phase-I, scheduled to be completed in March, 2013, have been completed with delays ranging
from eight months to seven years. In other six GMCs approved under Phase-II, the upgradation
of only three GMCs has been completed though with the delay of several years. In the
remaining three colleges, the work has not been completed even after many years. The
revised date of completion has been extended to 31st December, 2018 for GMCs which
initially that were scheduled to be completed in July, 2011. (Para 8.29)
The Committee takes serious note of issue of awarding of tender at the cost higher
than the approved cost indicating financial irregularities. The Committee believes that the
whole process should be objective and on merit rather than on any other consideration so

187
that the agency with the most economical and best offer of services is awarded the tender
for completion of the projects. (Para 8.30)
The Committee believes that the Ministry cannot in any way justify the inordinate
delays in the completion of the projects. Procedural and preparatory exercises need to be
done beforehand including availability of encumbrance free site by the respective State
Government. The Committee also recommends to the Ministry to frame operational guidelines
that would guide and regulate implementation of the scheme across the States and take
steps to expedite the completion of remaining work by better monitoring of the projects.
The Committee apprehends that such delays will increase the work load at the fag end of
completion schedule. The Committee, therefore, is of the considered view that priority
should be given to such projects for which there is imminent need to expedite the process
of granting necessary approvals only to avoid time and cost overruns are avoided.  The
Committee also strongly recommends that no new project be taken before completion of
these 73 projects. (Para 8.32)
The Committee understands that there may be certain practical difficulties that are
encountered on work sites during the course of upgradation project. However the, considerable
delays in planning and commencement of works in all phases of upgradation of GMCs under
PMSSY and the slow progress, both at the stage of planning and contracting indicates that
both planning of works and contract management were inadequate and improper, thus causing
adverse impact in the delivery of services. The Committee is of the firm view that the
Ministry/Central Government, instead of taking the responsibility, has shifted the blame on
the State Governments for the delay in the commencement of upgradation of GMCs under
PMSSY. The Committee feels that instead of blame game, the Ministry should work in
tandem with the State Governments and help in ironing out problem areas so that the
objectives of the scheme is not defeated by delays and faulty planning. The Committee,
therefore, recommends to the Ministry to review the status of construction timelines and
set realistically achievable targets in consultation with the State Governments concerned.
(Para 8.33)
PROCUREMENT OF EQUIPMENT
The Committee notes with concern the observations of CAG regarding improper gap
analysis, lacunae in CMC provisions and procurement of equipment which did not fall in the
approved list of medical equipment of the Ministry. The CAG Report further highlights that
against the procurement orders for 818 equipment costing ````` 482.21 crore for 11 GMCs, 191
equipment costing ````` 51.72 crore were yet to be made available. The Committee is concerned
to note that there are several tenders which are pending since very long and are being
retendered again and again. The Committee notes that the basic purpose of having a PSA
stands defeated if such delays are going to occur. The Committee, in this connection,
strongly recommends for a properly monitored mechanism, so as to ensure that time and
cost overruns in the procurement process are avoided. The Committee is surprised to note
that a total of 141 tenders for the procurement of equipment are pending under phase III
of the upgradation project under PMSSY. The Committee observes that these 141 tenders
have been retendered by the Ministry in the year 2018. Out of these, 4 tenders for surgical

188
loupes, extra corporeal membrane oxygenator, head saver and surgical xenon head light and
light sources have again been floated. Also, 24 types of equipment under phase-I and II and
7 types of equipment under phase-III have been procured on a single bid basis. The Committee
desires the PSA to submit a status note in all the procurement carried out on single bid
basis in Phase-I and Phase-II including reasons for the same, within three months of
presentation of this report. (Para 8.41)
The Committee observes that there is an acute shortage of equipment at the various
GMCs approved for upgradation under PMSSY. The primary reasons being delays in placement
of orders, change in the model of the equipment and lack of follow up by Institutes with
the nominated PSA after placement of orders. The Committee apprehends that in the
absence of the equipment, the scheme gets affected and patients are deprived of upgraded
services. The CAG has further highlighted a grave concern i.e 75 equipment have been
supplied by HLL at a cost of ````` 3.26 crore but are not anywhere in record either with HLL
or the concerned GMCs.  The Committee takes a serious note of this lapse and recommends
for suitable remedial action to address this criminal wastage of public fund. The Committee
would also like to be apprised of the action taken by the Ministry for fixing accountability
for this lapse. (Para 8.42)
The Committee is, therefore, of the considered view that procurement of equipment on
single bid basis reduces the competition in the market and minimizes the opportunity of
competitive bidding. The Committee would like the Ministry to adhere to the procedure of
the multiple bids in order to achieve competitive pricing. (Para 8.43)
The Committee observes that the revised policy and procedure formulated for procurement
of medical equipment has not been followed in letter and spirit. The Committee strongly
recommends that there should be minimum pendency and in the procurement process and
all pending procurement tenders be closed without any further delay. The Ministry should
also formulate effective monitoring mechanism for timely receipt, installation and proper
functioning of equipment at GMCs so that the equipment are utilized properly for the
purpose for which they were procured. A quarterly update on gap analysis, delivery of
equipment, delays if any, with respect to all GMCs should be submitted to the Committee
by the PSA. (Para 8.44)
ENHANCEMENT OF PG SEATS
The Committee notes that as per the joint gap analysis and DPR, the number of PG
seats would be 666 in 39 GMCs under Phase-III Projects and 355 PG seats in 14 GMCs
under Phase-IV and V Projects. The Committee, however, is of the view that the enhancement
of PG seats without having adequate faculty/staff would not be result-yielding. The Committee,
therefore, recommends to the Ministry to focus on the employment of the faculty along
with enhancement of the PG seats to the desired level of faculty-student ratio. Hence, the
Ministry/State Governments must strive to fill up the vacant seats within a stipulated time
period and basic other infrastructure for teaching must be in place for facilitating the
imparting of medical education to extended number of students. (Para 8.46)
The Committee, further, recommends to the Ministry that the PG seats in GMCs
should be increased in view of the existing infrastructure and adequate faculty. When

189
compared to new AIIMS like Institutions, GMCs are better equipped to provide medical
education and clinical exposure to the PG students. GMCs are in a better position to address
the regional imbalances in States and enhancing their PG seats could increase the outreach
of tertiary health care in the remote areas of the country. The Committee strongly recommends
for a substantial enhancement in the PG seats in all the GMCs so as to address this critical
gap in medical education. The Committee recommends that the Ministry should prepare a
comprehensive action plan for augmenting PG seats in GMCs which are being upgraded
under PMSSY. (Para 8.47)
SUPER SPECIALTY BLOCKS
The Committee is distressed to observe that out of the GMCs (58) approved under first
three phases of the upgradation project, nine GMCs do not have Super Specialty Services.
These are  Institute of Medical Sciences, BHU, Varanasi (Ph.I), Jawaharlal Nehru Medical
College, AMU, Aligarh (Ph.II), Srinagar Medical College (Ph.I), B.J. Medical College, Ahmadabad
(Ph.I),Grant Medical College, Mumbai (Ph.I), GMC, Nagpur (Ph.II), Govt. Medical College,
Malda (PH III), Vijayanagar Institute of Medical Sciences, Bellary (PH III), Pt. B.D. Sharma
PGIMS, Rohtak (Ph.II). The Committee, further, notes that none of the GMCs approved
under phase I, II and III have all the Super Specialty facilities as originally envisaged
under the upgradation project. The Committee apprehends that unless the upgradation
projects under the phase III is not completed, the Ministry would not be in a position to
focus on GMCs in the later phases. The Committee is of the considered view that the
scheme would be successful only when Super Specialty Blocks (SSB) envisaged under the
scheme are functional in all the GMCs approved for upgradation. The Committee, therefore,
recommends the Ministry to focus on the non-functional SSBs of GMCs for capacity building
to provide super-specialty services to suffering patients. The Committee also strongly recommends
for a gap analysis in the availability of super-speciality services in GMCs in locations where
there are functional regional AIIMS or new ones are proposed. (Para 8.51)
The Committee is, therefore, of the firm view that in the modern age where heart
disease have became so common, super specialty services without a CATH lab would be
short of special facility for delivery of adequate service to the suffering heart patient.  The
Committee, therefore, recommends that all the SSB envisaged should include functional
CATH lab under the project should be made functional without much procrastination.
(Para 8.52)
GENERATION OF EMPLOYMENT
The Committee observes with concern that no action has been taken by the Ministry
and the State Governments concerned to generate employment at the GMCs approved for
upgradation under PMSSY. The Committee is of the considered view that health care sector
has tremendous potential for employment generation. The Committee, therefore, strongly
recommends that the Central and State Government must chalk out suitable strategy to
explore various avenues for employment generation in different segments of healthcare
sector. The Committee, in this regard, believes that higher investment in the health sector
would yield greater opportunities for employment generation. The Committee is, however,

190
perturbed to observe that many Departments at the various GMCs are not functional due
to the shortage of manpower. The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends to the Ministry
to analyze the manpower requirement of each GMC and advise the State Governments to
create and fill up adequate number of posts, accordingly. The Committee also recommends
that vacant posts must be filled up as early as possible so as to pave the way for smooth
delivery of health services to the people. (Para 8.55)
MONITORING MECHANISM
The Committee is dissatisfied with the response of the Ministry regarding the existing
institutional arrangement for monitoring the implementation of upgradation Scheme of
Government Medical Colleges under PMSSY. The existing monitoring mechanism to review
the progress through an online dashboard and Project Management Software by the executive
agencies is not effective enough to ensure timely execution of upgradation projects.  In this
regard, the Committee also notes with concern the observation of CAG made in its report
that in eight GMCs mandatory State PMCs under the Chairmanship of Principal Secretary
(Health/Medical Education), to review the progress of upgradation scheme have not been
constituted.  Similarly, in many States, a State level Steering Committee, under the Chairmanship
of Chief Secretary, required to meet each quarter to review progress and resolve local
issues were  either not constituted or if constituted were not active. Utmost to the surprise
of the Committee, the Ministry has no record about the working of these Committees.
Moreover, the Ministry’s direction to have a system of Third Party Quality Assurance
(TPQA) have also not been complied with in 15 GMCs.  The Committee during its visit to
West Bengal and Bihar found the absence of these Committees in various GMCs, therefore,
the Committee concludes that ‘all is not well’ with the monitoring system over the execution
of programmes for upgradation schemes of GMCs under the PMSSY.  The Committee,
therefore, strongly recommends that mandatory provision of constitution of PMCs, State
Level Steering Committees must be complied with and the system of Third Party Quality
Assurance (TPQA) be placed in order to make resilient monitoring mechanism for efficient
and effective execution of upgradation projects in all GMCs. (Para 8.58)
STUDY VISIT OF THE COMMITTEE
The Committee, therefore, is of the view that actual condition of the upgradation
project in GMCs is totally different from what is reflected on paper. The Committee feels
that even after the utilization of the allocated funds there is no perceptible change at the
ground level which clearly means the Government funds have not been properly utilized
under the project. The Committee, therefore, recommends to the Ministry to ensure adherence
to contract provisions in execution of work and provision of services. The Committee feels
that accountability needs to be fixed for expenditure without adequate justification or where
Government funds have not been utilized properly or when there is misappropriation of
funds. Moreover, the Committee would like to reiterate that professional agencies with a
proven track record must be involved in the construction process so as to avoid building
flaws leading to wasteful expenditure. (Para 8.60)
The Committee noted that more than two years have passed since the inclusion of
PMCH in the upgradation program under Phase IV of PMSSY but the foundation stone of

191
the Super Speciality Department is yet to see the light of day. PMCH submitted that the
delay in transfer of land measuring merely 500 meter has stalled the construction of the
Super Speciality Block that cast aspersion on the administrative will and skill of PMCH
management. The Committee, however, welcomes the Institute’s proposal to increase the
number of seats in MBBS and also advocated the increase in the number of seats in PG
course. The Committee also endorsed PMCH’s suggestion for inclusion of important Departments
such as Cardiology, Onco-Haemetology in the Super Speciality Block. (Para 8.62)
The Committee is appalled at the lax attitude of the State in monitoring the progress
made under PMSSY and recommends that the State should conduct regular review meetings
with the GMCs to address the various hurdles in its smooth functioning. The Committee
strongly recommends that the State should transfer its share of funds well in time for the
upgradation of all the Government Medical Colleges. The Committee also strongly recommends
the State to develop a Third Party Quality Assurance Audit for the all the GMCs.
(Para 8.64)
The Committee also notes that wide regional imbalance exists in the delivery of Health
Care Services in the State. The remote regions of the State are still out of the reach of
tertiary health care services as most of such facilities are established in and around Patna.
There is a severe lack of adequate medical care and facilities in Government Sector in
North Bihar, especially in the districts of Saharsha, Madhepura, Purnea, Kathiar, Kishanganj
and Farbishganj, i.e., Koshi region.  The Committee desires that the Ministry may explore
the possibility for setting up of AIIMS like institutions in this extremely poor and backward
region to cater to the health facilities and services to the poor at the economical/lower cost.
The Committee, therefore, strongly urges upon the Central and the State Governments to
take into account the need and requirement of health care services in these economically
deprived regions and work towards decentralization of the health care facilities.
(Para 8.65)
The Committee notes that the shortage of specialists is a major problem in all the
AIIMS like Institutions and Government Medical Colleges. The Committee again emphasizes
the need to develop special packages and incentives for attracting the specialists to serve
in this part of the region. (Para 8.66)
CONCLUSION
The Committee observes that upgradation project of GMCs under PMSSY has failed to
provide the desired results which were expected from the project. The Committee opines
that there is a big gap between planning and the execution of the project due to lack of
synchronization and coordination of activities and serious gaps between planning and
implementation of the scheme. The Committee feels that the inadequate project management
and related delays impacted the delivery of envisaged benefits in terms of improvement in
health infrastructure. The reasons for delay in upgradation project are mainly due to non-
availability of encumbrance free land, delays in getting clearances for excavation and tree
cutting and other site related conditions, changes made in the scope and quantum of work,
delay in finalization/tendering of medical equipment. Apart from all these, poor planning
and coordination contributed to delays in upgradation of GMCs . The Committee appreciates

192
the potential in the scheme to transform the Government Medical Colleges in providing
quality health services to the people. The Committee is therefore of the firm view that
projects require meticulous planning and consistent efforts for timely execution/completion.
The Committee believes that preparatory activities should be completed beforehand which
should include realistic assessment of requirement of equipment, availability of site, analysis
of manpower and utilization of funds for the purpose it is earmarked. The Committee
recommends to the Ministry to work in tandem with the State Governments to work in a
coordinated manner to accomplish the objective and mission of upgradation of GMCs as
envisaged in PMSSY. (Para 8.67)
FINANCIAL ASSESSMENT
The Committee is taken aback at this skewed financial assessment of the Government’s
flagship health care service programme. This fluctuating assessment speaks volume of the
Ministry’s lack in planning and failure to formulate any operational guidelines for AIIMS
like Institutions. The Committee also notes that the approval of the revised cost was
obtained in March, 2010 i.e. after four years of the initial approval in 2006.  This represents
a 188% and 145% increase in capital costs since 2004 and 2006 respectively. The Committee,
therefore, recommends that the Ministry must undertake proper and practical financial
assessment with respect to the capital cost of new AIIMS like institutions in the subsequent
phases. (Para 9.3)
SITE SELECTION OF AIIMS LIKE INSTITUTIONS
The Committee is of the view that site selection for any AIIMS like Institution requires
a detailed assessment of the geographical location and the demographic distribution. Easy
accessibility and transportation availability is one major concern. The Committee is also
concerned at the duplication and concentration of tertiary care services at a particular place.
The Committee notes that at several places like Guwahati, Patna and now in Madurai
(where a new AIIMS has just been announced) the Government had already selected GMCs
for upgradation programme under PMSSY. In the places that have been selected for setting
up of AIIMS like Institutions, the critical gap in the tertiary health care is already being
filled by private sector.  The Committee strongly believes that AIIMS should be located in
an area with low Human Development Index that lags behind in tertiary health care.
(Para 9.4)
Attention of the Committee has also been drawn to a strange criterion for selection
of site for AIIMS like Institute. Air connectivity to the place has been one of the major
basis for site selection for AIIMS. It has been reasoned out that airport services will
facilitate employment of better trained human resources as most doctors are reluctant to
serve in remote areas. (Para 9.5)
The Committee observes that all the six AIIMS under Phase I are well connected and
are major cities in their respective States but still these new AIIMS are plagued by acute
shortage of manpower. There is no faculty from AIIMS Delhi or other premier Institutes
working full time as Head of Department or as Professors in the new AIIMS. Therefore,
the Committee is of the opinion that the perception that availability of an airport facility

193
results in faculty retention is based on a false premise. The Ministry and the Institute could
have addressed this issue with better manpower planning rather than focussing on feasibility
of airport. (Para 9.6)
The Committee strongly recommends that the Ministry while deciding the location of
the new AIIMS must take note of the already existing healthcare infrastructure in the
vicinity of the earmarked area so that duplication of healthcare facilities are avoided. The
healthcare system should be such that delivery of medical facility and care is regionally
distributed and even the remotest region is well within its outreach so as to address regional
imbalances in provision of health care facilities. (Para 9.7)
SELECTION OF PROJECT CONSULTANTS/CONTRACTORS
The Committee is of the view that the careless selection of the Project consultants did
more harm than good and caused unwarranted delays in the construction activity. The
Ministry failed to make a comprehensive assessment of selection of project-consultants/
contractors before the execution of the scheme. The CAG has also raised concerns regarding
the award of consultancy work to PSUs on nomination basis in its Report. The Committee
also notes that M/s HSCC and M/s HLL were appointed as consultants on nomination basis
under the garb of Rule 176 of General Financial Rules (GFR) 2005. This rule is always read
with the section 1.5.3 that states the selection by direct negotiations/ nomination is permissible
in terms of Rule 176 of General Financial Rules, 2005 only under exceptional circumstances
and such selection may normally be restricted to a financial ceiling of Rs ten lakhs. The
Committee notes that the cases did not fall under any of the special circumstances stipulated
in the section 1.5.3. (Para 9.9)
The Committee during the examination of the Demands for Grants has repeatedly
seen unprofessionalism and delay in almost all the projects being executed by HSCC.  During
the course of its study visit to Guwahati the Committee had a firsthand view of the manner
in which improper planning by HSCC had resulted in major design flaws.  The Committee,
therefore, strongly feels that the Ministry should follow a fair and transparent method for
the selection of the project consultants and ensure that the agencies involved are technically
competent to complete such big infrastructure projects, given the mixed track record of
HLL/HSCC in other projects. (Para 9.10)
The Committee is aggrieved at the failure of the Ministry to monitor the timely
completion of the project, mainly the construction activities in all the new AIIMS like
institutions. The Ministry should also ensure proper planning and timely execution for
completion of project. The Institute should always conduct detailed site investigations with
careful monitoring, collective working and effective coordination among all the stakeholders.
The poor performance of the project consultants and the contractors has been cited as the
most common reason behind the inordinate delay. The Committee is of the view that the
Ministry could have explored the idea of appointing a single agency for architectural design/
project consultancy and construction. The Committee also understands that employing a
single agency may raise concern about the flaws in any phase of the project being ignored.
Therefore, the Committee recommends that the Ministry should make a detailed assessment

194
of the consultants/contractors and employ a trusted company with a proven track record of
executing such infrastructural projects. The Committee also believes that designing and
planning is fundamental for the optimum utilisation of the space available. The Ministry
should learn from the architectural/design failures in AIIMS Rishikesh and ensure that
such mistakes are avoided in the construction of other AIIMS. (Para 9.13)
The Committee is concerned with the manner in which arbitration cases have been
dealt resulting in grant of compensations to contractors. The Committee recommends for an
arbitration mechanism/cell to be institutionalized in the Ministry that would deal with all
the arbitration cases and ensure speedy resolution. The Committee is concerned with arbitration
issues arising in case of reputed companies like L&T and would like to be apprised of all
such cases.  The Committee recommends that a report on the status of all arbitration cases
with respect to projects under PMSSY be provided to it so that a fair assessment of the
impact of arbitration cases can be done. (Para 9.15)
EXPANSION PROGRAMME OF AIIMS
The Committee is of the view that AIIMS like Institutes and hospitals should be
excluded from the NBC guidelines or some amendments in the guidelines can be made so
that AIIMS like Institutions are allowed to carry out vertical expansion. This will not only
ensure the optimum utilisation of land but also resolve the delays caused due to unavailability
of land. Vertical expansion will also lead to an organised set up. The OPDs can be operated
from the top floors and the other critical centres such as trauma and emergency, ICUs,
HDUs, OTs,  etc. can be functional from the lower floors of the building. The Ministry of
Health and Family Welfare should take up this proposal with the Ministry of Housing and
Urban Affairs and study the feasibility and implications of the recommendation so as to
facilitate vertical expansion in all AIIMS. (Para 9.18)
PROCUREMENT OF MEDICAL DEVICES AND EQUIPMENT
The Committee notes that the Procurement Support Agency (PSA) failed miserably in
its task to ensure timely delivery and installation of the medical equipment. Reasons cited
for non-installation of the medical equipment clearly highlight the fact that the PSA failed
to coordinate with the Institute that led to unnecessary delays. The Committee is of the
opinion that the PSA in collaboration with the Institute should have resolved the user
requirement, site details and other specific requirements with respect to the Medical Equipment
well in advance. The Committee is highly disappointed by the performance of PSA and
recommends for a strong monitoring of the function of PSA along with quarterly submission
of a status Report on the procurement of medical devices/equipment to the Committee
under the PMSSY scheme. (Para 9.21)
The Committee strongly underlines the need to follow a competitive tendering mechanism
for procurement of any equipment rather than a single bid tender procurement.  Downright
rejection on technical grounds should be avoided and any such rejection, if made, should
always be substantiated by a detailed evaluation.  The requirement of US FDA certification
for even some of the basic equipment is not only a burden on the exchequer but is also
an outright contempt of the Make in India campaign. The Committee, therefore, strongly

195
recommends that procurement of equipment/medical devices by the Institutes and the
procurement agencies should be determined by quality, price and fair trade practice.
(Para 9.23)
The Committee again emphasizes that non-transparent methods in tendering and award
of contracts raises high chances of corruption, irregularities, non-value addition and encourage
restrictive trade practices. Fair and equal opportunity to all the tenders promotes healthy
competition and also offers the best price to the procurement agency. (Para 9.24)
Attention of the Committee has also been drawn to the multiple rejections of bids on
technical grounds and unnecessary delays caused during finalizing the technical specification
of the equipment by individual AIIMS. In this regard, the Committee appreciates the suggestion
of AIIMS Bhubaneswar and strongly recommends finalizing uniform technical specifications
for equipment especially high-end/sophisticated equipment for all the AIIMS. The technical
specifications of all the equipment should be finalized by a team of technical experts only
after a detailed and scientific assessment. The Committee strongly recommends that the
Ministry/Institute should give serious consideration to the suggestions of AIIMS Bhubaneswar
for streamlining the procurement process. (Para 9.25)
The Committee further recommends that the other AIIMS like Institutions should
emulate AIIMS Bhubaneswar that has procured 130 medical equipment with ISO/BIS certification.
This would not only minimize the cost but also promote the domestic manufacturers thus
boosting Government’s flagship Make in India campaign. (Para 9.26)
The Committee also appreciates the guidelines regarding requirement/non-requirement
of US FDA/CE certification etc. in the procurement of medical devices. The Para 2(i) of the
guideline clearly states that wherever the Indian Standards are available the Hospitals/
Health Institutes/Organizations shall not insist on USFDA or CE certification. The Committee
is concerned regarding the misuse of Para 2(ii) of these guidelines that gives an opportunity
to the indenting organization to purchase medical equipment with USFDA certification in the
name of quality. (Para 9.27)
The Committee notes that even simple devices are being procured with USFDA mandatory
certification in the backdrop of the discretionary element in the guidelines. The Committee
terms such provision as just another attempt to camouflage the USFDA requirement in the
name of quality which is a setback to competitive pricing. The Committee, therefore, strongly
recommends that the Ministry should fix certification requirement as USFDA or CE or
Bureau of Indian Standards (BIS) or any other internationally accepted certification in the
interim till BIS standards are established universally so as to leave no option for discretion
and its misuse. BIS through its Medical Devices Management System Certification Scheme
IS/ISO 13485 gives certification to the medical equipment which are Made in India . The
Committee is of the view that procurement of indigenously produced medical equipment will
promote and encourage companies to manufacture their products under the  “Make in
India” scheme creating thereby additional employment opportunities and empowering secondary
and tertiary sector of the country.  The Committee, therefore, recommends that spirit of
the guideline under reference must be executed on the ground level to encourage flagship

196
“Make in India” programme. The matter may also be taken up on priority basis, with the
BIS so that every single equipment procured in India has a BIS standard which would be
equivalent to international quality certification. (Para 9.28)
The Committee has also come across several discrepancies in the procurement exercise.
Despite similar CMC/training/warranty conditions, a substantial price differential has been
observed in the procurement of the same medical equipment by the private corporate
hospitals and the new AIIMS. The Committee is concerned to note that AIIMS like Institutions
have purchased the same medical equipment at a higher cost than their counterparts in the
health industry. The Committee recommends that the Ministry should carry out a detailed
assessment in all such cases where price differential is more than 10% in the procurement
of equipment by private/corporate hospitals and AIIMS like Institutions. (Para 9.29)
The Committee would also like to flag the observations of CAG in its Report No. 10
of 2018 which inter alia  highlights the following:
(i) In six new AIIMS (Bhopal, Bhubaneswar, Jodhpur, Patna, Raipur and Rishikesh) 1,318
equipment valued at ````` 454 crore remained undelivered as on 31 March, 2017 for period
over two years from the due date of delivery. (Para 4.5.2)
(ii) Discrepancies in records relating to receipt and supply of medical equipment costing
````` 3.26 crore.  Audit noted that 75 equipment stated to have been supplied and installed
by M/s HLL at a cost of ````` 3.26 crore in three GMCIs viz. BMCRI-Bangalore, RIMS-
Ranchi and JMC-Jammu were either not received by the Institutes or were not traceable
in the records/premises of the institutes. (Case Study 5.1)
(iii) Procurement of equipment for GMCIs was to be made by the agencies appointed by the
Ministry or the State Government/GMCI Against the procurement orders for 818 equipment
costing ````` 482.21 crore for eleven GMCIs, 151 equipment costing ````` 51.72 crore were yet
to become available. (Para 5.5)
(iv) In ten GMCIs8, 408 equipment costing ````` 71.25 crore were either not installed or
installed with delay ranging from three months to over seven years as on 31 March, 2017.
The reasons for non-installation or delayed installation were again improper procurement
planning, pending civil and electrical works, non-availability of skilled manpower, etc.
(Para 5.6)
(v) In nine GMCIs 9, 977 equipment costing ````` 34.99 crore were idle/non-functional as on
31 March, 2017 due to lack of manpower, software problems lack of supporting equipment/
infrastructure, defect. (Para 5.7)
(vi) As per the procurement guidelines and provisions of the MoU signed between GMCIs
and the Ministry, GMCIs had to ensure that no alterations and additions were made
to the list of equipment and specifications firmed up by the medical experts. In
RIMS-Ranchi, JNMC-Aligarh, BMCRI-Bangalore, IMS-V aranasi, GMC-Mumbai and GMC-
Nagpur 293 medical equipment costing ````` 19.86 crore were procured for installation
in the upgraded facilities. Audit noted that these equipment did not fall in the
approved list of medical equipment of the Ministry. (Para 5.8)
(Para 9.30)

197
The Committee takes a serious view of the observations of CAG with respect to the
performance of the PSA and would like to be apprised of the time bound corrective actions
taken on the CAG observations so that the procurement process can be streamlined.
(Para 9.31)
SHORTAGE OF FACULTY AND NON-FACULTY STAFF
The Committee appreciates that the Ministry is also considering common recruitment
process for the non-faculty of all AIIMS by clubbing together all the vacancies. The Committee
concurs with the view of the Ministry that one AIIMS can hold recruitment of a particular
category for all the other AIIMS like Institutions. The Committee is of the view that the
Ministry should take this proposal with the Central Institute Body (CIB) and take necessary
steps to execute the same on ground level. The Committee expects that CIB would play a
pro-active role in this matter. (Para 9.34)
The Committee strongly recommends for a centralised recruitment process for all non-
faculty staff preferably monitored by AIIMS, Delhi or through a centralized reputed agency,
so that the irregularities in the recruitment process are minimised and the issue of staff
shortage is addressed through a nationwide selection process. The issues related to shortage
of faculty staff have been dealt in respective AIIMS Chapter. (Para 9.35)
The Committee would also like to be apprised of the number of faculty that have
been employed from other premier Medical Institutes  like AIIMS, JIPMER, PGI, etc.
and are working full-time in the new AIIMS like Institutions. The Institutes should also
submit a status note on the number of applications received from such candidates and
the number of candidates employed and rejected/not found suitable. The Committee also
desires the Institute to submit reasons for the rejection of such candidates.
(Para 9.36)
QUALITY OF MEDICAL EDUCATION
The Committee is of the considered view that starting PG courses is irrelevant until
and unless the Institute is able to provide quality training under the supervision of full-time
expert faculties. The Committee strongly emphasizes the importance of proper clinical training
of the post-graduate students. The Committee further recommends that the AIIMS like
Institutions should emphasize on the applied aspects of various disciplines through practical
training. AIIMS like Institutions should be the frontrunner in filling the critical gaps in the
health sector by producing well trained specialists in different disciplines.
(Para 9.38)
The Committee is also concerned to note that most of the AIIMS do not have any
faculty at the HOD/Professor level from AIIMS Delhi or PGI or JIPMER or any premier
National Institute. The experience and expertise of faculty/HOD has an impact on the
quality of Medical Graduates. In most of the AIIMS, the faculty lacks any teaching
experience from any of the premier Institutes. This raises serious concern about the
dilution of the AIIMS Delhi benchmark. The Committee would also like to be apprised of
the number of clinical cases, discipline-wise that have been dealt by the PG students from
various Departments. (Para 9.39)

198
LOW BED OCCUPANCY RATE
The Committee is of the opinion that the AIIMS like Institutions that were anticipated
to serve as an excellent centre of medical care have failed miserably to accomplish the
desired objective. The low bed occupancy rate speaks volumes about the prevalent distrust
in the healthcare facilities of the new AIIMS. The Committee is astonished to note that
even in Obstetrics and Gynaecology Department, only 37% bed occupancy exist.
(Para 9.41)
The Committee is coerced to draw comparison with a hitherto neglected institute i.e.
Regional Institute of Medical Sciences (RIMS) Imphal where the Committee witnessed for
itself during the course of its study visit that the bed occupancy rate is 100 % in RIMS
Imphal. The Committee also noted that there were 210 beds in Department of Obstetrics
and Gynaecology with 100% occupancy throughout the year and RIMS carried out  11,000
deliveries per year. The Committee would appreciate if the new AIIMS like Institutions
match at least 25% of the performance delivery of RIMS Imphal which the Committee
witnessed for itself during the study visit of the Committee (Para 9.42)
The Committee recommends for quarterly assessment of the bed occupancy rate in all
AIIMS. Further, all the AIIMS should submit a status note citing reasons for its failure
towards optimum utilisation of the tertiary care facilities and Low Bed Occupancy Rate.
(Para 9.43)
GOVERNING BODY (GB)
The Committee strongly believes regular review and progress meetings instils a sense
of accountability in the Institute and this system of check and balance is very important
for smooth running of the Institute. The Committee also recommends that all major decisions
by the Director and the President of the Regional AIIMS, should be approved by the IB/
GB and any new project should be taken up after due approval of the IB/GB.  The Committee
also recommends that no ex-post-facto  approval should be given under any circumstances.
The Ministry should explore the possibility to assign overall super visionary role to CIB,
while keeping the functional autonomy of each AIIMS intact. The Committee also recommends
that the vacancy in respect of Members of Parliament in the Governing Body of all new
AIIMS must be filled up without further delay. This will not only ensure representation of
the people in the Governing body but will also result in its better functioning in a democratic
manner. (Para 9.46)
The Committee also welcomes the recent process of decentralization in all the six AIIMS
with the appointment of their respective Presidents which would entail greater accountability
and better monitoring. The constitution of a Central Institute Body (CIB) is also a step in the
right direction, as this would facilitate the evolution of common standards across all AIIMS on
all important benchmarks like human resources, procurement, expansion and project execution.
The Committee would also like to appreciate the increasing receptivity and meticulous approach
of the Ministry in taking corrective measures in rectifying the flaws in the execution of PMSSY
scheme and hopes that objective of the PMSSY scheme i.e. to provide tertiary healthcare
facilities in the remotest regions of the country is realized. (Para 9.47)

199
MONITORING MECHANISM
The Committee strongly recommends that the State Project Monitoring Committee
(PMC) should meet regularly and monitor the progress of the work being carried out in
each AIIMS and GMCs. Each Institute should submit a quarterly report to the Ministry
updating it about the status of infrastructure, faculty and construction activities.
(Para 9.49)
The Committee also strongly recommends that AIIMS like Institutions should be included
in the Mera Aspataal/My hospital initiative of the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare
that captures patient feedback for the services received at the hospital through user-friendly
multiple channels such as Short Message Service (SMS), Outbound Dialling (OBD) mobile
application and web portal. The Committee is of the opinion that patients feedback is
critical for ensuring accountability of the healthcare system in the country. Therefore, the
Committee recommends for inclusion of all AIIMS like Institutions in the Mera Aspataal
initiative in order to enhance its operational proficiency. (Para 9.50)
The Committee also recommends that the Secretary/Divisional Head Level Officers
from the PMSSY should make frequent field visits to all the AIIMS like Institutions to
assess the ground realities of the Institutes. The Committee strongly urges the officers
from the PMSSY division to carry out regular inspections of the sites rather than just
relying on the progress report submitted by the respective Institutes. Ministry should also
ensure that the work on paper corresponds to the actual performance of the Institute.
(Para 9.51)
The Committee notes that there are several resource constraints in all the States in
the Health Sector. The Committee is of the view that despite the existing constraints, all
AIIMS like Institutions have got preferential treatment from the Ministry and the State but
these Institutes have failed to use this preferential treatment to their advantage. The
Committee is disappointed to note that all the AIIMS like Institutions struggle in optimally
utilizing the available resources. The Committee strongly recommends the Institutes to
take suo moto  cognizance of the delay in construction activities and play a more proactive
role in establishing themselves as centre of apex medical care and facilities.
(Para 9.52)
DIALYSIS FACILITIES
The Committee, therefore, strongly recommends that all new AIIMS should establish
a fully equipped Nephrology Department immediately. In view of the rising demand of
dialysis services, a fully functional Nephrology Department is critical in providing tertiary
healthcare facilities. The Committee also recommends that all AIIMS like Institutions should
ensure availability of adequate number of functional dialysis machines and skilled manpower
in the Institute. (Para 9.54)
Attention of the Committee has also been drawn to the procurement of Dialysis machine
at a much higher price in the new AIIMS. The Committee observes that same dialysis
machines with the same specifications have been procured by State Government at a much
lower price. Moreover, the same dialysis machines have been procured under the MPLAD

200
scheme at one third of the price paid by new AIIMS. The Committee takes this as a serious
irregularity in the procurement policy and process. The Committee, therefore, strongly
recommends for instituting an enquiry into the matter and accordingly apprise the Committee
about the reasons and compelling circumstances under which the dialysis machines were
purchased at such higher prices in the new AIIMS. (Para 9.55)
HIGHER EDUCATION FINANCING AGENCY (HEFA)
The Committee is aware that raising funds from the market through HEFA is a
herculean task and there would be a requirement of pledging of Government resources as
collateral to HEFA to obtain loans. It has also been further mentioned that these loans
would be serviced through internal accruals. The Committee is concerned to note that non-
judicious usage of loans from HEFA by the regional AIIMS will create a vicious cycle
wherein these AIIMS would be subject to a debt trap and would again seek budgetary
support to service the loans obtained from HEFA. The Committee is of the considered
opinion that any loan proposal sent to HEFA should be duly vetted by the CIB after obtaining
a comparative assessment from all the six AIIMS regarding the sourcing of funds and the
ability of AIIMS to service the funds.  The Committee foresees a situation where reckless
expenditure from the loans obtained from HEFA may result in a situation where the incumbent
Directors of the regional AIIMS may retire but the new incumbent would be faced with a
fresh challenge of servicing the loans obtained from HEFA. (Para 9.57)
The Committee would like to be updated on a quarterly basis regarding all loan/
advances obtained from HEFA alongwith the measures/proposals under consideration towards
timely repayment of these loans. The Committee also recommends that accountability of
these loans be clearly fixed so that there are no legal issues in this regard in future. The
Committee also recommends tapping donations and CSR funds as has been done by AIIMS
Bhubaneswar and AIIMS Patna for projects like Dharmshala, etc. by the regional AIIMS.
(Para 9.58)
SPECIALITY/SUPER SPECIALITY DEPARTMENTS AND ADV ANCE CENTRES
The Committee appreciates the good intention behind construction of such Departments
but after a broad study of all the AIIMS like Institutes, the Committee realizes that there
is an urgent need to scrutinize the proper functioning of such Departments. In many
AIIMS, the required infrastructure is present but due to acute shortage of the medical
faculty/specialist, the super-speciality departments are non-functional. This is a typical example
of constructing buildings without foundation. With no staff, the infrastructure and the
resources are lying untapped and unutilized. (Para 9.60)
The Committee is especially worried about the viability of such Advance Centres in
these six AIIMS like Institutions that have a very low average daily OPD footfall, with the
highest being 2949 at AIIMS Bhubaneswar  and the lowest being 1300 at AIIMS Raipur. The
Committee cannot comprehend the need for establishing advance centre for Institutes with
such low OPD footfall, especially when these Institutes have failed to make optimum utilization
of the available resource and infrastructure. The Committee also emphasizes that many
faculty posts are still lying vacant in various Departments and even Speciality and Super

201
Speciality Departments are non-functional. In such scenario, these Institutes vying for
Advance Centres only reflect a poor understanding of the existing grim reality.
(Para 9.62)
The Committee strongly recommends that the Ministry/Institute should carry out a
cost benefit analysis before any proposal for building an advance centre in the new AIIMS
is passed. The Committee would like the Ministry/Institute to furnish a status note and a
cost benefit analysis on the proposed and sanctioned advance centers in all the AIIMS like
Institutions. The Committee is of the view that the decisions regarding establishment of
Advance Centers in all the new AIIMS should be taken by Central Institute Body.  The
Committee also strongly recommends that in the new AIIMS, an advance centre should only
be established if the Institutions are able to achieve an OPD level which is 50% of the
average daily OPD footfall of AIIMS Delhi. The utility of such advance centres has to be
closely scrutinized so that the available resources are optimally put to use.
(Para 9.63)
The Committee also flags the fellowship programmes in different Departments that are
being carried out by these Institutes. The Committee strongly recommends that such fellowship
programmes should be carried out only when the Institutes are capable of providing the
required infrastructure and quality training and are operating at its full capacity.
(Para 9.64)
OPD FOOTFALL
The Committee also hopes that the possibility of duplicating OPD footfall data should
be avoided. The Committee strongly recommends that all the patients should be given a
unique ID that should form the basis of measuring patient load in an Institute. The possibility
to exaggerate OPD footfall/number of procedures (major and minor surgeries) when the
same patient visits multiple Departments in a day should be avoided. The Committee strongly
recommends all the Institutes to measure accurate OPD footfall/procedures and avert from
extrapolating the OPD/surgery numbers. The Committee strongly advocates strengthening
the system of online registration for all the AIIMS like Institutions and work towards
effective implementation of Hospital Management Information System. (Para 9.65)
The Committee would also like to be apprised whether the establishment of AIIMS in
the six States has had any impact on the patient load in AIIMS Delhi from these States.
The idea behind regional AIIMS was to provide tertiary health care facilities in all the
States so that the patients from these States do not have to rush to AIIMS Delhi for even
basic medical care. Therefore the Committee desires AIIMS Delhi to submit a report on the
impact these new AIIMS like Institutions had on patient load. AIIMS Delhi should also
submit a report on the number of patients that were transferred from the regional AIIMS
to AIIMS Delhi. (Para 9.66)
OPERATION THEATRES AND SURGERY ISSUES
The Committee strongly recommends all the AIIMS like Institute to expedite the
construction of the Operation Theatres and provide the required infrastructure. The Committee

202
also recommends all the AIIMS Like Institutes to carry out a detailed analysis of the
operation theatre utilisation at their respective Institutes and submit the same to the
Committee. The Committee also believes that there is an urgent need to follow strict
Operation Theatre guidelines so that OTs start on time and the time between two surgeries
in a single day is optimally utilised and not wasted. Better planning of operation schedule
and timings can definitely increase the efficiency of OTs. The Committee also takes a
strong note of the existing large discrepancies in the data provided by each Institute. The
Committee strongly recommends following a uniform criteria for classification of surgeries
as minor and major so that accurate data related to the number of surgeries conducted can
be obtained. (Para 9.68)
The CAG Report on PMSSY has highlighted various inadequacies and problem areas in
setting up of new AIIMS and upgradation programme of Government Medical Colleges. The
CAG Report No. 10 contains observations relating to cost and time overrun, excess payment
of mobilization advance, delay in delivery and installation of medical equipment, shortage
of faculty and non-faculty posts, non-functional speciality and super speciality Departments
etc. The Committee would like to be apprised of the measures that have been taken by the
Ministry and the Institutes to overcome all the inadequacies and address the CAG observations
in its audit Report. (Para 9.70)
The Committee beside recommending for decentralization of healthcare facilities in the
most remote areas of the country, is inclined to emphasize that Central Government and
State Government must make policy and chalk out strategy and formulate/appropriate course
of action for delivering Health Care Services at the Doorstep with the purpose of making
Ayushman Bharat a successful scheme. (Para 9.71)

I. Opening Remarks
2. At the outset, the Chairman welcomed the Members of the Committee and informed them agenda of
the meeting of the day i.e. to hear the views of the Secretary, Department of Health Research, Additional
Secretary of the Department of Health and Family Welfare along with the Directors of AIIMS, Delhi, Jodhpur,
Rishikesh, BMHRC, and other officials on the ‘Functioning of AIIMS like Institutions under PMSSY and
BMHRC’ in the light of visit of the Committee to Jodhpur, Rishikesh and Bhopal from 1st to 8th July 2018,
especially its observations about the ground realities and the challenges being faced by those Institutes in
their operational and routine functioning viz. irregularities in recruitment of faculty and staff, procurement
of equipments, delay in implementation of computerization projects, problem of contractual staff, bypassing
of the Governing bodies etc. Thus, during the day’s meeting, the Committee was to take into account its
observations/findings during its visit vis-a-vis comments of the Ministry thereon before making its final
observations and recommendations on the subject.
2.1 Thereafter, the Chairman the Committee about seeking of leave of absence by the Secretary, Department
of Health and Family Welfare on account of the necessity to participate in the PMNCH Board meeting in
Stockholm, Sweden and also by Director AIIMS, Bhopal required to be present in the prior fixed meeting of
Parliamentary Committee of Rajbhasha on the same day. He acceded to the request and permitted Additional
Secretary, Department of Health and Family Welfare and MS, AIIMS, Bhopal to attend the meeting.
2.2 The Committee, then, authorised the Chairman to share its findings/feedback with the Hon’ble Prime
Minister in order to apprise him of the ground realities about the problems being faced by these Institutes in
carrying out operational and routine matters so that corrective measures are initiated at the highest level on
priority basis.
II. Oral evidence of the Additional Secretary, Department of Health and Family Welfare
3. The Committee, then, heard the views of Additional Secretary of Department of Health and Family
Welfare on the ‘Functioning of AIIMS like Institution under PMSSY’. The Joint Secretary of the Department
of Health and Family Welfare gave a power point presentation on the performance and progress of six
functional AIIMS along with their views on Committees observations/findings regarding the functioning of
AIIMS, Jodhpur, Rishikesh and Bhopal. He inter alia highlighted the following points w.r.t Six AIIMS like
Institutions:-

207
(i) the status and expected date of completion of construction projects; (ii) status of important hospital
services like emergency, Trauma Care Specialities, Super Specialities etc. (iii) functioning of Hospital
blocks in terms of bed strength, functional operation; (iv) increase in faculty strength from 18.5% in
the year 2014 to 46% in 2018 as on 31st July 2018, increase in average annual OPD attendance from
5 lakh in the year 2014 to 30 lakh in the year 2018 and increase in the number of post graduation seats
from Nil in the year 2014 to 328 in the year 2018; (v) policy interventions and new initiatives for civil
construction projects and engagement of reputed construction companies; (vi) expansion of Intensive
Care Services in phased manner; (vii) procurement of State of Art Medical Equipment on single bid/
proprietary basis and Da Vinci Robotic Arm and reasons for price differences; (viii) criterion for the
site selection for construction of new AIIMS under PMSSY; (ix) faculty shortage at new AIIMS and
steps to enhance faculty availability at National level; (x) constitution of Central Institute Body (CIB)
to consider common policy matters and ensure uniform standards for new AIIMS; (xi) role of
Governing Body of AIIMS; (xii) merger of AIIMS, Bhopal with BMHRC; and (xiii) contractual
posts of AYUSH staff etc.
4. The Committee, then, took up in detail the issue of the merger of AIIMS, Bhopal and BMHRC.
Prof. Balram Bhargav, Secretary, Department of Health Research submitted before the Committee that one
time up gradation of Faculty of BMHRC is required to be done to move ahead with the merger of the two
Institutes.
5. Thereafter, Director AIIMS, Delhi suggested required measures/initiatives in order to enhance the
quality of patient care at regional AIIMS on lines of AIIMS, Delhi. He added that focus should be on
enhancing academic activities and promoting research projects by Intra-mural grants and projects funding.
6. During the course of the meeting, Members raised certain queries to which the witnesses replied. The
Chairman, then, directed the witnesses to furnish within a week period the detailed written replies to the
queries that remained unanswered in the meeting. The Chairman also directed that the Directors of AIIMS,
Delhi, Jodhpur, Rishikesh, Bhopal to furnish verbatim record of proceedings of meetings held during the
Committee’s visit from 1st to 8th July, 2018.
7. A verbatim record of the proceedings of the meeting was kept.
8. The Committee then adjourned at 6.16 
P.M.

I. Opening Remarks
2. At the outset, the Chairman welcomed the Members of the Committee and informed the Members that
the meeting has been convened to hear the views of the Secretary, Ministry of Health and Family Welfare
along with the Directors of AIIMS, Raipur, Patna, Bhubaneswar and Bhopal on the subject- “The functioning
of AIIMS like Institutions” with special reference to:- (i) upgradation of Government Medical Colleges
under the Pradhan Mantri Swasthya Suraksha Yojana (PMSSY) and (ii) status of AIIMS, Raipur, Patna,
Bhubaneswar and Bhopal.
3. The Committee while deliberating and taking cognizance of the various challenges in the functioning
of AIIMS like. Institutions and the vital importance of the subject with an objective to counter regional
imbalances in catering to the healthcare needs in the country, felt that it needed to visit AIIMS Patna. The
Committee, accordingly, decided to undertake a visit to Patna and Kolkata from 26
th November, 2018 to 30th
November, 2018 to examine :- (i) The functioning of AIIMS-like Institutions; and (ii) Affordability of Cancer
and Duchenne Muscular Dystrophy (DMD) treatment, provided that the Winter Session of Parliament is not
held during that period.
II. Oral evidence of Director, AIIMS Raipur and Director, AIIMS Patna
4. The Committee first heard the views of Director, AIIMS, Raipur who made a power point presentation
which inter alia highlighted the following points:-
(i) status of infrastructure, details of the super specialty disciplines, Nursing and Medical College
and other facilities like AYUSH, Trauma ICU, PMR (Physical Medicine and Rehabilitation),
Modular Operation Theatre etc;
(ii) post graduate courses being offered in 22 disciplines and 18 speciality and 11 super speciality
departments;
(iii) having a system to help and monitor the quality of research, through research cell and Institute
Ethics Committee; and
(iv) manpo wer details and recruitment status of faculty and non-faculty of AIIMS Raipur.
5. The Committee, then, heard the views of Director, AIIMS, Patna. In his power point presentation
inter alia he highlighted the following status of the Institute:-
(i) completion of 6 floors and basement of OPD building;
(ii) total 680 beds in the Institution, 30 Intensive Care Units (ICUs) and High Dependency Units
(HDUs) Beds alongwith 13 functional operation theatres as against 28 sanctioned;
(iii) details regarding average bed occupancy;
(iv) facilities of imaging Computed Tomography (CT), Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI), Digital
Subtraction Angiography (DSA) along with Blood Bank, Central Lab Services etc.;
(v) details of extramural research projects;
(vi) details of 39 functional departments and 128 faculty members;
(vii) scheduled commencement of Post Graduate courses; and
(vii) annual intake in MBBS and nursing courses.

210
6. During the course of the meeting, Members raised certain queries to which the witnesses replied. The
Chairman directed the Secretary, Ministry of Health and Family Welfare to furnish updated information
w.r.t. six AIIMS like Institutions under examination. The Chairman took up in detail the issue of contractual
workers in AIIMS Bhopal and regularization of AYUSH doctors. The Committee recommended the
regularization of contractual workers in AIIMS Bhopal on humanitarian grounds. The Committee also
deliberated on (i) shortage of faculty and super specialists; (ii) important departments such as Gastroenterology,
Nephrology, Medical Oncology etc. non-functional in the new AIIMS; (iii) need for strengthening of the
AYUSH department in AIIMS, Raipur and Patna. The Committee appreciated the functioning of AIIMS
Bhubaneswar.
7. Thereafter, the Members of the Committee gave suggestions w.r.t. improving the functioning of
AIIMS like institutions for meeting various challenges of faculty shortage, monitoring mechanism etc.
Members were of the view that there was a need for a centralized recruitment policy for recruitment in
AIIMS, incentivizing the faculty positions in AIIMS by offering special remuneration/special package, need
for increase in seats for nursing and paramedical staff, need for a centralized monitoring centre for all
AIIMS like Institutions etc.
8. A verbatim record of the proceedings of the meeting was kept.
9. The Committee then adjourned at 5.00 
P.M.
